The Common Law

by Oliver Wendell Holmes Jr.

1881 2026

This is an AI modernization of The Common Law into contemporary English. The original is available from Project Gutenberg. Also available as an epub. The original text is also here.

Editor's Introduction

Oliver Wendell Holmes Jr. was born in Boston in 1841, the son of the doctor and poet of the same name. He left Harvard in 1861 to fight in the Civil War and was wounded three times, at Ball's Bluff, at Antietam and in the Chancellorsville campaign. After the war he read law, practised in Boston, edited the American Law Review and brought out a new edition of Kent's Commentaries. In the winter of 1880 the Lowell Institute asked him to give a course of public lectures, and this book, published in 1881, is those lectures written up. It made his name. Within two years he was a judge of the Supreme Judicial Court of Massachusetts, and from 1902 to 1932 he sat on the Supreme Court of the United States.

The first paragraph is the most quoted thing he ever wrote: the life of the law has not been logic, it has been experience. The rest of the book is the evidence. Holmes takes the main branches of the common law in turn (crime, tort, the liability of carriers and innkeepers, possession, contract, inheritance) and traces each rule back to where it came from. Again and again the origin is something nobody believes any more: the ox that gored a man was surrendered to be punished, a ship was sued as if it were the wrongdoer, a buyer stood in the seller's shoes because an heir was thought to carry on the dead man's person. The reason disappears and the rule stays, and a later age invents a new reason for it.

His second argument is that the law's standards are external. The criminal law and the law of negligence ask what a reasonable man would have foreseen, not what this defendant felt, and the law does not care, in most cases, what a contracting party privately meant. That idea runs through modern American law, and much of what is still argued about in it starts from here.

The book is harder than its reputation, because Holmes wrote for lawyers. He cites year books, Roman jurists and German professors without introducing them, and he uses Latin and Law French as working vocabulary. This edition retells it in modern English. Terms of art are explained the first time they appear. The Latin and French are put into English or glossed where they stand. References to pages of the 1881 book now point to the Lecture. The original has more than eight hundred footnotes, almost all of them bare citations to the law reports; they have been dropped, and the 97 notes in which Holmes argues a point are kept in place as his. One slip of his own in Lecture Eight, where both printings name the plaintiff and the sense needs the promisor, has been corrected. The text as he published it, footnotes and all, is on the companion page.

Lecture I: Early Forms of Liability

The object of this book is to present a general view of the Common Law. To do that, we need other tools besides logic. It is something to show that the consistency of a system requires a particular result, but it is not everything. The life of the law has not been logic: it has been experience. The felt necessities of the time, the moral and political theories that prevail, intuitions of public policy, whether openly stated or unconscious, even the prejudices that judges share with their fellow men, have had a good deal more to do than the syllogism with deciding the rules by which men should be governed. The law embodies the story of a nation's development through many centuries, and it cannot be treated as if it contained only the axioms and corollaries of a book of mathematics. In order to know what it is, we must know what it has been, and what it tends to become. We must consult, by turns, history and the existing theories of legislation. But the hardest work will be to understand how the two combine into new products at every stage. The substance of the law at any given time corresponds pretty closely, so far as it goes, with what is then thought convenient. But its form and machinery, and how well it is able to produce the results we want from it, depend very much upon its past.

In Massachusetts today there are, on the one hand, a great many rules that are fully accounted for by their obvious good sense. On the other hand, there are some that can only be understood by going back to the infancy of legal procedure among the German tribes, or to the social condition of Rome under the Decemvirs, the board of ten men who first wrote down Roman law in the fifth century before Christ.

I shall use the history of our law as far as it is needed to explain an idea or to interpret a rule, and no further. In doing so there are two errors, equally to be avoided by writer and reader alike. One is to suppose that because an idea seems very familiar and natural to us, it has always been so. Many things we take for granted had to be painfully fought out or thought out in past times. The other mistake is the opposite one of asking too much of history. We start with man full grown. It may be assumed that the earliest barbarian whose practices we shall look at had a good many of the same feelings and passions as ourselves.

The first subject to be discussed is the general theory of liability, civil and criminal. The Common Law has changed a good deal since our series of law reports began, and the search for a theory that can now be said to prevail is very much a study of tendencies. I believe it will be instructive to go back to the early forms of liability and to start from them.

It is commonly known that the early forms of legal procedure were grounded in vengeance. Modern writers have thought that Roman law started from the blood feud, the private war of revenge between one family and another, and all the authorities agree that German law began that way. The feud led to the composition, a payment by which the feud was bought off, at first optional and then compulsory. The composition's gradual advance can be traced in the Anglo-Saxon laws, and by the time of William the Conqueror the feud was pretty well broken up, though not extinguished. The killings and house-burnings of an earlier day became the appeals of mayhem and arson. (An appeal, in old English law, was a private accusation of crime brought by the injured person himself.) The appeal "of the peace and of wounds" (de pace et plagis) and the appeal of mayhem became, or rather were in substance, the action of trespass, which is still familiar to lawyers: the action for a direct and forcible wrong to a man's person or property. But since the compensation recovered in the appeal was the alternative to vengeance, we might expect its scope to be limited to the scope of vengeance. Vengeance implies a feeling of blame, and an opinion, however distorted by passion, that a wrong has been done. It can hardly go very far beyond the case of a harm inflicted on purpose: even a dog distinguishes between being stumbled over and being kicked.

Whether for this reason or another, the early English appeals for personal violence seem to have been confined to intentional wrongs. Glanvill, the first great writer on English law, mentions brawls, blows, and wounds, all forms of intentional violence. In the fuller description of these appeals given by Bracton, the great lawyer of the thirteenth century, it is made quite clear that they were based on intentional assaults. The appeal de pace et plagis alleged an intentional assault, described the weapons used, and gave the length and depth of the wound. The accuser also had to show that he had immediately raised the hue and cry, the public outcry that called the neighborhood out in pursuit. So when Bracton speaks of the lesser offenses, which were not sued by way of appeal, he gives as examples only intentional wrongs, such as blows with the fist, flogging, wounding, insults, and so forth. The cause of action in the trespass cases reported in the earlier Year Books (the medieval reports of cases argued in the English courts) and in the Abbreviatio Placitorum, a digest of still older court records, is always an intentional wrong. It was only later, and after argument, that trespass was extended to cover harms that the defendant foresaw but did not intend as the consequence of his act. From there it was extended again to injuries nobody foresaw.

This order of development, you will notice, does not quite fit an opinion that some have held: that it was characteristic of early law not to look beyond the visible external fact, the damnum corpore corpori datum, "harm done by a body to a body." It has been thought that to inquire into the inner condition of the defendant, into his guilt or innocence, implies a refinement of legal thinking equally foreign to Rome before the Lex Aquilia (the Roman statute on wrongful damage to property) and to England when trespass took its shape. I do not know of any very satisfactory evidence that a man was generally held liable, either in Rome or in England, for the accidental consequences even of his own act. But whatever the earliest law may have been, the account just given shows the starting point of the system we have to deal with. Our system of private liability for the consequences of a man's own acts, that is, for his trespasses, started from the notion of actual intent and actual personal blame.

The original principles of liability for harm done by another person or by a thing have been less carefully studied so far than those governing trespass, and so I shall give the rest of this Lecture to them. I shall try to show that this liability, too, had its root in the passion of revenge, and to point out the changes by which it reached its present form. But I shall not confine myself strictly to what that purpose requires. It is not only most interesting to trace the transformation through its whole extent; the story will also give an instructive example of the way the law has grown, without a break, from barbarism to civilization. Furthermore, it will throw much light on some important and peculiar doctrines that there will be no chance to come back to later.

A very common phenomenon, and one very familiar to the student of history, is this. The customs, beliefs, or needs of a primitive time establish a rule or a formula. In the course of centuries the custom, belief, or necessity disappears, but the rule remains. The reason that gave rise to the rule has been forgotten, and ingenious minds set themselves to work out how it is to be accounted for. Some ground of policy is thought of that seems to explain it and to reconcile it with the present state of things. Then the rule adapts itself to the new reasons that have been found for it, and starts on a new career. The old form receives a new content, and in time even the form changes to fit the meaning it has received. The subject before us illustrates this course of events very clearly.

I will begin with a medley of examples, each embodying a distinct rule, and each with its own plausible and apparently sufficient ground of policy to explain it.

A man keeps an animal known to be vicious, which escapes and does his neighbor damage. He can prove that the animal got out through no negligence of his, but he is held liable all the same. Why? Because, says the analytical jurist, although he was not negligent at the moment it escaped, he was guilty of a more remote heedlessness, or negligence, or fault, in keeping such a creature at all. And one whose fault causes damage ought to pay for it.

A baker's man, driving his master's cart to deliver hot rolls one morning, runs another man down. The master has to pay for it. And when the master has asked why he should pay for the wrongful act of an independent and responsible human being, he has been answered, from the time of Ulpian down to that of Austin, that it is because he was to blame for employing an unfit person. If he replies that he took the greatest possible care in choosing his driver, he is told that this is no excuse. Then perhaps the reason shifts, and he is told that there ought to be a remedy against someone who can pay the damages, or that the wrongful acts that ordinary human experience shows are likely to happen in the course of the job are chargeable to the job.

Next, take a case where a limit has been set to a liability that used to be unlimited. In 1851 Congress passed a law, still in force, under which the owners of ships, in all the more common cases of loss at sea, can hand over the vessel and the freight then due on her to those who suffered the loss; and it provides that when they have done so, all further proceedings against the owners shall stop. The legislators to whom we owe this act argued that if a merchant puts part of his property into a risky venture, it is reasonable that his stake should be confined to what he puts at risk. That is a principle similar to the one on which corporations have been created in such numbers in America during the last fifty years.

It was a rule of criminal pleading in England down into the present century that an indictment for homicide must state the value of the instrument that caused the death. The purpose was to let the king, or whoever held the right from him, claim the forfeiture of the deodand, "as an accursed thing," in Blackstone's words. (The deodand was the object that had caused a man's death, forfeited to the Crown.)

I could go on multiplying examples, but these are enough to show how far apart the points are that have to be brought together. As a first step toward a generalization, we must consider what is to be found in ancient and independent systems of law.

There is a well-known passage in Exodus that we shall need to remember later: "If an ox gore a man or a woman, that they die: then the ox shall be surely stoned, and his flesh shall not be eaten; but the owner of the ox shall be quit." When we turn from the Jews to the Greeks, we find the principle of that passage built up into a system. Plutarch, in his life of Solon, tells us that a dog that had bitten a man was to be handed over tied to a log four cubits long. Plato, in his Laws, made elaborate provision for many cases of this kind. If a slave killed a man, he was to be given up to the dead man's relatives. If he wounded a man, he was to be given up to the injured party to do with as he pleased. The same held if he did damage to which the injured party had not himself contributed. In either case, if the owner failed to surrender the slave, he was bound to make good the loss. If a beast killed a man, it was to be killed and thrown out beyond the borders. If a lifeless thing caused a death, it was to be thrown beyond the borders in the same way, and expiation was to be made. Nor was all this an ideal creation of merely imagined law. In one of the speeches of Aeschines it is said that "we banish beyond our borders stocks and stones and steel, voiceless and mindless things, if they chance to kill a man; and if a man commits suicide, bury the hand that struck the blow afar from its body." This is mentioned as an everyday matter, obviously without any thought that it was extraordinary, and only to set up a contrast with the honors heaped on Demosthenes. As late as the second century after Christ, the traveler Pausanias noted with some surprise that the Athenians still sat in judgment on lifeless things in the Prytaneum, their ancient town hall. Plutarch credits Draco with founding the practice.

In Roman law we find similar principles of noxae deditio, noxal surrender (the handing over of the offending creature or thing to the injured party), gradually leading to further results. The Twelve Tables, Rome's first written code (451 B.C.), provided that if an animal had done damage, either the animal was to be surrendered or the damage paid for. We learn from Gaius that the same rule applied to wrongs done by children or slaves, and there is some trace of it for lifeless things as well.

The Roman lawyers, not looking beyond their own system or their own time, used their wits to find an explanation that would show the law as they found it to be reasonable. Gaius said it was unjust that the fault of children or slaves should cost their parents or owners more than the bodies of the wrongdoers themselves. Ulpian reasoned that this was true all the more, a fortiori, of things without life, which are incapable of fault. This way of approaching the question treats the right of surrender as if it were a limit on a liability incurred by the parent or owner, a liability that would naturally, and to begin with, be unlimited. But if that is what was meant, it puts the cart before the horse. The right of surrender was not introduced as a limit on liability. On the contrary, in Rome and Greece alike, payment was introduced as the alternative to a failure to surrender.

The action was not based, as it would be nowadays, on the fault of the parent or owner. If it had been, it would always have been brought against the person who had control of the slave or animal at the time it did the harm, the person who, if anyone, was to blame for not preventing it. Far from this being the practice, the person to be sued was whoever owned the slave or animal at the time of the suit. The action followed the guilty thing into whoever's hands it came. And in a curious contrast with the principle as later turned around to suit still more modern views of public policy, if the animal was of a wild nature—that is, in the very case of the most dangerous animals—the owner stopped being liable the moment it escaped, because at that moment he stopped being its owner. There seems to have been no other or wider liability under the old law, even where a slave did the wrong with his master's knowledge, unless perhaps the slave was a mere tool in his master's hands. Gaius and Ulpian showed an inclination to cut noxal surrender down to a privilege of the owner in cases of misdeeds committed without his knowledge. But Ulpian is obliged to admit that by the ancient law, according to Celsus, the action was noxal where a slave did wrong even with his master's knowledge and consent.

Footnote: The maxim was Noxa caput sequitur: the liability follows the offender himself.

All this shows very clearly that the owner's liability was merely a way of getting at the slave or animal that was the immediate cause of offense. In other words, the object of the Greek and early Roman process was vengeance on the immediate offender, not compensation from the master or owner. The owner's liability was simply a liability of the offending thing. In the primitive customs of Greece it was enforced by a legal proceeding directed expressly against the object, alive or lifeless. The Roman Twelve Tables made the owner the defendant instead of the thing itself, but did not in any way change the ground of liability or its limit. The change was simply a device to let the owner protect his interest.

Footnote: Gaius says that a noxal action may turn into a direct one, and a direct action into a noxal one. If the head of a family commits a wrong and is then adopted, or becomes a slave, a noxal action now lies against his new master in place of the direct action against himself as the wrongdoer.

But it may be asked how lifeless objects came to be pursued in this way, if the object of the procedure was to satisfy the passion of revenge. Learned men have been ready to find the reason in the habit, common to savages and children, of treating lifeless nature as if it were a person, and there is much to confirm this view. Without some such personification, anger toward lifeless things would have been brief at most. It is worth noticing that the commonest example in the most primitive customs and laws is a tree that falls on a man, or from which he falls and is killed. We can imagine fairly easily how a tree might be put on the same footing as an animal. It certainly was treated like one, and was handed over to the relatives, or chopped to pieces to satisfy a passion, real or put on.

In the Athenian process there is also, no doubt, a different thought to be traced. Expiation—the religious purging of guilt—is one of the ends Plato insists on most, and it appears to have been the purpose of the procedure Aeschines mentions. Some passages in the Roman historians, which will come up again, seem to point the same way.

Another peculiarity to notice is that the liability seems to have been thought of as attached to the body that did the damage, in an almost physical sense. An untrained mind carries out only imperfectly the analysis by which jurists trace responsibility back to the beginning of a chain of causes. The hatred of anything that gives us pain, which takes itself out on the obvious cause, and which leads even civilized man to kick a door when it pinches his finger, is embodied in noxal surrender and other related doctrines of early Roman law. There is a damaged passage in Gaius that seems to say that liability could sometimes be escaped by giving up even the dead body of the offender. So Livy relates that when Brutulus Papius had caused a breach of truce with the Romans, the Samnites decided to surrender him; and when he escaped disgrace and punishment by killing himself, they sent his lifeless body. It is worth noticing that the surrender seems to be regarded as the natural expiation for the breach of treaty, and that sending the body when the wrongdoer has died is treated as equally a matter of course.

Footnote: I follow Huschke's reading of that passage: it is lawful, if the offender has died a natural death, to give up the dead man; for although we have said that defendants are not permitted to surrender dead men, still, if someone gives up one who has died by his own fate, he is equally discharged. Ulpian's statement that the action is gone if the animal dies before the suit is joined is aimed only at the point that the liability is founded on possession of the thing.

Footnote: Livy adds that it was decided to hand over his property along with his body. Elsewhere he tells of a demand that the Fabii be surrendered for having violated the law of nations.

The most curious examples of this sort occur in the region of what we would now call contract. Livy again supplies one, if indeed the last case is not one already. The Roman consul Postumius concluded the disgraceful peace of the Caudine Forks—by a sponsio, a personal pledge, as Livy says, rejecting the usual story that it was by a formal treaty—and he was sent to Rome to get the people's approval. When he got there, however, he proposed that the persons who had made the contract, himself included, should be handed over in satisfaction of it. For, he said, since the Roman people had not approved the agreement, who is so ignorant of the jus fetialium, the sacred law of treaties and war kept by the priests called fetials, as not to know that the people are released from obligation by surrendering us? The formula of surrender seems to bring the case within noxal surrender. Cicero tells of a similar surrender of Mancinus to the Numantines by the pater patratus, the fetial priest who spoke for Rome in such matters; the Numantines, however, like the Samnites in the earlier case, refused to accept him.

Footnote: The formula of surrender ran: "Whereas these men, without the command of the Roman people, pledged that a treaty would be made, and have thereby done harm; therefore, so that the Roman people may be freed from impious guilt, I surrender these men to you." The words for doing harm, noxam nocuerunt, are the very words of the noxal action.

Footnote: Florus, in his account, says that Rome expiated the matter by the surrender of Mancinus. It has already been observed that the cases Livy mentions suggest that the object of the surrender was expiation as much as they suggest that it was satisfaction of a contract. Compare the provision attributed to the laws of Numa, that if a man killed another unintentionally, he should offer a ram in public to the dead man's kindred in place of the head of the slain.

It might be asked what likeness could be found between a breach of contract and the kinds of wrong that stir the desire for vengeance. But it must be remembered that the distinction between tort (a civil wrong other than a breach of contract) and breach of contract, and especially between the remedies for the two, is not found ready-made. It is conceivable that a procedure designed to redress violence was extended to other cases as they came up. Slaves were surrendered for theft as well as for assault; and it is said that a debtor who did not pay his debts, or a seller who failed to deliver goods he had been paid for, was dealt with on the same footing as a thief. This line of thought, together with the half-physical idea, already noticed, of legal obligations as binding the offending body, may explain the well-known law of the Twelve Tables on insolvent debtors. Under that law, if a man owed money to several creditors and could not pay, then after certain formalities they might cut up his body and divide it among them. If there was a single creditor, he might put his debtor to death or sell him as a slave.

If no other right had been given than that of reducing a debtor to slavery, the law might be taken to aim only at compensation, and to be modeled on the natural working of self-help. That seems to be the explanation of the principle of our own law that seizing a man's body on execution of a judgment satisfies the debt, even if he is not held for an hour. But the right to put him to death looks like vengeance, and the division of the body shows that the debt was thought of, quite literally, as lodged in the body or binding it with a vinculum juris, a "chain of law."

Footnote: Compare Varro's definition: a free man who gives his labor in servitude for money he owes, until he pays it, is called nexus, that is, "bound."

Whatever the true explanation of surrender in connection with contracts may be, for our present purpose we need not go further than the ordinary case of noxal surrender for wrongs. Nor is the apparent clinging of liability to the very body that did the harm of the first importance. Roman law dealt mainly with living creatures, with animals and slaves. If a man was run over, it did not surrender the wagon that crushed him, but the ox that drew the wagon. At this stage the idea is easy to understand. The desire for vengeance may be felt as strongly against a slave as against a free man, and even nowadays it is not unknown for a similar passion to be felt against an animal. The surrender of the slave or beast gave the injured party the power to do as he would with them. Payment by the owner was merely a privilege, in case he wanted to buy the vengeance off.

It is easy to imagine that such a system as I have described could not last once civilization had advanced to any considerable height. What had been a privilege of buying off vengeance by agreement—of paying for the damage instead of surrendering the body of the offender—no doubt became a general custom. The Aquilian law, passed a couple of centuries or so after the Twelve Tables, enlarged the range of compensation for bodily injuries. Interpretation enlarged the Aquilian law. Masters became personally liable for certain wrongs their slaves committed with their knowledge, where before they had only been bound to surrender the slave. If a pack mule threw its load onto a passer-by because it had been improperly overloaded, or a dog that could have been restrained got away from its master and bit someone, the old noxal action, as it was called, gave way to an action under the new law enforcing a general personal liability. Still later, shipowners and innkeepers were made liable, as if they themselves were wrongdoers, for wrongs committed by the people they employed on board ship or in the tavern, even though, of course, the wrongs were committed without their knowledge. The true reason for this exceptional responsibility was the exceptional trust that people were forced to place in carriers and innkeepers. But some of the jurists, who regarded the surrender of children and slaves as a privilege meant to limit liability, explained the new liability on the ground that the innkeeper or shipowner was to some degree guilty of negligence in having employed bad men. This was the first instance of a master being made liable without conditions for the wrongs of his servant. The reason given for it was of general application, and the principle expanded to fill the reach of the reason.

The law on shipowners and innkeepers introduced another and more startling innovation. It made them responsible when the people they employed were free, as well as when they were slaves. For the first time one man was made answerable for the wrongs of another who was answerable himself, and who had standing before the law. This was a great change from the bare permission to ransom one's slave as a privilege. But here we have the history of the whole modern doctrine of master and servant, and of principal and agent: the doctrine that makes an employer answer for what his employee does on the job. All servants are now as free as their masters, and as open to being sued. Yet the principle introduced on special grounds in a special case, when servants were slaves, is now the general law of this country and of England. Under it men every day have to pay large sums for other people's acts, in which they had no part and for which they are in no sense to blame. And to this day the reason the Roman jurists offered for an exceptional rule is made to justify this universal and unlimited responsibility.

So much for one of the parents of our common law. Now let us turn for a moment to the Germanic side. The Salic Law, the code of the Salian Franks, embodies customs that in all probability are too early to have been influenced either by Rome or by the Old Testament. The thirty-sixth chapter of the ancient text provides that if a man is killed by a domestic animal, the owner of the animal shall pay half the composition (the sum he would have had to pay to buy off the blood feud if he had killed the man himself), and for the other half shall give the beast up to the complainant. So, by chapter thirty-five, if a slave killed a free man, the slave was to be surrendered to the dead man's relatives for one half of the composition, and the master was to pay the other half. But according to the gloss, the explanatory commentary on the text, if the slave or his master had been mistreated by the slain man or his relatives, the master had only to surrender the slave. It is interesting to notice that the Northern sources that Wilda takes to represent a more primitive stage of German law limit liability for animals to surrender alone. There is also a trace that at a later date the master could, in some cases, clear himself by showing that the slave was no longer in his possession. There are later provisions making a master liable for wrongs his slave committed at his command. And in the laws the Thuringians adapted from the earlier sources, it is provided in so many words that the master is to pay for all damage done by his slaves.

Footnote: The Saxon law says that if a slave runs away after committing the deed, so that his master can no longer find him, the master pays nothing. The Ripuarian law forbids a man to turn his slave loose because of damage the slave has done to someone; the master must answer for him according to the damage, or offer him up for composition or punishment. But if the slave runs away after the wrong, so that the master cannot find him at all, the master may clear himself by oath that the deed was done neither by his will nor with his knowledge.

In short, so far as I can trace the order of development in the customs of the German tribes, it seems to have been entirely similar to the one we have already followed in the growth of Roman law. The earlier liability for slaves and animals was mainly confined to surrender; the later became personal, as at Rome.

The reader may begin to ask for proof that all this has any bearing on our law of today. As far as the influence of Roman law on our own is concerned, especially the Roman law of master and servant, the evidence is to be found in every book written on the subject for the last five hundred years. It has already been said that we still repeat the reasoning of the Roman lawyers, empty as it is, to the present day. We shall see shortly whether the German folk-laws can also be followed into England.

In the Kentish laws of Hlothhere and Eadric (A.D. 680) it is said: "If any one's slave slay a freeman, whoever it be, let the owner pay with a hundred shillings, give up the slayer," and so on. There are several other provisions like it. In the laws of Ine, from nearly the same time, surrender and payment are simple alternatives: "If a Wessex slave slay an Englishman, then shall he who owns him deliver him up to the lord and the kindred, or give sixty shillings for his life." Alfred's laws (A.D. 871–901) have a similar provision about cattle: "If a neat wound a man, let the neat be delivered up or compounded for"—a neat being an ox or cow, and to compound, to pay a composition. And Alfred, although he came two hundred years after the first English lawgivers quoted here, seems to have gone back to more primitive ideas than we find before his time. For he extends the same principle to the case of a tree that kills a man: "If, at their common work, one man slay another unwilfully, let the tree be given to the kindred, and let them have it off the land within thirty nights. Or let him take possession of it who owns the wood."

It is not out of place to compare what Mr. Tylor has reported about the Kukis, a hill people of Southern Asia. "If a tiger killed a Kuki, his family were in disgrace till they had retaliated by killing and eating this tiger, or another; but further, if a man was killed by a fall from a tree, his relatives would take their revenge by cutting the tree down, and scattering it in chips."

To return to the English: the later laws, from about a hundred years after Alfred down to the collection known as the laws of Henry I, compiled long after the Norman Conquest, increase the lord's liability for his household and make him a surety for the good conduct of his men. If they incur a fine to the king and run away, the lord has to pay it, unless he can clear himself of any part in the offense. But I cannot say that I find the unlimited liability of master for servant, which was worked out on the Continent both by the German tribes and at Rome, until a later period. Whether the principle, once established, was a native growth, or whether the last step was taken under the influence of the Roman law, of which Bracton made great use, I cannot say. It is enough that the soil was ready for it, and that it took root at an early day. This is all that need be said here about the liability of a master for the misdeeds of his servants.

Footnote: A record from the reign of Edward III holds that "it seemed to the court that every master of a ship is bound to answer for any trespass done by his servants in his ship," and the court relied there on the Laws of Oléron, the old sea code. Later on, the influence of the Roman law is clear.

The next thing to show is what became of the principle as applied to animals. Nowadays a man is bound at his peril to keep his cattle from trespassing, and he is liable for damage done by his dog or by any fierce animal if he has notice that the brute tends to do the kind of harm complained of. The question is whether any connection can be established between these very sensible and intelligible rules of modern law and the surrender ordered by King Alfred.

Let us turn to one of the old books of Scottish law, where the old principle still appears in full force and is stated together with its reasons as they were then understood. The passage says, in the Scots of its day, that if a wild or headstrong horse carries a man against his will over a cliff or into the water and the man drowns, the horse is forfeit to the king; but a tame, broken horse that a man foolishly spurs into the water is not forfeit, because the fault was the man's and not the horse's, and the man has already had his punishment by dying:

"Gif ane wylde or head-strang horse, carries ane man against his will over an craig, or heuch, or to the water, and the man happin to drowne, the horse sall perteine to the king as escheit.

"Bot it is otherwise of ane tame and dantoned horse; gif any man fulishlie rides, and be sharp spurres compelles his horse to take the water, and the man drownes, the horse sould not be escheit, for that comes be the mans fault or trespasse, and not of the horse, and the man has receaved his punishment, in sa farre as he is perished and dead; and the horse quha did na fault, sould not be escheit.

"The like reason is of all other beastes, quhilk slayes anie man, [it is added in a later work, "of the quhilk slaughter they haue gilt,"] for all these beasts sould be escheit."

"Escheit" (escheat) means forfeited to the Crown, and the later addition limits the rule to beasts that are guilty of the killing. "The Forme and Maner of Baron Courts" then goes on to ask whether a mill is forfeit when a man falls into the mill-dam, is carried down by the water to the wheel, and is killed by it. The answer is no, because a mill is a dead thing, and a dead thing can commit no felony:

"It is to witt, that this question is asked in the law, Gif ane lord hes ane milne, and any man fall in the damne, and be borne down with the water quhill he comes to the quheill, and there be slaine to death with the quheill; quhither aught the milne to be escheit or not? The law sayes thereto nay, and be this reason, For it is ane dead thing, and ane dead thing may do na fellony, nor be made escheit throw their gilt. Swa the milne in this case is not culpable, and in the law it is lawfull to the lord of the land to haue ane mylne on his awin water quhere best likes him."

The reader will see in this passage, as I remarked earlier of the Roman law, that a distinction is drawn between things capable of guilt and things that are not—between living things and dead ones. But he will also see that no one had any difficulty in treating animals as guilty.

Take next an early passage of English law, a report of what one of the English judges laid down. In 1333 it was stated as law that "if my dog kills your sheep, and I, freshly after the fact, tender you the dog, you are without recovery against me." In other words, if I promptly hand over the dog, you can get nothing more from me. More than three centuries later, in 1676, Justice Twisden said that "if one hath kept a tame fox, which gets loose and grows wild, he that hath kept him before shall not answer for the damage the fox doth after he hath lost him, and he hath resumed his wild nature." It is at least doubtful whether that sentence would ever have been written but for the lingering influence of the idea that the ground of the owner's liability was his ownership of the offending thing and his failure to surrender it. When the fox escaped, ownership was at an end by another principle of law. In fact, that very consideration was seriously pressed in England as late as 1846, in the case of a monkey that escaped and bit the plaintiff. So it seems a reasonable guess that the same way of thinking led Lord Holt, near the beginning of the eighteenth century, to suggest that one reason a man is bound at his peril to keep his cattle from trespassing is that he has a valuable property in such animals, whereas he has none in dogs, for which his responsibility is less. To this day, in fact, cautious judges state the law about cattle this way: "if I am the owner of an animal in which by law the right of property can exist, I am bound to take care that it does not stray into the land of my neighbor."

I do not mean that our modern law on this subject is only a survival, and that the only change from primitive notions was to put the owner in place of the offending animal. It is probable that the early law was one of the causes that led to the modern doctrine. But there has been too much good sense at every stage of our law for it to adopt the sweeping consequences that would follow from such a wholesale transfer of liability. An owner is not bound at his peril to keep his cattle from harming his neighbor's person. And in some of the earliest cases of personal liability, even for trespass on a neighbor's land, the ground seems to have been the owner's negligence.

Footnote: In the laws of Ine, personal liability seems to be imposed where a man fails to fence. But if an animal breaks through hedges, the only remedy mentioned is to kill it, the owner keeping the skin and flesh and forfeiting the rest. In a case of 1353 or 1354 the defendant was held liable "because it was found that this was for default of guarding them . . . for default of good guard." It is much later that the reason is stated in the absolute form, "because I am bound by law to keep my beasts without doing wrong to any one." The old Scottish book called Regiam Majestatem draws further distinctions about a horse that kills a man.

It is the nature of the animals the common law recognizes as things that can be owned to stray, and, when they stray, to do damage by trampling and eating crops. At the same time it is usual and easy to restrain them. A dog, on the other hand, is not treated as property, and does no harm simply by crossing land that is not its owner's. To this extent, then, the new law might have followed the old. The right of property in the offending animal, which was the ancient ground of responsibility, could safely enough have been adopted as the test of a liability based on the owner's fault. But responsibility for damage of a kind not to be expected from such animals is settled on grounds of policy, comparatively little disturbed by tradition. I have already explained how personal liability for fierce wild animals developed at Rome. Our law seems to have followed the Roman.

We will now follow the history of the branch of the primitive notion that was least likely to survive—the liability of inanimate things.

It will be remembered that King Alfred ordered the surrender of a tree, but that the later Scottish law refused to forfeit a mill because a dead thing could not have guilt. It will be remembered, too, that the animals the Scottish law forfeited went to the king. The same has remained true in England until well into the nineteenth century, even of inanimate objects. As long ago as Bracton, when a man was killed, the coroner was to value the object that caused the death, and it was to be forfeited as a deodand "for the king." It was to be given to God—that is, to the Church—on the king's behalf, to be spent for the good of his soul. A man's death had ceased to be the private affair of his friends, as it had been in the time of the barbarian folk-laws. The king, who provided the court, now sued for the penalty. He took the family's place in the claim on the guilty thing, and the Church took his.

In Edward I's time some of the cases remind us of the barbarian laws at their crudest stage. If a man fell from a tree, the tree was deodand. If he drowned in a well, the well was to be filled up. It did not matter that the forfeited instrument belonged to an innocent person. "Where a man killeth another with the sword of John at Stile, the sword shall be forfeit as deodand, and yet no default is in the owner." That is from a book written in the reign of Henry VIII, about 1530. And it has been repeated from Queen Elizabeth's time until within the last hundred years that if my horse strikes a man, and I afterward sell the horse, and after that the man dies, the horse shall be forfeited. This is why, in every indictment for homicide until very lately, it has been necessary to state the instrument that caused the death and its value—that the blow was given by a certain penknife, value sixpence, for instance—so as to secure the forfeiture. It is said that a steam engine has been forfeited in this way.

I now come to what I regard as the most remarkable transformation of this principle, and one that is a most important factor in our law as it is today. For the moment I must leave the common law and take up the doctrines of the Admiralty, the courts that deal with ships and the sea. In the early books just referred to, and long afterward, the fact of motion is treated as very important. A maxim of Henry Spigurnel, a judge in the time of Edward I, is reported: "where a man is killed by a cart, or by the fall of a house, or in other like manner, and the thing in motion is the cause of the death, it shall be deodand." So it was said in the next reign, in Latin, that everything which moves along with the thing that kills a man shall be deodand to the lord king, or to the fee of a clergyman. The reader sees how motion gives life to the object forfeited.

The most striking example of this kind is a ship. And so the old books say that if a man falls from a ship and drowns, the motion of the ship must be taken to have caused the death, and the ship is forfeited—provided, however, that this happens in fresh water. If the death took place on the high seas, it was outside the ordinary jurisdiction. This proviso has been supposed to mean that ships at sea were not forfeited. But a long series of petitions to the king in Parliament, asking that such forfeitures be done away with, tells a different story. The truth seems to be that the forfeiture did take place, but in a different court. A manuscript of the reign of Henry VI, only recently printed, reveals that if a man was killed or drowned at sea by the motion of the ship, the vessel was forfeited to the admiral in a proceeding in the admiral's court, subject to release by favor of the admiral or the king.

A ship is the most living of inanimate things. Servants sometimes say "she" of a clock, but everyone gives a gender to vessels. We need not be surprised, therefore, to find a way of dealing with things that has shown such extraordinary vitality in the criminal law applied even more thoroughly in the Admiralty. Only by supposing that the ship was treated as if it had a personality can the arbitrary-seeming peculiarities of maritime law be made intelligible; and on that supposition they at once become consistent and logical.

To see what those peculiarities are, take first a collision at sea. A collision takes place between two vessels, the Ticonderoga and the Melampus, through the fault of the Ticonderoga alone. That ship is under lease at the time; the lessee has put his own master in charge, and the owner of the vessel has no control over it whatever. The owner, therefore, is not to blame, and he cannot even be charged on the ground that the damage was done by his servants. On elementary principles he is free from personal liability. Yet it is perfectly settled that there is a lien on his vessel for the amount of the damage done—a legal claim against the ship itself. This means that the vessel may be arrested and sold to pay the loss by any admiralty court whose process can reach her. If a livery-stable keeper rents out a horse and wagon to a customer, and the customer runs a man down by careless driving, no one would think of claiming a right to seize the horse and wagon. Everyone would see that the only property that could be sold to pay for a wrong was the property of the wrongdoer.

But now suppose that the vessel, instead of being under lease, is in the charge of a pilot whom the laws of the port she is entering require her to employ. The Supreme Court of the United States holds the ship liable in this case too. The English courts would probably have decided otherwise, and in England the matter is settled by legislation. But there the court of appeal, the Privy Council, has been made up largely of common-law lawyers, and it has shown a marked tendency to bring admiralty into line with common-law doctrine. At common law, a person who could not make the owner personally liable could not bind a particular chattel—a particular piece of movable property—to answer for a wrong it had been the instrument of. But our Supreme Court has long recognized that a person may bind a ship when he could not bind the owners personally, because he was not their agent.

It may be admitted that if this doctrine were not supported by an appearance of good sense, it would not have survived. The ship is the only security available in dealing with foreigners. Rather than send one's own citizens abroad to look for a remedy in strange courts, it is easy to seize the vessel and satisfy the claim at home, leaving the foreign owners to get their indemnity as best they can. I dare say some such thought has helped keep the practice alive. But I believe its true historical foundation is elsewhere. The ship, no doubt, like a sword, would have been forfeited for causing death, in whosever hands it might have been. So, too, if the master and crew of a ship carrying letters of reprisal—a royal license to seize enemy shipping—committed piracy against a friend of the king, the owner lost his ship under admiralty law, although the crime was committed without his knowledge or consent. It seems most likely that the principle by which the ship was forfeited to the king for causing death, or for piracy, was the same as the one by which it was bound to private sufferers for other damage, in whosever hands it might have been when it did the harm.

If we said to an uneducated man today, "She did it and she ought to pay for it," it may be doubted whether he would see the fallacy. He might not be ready to explain that the ship was only property, and that "The ship has to pay for it" was simply a dramatic way of saying that somebody's property was to be sold and the proceeds used to pay for a wrong committed by somebody else.

It would seem that a similar form of words has been enough to satisfy the minds of great lawyers. Here is a passage from a judgment of Chief Justice Marshall, which Justice Story quoted with approval in giving the opinion of the Supreme Court of the United States: "This is not a proceeding against the owner; it is a proceeding against the vessel for an offence committed by the vessel; which is not the less an offence, and does not the less subject her to forfeiture, because it was committed without the authority and against the will of the owner. It is true that inanimate matter can commit no offence. But this body is animated and put in action by the crew, who are guided by the master. The vessel acts and speaks by the master. She reports herself by the master. It is, therefore, not unreasonable that the vessel should be affected by this report." And again Justice Story quotes from another case: "The thing is here primarily considered as the offender, or rather the offence is primarily attached to the thing."

In other words, those great judges, though of course aware that a ship is no more alive than a mill wheel, thought not only that the law did in fact deal with it as if it were alive, but that it was reasonable for the law to do so. The reader will notice that they do not simply say it is reasonable, on grounds of policy, to sacrifice justice to the owner for the sake of security for somebody else. They say it is reasonable to deal with the vessel as an offending thing. Whatever the hidden ground of policy may be, their thought still dresses itself in the language of personification.

Let us now follow the peculiarities of maritime law in other directions, for the cases stated so far are only parts of a larger whole.

By the maritime law of the Middle Ages the ship was not only the source of liability but its limit. The rule already prevailed—since borrowed by the English statutes and by our own act of Congress of 1851—that the owner is discharged from responsibility for the wrongful acts of a master he himself appointed if he surrenders his interest in the vessel and the freight she has earned. Freight here means the money paid for carrying cargo. By the doctrines of agency he would be personally liable for the whole damage. If the origin of this system of limited liability, which is believed to be so essential to modern commerce, is to be found in the considerations of public policy on which it would be defended today, then the system has nothing to do with the law of collision. But if the limit of liability here stands on the same ground as noxal surrender, it confirms the explanation already given of the ship's liability for wrongs it did while out of the owner's hands; and, conversely, the existence of that liability confirms the argument here.

Now take another rule for which, as usual, there is a plausible explanation of policy. Freight, it is said, is the mother of wages: the sailors are paid only if the ship earns its freight. For, we are told, "if the ship perished, if the mariners were to have their wages in such cases, they would not use their endeavors, nor hazard their lives, for the safety of the ship." The best commentary on this reasoning is that the law has recently been changed by statute. But even under the old law there was an exception inconsistent with the supposed reason. In a shipwreck, which was the usual way of failing to earn freight, the mariners' lien remained as long as any part of the ship was saved. I suppose it would have been said that this was because it was sound policy to encourage them to save all they could. But if we consider that the sailors were regarded as employed by the ship, we shall understand both the rule and the exception very readily. "The ship is the debtor," as was said in arguing a case decided in the time of William III. If the debtor perished, that was the end of the matter. If a part of it came ashore, that part might be proceeded against.

Even the rule in its modern form, that freight is the mother of wages, turns out, by the explanation commonly given of it, to be about whether the ship is lost or arrives safe. In the most ancient surviving source of maritime law that says anything on the matter, so far as I have been able to discover, the statement is that the mariners lose their wages when the ship is lost. In the same way, in what its English editor, Sir Travers Twiss, calls the oldest part of the Consulate of the Sea, the medieval Mediterranean sea code, we read that "whoever the freighter may be who runs away or dies, the ship is bound to pay the mariners." I think we may assume that the vessel was bound by its contract with the sailors much as it was by the wrongs it had to answer for—just as, under the ancient law of Rome, the debtor's body answered for his debts as well as for his crimes.

Footnote: The old French of the Judgments of the Sea says it in so many words: "they shall lose their wages when the ship is lost." According to its editor, that collection is the most ancient surviving source of modern maritime law, except the decisions of Trani. Molloy says the same: "If the ship perishes at sea they lose their wages."

Footnote: It should be added, however, that the same book lays down that if the vessel is detained in port by the local authorities, the master is not bound to pay the mariners wages, "for he has earned no freight."

The same is true of other maritime dealings with the vessel, whether by way of contract or otherwise. If salvage service is rendered to a vessel—if someone rescues her or her cargo from peril at sea—the admiralty court will hold the vessel liable for it, although it has been doubted whether an action of contract would lie if the owners were sued at law. So, too, the ship is bound by the master's contract to carry cargo, just as in the case of collision, even though she was under lease at the time. In such cases also, according to our Supreme Court, the master may bind the vessel when he cannot bind the general owners. "By custom the ship is bound to the merchandise, and the merchandise to the ship." "By the maritime law every contract of the master implies an hypothecation"—that is, a pledge of the ship as security. It might no doubt be urged, with force, that in the usual maritime contracts the dealing must in many cases be done on the security of the ship or the merchandise, and that it is therefore good policy to give this security in all cases; that the risk it puts on shipowners can be calculated, and that they must take it into account when they lease their vessels. Again, in many cases where a party claims a maritime lien by way of contract, he has improved the condition of the thing on which he claims it, and some systems have recognized this as a ground for such a lien. But this is not true universally, nor in the most important cases. I must leave it to the reader to decide whether I have not shown ground for believing that the same metaphysical confusion that naturally arose about the ship's wrongful acts affected the way of thinking about her contracts. The whole manner of dealing with vessels plainly took the form that prevailed in the cases first mentioned. Pardessus, a high authority, says that the lien for freight holds even against the owner of stolen goods, "as the master deals less with the person than the thing." So it was said in the argument of a famous English case that "the ship is instead of the owner, and therefore is answerable." In many cases of contract, as well as of tort, the vessel was not only the security for the debt but the limit of the owner's liability.

The principles of the Admiralty are built into its procedure. A suit may be brought there against a vessel by name. Anyone with an interest in the vessel is free to come in and defend, but the suit, if it succeeds, ends in a sale of the vessel and payment of the plaintiff's claim out of the proceeds. As long ago as the time of James I it was said that "the libel ought to be only against the ship and goods, and not against the party"—the libel being the plaintiff's written complaint in admiralty. And authority for the statement was cited from the reign of Henry VI, the same reign in which, as we have seen, the admiral claimed the forfeiture of ships for causing death. I am bound to say, however, that I cannot find any such authority of that date.

We have now followed the development of the chief forms of liability in modern law for anything other than the immediate and obvious consequences of a man's own acts. We have seen the parallel course of events in the two parents—the Roman law and the German customs—and in the offspring of those two on English soil, with regard to servants, animals, and inanimate things. We have seen a single germ multiply and branch into products as different from one another as the flower from the root. It hardly remains to ask what that germ was. We have seen that it was the desire for retaliation against the offending thing itself. It might be argued, no doubt, that many of the rules described came from seizing the offending thing as security for reparation—at first, perhaps, outside the law altogether. That explanation, like the one offered here, would show that modern views of responsibility had not yet been reached, since the owner of the thing might very well not have been the person at fault. But that has not been the view of those most competent to judge. A look at the earliest instances will show, as might be expected, that vengeance, not compensation—and vengeance on the offending thing—was the original object. The ox in Exodus was to be stoned. The axe in the Athenian law was to be banished. The tree, in Mr. Tylor's instance, was to be chopped to pieces. The slave, under every one of the systems, was to be surrendered to the relatives of the slain man, so that they might do with him what they liked. The deodand was an accursed thing. The original limitation of liability to surrender, when the owner himself was before the court, could not be accounted for if it was his liability, and not that of his property, that was in question. Even where, as in some of the cases, expiation seems to be intended rather than vengeance, the object is just as remote from a seizure for security outside the courts.

This history, apart from the purposes for which I have given it, is a good illustration of the paradox of form and substance in the development of law. In form its growth is logical. The official theory is that each new decision follows by syllogism from existing precedents. But just as the clavicle in the cat only tells of some earlier creature to which a collarbone was useful, precedents survive in the law long after the use they once served is at an end and the reason for them has been forgotten. The result of following them must often be failure and confusion from the merely logical point of view.

On the other hand, in substance the growth of the law is legislative. And it is legislative in a deeper sense than that what the courts declare to have always been the law is in fact new. It is legislative in its grounds. The very considerations that judges most rarely mention, and always with an apology, are the secret root from which the law draws all the juices of life. I mean, of course, considerations of what is expedient for the community concerned. Every important principle developed by litigation is in fact, and at bottom, the result of more or less clearly understood views of public policy. Most often, to be sure, under our practice and traditions, it is the unconscious result of instinctive preferences and inarticulate convictions, but it is traceable to views of public policy all the same in the last analysis. And because the law is administered by able and experienced men, who know too much to sacrifice good sense to a syllogism, it will be found that when ancient rules keep themselves alive in the way that has been shown, and will be shown in this book, new reasons better suited to the time have been found for them. Gradually they take on a new content, and at last a new form, from the grounds to which they have been transplanted.

But up to now this process has been largely unconscious. For that reason it is important to call to mind what the actual course of events has been. If it did no more than press for a more conscious recognition of the legislative function of the courts, as just explained, it would be useful, as we shall see more clearly later on.

What has been said will explain the failure of all theories that consider the law only from its formal side—whether they try to deduce the whole body of it from principles assumed in advance, or fall into the humbler error of supposing that the science of law lies in its elegance, the logical cohesion of part with part. The truth is, that the law is always approaching, and never reaching, consistency. It is forever adopting new principles from life at one end, and it always keeps old ones from history at the other, which have not yet been absorbed or sloughed off. It will become entirely consistent only when it stops growing.

The study we have been engaged in is necessary both for knowing the law and for revising it. However much we may codify the law into a series of seemingly self-sufficient propositions, those propositions will be only a phase in a continuous growth. To understand their scope fully, and to know how judges trained in the past that the law embodies will deal with them, we must ourselves know something of that past. The history of what the law has been is necessary to the knowledge of what the law is.

Again, the process I have described has involved the attempt to follow precedents and also to give a good reason for them. When we find that in large and important branches of the law the various grounds of policy used to justify the various rules are later inventions, made up to account for what are in fact survivals from more primitive times, we have a right to reconsider the popular reasons, take a broader view of the field, and decide anew whether those reasons are satisfactory. They may be, in spite of the way they came about. If truth were not often suggested by error, if old tools could not be adapted to new uses, human progress would be slow. But scrutiny and revision are justified.

None of these considerations, however, nor the aim of showing what materials for anthropology the history of the law contains, is the immediate object here. My aim and purpose have been to show that the various forms of liability known to modern law spring from the common ground of revenge. In the sphere of contract this fact will hardly matter outside the cases stated in this Lecture. But in the criminal law and the law of torts it is of the first importance. It shows that they started from a moral basis, from the thought that someone was to blame.

It remains to be proved that, while the vocabulary of morals is still kept, and while the law does still and always, in a certain sense, measure legal liability by moral standards, it is nevertheless, by the very necessity of its nature, continually transforming those moral standards into external or objective ones, from which the actual guilt of the party concerned is wholly eliminated.

Lecture II: The Criminal Law

At the beginning of the first Lecture it was shown that the appeals of the early law were aimed only at intentional wrongs. The appeal was a far older form of procedure than the indictment, the formal accusation of crime brought in the name of the state, and it may be said to have had a criminal as well as a civil side. It had a double object: to satisfy the private party for his loss, and to satisfy the king for the breach of his peace. On its civil side it was rooted in vengeance. It was a proceeding to recover those compositions, at first optional and afterwards compulsory, by which a wrong-doer bought the spear from his side. Whether, so far as the king was concerned, it had the same object of vengeance, or was aimed more particularly at revenue, does not matter, since the king's claim did not enlarge the scope of the action.

It seems a fair inference that indictable offences were originally limited in the same way as those which gave rise to an appeal. For whether the indictment arose by a splitting up of the appeal, or in some other way, the two were closely connected.

An acquittal of the appellee, the accused, on the merits was a bar to an indictment. And on the other hand, once an appeal was fairly started, the case might still be carried on in the king's behalf, even though the appellor, the accuser, failed to prosecute, or was defeated by a plea.

The presentment is the other parent of our criminal procedure: the accusation laid before the court by a body of neighbors sworn to report the crimes of their district. It had an origin distinct from the appeal. If, as has been thought, it was merely the successor of fresh suit, the hot pursuit of a wrong-doer by those he had wronged, and of lynch law, then this too is the child of vengeance, even more clearly than the other.

The desire for vengeance implies an opinion that its object is actually and personally to blame. It takes an internal standard, not an objective or external one, and condemns its victim by that. The question is whether such a standard is still accepted, either in this primitive form or in some more refined development. That is commonly supposed, and it seems not impossible, considering how slowly the criminal law has improved.

It certainly may be argued, with some force, that it has never ceased to be one object of punishment to satisfy the desire for vengeance. The argument becomes plain if we consider the cases in which, for one reason or another, compensation for a wrong is out of the question.

Thus an act may be of a kind that makes indemnity impossible, by putting an end to the principal sufferer, as in murder or manslaughter.

Again, these and other crimes, like forgery, although directed against one individual, tend to make others feel unsafe, and this general insecurity cannot be paid for.

Again, there are cases where there is no means of enforcing indemnity. In Macaulay's draft of the Indian Penal Code, breaches of contract for carrying passengers were made criminal. The palanquin-bearers of India, who carried travelers in covered litters on poles, were too poor to pay damages. Yet they had to be trusted to carry unprotected women and children through wild and desolate country, where their desertion would have left those in their charge in great danger.

In all these cases punishment remains as an alternative. A pain can be inflicted on the wrong-doer that does not restore the injured party to his former situation, or to another equally good, but is inflicted for the very purpose of causing pain. And so far as this punishment takes the place of compensation—whether because the person wronged is dead, or because the persons affected are countless, or because the suffering cannot be priced in money, or because the criminal is poor—it may be said that one of its objects is to gratify the desire for vengeance. The prisoner pays with his body.

The statement may be made stronger still. It may be said not only that the law does make the gratification of revenge an object, but that it ought to. This at any rate is the opinion of two authorities as great, and as opposed in their other views, as Bishop Butler and Jeremy Bentham. Sir James Stephen says, "The criminal law stands to the passion of revenge in much the same relation as marriage to the sexual appetite."

The first requirement of a sound body of law is, that it should correspond with the actual feelings and demands of the community, whether right or wrong. If people would gratify the passion of revenge outside the law when the law did not help them, the law has no choice but to satisfy the craving itself, and so avoid the greater evil of private retribution. At the same time, this passion is not one that we encourage, either as private individuals or as lawmakers. Moreover, it does not cover the whole ground. There are crimes that do not excite it, and we should naturally expect the most important purposes of punishment to reach as far as punishment itself reaches. It remains to be discovered whether such a general purpose exists, and if so, what it is. Different theories still divide opinion on the subject.

It has been thought that the purpose of punishment is to reform the criminal; that it is to deter the criminal and others from committing similar crimes; and that it is retribution. Few would now maintain that the first of these was the only purpose. If it were, every prisoner should be released as soon as it is clear that he will never repeat his offence, and if he is incurable he should not be punished at all. And of course it would be hard to reconcile the death penalty with this doctrine.

The main struggle lies between the other two. On one side is the notion that there is a mystic bond between wrong and punishment; on the other, that the infliction of pain is only a means to an end. Hegel, one of the great expounders of the first view, puts it in his almost mathematical form: wrong is the negation of right, and punishment is the negation of that negation, or retribution. So the punishment must be equal to the crime, in the sense of proportionate to it, because its only function is to destroy it. Others, without this logical apparatus, are content to rely on a felt necessity that suffering should follow wrong-doing.

It is objected that the preventive theory is immoral. It overlooks the ill-desert of wrong-doing, the objection runs, and it gives no measure of how much punishment is due, except the lawgiver's own opinion of how much suffering will be enough to prevent the crime. In the language of Kant, it treats man as a thing, not as a person; as a means, not as an end in himself. It is said to conflict with the sense of justice, and to violate the fundamental principle of all free communities, that their members have equal rights to life, liberty, and personal security.

In spite of all this, probably most English-speaking lawyers would accept the preventive theory without hesitation. As to the charge that it violates equal rights, it may be replied that the dogma of equality makes an equation between individuals only, not between an individual and the community. No society has ever admitted that it could not sacrifice individual welfare to its own existence. If it needs conscripts for its army, it seizes them, and marches them, with bayonets in their rear, to death. It runs highways and railroads through old family places in spite of the owner's protest. It pays the market value in that case, to be sure, because no civilized government sacrifices the citizen more than it can help; but it still sacrifices his will and his welfare to those of the rest.

Footnote: Even the law recognizes that this is a sacrifice.

If it were necessary to encroach further on the field of morals, it might be suggested that the dogma of equality, even between individuals, applies only within the limits of ordinary dealings in the common run of affairs. You cannot argue with your neighbor except on the admission, for the moment, that he is as wise as you, although you may by no means believe it. In the same way, you cannot deal with him, where both are free to choose, except on the footing of equal treatment and the same rules for both. The ever-growing value set on peace and on the social relations tends to make the law of social life look like the law of all life. But it seems to me clear that the ultima ratio—the last argument, not only of kings but of private persons—is force, and that at the bottom of all private relations, however tempered by sympathy and all the social feelings, is a justifiable self-preference. If a man is on a plank in the deep sea which will only float one, and a stranger lays hold of it, he will thrust him off if he can. When the state finds itself in a similar position, it does the same thing.

The considerations that answer the argument of equal rights also answer the objection that the theory treats man as a thing, and the like. If a man lives in society, he is liable to find himself so treated. The degree of civilization a people has reached is no doubt marked by their anxiety to do as they would be done by. It may be the destiny of man that the social instincts will grow to control his actions absolutely, even in anti-social situations. But they have not yet done so. And since the rules of law are, or should be, based on a morality that is generally accepted, no rule founded on a theory of absolute unselfishness can be laid down without a breach between law and working beliefs.

If it is true, as I shall presently try to show, that the general principles of criminal and civil liability are the same, it will follow from that alone that theory and fact agree in frequently punishing people who have been guilty of no moral wrong—people who could not be condemned by any standard that did not openly disregard the personal peculiarities of the individuals concerned. If punishment stood on the moral grounds proposed for it, the first thing to be considered would be the limits on the capacity for choosing rightly that arise from abnormal instincts, want of education, lack of intelligence, and all the other defects most marked in the criminal classes. I do not say that they should not be considered, or at least I do not need to say so for my argument. I do not say that the criminal law does more good than harm. I only say that it is not enacted or administered on that theory.

There remains the affirmative argument for the theory of retribution: that the fitness of punishment following wrong-doing is axiomatic, and is instinctively recognized by unperverted minds. I think it will be seen, on self-inspection, that this feeling of fitness is absolute and unconditional only in the case of our neighbors. Suppose a man has satisfied himself that an act of his was wrong, and that he will never do it again. It does not seem to me that he would feel the least need or propriety, as between himself and an earthly punishing power alone, in his being made to suffer for what he had done—although, once third persons came in, he might admit as a philosopher the necessity of hurting him to frighten others. But when our neighbors do wrong, we sometimes feel the fitness of making them smart for it, whether they have repented or not. The feeling of fitness seems to me to be only vengeance in disguise, and I have already admitted that vengeance was an element, though not the chief element, of punishment.

But again, the supposed intuition of fitness does not seem to me to reach as far as the thing it is meant to account for. The lesser punishments are just as fit for the lesser crimes as the greater for the greater. The demand that crime be followed by its punishment should therefore be equal and absolute in both. Again, a malum prohibitum, an act wrong only because the law forbids it, is just as much a crime as a malum in se, an act wrong in itself. If there is any general ground for punishment, it must apply to the one as much as to the other. But suppose the wrong in the case just imagined was a breach of the revenue laws, and the government had been repaid its loss. It will hardly be said that we should feel any inner necessity that a man who had thoroughly repented should be punished for it, except on the ground that his act was known to others. If it was known, the law would have to make good its threats, so that others might believe and tremble. But if the fact was a secret between the sovereign and the subject, the sovereign, if wholly free from passion, would undoubtedly see that punishment in such a case was wholly without justification.

On the other hand, there can be no case in which the lawmaker makes certain conduct criminal without thereby showing a wish and purpose to prevent that conduct. Prevention, then, would seem to be the chief and only universal purpose of punishment. The law threatens certain pains if you do certain things, intending thereby to give you a new motive for not doing them. If you persist in doing them, it has to inflict the pains in order that its threats may continue to be believed.

If this is a true account of the law as it stands, the law does undoubtedly treat the individual as a means to an end, and uses him as a tool to increase the general welfare at his own expense. It has been suggested above that this course is perfectly proper; but even if it is wrong, our criminal law follows it, and the theory of our criminal law must be shaped accordingly.

Further evidence that our law goes beyond the limits of retribution, and puts the public well-being ahead of the individual, will be found in some doctrines that cannot be satisfactorily explained on any other ground.

The first of these is that even the deliberate taking of life will not be punished when it is the only way of saving one's own. This principle is not so clearly established as the one I shall mention next, but it has the support of very great authority. If it is the law, it must rest on one of two grounds. Either self-preference is proper in the case supposed; or, even if it is improper, the law cannot prevent it by punishment, because a threat of death at some future time can never be a strong enough motive to make a man choose death now in order to avoid the threat.
