If the first ground is adopted, it admits that a single person may sacrifice another to himself, and a fortiori—all the more—that a people may. If the second is taken, then by abandoning punishment when punishment can no longer be expected to prevent the act, the law abandons the retributive theory and adopts the preventive one.

The next doctrine leads to still clearer conclusions. Ignorance of the law is no excuse for breaking it. This principle of substance is sometimes put in the form of a rule of evidence, that every one is presumed to know the law, and so it has been defended by Austin and others on the ground that ignorance is hard to prove. But if justice requires the fact to be found out, the difficulty of doing so is no ground for refusing to try. And every one must feel that ignorance of the law could never be admitted as an excuse, even if the fact could be proved by sight and hearing in every case. Furthermore, now that the parties to a case can testify, it may be doubted whether a man's knowledge of the law is any harder to investigate than many questions that the courts do go into. Whatever difficulty there is would be met by putting the burden of proving ignorance on the lawbreaker.

Nor can the principle be explained by saying that we are commanded not only to abstain from certain acts, but also to find out that we are so commanded. For if there were such a second command, it is very clear that the guilt of failing to obey it would bear no proportion to the guilt of knowingly disobeying the principal command. Yet the failure to know would receive the same punishment as the failure to obey the principal law.

The true explanation of the rule is the same as the one that accounts for the law's indifference to a man's particular temperament, faculties, and so forth. Public policy sacrifices the individual to the general good. It is desirable that the burden should be equal for all, but it is still more desirable to put an end to robbery and murder. No doubt there are many cases in which the criminal could not have known that he was breaking the law. But to admit the excuse at all would be to encourage ignorance where the lawmaker has determined to make men know and obey, and justice to the individual is rightly outweighed by the larger interests on the other side of the scales.

If these arguments are sound, it is already plain that liability to punishment cannot be finally and absolutely settled by considering the actual personal unworthiness of the criminal alone. That consideration will govern only so far as the public welfare permits or demands. And if we take into account the general result the criminal law is meant to bring about, we shall see that the actual state of mind accompanying a criminal act plays a different part from what is commonly supposed.

For the most part, the purpose of the criminal law is only to induce external conformity to rule. All law is directed to conditions of things manifest to the senses. It may bring those conditions about directly, by the use of force—as when it protects a house from a mob with soldiers, or takes private property for public use, or hangs a man under a judicial sentence. Or it may bring them about indirectly, through men's fears. Either way, its object is equally an external result. In directing itself against robbery or murder, for instance, its purpose is to put a stop to the actual physical taking and keeping of other men's goods, or the actual poisoning, shooting, stabbing, and otherwise putting to death of other men. If those things are not done, the law forbidding them is equally satisfied, whatever the motive.

Put this purely external purpose of the law together with the fact that the law is ready to sacrifice the individual so far as necessary to accomplish it, and we can see more readily than before that the actual degree of personal guilt in any particular offence cannot be the only element, if it is an element at all, in the liability incurred. It is often assumed that the condition of a man's heart or conscience ought to count for more in deciding criminal liability than civil. So far from that being true, it might almost be said to be the very opposite of the truth. For civil liability, in its immediate working, is simply a redistribution of an existing loss between two individuals; and it will be argued in the next Lecture that sound policy lets losses lie where they fall, except where a special reason can be shown for interfering. The most frequent of such reasons is that the party charged has been to blame.

I do not mean to deny that criminal liability, like civil, is founded on blameworthiness. Such a denial would shock the moral sense of any civilized community; or, to put it another way, a law that punished conduct which would not be blameworthy in the average member of the community would be too severe for that community to bear. I mean only to point out this: when we are dealing with the part of the law that aims more directly than any other at setting standards of conduct, we should expect to find there, more than anywhere else, that the tests of liability are external, and independent of the degree of evil in the particular person's motives or intentions. The conclusion follows directly from the nature of the standards to which conformity is required. These are not only external, as was shown above, but of general application. They do not merely require every man to get as near as he can to the best conduct possible for him. They require him at his own peril to come up to a certain height. They take no account of incapacities, unless the weakness is so marked as to fall within well-known exceptions, such as infancy or madness. They assume that every man is as able as every other to behave as they command. If they fall on any one class harder than on another, it is on the weakest. For it is precisely to those most likely to go wrong through temperament, ignorance, or folly that the threats of the law are most dangerous.

How the doctrine that liability is founded on blameworthiness is to be reconciled with liability where the party is not to blame will be worked out more fully in the next Lecture. The answer lies in the conception of the average man, the man of ordinary intelligence and reasonable prudence. Liability is said to arise out of such conduct as would be blameworthy in him. But he is an ideal being, represented by the jury when a question is put to them, and his conduct is an external or objective standard when applied to any given individual. That individual may be morally without stain, because he has less than ordinary intelligence or prudence. But he is required to have those qualities at his peril. If he has them, he will not, as a general rule, incur liability without blameworthiness.

The next step is to take up some crimes in detail, and to discover what analysis will teach us about them.

I will begin with murder. Sir James Stephen, in his Digest of Criminal Law, defines murder as unlawful homicide with malice aforethought. In his earlier work he had explained that malice meant wickedness, and that the law had settled which states of mind were wicked in the necessary degree. Without that preliminary, he goes on in his Digest as follows:—

"Malice aforethought means any one or more of the following states of mind..... (a.) An intention to cause the death of, or grievous bodily harm to, any person, whether such person is the person actually killed or not; (b.) Knowledge that the act which causes death will probably cause the death of, or grievous bodily harm to, some person, whether such person is the person actually killed or not, although such knowledge is accompanied by indifference whether death or grievous bodily harm is caused or not, or by a wish that it may not be caused; (c.) An intent to commit any felony whatever; (d.) An intent to oppose by force any officer of justice on his way to, in, or returning from the execution of the duty of arresting, keeping in custody, or imprisoning any person whom he is lawfully entitled to arrest, keep in custody, or imprison, or the duty of keeping the peace or dispersing an unlawful assembly, provided that the offender has notice that the person killed is such an officer so employed."

Malice, as used in common speech, includes intent, and something more. When an act is said to be done with intent to do harm, it is meant that a wish for the harm is the motive of the act. Intent, however, is perfectly consistent with regretting the harm as such, and wishing for it only as a means to something else. But when an act is said to be done maliciously, it is meant not only that a wish for the harmful effect is the motive, but also that the harm is wished for its own sake—or, as Austin would say more accurately, for the sake of the pleasure that knowing about the suffering would give. Now it is apparent from Sir James Stephen's list that, of these two elements of malice, only the intent matters in murder. It is just as much murder to shoot a sentry in order to free a friend as to shoot him because you hate him. Malice in the definition of murder does not have the same meaning as in common speech, and, for the reasons just given, it has been thought to mean criminal intention.

But intent in turn will be found to break down into two things: foresight that certain consequences will follow from an act, and the wish for those consequences, working as a motive that brings about the act. The question then is whether intent, in its turn, cannot be reduced to a lower term. Sir James Stephen's statement shows that it can be: knowledge that the act will probably cause death—that is, foresight of the consequences of the act—is enough in murder, as it is in tort.

For instance, a newborn child is laid naked out of doors, where it must perish as a matter of course. This is none the less murder because the guilty party would have been very glad to have a stranger find the child and save it.

But again, what is foresight of consequences? It is a picture of a future state of things, called up by knowledge of the present state of things, the future being seen as standing to the present in the relation of effect to cause. Again we must look for a reduction to lower terms. If the known present state of things is such that the act will very certainly cause death, and the probability is a matter of common knowledge, then one who does the act, knowing the present state of things, is guilty of murder, and the law will not inquire whether he actually foresaw the consequences or not. The test of foresight is not what this very criminal foresaw, but what a man of reasonable prudence would have foreseen.

On the other hand, there must be actual present knowledge of the present facts that make an act dangerous. The act is not enough by itself. An act, it is true, implies intention in a certain sense. It is a muscular contraction, and something more. A spasm is not an act. The contraction of the muscles must be willed. And since an adult who is master of himself foresees with mysterious accuracy the outward movement that will follow his inward effort, that movement may be said to be intended. But the intent that necessarily goes with the act ends there. Nothing would follow from the act except for its surroundings. All acts, taken apart from their surrounding circumstances, are indifferent to the law. To crook the forefinger with a certain force, for instance, is the same act whether the trigger of a pistol is next to it or not. It is only the surrounding circumstances—a pistol loaded and cocked, and a human being so placed as to be manifestly likely to be hit—that make the act a wrong. Hence it is no sufficient foundation for liability, on any sound principle, that the proximate cause of a loss, the cause nearest to it, was an act.

The reason for requiring an act is that an act implies a choice, and it is felt to be unwise and unjust to make a man answer for harm unless he might have chosen otherwise. But the choice must be made with a chance of contemplating the consequence complained of, or else it has no bearing on responsibility for that consequence. If this were not so, a man might be held answerable for everything that would not have happened but for some choice of his in the past. He might be held answerable, for instance, for falling on a man in a fit, which he would not have done had he not chosen to come to the city where he was taken ill.

All foresight of the future, all choice with regard to any possible consequence of action, depends on what is known at the moment of choosing. An act cannot be wrong, even when done in circumstances in which it will be hurtful, unless those circumstances are known or ought to be. A fear of punishment for causing harm cannot work as a motive unless the possibility of harm can be foreseen. So far, then, as criminal liability is founded on wrong-doing in any sense, and so far as the threats and punishments of the law are meant to deter men from bringing about various harmful results, they must be confined to cases where the circumstances making the conduct dangerous were known.

Still, in a more limited way, the same principle applies to knowledge as to foresight. It is enough that such circumstances were actually known as would have led a man of common understanding to infer from them the rest of the facts making up the present state of things. For instance, if a workman on a rooftop at midday knows that the space below him is a street in a great city, he knows facts from which a man of common understanding would infer that there were people passing below. He is therefore bound to draw that inference; in other words, he is charged with knowledge of that fact too, whether he draws the inference or not. If, then, he throws a heavy beam down into the street, he does an act which a person of ordinary prudence would foresee is likely to cause death or grievous bodily harm, and he is dealt with as if he foresaw it, whether in fact he did or not. If a death is caused by the act, he is guilty of murder. But if the workman has reasonable cause to believe that the space below is a private yard from which every one is shut out, and which is used as a rubbish heap, his act is not blameworthy, and the homicide is a mere misadventure—an accident for which no one is to blame.

To make an act that causes death murder, then, the actor ought, on principle, to know, or have notice of, the facts that make the act dangerous. There are certain exceptions to this principle, which will be stated presently, but they apply less to murder than to some lesser crimes created by statute. For the most part the general rule prevails in murder.

But furthermore, on the same principle, the danger that in fact exists under the known circumstances ought to be of a kind that a man of reasonable prudence could foresee. Ignorance of a fact and inability to foresee a consequence have the same effect on blameworthiness. If a consequence cannot be foreseen, it cannot be avoided. But there is this practical difference. In most cases the question of knowledge is a question about the actual condition of the defendant's mind, whereas the question of what he might have foreseen is decided by the standard of the prudent man—that is, by general experience. For it must be remembered that the object of the law is to prevent human life from being endangered or taken. The law considers blameworthiness in punishing so far as not to hold a man responsible for consequences that no one, or only some exceptional specialist, could have foreseen. But the reason for this limit is simply to make a rule that is not too hard for the average member of the community. Since the purpose is to compel men to abstain from dangerous conduct, and not merely to restrain them from evil inclinations, the law requires them at their peril to know the teachings of common experience, just as it requires them to know the law. Subject to these explanations, it may be said that the test of murder is the degree of danger to life attending the act under the known circumstances of the case.

It needs no further explanation to show that when the particular defendant does, for any reason, foresee what an ordinary man of reasonable prudence would not have foreseen, the ground of exemption no longer applies. A harmful act is excused only on the ground that the party neither did foresee harm nor could, with proper care, have foreseen it.

At first sight it would seem that this analysis ought to exhaust the whole subject of murder. But it does not, without some further explanation. If a man forcibly resists an officer who is lawfully making an arrest, and kills him, knowing him to be an officer, it may be murder, although no act is done which, but for the officer's official function, would be criminal at all. So, too, if a man does an act with intent to commit a felony, one of the graver crimes, and thereby accidentally kills another: if, for instance, he fires at chickens, meaning to steal them, and accidentally kills the owner, whom he does not see. A case like this last one seems hardly reconcilable with the general principles laid down above. It has been argued somewhat as follows. The only blameworthy act is firing at the chickens, knowing them to belong to another. It is neither more nor less blameworthy because an accident happens afterwards; and hitting a man whose presence could not have been suspected is an accident. The fact that the shooting is felonious does not make it any more likely to kill people. If the object of the rule is to prevent such accidents, it should make accidental killing with firearms murder, not accidental killing in the effort to steal; while if its object is to prevent stealing, it would do better to hang one thief in every thousand by lot.

Still, the law is intelligible as it stands. The general test of murder is the degree of danger attending the acts under the known state of facts. If certain acts are regarded as peculiarly dangerous under certain circumstances, a legislator may make them punishable when done under those circumstances, even though the danger was not generally known. The law often takes this step, although nowadays it seldom inflicts death in such cases. Sometimes it goes further still. It requires a man to find out present facts at his peril, as well as to foresee future harm at his peril, even when those facts are not such as would necessarily be inferred from what he knew.

Thus it is a statutory offence in England to abduct a girl under sixteen from the possession of the person who has lawful charge of her. If a man does things that lead a girl under sixteen to leave her parents, he cannot be charged if he had no reason to know that she was in the lawful charge of her parents; and presumably he could not be charged if he had reasonable cause to believe that she was a boy. But if he knowingly takes a girl away from her parents, he must find out her age at his peril. It is no defence that he had every reason to think her over sixteen. In the same way, under a law prohibiting the sale of liquor, it has been held that a man who sells "Plantation Bitters" has no defence in not knowing that they are intoxicating. And there are other examples of the same kind.

Now suppose experience shows, or the lawmaker believes it shows, that somehow or other deaths which the evidence makes out to be accidental happen disproportionately often in connection with other felonies, or with resistance to officers. Or suppose that on some other ground of policy it is thought desirable to make special efforts to prevent such deaths. Then the lawmaker may consistently treat acts which, under the known circumstances, are felonious, or amount to resisting an officer, as having a dangerous enough tendency to be put under a special ban. The law may therefore throw on the actor the risk not only of the consequences he foresaw, but also of consequences which common experience would not predict, but which the legislator fears. I do not mean to argue, however, that the rules under discussion actually arose from this reasoning, any more than I mean to argue that they are right, or that they would generally be applied in this country.

Returning to the main line of thought, it will be instructive to consider how manslaughter is related to murder. One great difference between the two will be found to lie in the degree of danger attaching to the act in the given state of facts. If a man strikes another with a small stick which is not likely to kill, and which he has no reason to suppose will do more than slight bodily harm, but the blow does kill, he commits manslaughter, not murder. But if the blow is struck as hard as possible with an iron bar an inch thick, it is murder. So, too, if at the time he strikes with a switch the man knows some further fact that lets him foresee that even a slight blow will cause death—for instance, that the other has heart disease—the offence is equally murder. To explode a barrel of gunpowder in a crowded street and kill people is murder, although the actor hopes no such harm will be done. But to kill a man by careless riding in the same street would commonly be manslaughter. Perhaps, however, a case could be put where the riding was so plainly dangerous that it would be murder.

To come back to an example already used for another purpose, here is Blackstone: "When a workman flings down a stone or piece of timber into the street, and kills a man; this may be either misadventure, manslaughter, or murder, according to the circumstances under which the original act was done: if it were in a country village, where few passengers are, and he calls out to all people to have a care, it is misadventure only; but if it were in London, or other populous town, where people are continually passing, it is manslaughter, though he gives loud warning; and murder, if he knows of their passing, and gives no warning at all." Misadventure is simply accident, for which there is no guilt.

The law of manslaughter contains one more doctrine which must be mentioned to complete our understanding of the general principles of the criminal law. It is that provocation may reduce to manslaughter an offence which would otherwise have been murder. According to current morality, a man is less to blame for an act done in the grip of great excitement, caused by a wrong done to himself, than for the same act done calmly. The law is made to govern men through their motives, and it must therefore take their mental constitution into account.

It might be urged on the other side that, if the object of punishment is prevention, the heaviest punishment ought to be threatened exactly where the strongest motive is needed to hold a man back; and early legislation seems sometimes to have gone on that principle. But if any threat will restrain a man in a passion, a threat of something less than death will be enough, and so the extreme penalty has been thought excessive.

Yet even here the objective nature of legal standards shows itself. The mitigation does not come from the fact that the defendant was beside himself with rage. It is not even enough that he had grounds which would have had the same effect on every man of his standing and education. The most insulting words are not provocation, although to this day, and still more when the rule was established, many people would rather die than suffer them without action. There must be provocation sufficient to justify the passion, and the law decides on general considerations which provocations are sufficient.

It is said that even what the law admits to be "provocation does not extenuate the guilt of homicide, unless the person provoked is at the time when he does the deed deprived of the power of self-control by the provocation which he has received." There are obvious reasons for taking the defendant's actual state of mind into account to this extent. The only ground for departing from the general rule is that the defendant was in such a state that he could not be expected to remember the fear of punishment, or be influenced by it. If he could be, the ground for the exception disappears. Yet even here, rightly or wrongly, the law has gone far toward adopting external tests. The courts seem to have decided between murder and manslaughter on such grounds as the kind of weapon used, or the length of time between the provocation and the act. In other cases, though, the question whether the prisoner was deprived of self-control by passion has been left to the jury.

The object of this Lecture is not to give an outline of the criminal law but to explain its general theory. So I shall consider only those offences which throw some special light on the subject, and I shall take them up in whatever order seems best for that purpose. It will now be useful to turn to malicious mischief—the crime of deliberately damaging another's property—and to compare the malice required for that offence with the malice aforethought of murder.

It has been shown that the charge of malice aforethought in an indictment for murder does not mean a state of the defendant's mind, as is often thought, except in the sense that he knew circumstances which did in fact make his conduct dangerous. It is really an allegation like the allegation of negligence. It asserts that the accused did not come up to the legal standard of action in the circumstances in which he found himself, and also that no exceptional fact or excuse was present to take the case out of the general rule. It is a statement of a legal conclusion, which is allowed to stand as a shorthand for the facts, positive and negative, on which it rests.

When a statute punishes "wilfully and maliciously" injuring another's property, it is arguable, if not clear, that something more is meant. We presume that the second word was not added without some meaning, and that presumption is backed by how unreasonable it would be to make every wilful trespass a crime. If this reasoning prevails, "maliciously" is used here in its popular sense. It means that the defendant's motive was a wish to harm the owner of the property—or the thing itself, if it was a living creature—as an end in itself, for the sake of the harm. Malice in this sense has nothing in common with the malice of murder.

A statute need not claim to be consistent with itself, or with the theory adopted by judicial decisions. So strictly speaking there is no need to reconcile such a statute with the principles which have been explained. But in fact there is no inconsistency. Punishment must be confined to compelling outward conformity to a rule of conduct, in the sense that it can always be avoided by avoiding certain acts, or doing them as required, whatever the intent or motive. Still, the prohibited conduct may be harmless unless it is accompanied by a particular state of feeling.

Ordinary disputes about property are satisfactorily settled by compensation. But everyone knows that sometimes secret harm is done by neighbor to neighbor out of pure malice and spite. The damage can be paid for, but the malignity calls for revenge. And since such wrongs are always done in secret, their authors are hard to detect, and that difficulty is a ground for punishment, even if revenge is thought an insufficient one.

How far the law will go in this direction is hard to say. Arson is defined as the malicious and wilful burning of another man's house, and it is generally discussed in close connection with malicious mischief. It has been thought that the burning was not malicious where a prisoner set fire to his prison, not out of any desire to destroy the building, but solely to make his escape. But the better opinion seems to be that this is arson, in which case any intentional burning is malicious within the meaning of the rule. When we remember that arson was the subject of one of the old appeals, which take us far back into the early law, we can readily understand that only intentional burnings were redressed in that way. The appeal of arson was brother to the appeal "of peace and wounds," the appeal for a breach of the peace with wounding. As that one was founded on a warlike assault, so the appeal of arson supposed a house-burning for robbery or revenge, like the one in which Njal perished in the Icelandic saga. But this crime seems to have had the same history as the others. As soon as intent is admitted to be enough, the law is on the high road to an external standard.

A man who intentionally sets fire to his own house, standing so near other houses that the fire will manifestly endanger them, is guilty of arson if one of the other houses burns as a result. Here an act which would not have been arson, if only its immediate consequences were counted, becomes arson because of more remote consequences which were manifestly likely to follow, whether they were actually intended or not. If that can be the effect of setting fire to things which a man has a right to burn, so far as they alone are concerned, why on principle should it not be the effect of any other act which is equally likely, in the surrounding circumstances, to cause the same harm? Cases are easily imagined in which firing a gun, or making a chemical mixture, or piling up oiled rags, or twenty other things, might be manifestly dangerous in the highest degree, and actually lead to a fire. If in such cases the crime is held to have been committed, an external standard has been reached, and the analysis already made of murder applies here too.

There is another class of cases in which intent plays an important part, for reasons quite different from those offered to account for the law of malicious mischief. The most obvious examples are criminal attempts. Attempt and intent, of course, are two distinct things. Intent to commit a crime is not itself criminal. There is no law against a man's intending to commit a murder the day after tomorrow. The law only deals with conduct. An attempt is an overt act—an act actually done in the world. It differs from the crime attempted in this: the act has failed to bring about the result that would have made it the principal crime. If an attempt to murder results in death within a year and a day, it is murder. If an attempt to steal results in carrying off the owner's goods, it is larceny.

Suppose an act is done whose natural and probable effect, in the circumstances, is to accomplish a substantive crime—a crime complete in itself, as opposed to an attempt at one. If the act does not have that effect in the particular case, the criminal law may properly enough lighten the punishment; but on any theory it can hardly refuse to punish it altogether. It has been argued that in such instances only an actual intent can give the act a criminal character. But if the views I have put forward about murder and manslaughter are sound, the same principles ought logically to decide whether acts in general are criminal. Acts should be judged by their tendency under the known circumstances, not by the actual intent that goes with them.

It may be true that in the field of attempts, as elsewhere, the law began with cases of actual intent, since those are the most obvious. But it cannot stop there, unless it cares more about the dictionary meaning of the word "attempt" than about the general principles of punishment. Accordingly there is at least some show of authority for the proposition that an act is punishable as an attempt if, supposing it had produced its natural and probable effect, it would have amounted to a substantive crime.

Footnote: The statement that a man is presumed to intend the natural consequences of his acts is a mere fiction disguising the true theory. I shall come back to it in Lecture IV.

But such acts are not the only punishable attempts. There is another class in which actual intent is clearly necessary, and the existence of this class, as well as the name "attempt," no doubt tends to color the whole doctrine. Some acts may be attempts or misdemeanors—lesser crimes, below the grade of felony—even though they could not have brought about the crime unless the wrongdoer had followed them with other acts of his own. For instance, lighting a match with intent to set fire to a haystack has been held to be a criminal attempt to burn it, although the defendant blew the match out when he saw he was being watched. So the purchase of dies for making counterfeit coin is a misdemeanor, although of course no coin would be counterfeited unless the dies were used.

In such cases the law goes on a new principle, different from the one governing most substantive crimes. The reason for punishing any act must generally be to prevent some harm which is foreseen as likely to follow that act in the circumstances in which it is done. In most substantive crimes the likelihood rests on the ordinary working of natural causes, as experience shows it. But when the law punishes an act whose natural effect, in the circumstances, is not harmful, that ground alone will not do. The probability of harm exists only if there are grounds for expecting the act to be followed by other acts, in combination with which its effect will be harmful, though it would not be harmful otherwise. But since no such acts have in fact followed, it cannot in general be assumed, from the mere doing of what has been done, that they would have followed if the actor had not been interrupted. They would not have followed unless the actor had chosen to do them; and the only means generally available for showing that he would have chosen to do them is to show that he intended to do them when he did what he did. In that case the accompanying intent makes the otherwise innocent act harmful, because it raises a probability that the act will be followed by other acts and events which will together result in harm. The importance of the intent is not to show that the act was wicked, but to show that it was likely to be followed by hurtful consequences.

It will readily be seen that there are limits to this kind of liability. The law does not punish every act done with the intent of bringing about a crime. If a man starts from Boston to Cambridge meaning to commit a murder when he gets there, but is stopped by the drawbridge and goes home, he is no more punishable than if he had sat in his chair and resolved to shoot somebody, and then on second thoughts given up the idea. On the other hand, a slave who ran after a white woman, but stopped before he caught her, has been convicted of an attempt to commit rape. We have seen what amounts to an attempt to burn a haystack; but it was said in the same case that if the defendant had gone no further than to buy a box of matches for the purpose, he would not have been liable.

Eminent judges have been puzzled where to draw the line between these two sets of cases, or even how to state the principle on which it should be drawn. But the principle is believed to be much like the one on which the law draws all its other lines. Public policy—that is to say, considerations of legislation—lies at the bottom of the matter; and the considerations in this case are the nearness of the danger, the greatness of the harm, and the degree of apprehension felt. When a man buys matches to fire a haystack, or sets off on a journey meaning to murder at the end of it, there is still a considerable chance that he will change his mind before he comes to the point. But when he has struck the match, or cocked and aimed the pistol, there is very little chance that he will not go through with it, and the danger becomes so great that the law steps in. With an object which could not be used innocently, the point where the law intervenes might be set further back, as in the case of buying a die for coining.

The degree of apprehension may affect the decision as well as the degree of probability that the crime will be carried out. No doubt the fears peculiar to a slaveowning community had their share in the conviction just mentioned.

There is one doubtful point which should not be passed over. It has been thought that to shoot at a block of wood, thinking it is a man, is not an attempt to murder, and that to put a hand into an empty pocket, intending to pick it, is not an attempt to commit larceny—although on the second question opinions differ. The reason given is that an act which could not have accomplished the crime, even if the actor had been allowed to follow it through to every result it could in the nature of things have led to, cannot be an attempt to commit that crime when it is interrupted. At some point or other, of course, the law must adopt this conclusion, unless it proceeds on the theory of retribution for guilt rather than prevention of harm.

But even to prevent harm effectively, it will not do to be too exact. I do not suppose that firing a pistol at a man with intent to kill him is any less an attempt to murder because the bullet misses. Yet there the act has produced the whole effect it could possibly produce in the course of nature. It is just as impossible for that bullet, in those circumstances, to hit that man as it is to pick an empty pocket. But there is no difficulty in saying that such an act, in such circumstances, is so dangerous, so far as human foresight can tell, that it ought to be punished. No one can know for certain, though many would be pretty sure, exactly where the bullet will strike; and if the harm is done, it is a very great harm. If a man fires at a block, no harm can possibly follow, and no theft can be committed on an empty pocket—besides which, the harm of a successful theft is less than that of murder. Yet it might be said that even such things as these should be punished, in order to make the discouragement broad enough and easy to understand.

There remain certain substantive crimes which differ in very important ways from murder and the like, and which the foregoing analysis of intent in criminal attempts and similar misdemeanors will help to explain.

The type of these is larceny, the common-law crime of theft. Under this name the law punishes acts which would not by themselves be enough to accomplish the evil it seeks to prevent, and which are treated as equally criminal whether that evil has come about or not. Murder, manslaughter, and arson, on the other hand, are not committed unless the evil is accomplished; and they all consist of acts whose tendency, in the surrounding circumstances, is to hurt or destroy person or property by the mere working of natural laws.

In larceny the consequences flowing directly from the act are generally used up with little or no harm to the owner. Goods are removed from his possession by trespass, and that is all there is when the crime is complete. But they must be kept from him permanently before the harm the law seeks to prevent is done. A momentary loss of possession is not what such severe penalties guard against. What the law means to prevent is the loss of it wholly and for ever, as is shown by the fact that it is not larceny to take a thing for temporary use without intending to deprive the owner of his property. If, then, the law punishes the mere act of taking, it punishes an act which will not of itself produce the evil it seeks to prevent, and punishes it before that evil has in any way come to pass.

The reason is plain enough. The law cannot wait until the property has been used up or destroyed in other hands than the owner's, or until the owner has died, in order to make sure that the harm it seeks to prevent has been done. And for the same reason it cannot confine itself to acts likely to do that harm. For the harm of permanent loss will not follow from the act of taking, but only from the series of acts which make up removing and keeping the property after it has been taken. With these preliminaries out of the way, the bearing of intent on the crime is easily seen.

According to Mr. Bishop, larceny is "the taking and removing, by trespass, of personal property which the trespasser knows to belong either generally or specially to another, with the intent to deprive such owner of his ownership therein; and perhaps it should be added, for the sake of some advantage to the trespasser, a proposition on which the decisions are not harmonious."

There must be an intent to deprive the owner of his ownership, it is said. But why? Is it because the law is more anxious not to put a man in prison for stealing unless he is actually wicked than it is not to hang him for killing another? That can hardly be. The true answer is that the intent is an index to the external event which probably would have happened, and that if the law is to punish at all, it must in this case go on probabilities, not on accomplished facts. The analogy with the way attempts are handled is plain. Theft may be called an attempt to deprive a man of his property permanently, which is punished with the same severity whether it succeeds or not. If theft can rightly be seen this way, intent must play the same part as in other attempts. An act which does not fully accomplish the forbidden result may be made wrongful by evidence that, but for some interference, it would have been followed by other acts working with it to produce that result. And this can only be shown by showing intent. In theft, the intent to deprive the owner of his property establishes that the thief would have kept the stolen goods, or at least would not have taken steps to return them. Nor would it matter if the thief afterwards changed his mind and returned the goods. From the point of view of attempt, the crime was already complete when the property was carried off.

It may be objected to this view that if intent is only a makeshift, standing in out of practical necessity for actual deprivation, it ought not to be required where the actual deprivation is wholly accomplished, so long as one and the same criminal act produces the whole effect. Suppose, for instance, that with a single motion a man seizes another's horse and backs it over a precipice. The whole evil the law seeks to prevent is the natural and manifestly certain consequence of the act under the known circumstances. In such a case, if the law of larceny is consistent with the theories maintained here, the act should be judged by its tendency, and the wrongdoer's actual intent should not be considered at all. Yet it is possible, to say the least, that even in such a case the intent would make all the difference. I assume that the act was wrongful and without excuse, and that it would have been larceny if done for the purpose of depriving the owner of his horse. Nevertheless, if it was done for the sake of an experiment, without actual foresight of the destruction and without any evil design against the owner, the trespasser might not be held a thief.

The inconsistency, if there is one, seems to be explained by the way the law has grown. The distinctions the common law draws about theft are not those of a broad theory of legislation. They are highly technical, and to a very large extent only history can explain them.

The type of theft is taking to one's own use. It used to be thought, and sometimes still is, that the taking must be lucri causa—for the sake of some advantage to the thief. In such cases the owner loses his property by the thief's keeping it, not by its destruction, and whether the loss will be permanent can only be judged beforehand by the intent to keep. The intent is therefore always necessary, and it is naturally stated as a self-regarding intent, an intent to benefit oneself. It was an advance on the old precedents when it was decided that the intent to deprive the owner of his property was enough. As late as 1815 the English judges stood only six to five in favor of holding that it was larceny to take a horse intending to kill it, for no other purpose than to destroy evidence against a friend. Even that case, however, did not end the universality of intent as a test. For there the destruction came after the taking, and it is an ancient rule that the criminality of the act must be judged by the state of things at the time of the taking, and not afterwards. Whether the law of larceny would follow what seems to be the general principle of the criminal law, or would be held back by tradition, could only be decided by a case like the one supposed above, where the same act accomplishes both the taking and the destruction. As I have already suggested, tradition might very possibly prevail.

Another crime in which the peculiarities noticed in larceny are still more clearly marked, and at the same time more easily explained, is burglary. It is defined as breaking and entering any dwelling-house by night with intent to commit a felony in it. The object of punishing such a breaking and entering is not to prevent trespasses, even at night, but only those trespasses which are the first step toward wrongs of greater magnitude, like robbery or murder. Here the function of intent, when it is proved, appears more clearly than in theft, but it is exactly the same. It is an index to the probability of certain future acts which the law seeks to prevent. And here the law itself gives evidence that this is the true explanation. For if the feared act did follow, it is no longer necessary to allege that the breaking and entering was done with that intent. An indictment for burglary charging that the defendant broke into a dwelling-house and stole certain property is just as good as one alleging that he broke in with intent to steal.

Footnote: This doctrine goes further than my argument requires. For if burglary were dealt with only on the footing of an attempt, the whole crime would have to be complete at the moment of breaking into the house.

I believe enough has now been said to explain the general theory of criminal liability as it stands at common law. The result may be summed up as follows. All acts are indifferent per se—in themselves, neither innocent nor criminal.

In the characteristic type of substantive crime, acts are made criminal because they are done under circumstances in which they will probably cause some harm which the law seeks to prevent.

The test of criminality in such cases is the degree of danger shown by experience to attend that act under those circumstances.

In such cases the mens rea, or actual wickedness of the party, is wholly unnecessary, and every reference to the state of his consciousness is misleading if it means anything more than this: that the circumstances by which the tendency of his act is judged are the circumstances known to him. Even the requirement of knowledge is subject to certain limits. A man must find out at his peril things which a reasonable and prudent man would have inferred from the things he actually knew. In some cases, especially of statutory crimes, he must go even further: when he knows certain facts, he must find out at his peril whether the other facts are present which would make the act criminal. A man who abducts a girl from her parents in England must find out at his peril whether she is under sixteen.

In some cases it may be that the consequence of the act, in the circumstances, must be actually foreseen, if it is a consequence which a prudent man would not have foreseen. The reference to the prudent man as a standard is the only form in which blameworthiness as such is an element of crime. And what would be blameworthy in such a man is an element for two reasons: first, as a survival of true moral standards; second, because to punish what would not be blameworthy in an average member of the community would be to enforce a standard which was indefensible in theory, and in practice too high for that community.

In some cases, actual malice or intent, in the common meaning of those words, is an element in crime. But it will be found that when it is, it is either because the act, when done maliciously, is followed by harm which would not have followed the act alone, or because the intent raises a strong probability that an act innocent in itself will be followed by other acts or events, in connection with which it will accomplish the result the law seeks to prevent.

Lecture III: Torts—Trespass and Negligence

The object of the next two Lectures is to discover whether there is any common ground at the bottom of all liability in tort, and if so, what that ground is. If the attempt succeeds, it will reveal the general principle of civil liability at common law. The liabilities a man takes on by contract are more or less expressly fixed by the agreement of the parties. Those that arise from a tort are independent of any earlier consent by the wrongdoer to bear the loss his act causes. If A makes a binding promise to pay a certain sum on a certain day, or to deliver a lecture on a certain night, and fails to do it, the damages he has to pay are recovered in accordance with his own consent that some or all of the harms his failure might cause should fall upon him. But when A assaults or slanders his neighbor, or converts his neighbor's property—that is, deals with it as if it were his own—he does a harm that he has never consented to bear. If the law makes him pay for it, the reason must be found in some general view of the conduct that everyone may fairly expect and demand from everyone else, whether that other person has agreed to it or not.

Such a general view is very hard to find. The law did not begin with a theory. It has never worked one out. The point from which it started and the point at which I shall try to show it has arrived are on different planes. In the progress from one to the other, we should expect its course not to be straight and its direction not always to be visible. All that can be done is to point out a tendency, and to justify it. The tendency, which is our main concern, is a matter of fact to be gathered from the cases. But it is much harder to show because, until lately, the substance of the law has been approached only through the categories of the forms of action—the fixed set of writs, each with its own procedure, into one of which every lawsuit had to fit. Discussions of the principles a lawmaker ought to follow have been darkened by arguments over the boundary between trespass and case, or over the scope of a general issue. In place of a theory of tort, we have a theory of trespass. And even within that narrower limit, precedents from the time of the assize and the jurata, the early forms of sworn inquest that came before the modern jury, have been applied without a thought of their connection with a long-forgotten procedure.

Now that the ancient forms of action have disappeared, a broader treatment of the subject ought to be possible. Ignorance is the best of law reformers. People are glad to discuss a question on general principles once they have forgotten the special knowledge needed for technical reasoning. But the present willingness to generalize rests on more than merely negative grounds. The philosophical habit of the day, the frequency of legislation, and the ease with which the law may be changed to meet the opinions and wishes of the public all make it natural and unavoidable that judges, as well as others, should openly discuss the legislative principles on which their decisions must always rest in the end. It is natural, too, that they should base their judgments on broad considerations of policy, which the traditions of the bench would hardly have allowed them to mention fifty years ago.

The business of the law of torts is to fix the dividing lines between the cases in which a man is liable for harm he has done and those in which he is not. But it cannot let him predict with certainty whether a given act under given circumstances will make him liable. An act will rarely have that effect unless damage follows, and for the most part, if not always, the consequences of an act are not known but only guessed at as more or less probable. All the rules the law can lay down beforehand are rules for deciding what conduct will be followed by liability if it is followed by harm—that is, the conduct a man pursues at his peril. The only guide for the future that can be drawn from a decision against a defendant in an action of tort is this: similar acts, done in circumstances that cannot be told apart from the defendant's except by the result, are done at the peril of the actor. If he escapes liability, it is simply because, by good fortune, no harm comes of his conduct on that particular occasion.

If, therefore, there is any common ground for all liability in tort, we shall best find it by setting aside the event as it actually turns out, and considering only the principles on which the peril of his conduct is thrown upon the actor. We are to ask what the elements are, on the defendant's side, that must all be present before liability is possible, and whose presence will commonly make him liable if damage follows.

The law of torts abounds in moral phraseology. It has much to say of wrongs, of malice, fraud, intent, and negligence. So it might naturally be supposed that the risk of a man's conduct is thrown upon him as the result of some moral shortcoming. But while this notion has had its supporters, the extreme opposite will be found to have been a far more popular opinion: I mean the notion that a man is answerable for all the consequences of his acts—in other words, that he acts at his peril always, and wholly regardless of the state of his mind on the matter.

To test the first opinion, it would be natural to take up one by one the words, such as negligence and intent, that in the language of morals name various well-understood states of mind, and to show what they mean in the law. To test the second, it would perhaps be more convenient to consider it form of action by form of action. So many of our authorities are decisions under one or another of these forms that it would not be safe to neglect them, at least at first. A compromise between the two approaches may be reached by beginning with the action of trespass and the notion of negligence together, and leaving the wrongs that are defined as intentional for the next Lecture.

Trespass lies for unintentional as well as intended wrongs. Any wrongful and direct application of force is redressed by that action. It therefore offers a fair field for discussing the general principles of liability for unintentional wrongs at common law. For it can hardly be supposed that a man's responsibility for the consequences of his acts varies according to whether the remedy happens to fall on one side or the other of the penumbra that separates trespass from the action on the case—the action, developed later, for harm done indirectly rather than by direct force. And the greater part of the law of torts will be found under one or the other of those two heads.

It might be hastily assumed that the action on the case is founded on the defendant's negligence. But if that is so, the same doctrine must prevail in trespass. It might be assumed that trespass is founded on the defendant's having caused damage by his act, without regard to negligence. But if that is true, the law must apply the same test to other wrongs that differ from trespass only in some technical point—for instance, that the property damaged was in the defendant's possession. Neither assumption, however, can be allowed to pass so quickly. It might very well be argued that the action on the case adopts the severe rule just suggested for trespass, except when the action is founded on a contract. Negligence, it might be said, had nothing to do with the common-law liability for a nuisance. And it might be added that, where negligence was a ground of liability, a special duty had to be found either in the defendant's super se assumpsit—his having "taken it upon himself," that is, undertaken the task—or in his public calling, such as that of an innkeeper or a carrier. On the other hand, we shall see what can be said for the proposition that even in trespass there must at least be negligence. But whichever argument prevails for the one form of action must prevail for the other. The technical side of the discussion can therefore be shortened by confining it to trespass, as far as that can be done without shutting out the light to be had from other parts of the law.

Footnote: I return to the undertaking and the public calling in Lecture VII.

As has just been hinted, there are two theories of the common-law liability for unintentional harm. Both seem to have the tacit approval of the popular textbooks, and neither lacks plausibility or the appearance of authority.

The first is that of Austin, the English writer on jurisprudence, and it is essentially the theory of a criminal lawyer. According to him, the defining feature of law, properly so called, is a sanction—a detriment threatened and imposed by the sovereign for disobedience to the sovereign's commands. Since the greater part of the law only makes a man answerable in a civil action for breaking it, Austin is compelled to regard liability to an action as a sanction, or, in other words, as a penalty for disobedience. It follows from this, on the prevailing views of penal law, that such liability ought to be based only on personal fault. Austin accepts that conclusion, with its corollaries, one of which is that negligence means a state of the party's mind. These doctrines will be taken up later, so far as necessary.

The other theory is directly opposed to this one. It seems to be adopted by some of the greatest common-law authorities, and it needs serious discussion before it can be set aside in favor of any third opinion. According to this view, broadly stated, under the common law a man acts at his peril. It may be held, as a sort of offset, that he is never liable for omissions except as a result of some duty he has voluntarily undertaken. But outside that class, the whole and sufficient ground for such liabilities as he does incur is supposed to be that he acted voluntarily and that damage followed. If the act was voluntary, it makes no difference at all that the harm that followed was neither intended nor due to the actor's negligence.

To do justice to this way of looking at the subject, we must remember that abolishing the common-law forms of pleading has not changed the rules of substantive law. So although pleaders now generally allege intent or negligence, anything that would formerly have been enough to hold a defendant liable in trespass is still enough, even though the ancient form of action and of declaration—the plaintiff's formal written statement of his claim—has disappeared.

In the first place, it is said, consider in general the protection the law gives to property, both within and outside the limits of the action of trespass. If a man crosses his neighbor's boundary by however innocent a mistake, or if his cattle escape into his neighbor's field, he is said to be liable in trespass quare clausum fregit—the action of trespass "for breaking the close," that is, for entering another man's land. If an auctioneer, in perfect good faith and in the regular course of his business, sells goods sent to his rooms to be sold, he may be made to pay their full value if a third person turns out to be the owner, even though he has paid over the proceeds and has no way of getting reimbursed.

Now suppose that, instead of dealing with the plaintiff's property, the defendant has applied force directly from his own body to the plaintiff's body. It is urged that, since the law cannot be less careful of its subjects' persons than of their property, the only defenses possible are like those that would be open against a charge of trespass on land. You may show that there was no trespass by showing that the defendant did no act—as where he was thrown from his horse onto the plaintiff, or where a third person took his hand and struck the plaintiff with it. In such cases the defendant's body is the passive instrument of an outside force, and the bodily motion the plaintiff relies on is not his act at all. So, too, you may show a justification or excuse in the conduct of the plaintiff himself. But if no such excuse is shown, and the defendant acted voluntarily, he must answer for the consequences, however little intended and however unforeseen. If, for instance, the defendant was attacked by a third person, lifted his stick, and accidentally hit the plaintiff, who was standing behind him, then on this view he is liable, whether or not he was negligent toward the person he hurt.

The arguments for this doctrine are drawn for the most part from precedent, but it is sometimes thought to be defensible as sound in theory. Every man, it is said, has an absolute right to his person, and so forth, free from harm at the hands of his neighbors. In the cases put, the plaintiff has done nothing; the defendant, on the other hand, has chosen to act. As between the two, the party whose voluntary conduct has caused the damage should suffer, rather than one who had no share in producing it.

We have harder material to deal with when we turn to the pleadings and precedents in trespass. The declaration says nothing of negligence, and it is clear that the damage need not have been intended. The words vi et armis, "with force and arms," and contra pacem, "against the peace," which might seem to imply intent, are supposed to have been put in merely to give the king's court jurisdiction. Glanvill says that it belongs to the sheriff, when the lords who held their own local courts neglected the matter, to take charge of brawls, blows, and even wounds, unless the accuser adds a charge of breach of the king's peace (nisi accusator adjiciat de pace Domini Regis infracta). Reeves, the historian of English law, observes: "In this distinction between the sheriff's jurisdiction and that of the king, we see the reason of the allegation in modern indictments and writs, vi et armis, of 'the king's crown and dignity,' 'the king's peace,' and 'the peace,'—this last expression being sufficient, after the peace of the sheriff had ceased to be distinguished as a separate jurisdiction."

Footnote: Fitzherbert says that in the writ of trespass brought before the sheriff, which does not come into the king's court, the words "with force and arms" are not to be used.

Again, it might be said that if the defendant's intent or neglect were essential to his liability, the absence of both would take away his act's character as a trespass, and so ought to be provable under the general issue—the plea of "Not guilty," which denies the plaintiff's charge as a whole. But it is perfectly well settled at common law that "Not guilty" only denies the act.

Next comes the argument from authority. I will begin with an early and important case, from the year 1466. It was trespass quare clausum. The defendant pleaded that he owned the neighboring land, on which there was a thorn hedge; that he cut the thorns, and that they, against his will (ipso invito), fell onto the plaintiff's land; and that he went quickly onto that land and took them away, which was the trespass complained of. On demurrer—that is, on the plaintiff's answer that, even if all this were true, it was no defense—judgment was given for the plaintiff. The plaintiff's counsel put cases that have often been repeated since. One of them, Fairfax, said: "There is a diversity between an act resulting in a felony, and one resulting in a trespass.... If one is cutting trees, and the boughs fall on a man and wound him, in this case he shall have an action of trespass, etc., and also, sir, if one is shooting at butts, and his bow shakes in his hands, and kills a man, ipso invito, it is no felony, as has been said, etc.; but if he wounds one by shooting, he shall have a good action of trespass against him, and yet the shooting was lawful, etc., and the wrong which the other receives was against his will, etc.; and so here, etc." (Shooting at butts means target practice with a bow.) Brian, another counsel, states the whole doctrine, and uses equally familiar illustrations. "When one does a thing, he is bound to do it in such a way that by his act no prejudice or damage shall be done to etc. As if I am building a house, and when the timber is being put up a piece of timber falls on my neighbor's house and breaks his house, he shall have a good action, etc.; and yet the raising of the house was lawful, and the timber fell, me invito, etc. And so if one assaults me and I cannot escape, and I in self-defence lift my stick to strike him, and in lifting it hit a man who is behind me, in this case he shall have an action against me, yet my raising my stick was lawful in self-defence, and I hit him, me invito, etc.; and so here, etc."

Footnote: For the translation of this case I have for the most part followed the one given by Ames.

The judges spoke to the same effect. "Littleton, J. to the same intent, and if a man is damaged he ought to be recompensed.... If your cattle come on my land and eat my grass, notwithstanding you come freshly and drive them out, you ought to make amends for what your cattle have done, be it more or less.... And, sir, if this should be law that he might enter and take the thorns, for the same reason, if he cut a large tree, he might come with his wagons and horses to carry the trees off, which is not reason, for perhaps he has corn or other crops growing, etc., and no more here, for the law is all one in great things and small.... Choke, C. J. to the same intent, for when the principal thing was not lawful, that which depends upon it was not lawful; for when he cut the thorns and they fell on my land, this falling was not lawful, and therefore his coming to take them out was not lawful. As to what was said about their falling in ipso invito, that is no plea, but he ought to show that he could not do it in any other way, or that he did all that was in his power to keep them out."

Forty years later, the Year Books report Justice Rede as adopting Fairfax's argument in that case. In trespass, he says, "the intent cannot be construed; but in felony it shall be. As when a man shoots at butts and kills a man, it is not felony"—and it shall be as if he had no intent to kill him—"and so of a tiler on a house who with a stone kills a man unwittingly, it is not felony. But when a man shoots at the butts and wounds a man, though it is against his will, he shall be called a trespasser against his intent."

There is a series of later shooting cases—Weaver v. Ward, Dickenson v. Watson, and Underwood v. Hewson, running from 1616 to 1723, and followed by the Court of Appeals of New York in Castle v. Duryee in 1865—in which defenses to the effect that the damage was done accidentally and by misfortune, and against the defendant's will, were held not to be enough.

In the reign of Queen Elizabeth it was held that where a man with a gun at the door of his house shot at a bird, and thereby set fire to his own house and to his neighbor's, he was liable in an action on the case in general terms, the declaration not being based on the custom of the realm, "viz. for negligently keeping his fire." "For the injury is the same, although this mischance was not by a common negligence, but by misadventure."

These instances of the stick and of shooting at butts became standard illustrations. They are repeated by Sir Thomas Raymond in Bessey v. Olliot, by Sir William Blackstone in the famous squib case (a squib is a small firework), and by other judges, and they have become familiar through the textbooks. Sir Thomas Raymond, in that case, also repeats the thought and almost the words of Justice Littleton quoted above, and goes on: "In all civil acts the law doth not so much regard the intent of the actor, as the loss and damage of the party suffering." Blackstone also adopts a phrase from Dickenson v. Watson, just cited: "Nothing but inevitable necessity" is a justification. So Lord Ellenborough, in Leame v. Bray: "If the injury were received from the personal act of another, it was deemed sufficient to make it trespass"; or, in the more often quoted words of Justice Grose in the same case: "Looking into all the cases from the Year Book in the 21 H. VII. down to the latest decision on the subject, I find the principle to be, that if the injury be done by the act of the party himself at the time, or he be the immediate cause of it, though it happen accidentally or by misfortune, yet he is answerable in trespass." Further citations are unnecessary.

In spite of all the arguments that may be urged for the rule that a man acts at his peril, however, it has been rejected by very eminent courts, even under the old forms of action. Given this, and given too that since the old forms were abolished the allegation of negligence has spread from the action on the case to all the ordinary declarations in tort that do not allege intent, many lawyers would probably be surprised that anyone should think the present discussion worth the trouble. That is the natural impression to be drawn from daily practice. But even if the doctrine we are considering had no followers left, which is not the case, it would be well to have something more than daily practice to support our views on so fundamental a question. It seems to me, at least, that the true principle is far from being clearly grasped by all who are interested in it, and that it can be reached only after a careful analysis of what has been thought so far. It might be thought enough to cite the decisions against the rule of absolute responsibility, and to show that such a rule is inconsistent with accepted doctrines and with sound policy. But we may go further with profit, and ask whether there are not strong grounds for thinking that the common law has never known such a rule, except in that period of dry precedent so often found midway between a creative age and a period when philosophy begins to dissolve and remake it. To those who, unlike most modern practitioners, still hold to the strict doctrine, I would say once more that there are weighty decisions against it; and that if those decisions were an innovation, the fact that it was made by judges like Chief Justice Shaw of Massachusetts goes far to prove that the change was wise. Having said that, I think I may assert that a little reflection will show the change was required not only by policy but by consistency. I will begin with consistency.

The same reasoning that would make a man answerable in trespass for all damage to another by force resulting directly from his own act, regardless of negligence or intent, would make him answerable in case for the same kind of damage resulting in the same way from the act of his servant in the course of the servant's employment. The discussions of the company's negligence in many railway cases would then be wholly out of place. There is, to be sure, a contract that would make the company liable for negligence, but that contract cannot be taken to reduce any liability that would otherwise exist for a trespass by its employees.

More than this, the same reasoning would make a defendant responsible for all damage, however remote, of which his act could be called the cause. At least so long as only physical forces, or agents not responsible for themselves, co-operated with the act complained of to produce the result, however unforeseen they were, the argument that would decide against the defendant who accidentally struck the plaintiff while lifting a stick in necessary self-defense would require a decision against him in every case where his act was a factor in the harm. The distinction between a direct application of force and causing damage indirectly, as a more remote consequence of one's act, may decide whether the form of action should be trespass or case. But it does not touch the theory of responsibility, if that theory is that a man acts at his peril.

As was said at the outset, if the strict liability is to be kept at all, it must be kept throughout. No principle can be stated that would keep the strict liability in trespass while abandoning it in case. It cannot be said that trespass is for acts alone, and case for the consequences of those acts. All actions of trespass are for the consequences of acts, not for the acts themselves. And some actions of trespass are for consequences more remote from the defendant's act than in other instances where the remedy would be case.

An act is always a voluntary muscular contraction, and nothing else. The chain of physical events that it sets going, or steers toward the plaintiff's harm, is no part of it, and very often a long train of such events comes in between. An example or two will make this extremely clear.

When a man commits an assault and battery with a pistol, his only act is to contract the muscles of his arm and forefinger in a certain way; but elementary writers delight in pointing out what a vast series of physical changes must take place before the harm is done. Suppose that, instead of firing a pistol, he picks up a hose that is spraying water on the sidewalk and turns it on the plaintiff. He does not even set in motion the physical causes that must work together with his act to make a battery. And not only natural causes but a living being may come between the act and its effect. Gibbons v. Pepper decided that there was no battery when a man's horse was frightened, by accident or by a third person, and ran away with him and ran over the plaintiff; and it drew the distinction that if the rider, by spurring, caused the accident, then he is guilty. In Scott v. Shepherd, the squib case already mentioned, trespass was upheld against a man who had thrown a lit squib into a crowd, where it was tossed from hand to hand in self-defense until it burst and injured the plaintiff. Here even human agents were part of the chain between the defendant's act and the result, although the court treated them as more or less automatic in order to reach its decision.

Now I repeat that if principle requires us to hold a man liable in trespass when his act has brought force to bear on another through a fairly short train of intervening causes, even though he used all possible care, it requires the same liability however numerous and unexpected the events between the act and the result. If running a man down is a trespass when the accident can be traced to the rider's act of spurring, why is it not a tort in every case, as was argued in Vincent v. Stinehour, since it can always be traced, more remotely, to his act of mounting the horse and taking it out?

Why is a man not responsible for the consequences of an act that is innocent in its direct and obvious effects, when those consequences would not have followed but for a series of extraordinary, though natural, events coming in between? The reason is that if the intervening events are of such a kind that no foresight could have been expected to look out for them, the defendant is not to blame for having failed to do so. The English judges seem to admit this even on the question whether leaving dry trimmings by the side of a railroad in hot weather, and then sending an engine over the track, are negligent acts—that is, a ground of liability: the consequences that might reasonably have been anticipated are material. Yet these are acts that, in the circumstances, can hardly be called innocent in their natural and obvious effects. The same doctrine has been applied to acts in violation of a statute that could not reasonably have been expected to lead to the result complained of.

Footnote: Many American cases could be cited that carry the doctrine further. But I wish to lay down no proposition that admits of controversy, and it is enough for present purposes that, in the words of an old case, if a man does a lawful act that afterwards becomes unlawful, it is damnum sine injuria—damage without legal wrong. I purposely leave aside any discussion of the true rule of damages once it is settled that a wrong has been done. The text is concerned only with the tests by which it is decided whether a wrong has been done.

But there is no difference in principle between the case where a natural cause or physical factor comes in after the act, in some way that could not be foreseen, and turns what seemed innocent into harm, and the case where such a cause or factor is already present, unknown, at the time of the act—as, for that matter, it was in the English cases cited. If a man is excused in the one case because he is not to blame, he must be excused in the other. The distinction drawn in Gibbons v. Pepper is not between results that are and results that are not the consequences of the defendant's acts. It is between consequences he was bound, as a reasonable man, to contemplate, and those he was not. Hard spurring is so much more likely to lead to harm than merely riding a horse in the street that the court thought the defendant would be bound to look out for the consequences of the one, while it would not hold him liable for those that came merely from the other; for the chance of being run away with when riding quietly, though familiar, is comparatively slight. If, however, the horse had been unruly, and had been taken into a crowded place to be broken in, the owner might have been liable, because "it was his fault to bring a wild horse into a place where mischief might probably be done."

To return to the example of the accidental blow with a stick lifted in self-defense: there is no difference between hitting a person standing behind you and hitting one who was pushed by a horse within range of the stick just as it was lifted, provided that it was not possible, in the circumstances, in the one case to have known, and in the other to have anticipated, that the person was so close. In either case there is missing the only element that distinguishes voluntary acts from spasmodic muscular contractions as a ground of liability. In neither, that is to say, has there been an opportunity of choice with reference to the consequence complained of—a chance to guard against the result that has come to pass. A choice which entails a concealed consequence is as to that consequence no choice.

The general principle of our law is that loss from accident must lie where it falls, and this principle is not affected by the fact that a human being is the instrument of misfortune. But relative to a given human being, anything is an accident which he could not fairly have been expected to contemplate as possible, and therefore to avoid. In the words of the late Chief Justice Nelson of New York: "No case or principle can be found, or if found can be maintained, subjecting an individual to liability for an act done without fault on his part.... All the cases concede that an injury arising from inevitable accident, or, which in law or reason is the same thing, from an act that ordinary human care and foresight are unable to guard against, is but the misfortune of the sufferer, and lays no foundation for legal responsibility." If this were not so, any act would be enough, however remote, that set in motion or opened the door to a chain of physical events ending in damage—riding the horse, in the case of the runaway, or even coming to a place where one is seized with a fit and strikes the plaintiff in an unconscious spasm. Indeed, why need the defendant have acted at all? Why is it not enough that his mere existence has been at the plaintiff's expense? The requirement of an act is the requirement that the defendant should have made a choice.
