But the only possible purpose of bringing in this moral element is to make the power of avoiding the harm complained of a condition of liability. And there is no such power where the harm cannot be foreseen. Here we reach the argument from policy, and so I shall put off for a moment the discussion of trespasses upon land and of conversions, and will take up the liability for cattle separately at a later stage.

A man need not, it is true, do this or that particular act—the term act implies a choice—but he must act somehow. Furthermore, the public generally profits by individual activity. Since action cannot be avoided, and since it tends to the public good, there is obviously no sound policy in throwing the risk of what is at once desirable and inevitable upon the man who acts. The state might conceivably make itself a mutual insurance company against accidents, and spread the burden of its citizens' mishaps among all its members. There might be a pension for the paralyzed, and state aid for those who suffered in person or property from storms or wild beasts. As between individuals, it might adopt the mutual insurance principle pro tanto—to that extent—and divide the damages when both parties were at fault, as admiralty does under its rule of splitting the loss in a collision; or it might throw all loss upon the actor regardless of fault. The state does none of these things, however, and the prevailing view is that its cumbersome and expensive machinery ought not to be set in motion unless some clear benefit is to be gained by disturbing the status quo. State interference is an evil, where it cannot be shown to be a good. Universal insurance, if people want it, can be provided better and more cheaply by private enterprise. An attempt to redistribute losses simply on the ground that they resulted from the defendant's act would be open to all these objections. It would also, as I hope the preceding discussion has shown, be open to the still graver one of offending the sense of justice. Unless my act is of a kind to threaten others—unless, under the circumstances, a prudent man would have foreseen the possibility of harm—it is no more justifiable to make me indemnify my neighbor against the consequences than to make me do the same if I had fallen upon him in a fit, or to compel me to insure him against lightning.

I must now return to the conclusions drawn from innocent trespasses upon land and from conversions—the wrongful dealing with another man's goods as if they were one's own—and to the supposed analogy between those cases and trespasses against the person. Otherwise the law concerning the latter might be thought to sit between two contradictory principles, each driving with equal force to the opposite conclusion from the other.

Take first the case of a trespass upon land that does actual damage. When a man goes upon his neighbor's land thinking it is his own, he intends the very act or consequence complained of. He means to meddle with a certain thing in a certain way, and it is exactly that intended meddling for which he is sued. But if he accidentally hits a stranger as he raises his staff in self-defense, the fact that is the heart of the action—namely, the contact between the staff and his neighbor's head—was not intended, and could not have been foreseen. It might be answered, to be sure, that a man is sued not for meddling with property, but for meddling with the plaintiff's property. On that view, in the cases supposed, just as much as in that of the accidental blow, the defendant is ignorant of one of the facts that make up the whole situation, and which must be present to make his action wrong. He does not know, that is, that the true owner has or claims any interest in the property in question. And so he does not intend a wrongful act, because he does not mean to deal with his neighbor's property. But the answer to this is that he does intend to do the damage complained of. One who lessens the value of property by intentional damage knows that it belongs to somebody. If he thinks it belongs to himself, he expects whatever harm he does to come out of his own pocket. It would be odd if he were to escape the burden by discovering that it belonged to his neighbor. It is a very different thing to say that he who intentionally does harm must bear the loss, from saying that one whose acts happen to lead to harm, as a consequence that could not have been foreseen, must bear it.

Next, suppose the act complained of is an exercise of control over the plaintiff's property, such as a merely technical trespass or a conversion. If the defendant thought the property belonged to himself, there seems to be no abstract injustice in requiring him to know the limits of his own titles. If he thought it belonged to someone else, there is none in holding him bound to get proof of title before acting. Consider, too, what the defendant's liability amounts to when the act—whether an entry upon land or a conversion of goods—has done no damage to the property, and the thing has come back into the true owner's hands. The sum recovered is merely nominal, and the payment is nothing more than a formal acknowledgment of the owner's title. Considering how prescription and the statutes of limitation can turn repeated acts of control into ownership, that is no more than right. All appearance of injustice disappears when the defendant is allowed to escape the costs of a lawsuit by a tender—an offer of payment made in advance—or in some other way.

But suppose the property has not come back into the true owner's hands. If the thing remains with the defendant, it is clearly right that he should surrender it. And if, instead of the thing itself, he holds the proceeds of a sale, it is as reasonable to make him pay over its value in trover (the action for the value of goods wrongfully converted) or in assumpsit (the action upon a promise) as it would have been to compel him to surrender the thing. But whether the defendant has afterward paid the proceeds of the sale of a chattel over to a third person cannot affect the rights of the chattel's true owner. Take the supposed case of an auctioneer. If he had paid the true owner, that would have been an answer to the claim of his bailor, the man who gave him the goods to sell. If he has paid his bailor instead, he has paid someone he was not bound to pay, and no general principle requires that this should be held to take away the plaintiff's right.

There is another point bearing on the argument that the law of trespasses upon property establishes a general principle. Whether the defendant knew of the plaintiff's title is likely to lie entirely in his own breast, and so hardly admits of satisfactory proof. Indeed, in many cases it cannot have been open to evidence at all at the time the law was settled, before the parties to a suit were allowed to testify. So in Basely v. Clarkson, an action of trespass quare clausum, the defense was that the defendant, in mowing his own land, had involuntarily and by mistake mowed down some of the plaintiff's grass. The plaintiff had judgment on demurrer. "For it appears the fact was voluntary, and his intention and knowledge are not traversable; they can't be known."

This language suggests that it would be enough to explain the law of trespass upon property historically, without attempting to justify it. For it seems to be admitted that if the defendant's mistake could be proved, it might matter. It will be noticed, further, that any general argument from the law of trespass upon land to the law governing trespass against the person is shown to be misleading by the law as to cattle. The owner is bound at his peril to keep them off his neighbor's land, but he is not bound at his peril in every case to keep them from injuring his neighbor's person.

Footnote: Compare the rule as to cattle in the Year Books, which is stated later in this Lecture.

The objections to such a decision as the one supposed in the case of the auctioneer do not rest on the general theory of liability. They spring entirely from the special needs of commerce. It does not become unjust to hold a person liable for unauthorized meddling with another's property until there arises the practical necessity for rapid dealing. But where that necessity exists, it is not surprising to find—and we do find—a different tendency in the law. The absolute protection of property, however natural to a primitive community more occupied with production than with exchange, is hardly consistent with the requirements of modern business. Even when the rules we have been considering were established, the traffic of the public markets was governed by more liberal principles. On the continent of Europe it was decided long ago that the policy of protecting titles must yield to the policy of protecting trade. Casaregis held that the general principle, nemo plus juris in alium transferre potest quam ipse habet—no one can transfer to another a greater right than he has himself—must give way in mercantile dealings to the rule possession vaut titre, possession is as good as title. In later times, as markets overt (the open public markets where a buyer in good faith got a good title) have lost their importance, the Factors' Acts and their successive amendments, which protect those who deal in good faith with agents entrusted with goods, have moved more and more in the direction of adopting the Continental doctrine.

Footnote: As to the historical origin of the rule that possession is as good as title, compare Lecture V.

I must preface the argument from precedent by recalling what was said in the first Lecture about early forms of liability, and especially about the appeals. It was shown there that the appeals de pace et plagis—of breach of the peace and wounding—and the appeals of mayhem became the action of trespass, and that those appeals and the early actions of trespass were always, so far as appears, for intentional wrongs.

The words contra pacem, "against the peace," in the writ of trespass were no doubt put there to lay a foundation for the king's jurisdiction over the case. But there seems to be no reason to give a similar purpose to vi et armis, "with force and arms," or cum vi sua, "with his force," as it was often put. Glanvill says that wounds are within the sheriff's jurisdiction unless the accuser adds a charge of breach of the king's peace. Yet the wounds are given with force and arms just as much in the one case as in the other. Bracton says that the lesser wrongs he describes belong to the king's jurisdiction "because they are sometimes against the peace of our lord the king"—while, as has been observed, they were supposed always to be committed intentionally. It might even be inferred that the allegation "against the peace" was originally material, and it will be remembered that trespasses once carried the liability to pay a fine to the king.

If it is true that trespass was originally confined to intentional wrongs, there is hardly any need to consider the argument drawn from the scope of the general issue. In form, the general issue was a softening of the strict word-for-word denial (de verbo in verbum) of the ancient procedure, to which the inquest granted by the king's writ was unknown. The strict form seems to have lasted in England for some time after trial of the issue by recognition—by a sworn body of neighbors—was introduced. When a recognition was granted, the inquest was, of course, competent only to speak to the facts, as has been said above. And when the general issue was introduced, trespass was still confined to intentional wrongs.

Footnote: An example of the year 1195 will be found in Mr. Bigelow's very interesting and valuable Placita Anglo-Normanica. The suit was by way of appeal; the cause of action, a felonious trespass.

We may now take up the authorities. It will be remembered that the earlier precedents date from a time when the assize and the jurata had not yet given way to the modern jury. Those bodies spoke from their own knowledge, either to an issue defined by the writ or to certain familiar questions of fact arising in the trial of a case. They did not hear the whole case upon evidence brought before them. Their function was narrower than the one the jury has since gained, and so it naturally happened that, once they had declared what the defendant had done, the judges laid down the standard by which those acts were to be measured without their help. Hence the question in the Year Books is not a loose, general inquiry of the jury whether they think the alleged trespasser was negligent on whatever facts they may find. It is a well-defined issue of law, to be decided by the court: whether certain acts set out upon the record are a ground of liability. It is possible that the judges dealt pretty strictly with defendants. And it is quite easy to pass from the premise that defendants have been held trespassers for a variety of acts, with no mention of neglect, to the conclusion that any act by which another was damaged will make the actor liable. But a more exact scrutiny of the early books will show that liability in general, then as later, was founded on the tribunal's opinion that the defendant ought to have acted otherwise—in other words, that he was to blame.

Returning first to the case of the thorns in the Year Book, it will be seen that the falling of the thorns into the plaintiff's close, although a result the defendant did not wish, was in no other sense against his will. When he cut the thorns, he did an act that obviously and necessarily would have that consequence, and he must be taken to have foreseen it and not to have prevented it. Chief Justice Choke says: "As to what was said about their falling in, ipso invito, that is no plea, but he ought to show that he could not do it in any other way, or that he did all in his power to keep them out." And both judges treat the unlawfulness of the entry upon the plaintiff's land as following from the unlawfulness of dropping the thorns there. Choke admits that if the thorns or a tree had been blown over onto the plaintiff's land, the defendant might have entered to get them. Chief Justice Crew says of this case, in Millen v. Fawdry, that the opinion was that "trespass lies, because he did not plead that he did his best endeavor to hinder their falling there; yet this was a hard case." The statements of law made by counsel in argument may be left to one side, although Brian, who was one of them, is quoted and mistaken for one of the judges by Sir William Blackstone in Scott v. Shepherd.

The principal authorities are the shooting cases. Since shooting is an extra-hazardous act, it would not be surprising if men were held to do it at their peril in public places. The liability has been put on the general ground of fault, however, wherever the line of necessary precaution may be drawn. In Weaver v. Ward, the defendant pleaded that he and the plaintiff were skirmishing in a trainband—a company of citizen militia at drill—and that in discharging his musket he wounded the plaintiff by accident and misfortune, and against his own will. On demurrer, the court said that "no man shall be excused of a trespass,... except it may be judged utterly without his fault. As if a man by force take my hand and strike you, or if here the defendant had said, that the plaintiff ran cross his piece when it was discharging, or had set forth the case with the circumstances so as it had appeared to the court that it had been inevitable, and that the defendant had committed no negligence to give occasion to the hurt." The later cases simply follow Weaver v. Ward.

The passages quoted above in favor of the strict doctrine—from Sir Thomas Raymond in Bessey v. Olliot, and from Sir William Blackstone in Scott v. Shepherd—are both taken from dissenting opinions. In the latter case it is pretty clear that the majority of the court thought it was not a trespass to ward off personal danger by instantly tossing away a squib, a lighted firework, that someone else had thrown onto one's market stall, even though this gave the squib a new motion and the plaintiff's eye was put out as a result. The last case cited above, in stating the arguments for absolute responsibility, was Leame v. Bray. The question there was whether the action, for running down the plaintiff, should have been case rather than trespass. The defendant objected to trespass on the ground that the injury happened through his neglect but was not done willfully. So there was no question before the court of absolute responsibility for one's acts, since negligence was admitted; and the language used is all aimed simply at the proposition that the damage need not have been done intentionally.

In Wakeman v. Robinson, another runaway case, there was evidence that the defendant pulled the wrong rein, and that he ought to have kept a straight course. The jury were instructed that if the injury was caused by an immediate act of the defendant, it did not matter whether the act was willful or accidental. On a motion for a new trial, Chief Justice Dallas said: "If the accident happened entirely without default on the part of the defendant, or blame imputable to him, the action does not lie.... The accident was clearly occasioned by the default of the defendant. The weight of evidence was all that way. I am now called upon to grant a new trial, contrary to the justice of the case, upon the ground, that the jury were not called on to consider whether the accident was unavoidable, or occasioned by the fault of the defendant. There can be no doubt that the learned judge who presided would have taken the opinion of the jury on that ground, if he had been requested so to do." This language may not have fitted the defendant's plea, which was the general issue; but the pleadings were not referred to, and the doctrine is believed to be sound.

In America there have been several decisions on the point. In Brown v. Kendall, Chief Justice Shaw settled the question for Massachusetts. That was an action of trespass for assault and battery. It appeared that the defendant, while trying to separate two fighting dogs, had raised his stick over his shoulder in the act of striking, and had accidentally hit the plaintiff in the eye, injuring him severely. The case was stronger for the plaintiff than if the defendant had been acting in self-defense. But the court held that although the defendant was under no duty to separate the dogs, still, if he was doing a lawful act, he was not liable unless he fell short of the care that men of ordinary prudence would use under the circumstances; and that the burden was on the plaintiff to prove the lack of such care.

In such a matter no authority deserves more respect than that of Chief Justice Shaw, for the strength of that great judge lay in an accurate appreciation of the needs of the community whose officer he was. Some, indeed many, English judges could be named who have surpassed him in exact technical knowledge. But few have lived who were his equals in understanding the grounds of public policy to which all laws must ultimately be referred. It was this which made him, in the words of the late Judge Curtis, the greatest magistrate this country has produced.

Brown v. Kendall has been followed in Connecticut, in a case where a man fired a pistol—in lawful self-defense, as he claimed—and hit a bystander. The court was strongly of the opinion that the defendant was not answerable on the general principles of trespass unless he had failed to use such care as was practicable under the circumstances. The foundation of liability in trespass, as well as in case, was said to be negligence. The Supreme Court of the United States has given its approval to the same doctrine. The language of Harvey v. Dunlop, Chief Justice Nelson's case, has been quoted already, and there is a case in Vermont that tends in the same direction.

Supposing it now conceded that the general idea on which liability to an action is founded is fault or blameworthiness in some sense, the question arises whether it is so in the sense of personal moral shortcoming—which is what Austin's teaching would come to in practice. The words of Justice Rede, quoted earlier from the Year Book, give a sufficient answer: "In trespass the intent" (we may say, more broadly, the defendant's state of mind) "cannot be construed." Suppose a defendant were allowed to testify that, before acting, he had considered carefully what a prudent man would do under the circumstances, and, having formed the best judgment he could, had acted on it. If his story were believed, it would be conclusive against his negligence, judged by a moral standard that took his personal characteristics into account. But supposing any such evidence had got before the jury, it is very clear that the court would say: Gentlemen, the question is not whether the defendant thought his conduct was that of a prudent man, but whether you think it was.

Some middle point must be found between the horns of this dilemma.

The standards of the law are standards of general application. The law takes no account of the infinite varieties of temperament, intellect, and education which make the inner character of a given act so different in different men. It does not attempt to see men as God sees them, for more than one sufficient reason. In the first place, the impossibility of nicely measuring a man's powers and limitations is far clearer than the impossibility of finding out his knowledge of law—and that has been thought to account for what is called the presumption that every man knows the law. But a more satisfactory explanation is that, when men live in society, a certain average of conduct, a sacrifice of individual peculiarities going beyond a certain point, is necessary to the general welfare. If, for instance, a man is born hasty and awkward, and is always having accidents and hurting himself or his neighbors, no doubt his inborn defects will be allowed for in the courts of Heaven. But his slips are no less troublesome to his neighbors than if they sprang from guilty neglect. His neighbors accordingly require him, at his proper peril, to come up to their standard, and the courts which they establish decline to take his personal equation into account.

The rule that the law does, in general, determine liability by blameworthiness is subject to the limitation that small differences of character are not allowed for. The law considers, in other words, what would be blameworthy in the average man, the man of ordinary intelligence and prudence, and determines liability by that. If we fall below that level in those gifts, it is our misfortune; so much we must have at our peril, for the reasons just given. But he who is intelligent and prudent does not act at his peril, in theory of law. On the contrary, it is only when he fails to use the foresight of which he is capable, or uses it with evil intent, that he is answerable for the consequences.

There are exceptions to the principle that every man is presumed to have ordinary capacity to avoid harming his neighbors, and they illustrate both the rule and the moral basis of liability in general. When a man has a distinct defect of such a kind that everyone can recognize it as making certain precautions impossible, he will not be held answerable for not taking them. A blind man is not required to see at his peril. No doubt he is bound to consider his infirmity in governing his actions. But if he is properly in a certain situation, his failure to take precautions that require eyesight would not prevent his recovering for an injury to himself, and, it may be presumed, would not make him liable for injuring another. So it is held that, where he is the plaintiff, a very young child is bound only to take the precautions of which a child is capable; and the same principle may be cautiously applied where he is the defendant. Insanity is a harder matter to deal with, and no general rule can be laid down about it. There is no doubt that in many cases a man may be insane and yet perfectly capable of taking the precautions, and of being moved by the motives, that the circumstances demand. But if insanity of a pronounced kind exists, plainly making the sufferer incapable of complying with the rule he has broken, good sense would require it to be admitted as an excuse.

Putting together the qualification just established and the general proposition laid down before it, it will now be assumed as follows. On the one hand, the law presumes or requires a man to have ordinary capacity to avoid harming his neighbors, unless a clear and manifest incapacity is shown. On the other hand, it does not in general hold him liable for unintentional injury unless, having that capacity, he might and ought to have foreseen the danger—in other words, unless a man of ordinary intelligence and forethought would have been to blame for acting as he did. The next question is whether this vague test is all the law has to say on the matter; and, which is the same question in another form, by whom the test is to be applied.

Although the grounds of legal liability are moral to the extent just explained, it must be borne in mind that law works only within the sphere of the senses. If the external phenomena—the visible acts and omissions—are such as the law requires, it is wholly indifferent to the internal phenomena of conscience. A man may have as bad a heart as he chooses, if his conduct is within the rules. In other words, the standards of the law are external standards. However much the law may take moral considerations into account, it does so only for the purpose of drawing a line between the bodily motions and rests it permits and those it does not. What the law really forbids, and the only thing it forbids, is the act on the wrong side of the line, be that act blameworthy or otherwise.

Again, any legal standard must in theory be one that would apply to all men, not specially excepted, under the same circumstances. It is not intended that the public force should fall upon an individual by accident, or at the whim of any body of men. The standard, that is, must be fixed. In practice, no doubt, one man may have to pay and another may escape, according to the different feelings of different juries. But this merely shows that the law does not perfectly accomplish its ends. The theory or intention of the law is not that the approval or blame which a particular twelve men may feel should be the test. They are supposed to leave their own peculiarities to one side and to represent the feeling of the community. The ideal average prudent man—whose equivalent the jury is taken to be in many cases, and whose guilt or innocence is the supposed test—is a constant, and his conduct under given circumstances is in theory always the same.

Finally, any legal standard must in theory be capable of being known. When a man has to pay damages, he is supposed to have broken the law, and he is further supposed to have known what the law was.

If, then, the ordinary liabilities in tort arise from failure to comply with fixed and uniform standards of external conduct, which every man is presumed and required to know, it is obvious that it ought to be possible, sooner or later, to put these standards into words, at least to some extent—and that doing so must in the end be the business of the court. It is equally clear that the featureless generality, that the defendant was bound to use such care as a prudent man would use under the circumstances, ought to be continually giving way to the specific rule that he was bound to use this or that precaution under these or those circumstances. The standard the defendant was bound to meet was a standard of specific acts or omissions, with reference to the specific circumstances in which he found himself. If, in the whole field of unintentional wrongs, the courts never got further than the question of negligence, and left every case to the jury without rudder or compass, they would simply be confessing that they could not state a very large part of the law which they required the defendant to know. And they would be asserting, by implication, that nothing could be learned by experience. But neither courts nor legislatures have ever stopped at that point.

From the time of Alfred to the present day, statutes and decisions have busied themselves with defining the precautions to be taken in certain familiar cases—that is, with substituting for the vague test of the care used by a prudent man a precise test of specific acts or omissions. The fundamental thought is still the same: the way prescribed is the way in which prudent men are in the habit of acting, or else it is a way laid down for cases where prudent men might otherwise be in doubt.

It will be observed that the external tests of liability I am about to mention illustrate the tendency of the law of tort to become more and more concrete, by judicial decision and by statute; but their existence does not interfere with the general doctrine I have maintained as to the grounds of liability. The argument of this Lecture, although opposed to the doctrine that a man acts or exerts force at his peril, is by no means opposed to the doctrine that he does certain particular acts at his peril. It is the coarseness, not the nature, of the standard that is objected to. If, when the question of the defendant's negligence is left to a jury, negligence does not mean the actual state of the defendant's mind but a failure to act as a prudent man of average intelligence would have acted, then he is required to conform to an objective standard at his peril, even in that case. When a more exact and specific rule has been reached, he must obey that rule at his peril to the same extent. But, further, if the law is wholly a standard of external conduct, a man must always comply with that standard at his peril.

Some examples of this process of specification will be useful. The laws of Alfred provide for the case of a man who runs himself onto a spear that another man is carrying, and we read: "Let this (liability) be if the point be three fingers higher than the hindmost part of the shaft; if they be both on a level,... be that without danger." In other words, the lawgiver did not ask the vague question whether the spear-carrier was careless. He named the exact position of the point.

The rule of the road, and the sailing rules that Congress adopted from England, are modern examples of statutes of the same kind. The rule of the road has narrowed the question from the vague one, Was the party negligent? to the precise one, Was he on the right or the left of the road?

To avoid a possible misunderstanding, I should add that this question does not, of course, decide the question of liability in every case and under all circumstances. A plaintiff may have been on the wrong side of the road, just as he may have been negligent, and yet the defendant's conduct may still have been unjustifiable and a ground of liability. And no doubt a defendant could, under some circumstances, justify or excuse being on the wrong side. The difference between alleging that a defendant was on the wrong side of the road and alleging that he was negligent is this. The first is an allegation of facts, which will be a ground of liability unless the other side excuses them by alleging further facts. The second is an allegation that involves a conclusion of law, and denies in advance that any excuse exists. Whether the first allegation ought to be enough, and whether proving the fact ought to shift the burden of proof to the other side, are questions that belong to the theory of pleading and evidence, and they could be answered either way consistently with analogy. I should have no difficulty in saying that an allegation of facts which are ordinarily a ground of liability, and which would be so unless excused, ought to be sufficient. But the forms of the law, and especially the forms of pleading, do not change with every change of its substance, and a prudent lawyer would use the broader and safer phrase.

The same course of specification that has been illustrated from the statute book ought also to be taking place as judicial decisions grow. That this should happen agrees with the past history of the law. I have already suggested that in the days of the assize and the jurata, the court decided, in all ordinary cases, whether the facts amounted to a ground of liability. A question of negligence might, no doubt, have gone to the jury. Common sense and common knowledge are as often enough to decide whether proper care has been taken of an animal as they are to say whether A or B owns it. The cases that arose first were not of a kind to invite analysis, and for a long time negligence was used as a roughly simple element before anyone felt the need, or saw the possibility, of analyzing it. Still, when an issue of this sort turns up, the dispute is more about what the defendant did or failed to do than about the standard of conduct. The division of work between court and jury does not come into question until the parties disagree about the standard of conduct.

Negligence, like ownership, is a complex idea. Ownership implies that certain facts exist, and also implies the consequence the law attaches to those facts—protection against all the world. In the same way, negligence implies that certain facts exist (conduct), and also the consequence the law attaches to those facts (liability). In most cases the question is about the facts, and only occasionally does one arise about the consequence.

Footnote: In one of the Year Book cases the plea was that the defendant had performed the cure as well as he knew how, and denied that the horse died for want of his care. The opening of that plea, at least, seems to treat negligence as meaning the actual state of the party's mind.

The reader will have noticed how the judges pass on the defendant's acts, on grounds of fault and public policy, in the case of the thorns, and that in Weaver v. Ward it is said that the facts making up an excuse, and showing that the defendant was free from negligence, should have been set out on the record so that the court might judge them. A similar requirement was laid down for the defense of probable cause—reasonable grounds for bringing a charge—in an action for malicious prosecution, the action against a man who has set the criminal law going against another without such grounds. To this day the question of probable cause is always decided by the court. Further evidence will be found in what follows.

There is, however, an important consideration that I have not yet mentioned. It is certainly possible that those who make the law may think it wise, in some cases, to set the mark higher than the point, fixed by common practice, at which blameworthiness begins. For instance, in Morris v. Platt the court declared in the strongest terms that negligence is, in general, the foundation of liability for accidental trespasses. Yet it hinted that, if it had to decide the point, it might hold a defendant to a stricter rule where the damage was done by a pistol, in view of the danger to the public from the growing habit of carrying deadly weapons.

Again, it might well seem that entering a man's house to bring him a present, or to ask after his health when he was ill, was a harmless and rather praiseworthy act, even though crossing the owner's boundary was intentional. No one supposes that an action would lie today for such a thing, unless the visitor had been forbidden the house. Yet in the time of Henry VIII it was said to be actionable if done without permission, "for then under that color my enemy might be in my house and kill me." There is a clear case where public policy sets a standard of outward acts without regard to fault in any sense. In the same way, policy established exceptions to the general rule against entering another's land: as in the instances put by Chief Justice Choke in the Year Book, of a tree blown over onto it, or of a highway become impassable, or of entering to keep the peace.

Another example may perhaps be found in the shape that modern times have given to the liability for animals, and in the principle derived from it in Rylands v. Fletcher: that when a person brings onto his land, and collects and keeps there, anything likely to do mischief if it escapes, he must keep it in at his peril; and if he does not, he is prima facie—on the face of it, unless he can show otherwise—answerable for all the damage that is the natural consequence of its escape. Cases of this sort do not rest on the notion that it is wrong to keep cattle, or to have a reservoir of water, as might have been argued with more plausibility when only fierce and useless animals were in question. It may even be very much for the public good that the dangerous accumulation should be made. (That consideration might affect the decision in some instances, and differently in different jurisdictions.) But since there is a limit to how finely a trial can inquire into things, it may be thought that the safest way to secure care is to throw the risk on the person who decides what precautions shall be taken. The liability for trespasses of cattle seems to lie on the boundary line between rules based on policy without regard to fault, and requirements meant to put into words the conduct of a prudent man.

I showed in the first Lecture how this liability for cattle arose in the early law, and how far the influence of early notions might be traced in the law of today. Subject to what was said there, it is evident that the early discussions turn on the general question whether the owner is or is not to blame. But they do not stop there. They go on to draw practical distinctions based on common experience. Thus, a defendant chased sheep off his land with a dog, and as soon as the sheep were off he called in his dog; but the dog chased them on into the neighboring land. The chasing of the sheep beyond the defendant's line was held no trespass, because "the nature of a dog is such that he cannot be ruled suddenly."

Footnote: In one report of this case, after the court's opinion is given as in the text, it is said that judgment was nevertheless entered for the plaintiff. That contradicts the earlier statement in the same book, and the other reports; but the principle was at all events admitted. For its limit, see Read v. Edwards.

It was lawful, in ploughing, to turn the horses on a neighbor's land; and if, while turning, the beasts took a mouthful of grass, or the plough tore up the soil, against the driver's will, he had a good justification, because the law recognizes that a man cannot at every instant govern his animals as he wishes. So it was said that if a man is driving cattle through a town, and one of them goes into another man's house, and he follows it in, trespass does not lie for this. So, too, Justice Doderidge said in the same case that if deer come onto my land out of the forest and I chase them with dogs, it is excuse enough for me to wind my horn to call back the dogs, because by this the warden of the forest is given notice that a deer is being chased.

Footnote: This is a further illustration of the very practical grounds on which the law of trespass was settled.

The very case of Mason v. Keeling, which I referred to in the first Lecture for its echo of primitive notions, shows that the working rules of the law had long been founded on good sense. As to animals not then treated as property, which were mainly the wilder animals, the law was settled that "if they are of a tame nature, there must be notice of the ill quality; and the law takes notice, that a dog is not of a fierce nature, but rather the contrary." If the animals "are such as are naturally mischievous in their kind, he shall answer for hurt done by them, without any notice." This second principle has been applied to the case of a bear. It also amply accounts for the liability of the owner of animals like horses and oxen for their trespasses on land, although, as we have seen, that liability was at one time thought to rest on his ownership. It is said to be the universal nature of cattle to stray, and, when they stray into cultivated land, to do damage by trampling down and eating the crops, whereas a dog does no harm. It is also said to be usual and easy to restrain them. If, as I have suggested, the rule had a different historical origin, that does not matter.

Following the same line of thought, the owner of cattle is not held absolutely answerable for all the damage they may do to a person. According to Lord Holt in the opinion just quoted, these animals, "which are not so familiar to mankind" as dogs, "the owner ought to confine, and take all reasonable caution that they do no mischief.... But... if the owner puts a horse or an ox to grass in his field, which is adjoining to the highway, and the horse or the ox breaks the hedge and runs into the highway, and kicks or gores some passenger, an action will not lie against the owner; otherwise, if he had notice that they had done such a thing before."

Perhaps the most striking authority for the view that the judge's duties are not at an end when the question of negligence is reached is found in the discussions of the law of bailment—the law of goods held by one person for another. Consider the judgment in Coggs v. Bernard, the treatises of Sir William Jones and of Story, and Kent's chapter on the subject. They are so many attempts to state the bailee's duty specifically, according to the nature of the bailment and of the thing bailed. Those attempts, to be sure, did not succeed. That was partly because they tried to graft onto the native stock a branch of the Roman law too large to survive the operation, but more especially because the distinctions they drew were purely qualitative, and therefore useless when dealing with a jury. To instruct a jury that they must find the defendant guilty of gross negligence before he can be held liable is open to the reproach that, for such a body, the word "gross" is only a term of abuse. But it would not be so with a judge sitting in admiralty, the court for maritime cases, without a jury. The Roman law and the Supreme Court of the United States agree that the word means something. Successful or not, it is enough for the present argument that the attempt has been made.

The principles of substantive law that the courts have established have, I believe, been somewhat obscured by appearing most often in the form of rulings on the sufficiency of evidence. When a judge rules that there is no evidence of negligence, he does something more than he does in an ordinary ruling that there is no evidence of a fact. He rules that the acts or omissions proved, or in question, do not make a ground of legal liability; and in this way the law is gradually enriching itself from daily life, as it should.

Thus, in Crafton v. Metropolitan Railway Co., the plaintiff slipped on the defendant's stairs and was badly hurt. He slipped because the brass edging on the stairs had been worn smooth by the people walking over it, and a builder testified that in his opinion the staircase was unsafe for that reason and because there was no hand-rail. Nothing contradicted this except that great numbers of people had used the stairs and no accident had happened there, and the plaintiff won a verdict. The court set the verdict aside and ordered a nonsuit—a judgment putting the plaintiff out of court because he has not made a case. In form, the ruling was that there was no evidence of negligence to go to the jury. But this was plainly the same as saying, and did in fact mean, that the railroad company had done all it was bound to do in keeping up such a staircase as the plaintiff had proved. A hundred other equally concrete instances will be found in the textbooks.

On the other hand, if the court should rule that certain acts or omissions, coupled with damage, were conclusive evidence of negligence unless explained, it would in substance and in truth be ruling that those acts or omissions were a ground of liability, or barred a recovery, as the case might be. Thus it is said to be actionable negligence to let a house as a dwelling knowing that it is so infected with smallpox as to be dangerous to health, and to conceal that knowledge. To "explain" the acts or omissions in such a case would mean proving different conduct from the conduct ruled on, or showing that they were not, legally speaking, the cause of the damage complained of. The ruling assumes, for the purposes of the ruling, that the facts in evidence are all the facts.

The cases that have raised difficulties needing explanation are those in which the court has ruled that there was prima facie evidence of negligence, or some evidence of negligence to go to the jury.

Many have noticed the confusion of thought in speaking of such cases as presenting mixed questions of law and fact. No doubt, as I said above, the allegation that the defendant has been guilty of negligence is a complex one. It says, first, that he did or failed to do certain things; and second, that his conduct so alleged does not come up to the legal standard. So long as the dispute is only about the first half, the whole complex allegation is plain matter for the jury without special instructions, just as a question of ownership would be where the only dispute was about the fact on which the legal conclusion rested. But when a dispute arises about the second half, the question whether court or jury ought to judge the defendant's conduct is wholly unaffected by the accident of whether there is or is not also a dispute about what that conduct was. If there is such a dispute, it is entirely possible to give a series of hypothetical instructions fitted to every state of facts the jury may find. If there is no such dispute, the court may still take the jury's opinion on the standard. The problem is to explain the relative work of court and jury with regard to the standard.

When a case arises in which the standard of conduct, pure and simple, is put to the jury, the explanation is plain. The court has no clear views of public policy that apply to the matter, and so it draws the rule to be applied from daily experience, just as, we have agreed, the great body of the law of tort has been drawn. But the court further feels that it does not itself have enough practical experience to lay down the rule intelligently. It believes that twelve men taken from the practical part of the community can help its judgment. Therefore it aids its conscience by taking the opinion of the jury.

But suppose a state of facts that is often repeated in practice. Are we to imagine that the court will go on leaving the standard to the jury forever? Is it not plain, on the contrary, that if the jury is on the whole as fair a tribunal as it is said to be, the lesson to be had from that source will be learned? Either the court will find that the fair teaching of experience is that the conduct complained of usually is, or is not, blameworthy, and therefore, unless explained, is or is not a ground of liability; or it will find the jury swinging back and forth, and will see that it must make up its own mind. There is no reason why any other such question should not be settled, as well as the question of liability for stairs with smooth strips of brass on their edges. The exceptions would be found mainly where the standard was changing rapidly, as, for instance, in some questions of medical treatment.

Footnote: In the smallpox case, while the court ruled on the defendant's conduct as I have described, it held that whether the plaintiff was guilty of contributory negligence—a want of care on his own part that helped bring on the harm—in not having his children vaccinated was "a question of fact, and was properly left to the jury."

If this is the right conclusion in plain cases, further consequences follow. Facts do not often repeat themselves exactly in practice, but cases differing from one another only a little do. A judge who has long sat at nisi prius—presiding over jury trials—ought gradually to gain a fund of experience that lets him represent the common sense of the community in ordinary cases far better than an average jury. He should be able to lead them and to instruct them in detail, even where he thinks it best, on the whole, to take their opinion. Furthermore, the range of cases in which he can rule without taking their opinion at all should be continually growing.

It has often been said that negligence is purely a matter of fact, or that, once the court has declared the evidence to be such that negligence may be inferred from it, the jury are always to decide whether the inference shall be drawn. But I believe that the courts, when they lay down this broad proposition, are thinking of cases where the conduct to be judged is not proved directly, and the main or only question is what that conduct was—not what standard shall be applied to it once it is established.

Most cases that go to the jury on a ruling that there is evidence from which they may find negligence go to them mainly because of a doubt about the conduct, not about the standard. Take the case where the fact proved is an event such as a brick dropping from a railway bridge onto the plaintiff on the highway below. Before there can be any question about the standard of conduct, it must be inferred that the brick fell not because of a sudden action of the weather, but because of a gradual falling into disrepair that the defendant could physically have prevented.

So, in the case of a barrel falling from a warehouse window, it must be found that the defendant or his servants were in charge of it before any question of standard can arise. It will be seen that in each of these well-known cases the court assumed a rule that would make the defendant liable if his conduct was what the evidence tended to prove. When there is no question about the conduct established by the evidence, as in a collision between two trains belonging to the same company, the jury have, sometimes at least, been told in effect that if they believed the evidence, the defendant was liable.

The main argument urged for the view that a wider function belongs to the jury as a matter of right is the need to keep our standards always in line with experience. No doubt the general foundation of legal liability in blameworthiness, as measured by the existing average standards of the community, should always be kept in mind, so that whatever concrete rules are laid down from time to time are kept in line with daily life. No doubt this agreement with daily life is the practical justification for requiring a man to know the civil law, just as the fact that crimes are generally also sins is one of the practical justifications for requiring him to know the criminal law. But these considerations lead only to the conclusion that precedents should be overruled when they become inconsistent with present conditions; and this has generally happened, except in the interpretation of deeds and wills. On the other hand, it is very desirable to know as nearly as we can the standard by which we shall be judged at a given moment; and, moreover, the standards for a very large part of human conduct do not vary from century to century.

The considerations urged in this Lecture are of special importance in this country, or at least in States where the law stands as it does in Massachusetts. In England, the judges at nisi prius freely express their opinions on the value and weight of the evidence, and the judges in banc—the full court, sitting to hear questions of law—constantly draw inferences of fact with the consent of the parties. So fine distinctions about the province of court and jury are not of the first necessity there. But when judges are forbidden by statute to charge the jury on matters of fact, and when the full court will never hear a case that calls for inferences of fact, it becomes vitally important to understand this: when standards of conduct are left to the jury, that is a temporary surrender of a judicial function, which the court may take back at any moment, in any case, when it feels competent to do so. If this were not so, then the almost universal acceptance of the first proposition in this Lecture—that the general foundation of liability for unintentional wrongs is conduct different from that of a prudent man under the circumstances—would leave all our rights and duties, throughout a great part of the law, to the necessarily more or less accidental feelings of a jury.

It is perfectly consistent with the views held in this Lecture that the courts have been very slow to take questions of negligence away from the jury, without distinguishing carefully whether the doubt concerned the facts or the standard to be applied. Legal divisions, like natural ones, however clear in their general outline, will be found on close scrutiny to end in a penumbra, a debatable land. This is the region of the jury, and only cases falling on this doubtful border are likely to be fought far through the courts. Still, the tendency of the law must always be to narrow the field of uncertainty. That is what analogy, as well as the decisions on this very subject, would lead us to expect.

The growth of the law is very apt to take place in this way. Two widely different cases suggest a general distinction, which is a clear one when stated broadly. But as new cases cluster around the opposite poles and begin to approach each other, the distinction becomes harder to trace. The decisions are made one way or the other on a very slight preponderance of feeling, rather than of articulate reason. And at last a mathematical line is arrived at by the contact of contrary decisions—a line so far arbitrary that it might equally well have been drawn a little farther to one side or the other, but which must have been drawn somewhere in the neighborhood of where it falls.

In this way exact distinctions have been worked out on questions where the elements to be considered are few: for instance, what is a reasonable time for presenting negotiable paper (checks, notes and bills of exchange) for payment; or what is a difference in kind and what only a difference in quality; or the rule against perpetuities, which limits how far into the future a gift of property may tie it up.

An example of decisions approaching each other from opposite poles, with the jury at work in the middle, is found in the Massachusetts rulings. If a child of two years and four months is needlessly sent alone across and down a street in a large city, he cannot recover for an injury negligently done to him. To allow a boy of eight to be out alone is not necessarily negligent. And the effect of allowing a boy of ten to be out after dark is for the jury. To these one may add a statement, which may be ventured without authority, that such permission given to a young man of twenty of ordinary intelligence has no effect whatever.

Take again the English law of ancient lights—the right of a house that has long enjoyed the light through its windows not to have that light blocked by a neighbor's building. To be actionable, an obstruction must be substantial. Under ordinary circumstances, putting up a structure a hundred yards away and one foot above the ground would not be actionable. One within a foot of the window and covering it would be, without any finding by a jury beyond those facts. In doubtful cases in between, the question whether the interference was substantial has been left to the jury. But because the elements are few and permanent, the courts have shown an inclination to lay down a definite rule: that in ordinary cases the building complained of must not be higher than the distance of its base from the windows entitled to the light. And although this attempt to work out an exact line calls for much caution, it is entirely philosophical in spirit.

The same principle applies to negligence. If the whole evidence in a case was that a man in full command of his senses and his wits stood on a railway track, watching an approaching engine until it ran him down, no judge would leave it to the jury to say whether his conduct was prudent. If the whole evidence was that he tried to cross a level track, visible for half a mile each way, on which no engine was in sight, no court would allow a jury to find negligence. Between these extremes are cases that would go to the jury. But it is obvious that the limit of safety in such cases, supposing no other elements were present, could be fixed almost to a foot by mathematical calculation.

The trouble with many cases of negligence is that they are of a kind that does not recur often enough for any one judge to profit by long experience with juries and lay down rules, and that their elements are so complex that the courts are glad to leave the whole matter in a lump for the jury to decide.

I reserve the relation between negligent and other torts for the next Lecture.

Lecture IV: Fraud, Malice, and Intent—The Theory of Torts

The next subjects to be considered are fraud, malice, and intent. In discussing unintentional wrongs, the greatest difficulty to be overcome was the doctrine that a man always acts at his peril. In what follows, the difficulty runs the other way. It will be to prove that actual wickedness—the kind these three words describe—is not an element in the civil wrongs to which they are applied.

It was shown, in dealing with the criminal law, that when we call an act malicious in everyday speech, we mean that harm to another person was intended to come of it, and that the harm was desired for its own sake, as an end in itself. For the purposes of the criminal law, however, intent alone was found to matter, and to have the same consequences as intent with ill will added on top. Carrying the analysis further, intent was found to be made up of two things: foresight of the harm as a consequence, and a desire to bring it about, the desire being thought of as the motive for the act. Of these two, again, only foresight seemed to matter. As a last step, foresight was reduced to its lowest terms. The conclusion was that, subject to certain exceptions which were explained, the general basis of criminal liability was knowledge, at the time of acting, of facts from which common experience showed that certain harmful results were likely to follow.

It remains to be seen whether a similar reduction is possible on the civil side of the law, and whether fraudulent, malicious, intentional, and negligent wrongs can thus be brought into a single, philosophically continuous series.

A word of preliminary explanation will be useful. It was shown in the Lecture just referred to that an act, although it always implies intent, is in itself indifferent to the law. An act is a willed, and therefore an intended, coordination of muscular contractions. But the intent that the act necessarily implies stops there. And all muscular motions, or combinations of them, are harmless apart from the circumstances that accompany them, and the act itself does not necessarily imply that those circumstances are present. To strike out with the fist is the same act whether it is done in a desert or in a crowd.

The same considerations which were urged to show that an act alone, by itself, does not and ought not to impose either civil or criminal liability, apply, at least often, to a series of acts—that is, to conduct—even though the series shows a further coordination and a further intent. For instance, to utter a sentence falsely stating that a certain barrel contains No. 1 Mackerel is the same series of acts whether the sentence is spoken in the privacy of one's own room or to another man in the course of a bargain. In either case, to be sure, there is a further intent, beyond the coordination of muscles for a single sound: the intent to assert that a certain barrel has certain contents, which the ordering of the words necessarily shows. But both the series of acts and the intent are in themselves indifferent. They are innocent when spoken in solitude, and they become a ground of liability only when certain accompanying circumstances are shown.

The intent that is meant when we speak of it as an element of legal liability is an intent directed toward the harm complained of, or at least toward harm. It is not necessary in every case to carry the analysis all the way back to the simple muscular contractions that make up a course of conduct. On the same principle that requires something more than an act followed by damage to make a man liable, we are constantly free to treat a coordinated series of acts as a practically simple element, indifferent in itself, when we ask what further circumstances or facts must be present before the conduct in question is at the actor's peril. It will save confusion, and the need for repetition, if this is kept in mind in the discussion that follows.

The chief forms of liability in which fraud, malice, and intent are said to be necessary elements are deceit, slander and libel, malicious prosecution, and conspiracy; to these, perhaps, trover may be added.

Deceit is a notion drawn from the moral world, and in its popular sense it plainly implies wickedness. The common-law doctrine about it is generally stated in terms that fit only actual guilt and actual guilty intent. It is said that a man is liable to an action for deceit if he makes a false representation to another, knowing it to be false, and intending that the other should believe it and act on it—provided the person addressed does believe it, and is thereby persuaded to act to his own harm. This is no doubt the typical case, and it is a case of intentional moral wrong. Now, what is the party's conduct here? It consists in uttering certain words, arranged so that uttering them implies a knowledge of the meaning they would convey if heard. But that conduct, with only that knowledge, is neither moral nor immoral. Go one step further and add the knowledge that another person is within hearing; still the act has no definite character. The elements that make it immoral are the knowledge that the statement is false, and the intent that it shall be acted on.

The principal question, then, is whether this intent can be reduced to the same terms as it has been in other cases. The answer is not difficult. It is perfectly clear that the intent that a false representation should be acted on would be conclusively established by proof that the defendant knew the other party intended to act on it. If the defendant foresaw the consequence of his acts, he is chargeable, whether his motive was a desire to get the other party to act, or simply an unwillingness, for private reasons, to tell the truth. If the defendant knew a present fact—the other party's intent—which, according to common experience, made it likely that his act would have the harmful consequence, he is chargeable, whether or not he in fact foresaw the consequence.

In this matter the general conclusion follows from a single instance. For the moment it is admitted that in one case knowledge of a present fact, such as the other party's intent to act on the false statement, makes proof of an intent to induce him to act on it unnecessary, it is admitted that the lesser element is all that is needed in the larger compound. Intent includes knowledge enough for foresight, as has been shown. So when you prove intent you prove knowledge, and intent may often be the easier of the two to prove. But when you prove knowledge you do not prove intent.

It may be said, however, that in such a case as the one supposed, intent is implied or presumed. But this is only propping up a false theory with a fiction. It is very much like saying that consideration—what is given in return for a promise—is presumed for an instrument under seal, a formal document sealed by the party bound. That is merely a way of reconciling the formal theory that every contract must have a consideration with the obvious fact that sealed instruments do not need one. Whenever it is said that a certain thing is essential to liability, but that it is conclusively presumed from something else, there is always reason to suspect that the essential element is to be found in that something else, and not in what is said to be presumed from it.

As to the intent required for deceit, we need not stop at the single instance just given. The law goes no further than to require proof either of the intent, or that the other party was justified in inferring such an intention. So the whole meaning of the requirement is this: the natural and manifest tendency of the representation, under the known circumstances, must have been to create the belief that it was made with a view to action, and so to lead to action on the faith of it. What is called the standard of intent is thus really an external standard of conduct under the known circumstances, and the analysis of the criminal law holds good here too.

Nor is this all. The law, pursuing the course of specification explained in the last Lecture, decides what the tendency of representations is in certain cases—for instance, a statement at the time of a sale that a horse is sound; or, in general, any statement of fact which it is known the other party intends to rely on. Beyond these specific rules lies the vague realm of the jury.

The other moral element in deceit is knowledge that the statement was false. Strictly, I am not concerned with this, because everything necessary is done once the elements of risk are reduced to action and knowledge. But it will help the general object of showing that the tendency of the law everywhere is to pass beyond moral standards and reach external ones, if this knowledge of falsehood can be turned into a formula that does not necessarily imply guilt, although of course in fact it is generally accompanied by guilt. The moment we look at it critically, we find the moral side shade away.

The question is, what known circumstances are enough to throw the risk of a statement on the man who makes it, if it leads another man to act and turns out to be untrue? Now, a man may plainly take the risk of his statement by express agreement, or by an implied one which the law reads into his bargain. In legal language, he may warrant its truth; and if it is not true, the law treats it as a fraud just as much when he makes it believing it fully as when he knows it to be untrue and means to deceive. If, in selling a horse, the seller warranted him to be only five years old, and in fact he was thirteen, the seller could be sued for deceit at common law, although he thought the horse was only five. The common-law liability for the truth of statements is therefore wider than the sphere of actual moral fraud. But, again, it is generally enough if a representation is made recklessly, without knowing whether it is true or false. Now what does "recklessly" mean? It does not mean actual personal indifference to the truth of the statement. It means only that the grounds for the statement were so far insufficient that a prudent man could not have made it without leading to the inference that he was indifferent. That is to say—repeating an analysis we have gone through before—it means that the law, applying a general objective standard, decides that if a man makes his statement on those grounds he is liable, whatever the state of his mind, and even though he personally may have been perfectly free from wickedness in making it.

Hence the same reasoning already applied to intent may be applied to knowledge of falsity. Actual knowledge may often be easier to prove than that the evidence was not enough to warrant the statement, and when it is proved, it contains the lesser element. But as soon as the lesser element is shown to be enough, it is shown that the law is ready to apply an external or objective standard here also.

Courts of equity—the courts that grew up beside the common-law courts to give relief where the strict common law gave none—have laid down the doctrine in terms so completely detached from the defendant's actual moral condition that they go to the opposite extreme. It is said that "when a representation in a matter of business is made by one man to another calculated to induce him to adapt his conduct to it, it is perfectly immaterial whether the representation is made knowing it to be untrue, or whether it is made believing it to be true, if, in fact, it was untrue."

Perhaps the actual decisions could be reconciled on a narrower principle. But the rule just stated goes so far as to say that in business matters a man makes every statement likely to be acted on at his peril. This seems hard to justify as policy. The moral starting point of liability in general should never be forgotten, and the law cannot, without disregarding it, hold a man answerable for statements based on facts that would have convinced a wise and prudent man of their truth. The public advantage and necessity of freedom in giving information is strong enough to protect even the slander of a third person. It ought all the more, it seems to me, to protect statements made at the request of the very party who complains of them.

The common law, at any rate, keeps the reference to morality by making fraud the ground on which it proceeds. It does not hold that a man always speaks at his peril. But starting from the moral ground, it works out an external standard of what would be fraudulent in the average prudent member of the community, and requires every member, at his peril, to avoid that. As in other cases, it is gradually accumulating precedents which decide that certain statements, under certain circumstances, are at the peril of the party who makes them.

The elements of deceit which throw the risk of his conduct on a party, then, are these. First, making a statement of facts that purports to be serious. Second, the known presence of another person within hearing. Third, known facts sufficient to warrant the expectation, or suggest the probability, that the other party will act on the statement. (What facts are sufficient has been specifically decided by the courts in some instances; in others the question would no doubt go to the jury on the principles already explained.) Fourth, the falsehood of the statement. This must be known; or else the known evidence about the matter stated must be such as would not warrant belief according to the ordinary course of human experience. (On this point, too, the court may be found to lay down specific rules in some cases.)

I next take up the law of slander. It has often been said that malice is one of the elements of liability, and the doctrine is commonly stated like this. Malice must exist, but the law presumes it from the mere speaking of the words. You may rebut this presumption of malice by showing that the words were spoken under circumstances that made the communication privileged—that is, protected—as when a lawyer speaks in the necessary course of his argument, or a person answers in good faith an inquiry about the character of a former servant. And then, it is said, the plaintiff may in some cases meet this defense by showing that the words were spoken with actual malice.

All this sounds as if actual intent to cause the damage complained of, if not ill will, were at the bottom of this class of wrongs. Yet it is not so. The word "malice" points, as usual, to an original moral standard. But the rule that malice is presumed on proof of speaking certain words amounts to saying that the outward conduct of speaking those words may be actionable whether or not the damage to the plaintiff was intended. And this falls in with the general theory, because the manifest tendency of slanderous words is to harm the person they are spoken of. Again, the real substance of the defense is not that the damage was not intended—that would be no defense at all. It is that, whether it was intended or not—that is, even if the defendant foresaw it, and foresaw it with pleasure—the manifest facts and circumstances under which he spoke were such that the law considered the damage to the plaintiff less important than the benefit of free speech.

It is harder to apply the same analysis to the last stage of the process, but perhaps not impossible. It is said that the plaintiff may meet a case of privilege made out by the defendant by proving actual malice, that is, an actual intent to cause the damage complained of. But how is this actual malice made out? By showing that the defendant knew his statement was false, or that his untrue statements went grossly beyond what the occasion required. Now is it not very plain that the law is looking at something entirely different from the defendant's intent? The fact that the defendant foresaw the damage to the plaintiff, and foresaw it with pleasure, matters no more here than it would where the communication was privileged. The question, again, is entirely one of knowledge, or of some other external standard. And what makes even knowledge important? It is that the reason for which a man is allowed, in the other instances, to make false charges against his neighbors is missing. It is in the public interest that people should be free, in certain circumstances, to give the best information they can without fear. But there is no public benefit in having lies told at any time. When a charge is known to be false, or goes beyond what the occasion requires, it is not necessary to make it in order to speak freely. So it falls under the ordinary rule, that certain charges are made at the party's peril in case they turn out to be false, whether evil consequences were intended or not. The defendant is liable, not because his intent was evil, but because he made false charges without excuse.

It will be seen that the peril of conduct begins further back here than with deceit, since slander is more universally harmful in its tendency. There must be some accompanying circumstances. At the least, there must be a human being in existence whom the statement points to. There must be another human being within hearing who understands the statement, and the statement must be false. But it is arguable that the presence of a hearer need not be known—just as the falsity of the charge certainly need not be—and that a man must take the risk of even an idle statement being overheard, unless he made it under known circumstances of privilege. It would be no great curtailment of freedom to deny a man immunity for attaching a charge of crime to his neighbor's name, even when he supposes himself alone. But it is not clear that the law would go quite that far.

The next form of liability is comparatively unimportant. I mean the action for malicious prosecution. A man may recover damages against another who, maliciously and without probable cause, has started a criminal prosecution against him on a false charge—or, in some cases, a civil one. The want of probable cause refers, of course, only to the state of the defendant's knowledge, not to his intent. It means that the facts known to the defendant when he began the suit did not amount to probable cause. But the standard applied to what the defendant knew is external to him. The question is not whether he thought the facts amounted to probable cause, but whether the court thinks they did.

Then as to malice. The defendant's conduct consists in starting proceedings on a charge which is in fact false, and which has failed. That is the root of the whole matter. If the charge was true, or if the plaintiff was convicted—even though he may now be able to prove that he was wrongly convicted—the defendant is safe, however great his malice, and however little ground he had for his charge.

Suppose, however, that the charge is false and fails. It may readily be admitted that malice did originally mean an evil motive, an actual intent to harm the plaintiff by making a false charge. Here again the legal remedy started from the moral basis. The occasion for it was no doubt similar to the one that gave rise to the old law of conspiracy: a man's enemies would sometimes try to destroy him by setting the criminal law in motion against him. Since it was punishable for several to combine for such a purpose, it was concluded, with some hesitation, that when a single individual wickedly attempted the same thing, he should be liable on similar grounds. I must fully admit that there is weighty authority for the view that malice, in its ordinary sense, is to this day a distinct fact, to be proved and to be found by the jury.

But this view cannot be accepted without hesitation. It is admitted, on the one side, that the existence of probable cause, believed in, is a justification in spite of malice. It is admitted, on the other, that "it is not enough to show that the case appeared sufficient to this particular party, but it must be sufficient to induce a sober, sensible and discreet person to act upon it, or it must fail as a justification for the proceeding upon general grounds." On the one side, malice alone will not make a man liable for starting a groundless prosecution; on the other, his justification depends not on his opinion of the facts but on the court's. When his actual moral condition is disregarded to this extent, it is a little hard to believe that the existence of an improper motive should matter. Yet that is what malice must mean in this case, if it means anything; for the evil effects of a successful indictment are of course intended by anyone who gets another indicted. I cannot help thinking that a jury would be told that knowledge or belief that the charge was false, at the time it was made, was conclusive evidence of malice. And if so, for reasons that need not be repeated, malice is not the important thing, but the facts known to the defendant.

Nevertheless, it is obviously treading on delicate ground to make it actionable to set the regular processes of the law in motion. So it is, of course, entirely possible to say that the action shall be limited to cases where the charge was brought from improper motives, at least if the defendant thought there was probable cause. Such a limitation would stand almost alone in the law of civil liability. But the nature of the wrong is peculiar; and, moreover, it is quite consistent with the theory of liability advanced here that liability should, in a given instance, be confined to actual wrongdoing in the moral sense.

The only other cause of action in which the moral state of the defendant's mind might seem important is conspiracy. The old action of that name was much like malicious prosecution, and no doubt it was originally confined to cases where several persons had conspired to get another indicted from evil motives. But in the modern action on the case in which conspiracy is charged, the allegation as a rule means only that two or more persons were cooperating so closely in their acts that the act of any one was the act of all. Generally speaking, the liability depends not on the cooperating or conspiring, but on the character of the acts done, taking them all as if done by one man, or without asking whether they were done by one person or by several. There may be cases, to be sure, in which the result could not be accomplished, or the offense could not ordinarily be proved, without a combination of several people—for instance, the removal of a teacher by a school board.
