The conspiracy would not affect such a case except in a practical way. But the question would be raised whether, notwithstanding the board's right to remove the teacher, proof that its members were moved by ill will would make the removal actionable. Policy, it might be said, forbids going behind their judgment. But actual evil motives, together with the absence of grounds, take away this protection; because policy, although it does not require them to take the risk of being right, does require that they judge honestly on the merits.

Other isolated instances like the last might perhaps be found in different parts of the law, in which actual ill will would affect a man's liability for his conduct. Again, in trover for the conversion of another's chattel, where the control exercised over it was slight and ambiguous, it has been said that the taking must be "with the intent of exercising an ownership over the chattel inconsistent with the real owner's right of possession." But this seems to be no more than a faint shadow of the doctrine explained in connection with larceny, and it needs no further or special discussion. Trover is commonly understood to rest, like larceny, on the plaintiff's being deprived of his property; although in practice every possessor has the action, and, generally speaking, the briefest wrongful withholding of possession is a conversion.

Whether the exceptions are more or fewer, the general purpose of the law of torts is to secure a man compensation for certain forms of harm to his person, reputation, or property at the hands of his neighbors—not because those harms are wrong, but because they are harms. The true explanation of why liability refers to a moral standard, in the sense that has been explained, is not that the law aims at improving men's hearts. It is that the law means to give a man a fair chance to avoid doing the harm before he is held responsible for it. It is meant to reconcile the policy of letting accidents lie where they fall, and the reasonable freedom of others, with the protection of the individual from injury.

But the law does not even try to protect a man from all harms. An unrestricted enjoyment of all his possibilities would interfere with other, equally important enjoyments on the part of his neighbors. There are certain things the law allows a man to do even though he foresees that harm to another will follow. He may charge a man with a crime if the charge is true. He may set himself up in business where he foresees that the effect of his competition will be to reduce another shopkeeper's trade, perhaps to ruin him. He may put up a building that cuts off another's beautiful view, or he may drain the water under his own ground and thereby drain another man's well; and many other cases might be given.

Since any of these things may be done with foresight of their evil consequences, it would seem that they might be done with intent, and even with malevolent intent, to produce them. The whole argument of this Lecture and the one before tends to this conclusion. If the aim of liability is simply to prevent harm, or to compensate for it, so far as is consistent with avoiding the extreme of making a man answer for accident, then when the law permits the harm to be knowingly inflicted, it would be a strong thing if the presence of malice made any difference to its decisions. That might happen, to be sure, without affecting the general views maintained here; but it is not to be expected, and the weight of authority is against it.

As the law, at one extreme, allows certain harms to be inflicted regardless of the moral condition of the person who inflicts them, so, at the other extreme, it may on grounds of policy throw the absolute risk of certain undertakings on the person who engages in them, regardless of blame in any sense. Instances of this kind were mentioned in the last Lecture, and will be referred to again.

Most liabilities in tort lie between these two extremes. They are founded on the infliction of harm which the defendant had a reasonable opportunity to avoid at the time of the acts or omissions that were its proximate cause. But as fast as specific rules are worked out in place of the vague reference to the conduct of the average man, they line up alongside other specific rules based on public policy, and the grounds they grew from cease to be visible. So, as will be seen shortly, rules that seem to lie outside blameworthiness in any sense have sometimes been traced to remote fault, while others that started from the general notion of negligence may just as easily be traced to some outside ground of policy.

Apart from the two extremes just mentioned, it is now easy to see how the law generally fixes the point at which a man's conduct begins to be at his own peril. When we understand the principle by which the law of torts fixes that point, we have a common ground of classification, and a key to the whole subject, so far as tradition has not turned the law aside from a consistent theory. It has been made fairly clear from what came before that I find that ground in knowledge of the circumstances accompanying an act or course of conduct which, but for those circumstances, would be indifferent.

But before that test is discussed, it is worth remarking that a possible common ground is reached one step earlier in the descent from malice through intent and foresight. Foresight is a possible common denominator of the wrongs at the two extremes, malice and negligence. The purpose of the law is to prevent harm to a man at the hands of his neighbors, or to secure him compensation for it, so far as is consistent with the other considerations that have been mentioned—excepting, of course, such harm as the law permits to be intentionally inflicted. When a man foresees that harm will result from his conduct, the principle that excuses him for accident no longer applies, and he is liable. But, as has been shown, he is bound to foresee whatever a prudent and intelligent man would have foreseen; and therefore he is liable for conduct from which such a man would have foreseen that harm was likely to follow.

Accordingly, it would be possible to state all cases of negligence in terms of imputed or presumed foresight. One could even push the presumption further, applying the very inaccurate maxim that every man is presumed to intend the natural consequences of his own acts; and this way of speaking will in fact be found to have been used now and then, especially in the criminal law, where the notion of intent has a stronger foothold. The second fiction is more remote and less philosophical than the first; but, after all, both are equally fictions. Negligence is not foresight, but precisely the want of it. And if foresight were presumed, the ground of the presumption, and therefore the essential element, would be the knowledge of facts which made foresight possible.

Taking knowledge, then, as the true starting point, the next question is how to determine what circumstances a man must have known, in any given case, in order to be liable for the consequences of his act. They must be such as would have led a prudent man to perceive danger, although not necessarily to foresee the specific harm. But this is a vague test. How is it decided what those circumstances are? The answer must be: by experience.

But there is one point which has been left ambiguous, both in the preceding Lecture and here, and it must be touched upon. It has been assumed that conduct which the man of ordinary intelligence would perceive to be dangerous under the circumstances would be blameworthy if he pursued it. That might not be so, however. Suppose that a man, acting under the threats of twelve armed men which put him in fear of his life, enters another man's close—his enclosed land—and takes a horse. In such a case he actually contemplates and chooses harm to another as the consequence of his act. Yet the act is neither blameworthy nor punishable. It might, however, be actionable, and Chief Justice Rolle ruled that it was, in Gilbert v. Stone, in 1648. If that is law, it goes the full length of deciding that it is enough if the defendant had a chance to avoid inflicting the harm complained of. And it may well be argued that, although he does wisely to ransom his life as best he can, there is no reason why he should be allowed to transfer his misfortunes, intentionally and permanently, to the shoulders of his neighbors.

It cannot be inferred, from the mere fact that certain conduct is made actionable, that the law therefore regards it as wrong or seeks to prevent it. Under our mill acts—the statutes that let a man dam a stream to run a mill, even though the water backs up over his neighbor's land—a man has to pay for flooding his neighbor's lands, just as in trover he has to pay for converting his neighbor's goods. Yet the law approves and encourages the flooding of lands for the building of mills.

Moral predilections must not be allowed to influence our minds in settling legal distinctions. If we look only at the liability, how are we to distinguish between trover and the mill acts? Or between conduct which is prohibited and conduct which is merely taxed? The only distinction I can see lies in the different collateral consequences attached to the two classes of conduct. In the first class, the maxim in pari delicto potior est conditio defendentis—where both parties are equally in the wrong, the defendant is in the stronger position—together with the invalidity of contracts that contemplate such conduct, shows that the conduct is outside the protection of the law. In the second class, it is otherwise. This opinion is confirmed by the fact that almost the only cases in which the distinction between prohibition and taxation comes up at all concern the application of these maxims.

But if this is true, liability to an action does not necessarily imply wrongdoing. And this may be admitted without in the least weakening the argument of the foregoing Lecture, which only requires that people should not be made to pay for accidents which they could not have avoided.

It is doubtful, however, whether Chief Justice Rolle's ruling would be followed now. The squib case, Scott v. Shepherd—where a lighted firework thrown into a crowded market was tossed on from stall to stall until it exploded in a man's face—and the language of some textbooks are more or less opposed to it. If this latter view is the law, then in general an act must be not only dangerous, but one which would be blameworthy on the part of the average man, in order to make the actor liable. But apart from such exceptional cases as Gilbert v. Stone, the two tests agree, and the difference need not be considered in what follows.

Footnote: The broad language of one of these textbooks rests on an old case in the Year Books which hardly sustains it.

I therefore repeat that experience is the test by which it is decided whether the degree of danger attending given conduct, under certain known circumstances, is great enough to throw the risk upon the party who pursues it.

For instance, experience shows that a good many guns supposed to be unloaded go off and hurt people. The ordinarily intelligent and prudent member of the community would foresee the possibility of danger from pointing a gun which he had not inspected into a crowd and pulling the trigger, even though he had been told it was unloaded. Hence it may very properly be held that a man who does such a thing does it at his peril, and that if damage follows he is answerable for it. The co-ordinated acts needed to point a gun and pull a trigger, and the intent and knowledge shown by the co-ordination of those acts, are all consistent with entire blamelessness. Without further facts they threaten harm to no one. But the one additional circumstance of a man standing in the line of fire and within range makes the conduct manifestly dangerous to anyone who knows that fact. There is no longer any need to refer to the prudent man, or to general experience. The facts have taught their lesson, and have generated a concrete and external rule of liability. He who snaps the cap of a gun pointed toward another person, whom he knows to be there, is answerable for the consequences.

The question what a prudent man would do under given circumstances is, then, the same as the question what experience teaches about the dangerous character of this or that conduct under these or those circumstances. And since the teachings of experience are matters of fact, it is easy to see why the jury should be consulted about them. They are, however, facts of a special and peculiar function. Their only bearing is on the question what ought to have been done or left undone in the circumstances of the case, not on what was done. Their function is to suggest a rule of conduct.

Sometimes courts are led to lay down rules by facts of a more specific kind: that the legislature passed a certain statute, and that the case before the court is within the fair meaning of its words; or that the practice of a specially interested class, or of the public at large, has produced a rule of conduct outside the law which it is desirable for the courts to recognize and enforce. These are matters of fact, and have sometimes been pleaded as such. But their only importance is that, if believed, they will lead the judges to lay down a rule of conduct—in other words, a rule of law—which they suggest. So their tendency in most instances is to disappear as fast as the rules they suggest become settled. While the facts are uncertain, they are still only motives for deciding the law—grounds for legislation, so to speak—and the judges may ascertain them in any way which satisfies their conscience. Thus courts take judicial notice of the statutes of their own jurisdiction, that is, they recognize them without proof, although the laws of other jurisdictions, with doubtful wisdom, are left to the jury. They may take judicial notice of a custom of merchants. In former days, at least, they might inquire into such a custom out of court, in pais, after a demurrer. They may act on the statement of a special jury—a jury of merchants or other men with knowledge of the trade—as in the time of Lord Mansfield and his successors, or on the finding of a common jury based on the testimony of witnesses, as is the practice in this country today. But many instances will be found in the textbooks which show that, once the facts are ascertained, they soon cease to be referred to, and give place to a rule of law.

The same transition can be seen in the teachings of experience. There are many cases, no doubt, in which the court would lean on a jury for help; but there are also many in which the teaching has been formulated in specific rules. These rules will be found to vary a good deal in the number of accompanying circumstances needed to throw the peril of conduct, otherwise indifferent, on the actor. As the circumstances become more numerous and complex, the tendency to cut the knot by handing it to the jury grows greater. It will be useful, by way of illustration, to follow a line of cases up from the simple to the more complicated. Along the way, particular notice will be taken of how hard it is to distinguish rules based on other grounds of policy from those which have been worked out in the field of negligence.

In all these cases it will be found that there has been a voluntary act on the part of the person to be charged. The reason for this requirement was shown in the foregoing Lecture. Although it is unnecessary for the defendant to have intended or foreseen the evil he has caused, it is necessary that he should have chosen the conduct which led to it. But it has also been shown that a voluntary act is not enough, and that even a co-ordinated series of acts, or conduct, is often not enough by itself. Still, the co-ordination of a series of acts shows a further intent than any single act necessarily shows, and sometimes proves, with almost equal certainty, knowledge of one or more accompanying circumstances. And there are cases where conduct carrying only the intent and knowledge thus necessarily implied is enough to throw its risk on the actor.

For instance, when a man performs the series of acts called walking, it is assumed for all purposes of responsibility that he knows the earth is under his feet. The conduct in itself is indifferent, to be sure. A man may go through the motions of walking without legal peril if he chooses to practise on a private treadmill; but if he goes through the same motions on the surface of the earth, it cannot be doubted that he knows the earth is there. With that knowledge he acts at his peril in certain respects. If he crosses his neighbor's boundary, he is a trespasser. The reasons for this strict rule were partly discussed in the last Lecture. Possibly there is more of history, or of past or present notions of policy, in its explanation than was suggested there, and at any rate I do not care to justify the rule. But it is intelligible. A man who walks knows that he is moving over the surface of the earth; he knows that he is surrounded by private estates which he has no right to enter; and he knows that his motion, unless properly guided, will carry him into those estates. He is thus warned, and the burden of his conduct is thrown upon himself.

But the act of walking does not throw the peril of all its possible consequences upon him. He may run a man down in the street, but he is not liable for that unless he does it negligently. Confused as the law is by the cross-lights of tradition, and hard as we may find it to arrive at a perfectly satisfactory general theory, it does distinguish, in a pretty sensible way, according to the nature and degree of the different perils that belong to a given situation.

From the simple case of walking we may go on to the more complex cases of dealing with tangible things that are someone's property. Generally speaking, it may be said that a man meddles with such things at his own risk. It does not matter how honestly he believes that they belong to him, or are free to the public, or that he has the owner's permission, or that the case is one in which the law has limited the rights of ownership. He takes his chance on how the facts turn out, and if they are otherwise than he supposes, he must answer for his conduct. As has already been suggested, he knows that he is exercising more or less control over property, or that he is injuring it; he must make good his right if it is challenged.

Whether this strict rule rests on the common grounds of liability or on some special consideration of past or present policy, policy has set some limits to it, as was mentioned in the foregoing Lecture.

Another kind of conduct which is at the party's risk, without any further knowledge than the conduct itself implies, is keeping a tiger or a bear, or any other animal of a species commonly known to be ferocious. If such an animal escapes and does damage, the owner is liable simply on proof that he kept it. Notice in particular, in this instance, how far back along the chain of causes the moment of choice lies from the harm complained of. Ordinary cases of liability arise out of a choice which was the proximate cause of the harm on which the action is founded. But here there is usually no question of negligence in guarding the beast. In most cases, if not in all, it is enough that the owner chose to keep it. Experience has shown that tigers and bears are alert to find ways of escape, and that if they escape they are very likely to do serious harm. The possibility of a great danger has the same effect as the probability of a lesser one, and the law throws the risk of the venture on the person who brings the peril into the community.

This remoteness of the opportunity to choose goes far to show that the risk is thrown on the owner for some reason other than the ordinary one of imprudent conduct. It has been suggested that the liability rests on remote inadvertence—on a carelessness far back in the chain. But the law does not forbid a man to keep a menagerie, or regard it as in any way blameworthy. And it has applied nearly as strict a rule to activities which are even more clearly useful to the community than a show of wild beasts.

This seems to be one of those cases where the ground of liability is to be sought in policy coupled with tradition, rather than in any form of blameworthiness, or in the existence of such a chance to avoid doing the harm as a man is usually allowed. But the very fact that remote inadvertence has been offered as an explanation illustrates what has been said about the difficulty of deciding, once a special rule has been laid down, whether it is founded on special grounds or has been worked out within the sphere of negligence.

It should further be noticed that no question is raised about the defendant's knowledge of the nature of tigers, although without that knowledge he cannot be said to have intelligently chosen to expose the community to danger. Here again, even in the domain of knowledge, the law applies its principle of averages. That tigers and bears are dangerous is so generally known that a man who keeps them is presumed to know their peculiarities. In other words, he does actually know that he has an animal with certain teeth, claws, and so forth, and he must find out the rest of what an average member of the community would know, at his peril.

What is true of damage in general done by ferocious wild beasts is true of one particular class of damage done by domestic cattle, namely, trespasses on another's land. This has been dealt with in earlier Lectures, so it is needless to do more than recall it here and point out the distinction, based on experience and policy, between damage which is of a kind to be expected and damage which is not. Cattle generally stray, and damage cultivated land when they get onto it. Only exceptionally do they hurt human beings.

I need not return to the possible historical connection of either of these last two forms of liability with noxal surrender. Whether that origin is proved or not, the policy of the rule has been accepted as sound, and within the last few years it has been carried further in England, by the doctrine of Rylands v. Fletcher: that a man who brings onto his land and keeps there anything likely to do mischief if it escapes must keep it in at his peril. How strict this principle is will vary from one jurisdiction to another, as the balance varies between the advantages to the public and the dangers to individuals from the conduct in question. Danger of harm to others is not the only thing to be considered, as has already been said. The law allows some harms to be inflicted intentionally, and all the more, a fortiori, some risks to be run intentionally. In some Western States a man is not required to keep his cattle fenced in. Some courts have refused to follow Rylands v. Fletcher. On the other hand, the principle has been applied to artificial reservoirs of water, to cesspools, to accumulations of snow and ice on a building caused by the shape of its roof, and to party walls—the shared walls between neighboring buildings.

In these cases, as in that of ferocious animals, it is no excuse that the defendant did not know, and could not have found out, the weak point through which the dangerous thing escaped. The moment of choice lay further back; and although he was not to blame, he was bound at his peril to know that the thing was a continual threat to his neighbors, and that is enough to throw the risk of the business on him.

I now pass to cases one degree more complex than those considered so far. In these, the party must have known one more accompanying circumstance, in addition to those whose knowledge is necessarily or practically proved by his conduct. The cases which naturally come to mind again concern animals. Experience, as interpreted by the English law, has shown that dogs, rams, and bulls are in general tame and mild by nature, and that if one of them does happen to show a tendency to bite, butt, or gore, it is an exceptional thing. Hence it is not the law that a man keeps dogs, rams, bulls, and other such tame animals at his peril as to the personal injuries they may inflict—unless he knows, or has notice, that the particular animal he keeps has the abnormal tendency which they do sometimes show. The law has, however, been brought a little nearer to actual experience by statute in many jurisdictions.

Now let us go one step further still. A man keeps an unbroken and unruly horse, knowing it to be so. That is not enough to throw the risk of its behavior on him. The known wildness is not dangerous in general, but only in particular circumstances. Add to the keeping an attempt to break the horse; still no danger to the public is shown. But if the place where the owner tries to break it is a crowded thoroughfare, the owner knows an additional circumstance which, by common experience, makes this conduct dangerous, and he must therefore take the risk of whatever harm is done. On the other hand, if a man who was a good rider bought a horse with no sign of vice and mounted it to ride home, there would be no such apparent danger as to make him answerable if the horse became unruly and did damage. Experience has measured the probabilities, and it draws the line between the two cases.

Whatever the true explanation of the rule applied to keeping tigers, or of the principle of Rylands v. Fletcher, in these last cases we have entered the sphere of negligence. And if we take a case lying somewhere between the two just stated, and add a little to the complexity of the circumstances, we shall find that both the conduct and the standard would probably be left to the jury without much sorting, on the broad issue whether the defendant had acted as a prudent man would have done under the circumstances.

As for the wrongs called malicious or intentional, it is not necessary to list the different classes a second time and find each a place in this series. As has been seen, they vary in the number of circumstances which must be known. Slander is conduct very generally at the speaker's risk. Charges of the kind it deals with are manifestly harmful, so the questions which actually arise mostly concern the defenses of truth or privilege. Deceit requires more, but still simple facts. Statements do not threaten the harm in question unless they are made under such circumstances as would naturally lead someone to act on them, and are made on insufficient grounds.

It is not without significance, however, that certain wrongs are described in language implying intent. In such cases the harm is most often done intentionally; and if intent to cause a certain harm is shown, there is no need to prove knowledge of facts which made it likely that harm would follow. Moreover, it is often much easier to prove intent directly than to prove the knowledge which would make it unnecessary.

The cases in which a man is treated as the responsible cause of a given harm extend, on the one hand, beyond those in which his conduct was chosen in actual contemplation of that result, and in which, therefore, he may be said to have chosen to cause that harm. On the other hand, they do not extend to every instance in which the damage would not have happened but for some remote choice on his part. Generally speaking, the choice will be found to have gone further than a simple act, and to have joined co-ordinated acts into conduct. Very commonly it will have gone further still, to some external consequence. But generally, too, it will be found to have stopped short of the consequence complained of.

The question in each case is whether the actual choice—in other words, the result actually contemplated—was near enough to the remoter result complained of to throw the peril of it on the actor.

Many of the cases put so far are cases in which the defendant intended to produce the proximate cause of the loss. But it will be seen that the same result may be caused by a choice made at different points. Suppose, for instance, a man is sued for having caused his neighbor's house to burn down. The simplest case is that he actually intended to burn it down. If so, the length of the chain of physical causes in between is of no importance, and has no bearing on the case.

But the choice may have stopped one step further back. The defendant may have intended to light a fire on his own land, and not intended to burn the house. Then the nature of the physical causes that intervened and accompanied the act becomes of the highest importance. The question will be the degree of danger attending the contemplated (and therefore chosen) effect of the defendant's conduct, under the circumstances known to him. If the danger was very plain and very great—if, for instance, his conduct consisted in lighting stubble near a haystack close to the house, and the evident circumstances were that the house was of wood, the stubble very dry, and the wind in a dangerous quarter—the court would probably rule that he was liable. If the defendant lit an ordinary fire in a fireplace in the house next door, knowing nothing of the fireplace's being unsafely built, the court would probably rule that he was not liable. In the middle, complicated and doubtful cases would go to the jury.

But the defendant may not even have intended to set a fire. His conduct and intent may have been simply to fire a gun, or, more remote still, to walk across a room, in doing which he involuntarily upset a bottle of acid. So cases may go to the jury because the choice lies far back in the series of events, as well as because of the complexity of the circumstances attending the act or conduct. The difference is perhaps more dramatic than substantial.

But the philosophical analysis of every wrong begins by determining what the defendant has actually chosen—that is, what his voluntary act or conduct has been, and what consequences he has actually contemplated as flowing from it—and then goes on to determine what dangers attended either the conduct under the known circumstances, or its contemplated consequence under the contemplated circumstances.

Take a case like the glancing of Sir Walter Tyrrel's arrow, which, as the story goes, glanced off a tree in the New Forest and killed King William Rufus. If an expert marksman contemplated that the arrow would hit a certain person, cadit quaestio—the question is settled. If he contemplated that it would glance in the direction of another person, but contemplated no more than that, then in order to judge his liability we must go to the end of his foresight, assume the foreseen event to happen, and consider what the manifest danger was at that point. But if no such event was foreseen, the marksman must be judged by the circumstances known to him at the time he shot.

The theory of torts may be summed up very simply. At the two extremes of the law are rules determined by policy without any reference to morality. Certain harms a man may inflict even wickedly; for certain others he must answer, although his conduct has been prudent and beneficial to the community.

But in the main, the law started from those intentional wrongs which are the simplest and most pronounced cases, as well as the nearest to the feeling of revenge which leads men to take the law into their own hands. It thus naturally adopted the vocabulary, and to some degree the tests, of morals. But as the law has grown, even where its standards have continued to model themselves on those of morality, they have necessarily become external. For they have considered, not the actual condition of the particular defendant, but whether his conduct would have been wrong in the fair average member of the community, whom he is expected to equal at his peril.

In general, this question will be settled by considering the degree of danger attending the act or conduct under the known circumstances. If there is danger that harm to another will follow, the act is generally wrong in the sense of the law.

But in some cases the defendant's conduct may not have been morally wrong, and yet he may have chosen to inflict the harm, as where he acted in fear of his life. In such cases he will be liable or not according to whether the law makes moral blameworthiness, within the limits explained above, the ground of liability, or thinks it enough that the defendant had reasonable warning of danger before acting. This distinction, however, is generally unimportant, and the known tendency of the act, under the known circumstances, to do harm may be accepted as the general test of conduct.

The tendency of a given act to cause harm under given circumstances must be determined by experience. And experience, either at first hand or through the voice of the jury, is continually working out concrete rules which in form are still more external, and still further removed from any reference to the moral condition of the defendant, than even the test of the prudent man, which marks the first stage in the division between law and morals. It does this in the domain of wrongs described as intentional as systematically as in those called unintentional or negligent.

But while the law is thus continually adding to its specific rules, it does not adopt the coarse and impolitic principle that a man always acts at his peril. On the contrary, its concrete rules, as well as the general questions put to the jury, show that the defendant must have had at least a fair chance of avoiding the infliction of harm before he becomes answerable for such a consequence of his conduct. And it is certainly arguable that even a fair chance to avoid bringing harm to pass is not enough to throw upon a person the peril of his conduct, unless, judged by average standards, he is also to blame for what he does.

Lecture V: The Bailee at Common Law

So far the discussion has been confined to the general principles of liability, and to the way we find the point at which a man begins to act at his own peril. But it does not matter to a man whether he acts at his own peril or not unless harm comes of it, and there must always be someone within reach of the consequences of the act before any harm can be done. Furthermore, and more to the point, there are certain forms of harm which are not likely to be suffered, and which can never be complained of, by anyone except a person who stands in a particular relation to the actor or to some other person or thing. Thus it is neither a harm nor a wrong to take fish from a pond unless the pond is possessed or owned by someone, and then it is a wrong only to the possessor or owner. It is neither a harm nor a wrong to fail to deliver a bale of wool at a certain time and place, unless a binding promise has been made to deliver it, and then it is a wrong only to the person who was promised.

The next thing to be done is to analyze those special relations out of which special rights and duties arise. The chief of them—and by "relations" I mean simply relations of fact—are possession and contract, and I shall take up those subjects one after the other.

The test of the theory of possession that prevails in any system of law is how it deals with people who have a thing within their power but who do not own it, or claim the position of an owner for themselves with regard to it—bailees, in a word. It is necessary, therefore, as a first step toward understanding the common-law theory of possession, to study what the common law says about bailees.

The state of things on the border between England and Scotland until fairly recent times, which is brought back to life in the ballad of the Fray of Suport, is very like the state of things that, in an earlier century, left its skeleton in the folk-laws of Germany and England. Cattle were the chief property people knew, and cattle-stealing was the chief way of wrongfully taking property. There was very little law, and what there was depended almost wholly on the injured party himself to enforce it. The Salic Law of the fifth century and the Anglo-Saxon laws of Alfred give very full directions about following the trail. If the pursuer came up with the cattle before three days had passed, he had the right to take them and keep them, subject only to swearing that he had lost them against his will. If more than three days went by before the cattle were found, the defendant might swear, if he could, to facts that would disprove the claimant's loss.

This procedure was truly a legal procedure; but it depended on the party making the claim both to start it and to carry it out. Being "executive" in nature—a matter of doing rather than of pleading—it could hardly have been started by anyone other than the person on the spot, in whose keeping the cattle had been. The oath was to the effect that the party had lost possession against his will. But if all a man had to swear was that he had lost possession against his will, it is a natural conclusion that the right to take the oath and use the procedure depended on possession, not on ownership. Possession was not merely enough; it was essential. Only the man who had possession could say that he had lost the property against his will, just as only the man who was on the spot could follow the cattle.

Footnote: Laband and Heusler correct the earlier opinion of Bruns on this point, which Sohm adopted. Compare the discussion of the word "his" (sua) in writs of trespass in the English law, at the end of Lecture VI. Our knowledge of the primitive form of action is somewhat meager and depends on inference. The edict of Liutprand, dealing with housebreaking followed by theft of property the householder had been left in charge of, lays down that the owner shall look to the bailee alone, and the bailee shall hold the thief responsible both for the housebreaking and for the stolen goods—because, as it says, we cannot raise two claims out of one cause. This is somewhat as our own law was unable to split the severing of a thing from the land, and the conversion of it, into two different wrongs.

This, so far as we know, was the one means the early law of our race provided for getting back property lost against one's will. So, in a word, this procedure, modeled on the self-help that came naturally in the situation that gave rise to it, was the only remedy. It was confined to the man in possession, and it was not open to the owner unless he was that man.

To this primitive condition of society has been traced a rule that survived into later times and a more civilized procedure: if the owner entrusted chattels to another person, it was the bailee, and not the bailor, who was the proper party to sue when a third person wrongfully took them. It followed that if the bailee—the person so entrusted—sold or gave the goods in his charge to someone else, the owner could look only to the bailee and could not sue the stranger. This was not from any principle in favor of trade, meant to protect those who bought in good faith from people in possession, but because there was no form of action open to him. But since all the remedies were in the bailee's hands, it also followed that he was bound to hold his bailor harmless. If the goods were lost, it was no excuse that they had been stolen without his fault. He alone could recover the lost property, and therefore he was bound to do so.

In the course of time this reason ceased to exist. An owner out of possession could sue the wrongful taker of his property, just as a possessor could. But the strict liability of the bailee remained, as such rules do remain in the law, long after the causes that gave rise to it had disappeared, and at length we find cause and effect turned upside down. We read in Beaumanoir, the French jurist, writing in 1283, that if a hired thing is stolen, the right to sue belongs to the bailee, because he is answerable to the person from whom he hired it. At first the bailee was answerable to the owner because he was the only person who could sue. Now it was said that he could sue because he was answerable to the owner.

All the peculiarities just described reappear in the Anglo-Norman law, and from that day to this every kind of bailee has been treated as having possession in the legal sense, as I shall presently show.

It is worth proving that our law of bailment is of native origin, so that, when we come to theory, modern German opinion may not be valued at more than it is worth. The only existing theories on the subject come from Germany. The German philosophers who have written on law have known no system but the Roman, and the German lawyers who have philosophized have been professors of Roman law. Some rules that we think clear go against what the German civilians—scholars of the Roman, or civil, law—would regard as first principles. To test the value of those principles, or at least to prevent the hasty assumption that they are universal (and English writers show a slight tendency to make it), it is well to realize that we are dealing with a new system, which philosophy has not yet taken into account.

In the first place, we find an action to recover stolen property which, like the Salic procedure, was based on possession, not on title. Bracton says that a man may sue for his chattel as stolen, on the testimony of good men, and that it does not matter whether the thing so taken was his own property or another's, provided it was in his custody.

The point of special importance, it will be remembered, was the oath. From the wording of Bracton, the oath of the good men (probi homines) seems to have been that the thing was lost (adirata), and a report of the year 1294 tells us expressly that this was so. It says, in effect, that the loser may state his claim this way: "Note that where a man's chattel is lost (ou la chosse de un home est endire), he may count that he [the finder] tortiously detains it, etc., and tortiously for this that whereas he lost the said thing on such a day, etc., he [the loser] came on such a day, etc. (la vynt yl e en jour), and found it in the house of such an one, and told him, etc., and prayed him to restore the thing, but that he would not restore it, etc., to his damage, etc.; and if he, etc. In this case, the demandant must prove (his own hand the twelfth) that he lost the thing." To "count" was to state one's claim in court, and to prove a thing "his own hand the twelfth" was to swear to it backed by eleven others who swore with him.

Footnote: This is not trover. The claim to the return of a thing on the ground that the plaintiff had lost it and the defendant had found it was called, in the fifteenth century, "a newfound holiday."

Assuming, then, that as a first step we find a procedure kindred to that of the early German folk-laws, the more important question is whether we find any principles like those just explained. One of them, it will be remembered, concerned a wrongful transfer by the bailee. We find it laid down in the Year Books that if I deliver goods to a bailee to keep for me, and he sells or gives them to a stranger, the property passes to the stranger by the gift, and I cannot bring trespass against him; but I have a good remedy against the bailee by the writ of detinue—the action to get back a specific thing wrongfully kept from its owner—for his failure to return the goods. These cases have been understood, and on the whole it seems rightly, to deny the bailor not merely trespass but any action whatever. Modern writers have added, however, the characteristically modern qualification that the purchase must be bona fide and without notice—that is, made in good faith by a buyer who did not know of the owner's claim. The answer is that the rule extends to gifts as well as to sales by the bailee, that there is no such condition in the old books, and that it goes against the spirit of the strict doctrines of the common law to read one in. No lawyer needs to be told that, even so qualified, this is no longer the law. The doctrine of the Year Books must be regarded as a survival from the primitive times when we have seen the same rule in force—unless we are prepared to believe that in the fifteenth century they had a finer feeling for the rights of good-faith purchasers than we have now.

The next point in logical order would be how far the bailee was held responsible to the bailor who entrusted him. But for convenience I will first take up the explanation given for the bailee's right of action against third persons who wrongfully took the goods from his possession. The reader will remember Beaumanoir's inverted explanation: that the bailee could sue because he was answerable over to the owner, instead of the original rule, that he was answerable over so strictly because only he could sue. We find the same reasoning repeated often in the Year Books, and indeed from that day to this it has been one of the commonplaces of the law. Thus Hankford, then a judge of the Common Bench, says, around 1410: "If a stranger takes beasts in my custody, I shall have a writ of trespass against him, and shall recover the value of the beasts, because I am chargeable for the beasts to my bailor, who has the property." There are cases in which this reasoning was pushed to the conclusion that if, by the terms of the trust, the bailee was not answerable for the goods if they were stolen, he would have no action against the thief. The same explanation is repeated to this day. Thus we read in a well-known textbook, "For the bailee being responsible to the bailor, if the goods be lost or damaged by negligence, or if he do not deliver them up on lawful demand, it is therefore reasonable that he should have a right of action," etc. But in general, nowadays, a borrower or hirer of property is not answerable if it is taken from him against his will. If the reason offered were the true one, it would follow that, since he was not answerable over, he could not sue the wrongdoer. The wrongdoer would only have to commit a wrong so gross that it freed the bailee from responsibility, and the bailee would lose his right of action. The truth is that any person in possession, whether entrusted and answerable over or not—a finder of property as well as a bailee—can sue anyone except the true owner for interfering with his possession, as will be shown more fully at the end of the next Lecture.

Footnote: Because the bailee recovered the whole value of the goods, the old reason, that he was answerable over, has in some cases turned into a new rule, seemingly based on a misunderstanding: that the bailee holds in trust for the bailor whatever he recovers beyond his own damage. From there the new rule has been extended to insurance money recovered by a bailee. In this form it ceases to be a reason for allowing the action at all.

The bailor also got a right of action against the wrongdoer at a fairly early date. In the forty-eighth year of Edward III, in an action of trespass brought by an agister of cattle—a man paid to pasture other people's cattle—counsel laid it down that "in this case, he who has the property may have a writ of trespass, and he who has the custody another writ of trespass. Persay: Sir, it is true. But he who recovers first shall oust the other of the action, and it shall be in many cases, as if tenant by elegit is ousted, each shall have the assize, and, if the one recover first, the writ of the other is abated, and so here." The comparison is to land held by a creditor under a writ of elegit until his debt is paid: if he is put out, both he and the owner may bring the assize, and whichever wins first cuts off the other's writ.

Other books suggest that this was said of bailments generally, and was not limited to those the bailor could end whenever he pleased. Thus in the twenty-second year of Edward IV, counsel say, "If I bail to you my goods, and another takes them out of your possession, I shall have good action of trespass quare vi et armis." And this seems to have been Rolle's understanding in the passage modern courts usually rely on.

It was to be expected that the bailor would be given some action as soon as the law had machinery that could be worked without the fresh pursuit and the armed hands of the possessor and his friends. Before the action on the case was heard of, allowing the bailor to sue and giving him trespass came to pretty nearly the same thing. Many early writs will be found which show that trespass did not always have the sharp outline it developed later. The point the Year Books seem to insist on is, as Brooke sums it up in the margin of his Abridgment, that two people shall have an action for a single act—not that both shall have trespass rather than case. It should be added that the Year Books quoted go no further than a wrongful taking out of the bailee's custody, which is the old case of the folk-laws. Even so, the right to bring trespass is now denied to the bailor where the bailee has the exclusive right to the goods by a lease or a lien; although the doctrine has been repeated for bailments the bailor can end at pleasure. But the modified rule does not concern the present discussion any more than the earlier form did, because it still leaves the possessory remedies—the actions that protect possession as such—open to all bailees without exception. This appears from the relation of the modified rule to the ancient law; from the fact that Baron Parke, in Manders v. Williams, hints that he would have been prepared to apply the old rule to its full extent but for Gordon v. Harper; and, still more plainly, from the fact that the bailee's right to trespass and trover is asserted in the same breath as the bailor's, and is proved as well by express decisions, which will be cited.

Footnote: In one instance, where the bailor was allowed, against Brian's opinion, to sue a stranger for damage to the chattel, the action seems to have been case.

It is true that in Lotan v. Cross Lord Ellenborough ruled at nisi prius that a lender could bring trespass for damage done to a chattel in the hands of a borrower, and that the case is often cited as authority without comment. Indeed, reputable textbooks sometimes lay it down generally that a gratuitous bailment—one made without payment—does not change the possession but leaves it in the bailor; that a gratuitous bailee is a sort of servant of the bailor, so that the possession of one is the possession of the other; and that this is why, although the bailee may sue on his possession, the bailor has the same actions. Part of this confusion has already been explained, and the rest will be when I come to speak of servants, between whom and all bailees there is a broad and well-known distinction. But whatever ground Lotan v. Cross may stand on, if it stands on any, it cannot for a moment be admitted that borrowers in general do not have trespass and trover. A gratuitous deposit, made for the sole benefit of the person depositing the goods, is a much stronger case for denying these remedies to the depositary, the person who keeps them. Yet we have a decision of the full court, in which Lord Ellenborough also took part, that a depositary has case; and the reasoning implies that a borrower, all the more (a fortiori), would have trespass. And this has always been the law. We have seen that a similar doctrine followed necessarily from the nature of the early German procedure; and the cases show that in this, as in other respects, the English followed the traditions of their race.

The meaning of the rule that all bailees have the possessory remedies is this: in the theory of the common law every bailee has a true possession, and a bailee recovers on the strength of his possession, just as a finder does, and just as even a wrongful possessor may get full damages, or the return of the specific thing, from a stranger to the title. On the other hand, so far as the possessory actions are still allowed to bailors, it is not on the ground that they too have possession. It is probably by a survival, which has been explained, and which in its modern form is an anomaly. The reason usually given is that a right to immediate possession is enough—a reason which itself shuts out the notion that the bailor actually has possession.

The point essential to understanding the common-law theory of possession is now established: from time immemorial the English law has regarded all bailees as possessors, entitled to the possessory remedies. It is not strictly necessary to go on and complete the proof that our law of bailment is of pure German descent. But, curiosity aside, the doctrine that remains to be discussed has had so important an influence on the law of the present day that I shall follow it out with some care. That doctrine was the absolute responsibility of the bailee to the bailor if the goods were wrongfully taken from him.

The early textbook writers are not as instructive as one might hope, because of the influence of the Roman law. Glanvill, however, says in so many words that if a borrowed thing is destroyed or lost in any way while in the borrower's custody, he is absolutely bound to pay a reasonable price for it. So does Bracton, who partly repeats, but modifies, what Justinian says about commodatum, depositum, and pignus—the Roman loan for use, deposit for safekeeping, and pledge—and about the duty of the hirer to use the care of a diligentissimus paterfamilias, the most careful head of a household.

Footnote: Bracton's passage says that one who has received a thing on loan is bound to return it, or its price, even if it has been destroyed or lost by fire, the fall of a building, shipwreck, or the attack of robbers or enemies. This has been thought a corrupt text, but it agrees with Glanvill and with Fleta.

The language and decisions of the courts are perfectly clear, and there we find the German tradition kept alive for several centuries. I begin with the time of Edward II, about 1315. In an action of detinue, the defendant's plea was that the plaintiff had delivered to him a chest locked with the plaintiff's own key, that the goods were in the chest, and that they had been taken from the defendant by robbery together with his own goods. The replication—the plaintiff's answer to the plea—was that the goods had been delivered to the defendant outside any enclosure, and Fitzherbert says the defendant was driven to take issue on that point. This implies that if the goods were not in the chest but simply in the defendant's custody, he was liable. Lord Holt, in Coggs v. Bernard, denies that the chest would make any difference; but the old books agree that there is no delivery if the goods are under lock and key, and this is the origin of the distinction about carriers "breaking bulk"—opening the package entrusted to them—in modern criminal law. In the reign of Edward III the case of a pledge came up; a pledge seems always to have been regarded as a special bailment, to be kept as the holder kept his own goods. The defense was that the goods had been stolen along with the defendant's own. The plaintiff was driven to reply that he had made a tender of the debt before the theft, which would have ended the pledge and left the defendant an ordinary bailee. Issue was joined on that point, which confirms the other cases, since it implies that in that event the defendant would be liable.

Next I take a case from the time of Henry VI, in 1455. It was an action of debt against the Marshal of the Marshalsea, the jailer of the King's Bench prison, for the escape of a prisoner. Jailers in charge of prisoners were governed by the same law as bailees in charge of cattle. The prisoner's body was delivered to the jailer to keep under the same liabilities that cows or goods would have carried. He pleaded in defense that enemies of the king broke into the prison and carried off the prisoner against the defendant's will. The question was whether this was a good defense. The court said that if alien enemies of the king—the French, for instance—released the prisoner, or perhaps if the burning of the prison gave him a chance to escape, the excuse would be good, "because then [the defendant] has remedy against no one." But if the king's own subjects broke the prison, the defendant would be liable, for they are not enemies but traitors; and then, it is implied, the defendant would have a right of action against them, and therefore would himself be answerable. In this case the court came very near the original ground of liability, and drew its distinction accordingly. The person entrusted was liable in those cases where he had a remedy over against the wrongdoer (and in which, originally, he was the only person who had such a remedy); and, on the other hand, his liability, being founded on that circumstance, ended where the remedy ended. The jailer could not sue the soldiers of an invading army of Frenchmen; but in theory he could sue any British subject who carried off the prisoner, however unlikely it was that he would get much satisfaction that way.

Footnote: This case is cited and heavily relied on in all the leading cases on bailment, among them Woodlife's Case, Southcote v. Bennett, Pickering v. Barkley, and Morse v. Slue.

A few years later the famous Littleton states the law in the same way. He says that if goods are delivered to a man, he shall have an action of trespass if they are carried off, because he is chargeable over. That is, he is bound to make the loss good to the person who trusted him with them.

Footnote: It is proper to add that in the second of these cases Littleton does not seem to distinguish between servants and bailees.

In the ninth year of Edward IV, Danby says that if a bailee received goods to keep as his own goods, then robbery shall excuse him; otherwise not. Again, in a later case, robbery is said to be no excuse at all. There may have been some hesitation about robbery when the robber was unknown, so that the bailee had no remedy over. There may even have been hesitation about robbery generally, on the ground that, because the robbery was a felony, the bailee could not go against either the robber's body or his estate: the one was hanged and the other forfeited. But there is not a shadow of doubt that the bailee was not excused by an ordinary wrongful taking. "If the goods are taken by a trespasser, of whom the bailee has conusance"—that is, whom he knows—"he shall be chargeable to his bailor, and shall have his action over against his trespasser." The same point was touched in other passages of the Year Books, and the rule of law is clearly implied by the reason given for the bailee's right to sue in the cases cited above.

The principle was decided directly, in accordance with the ancient law, in the famous case of Southcote v. Bennet. This was an action of detinue for goods delivered to the defendant to keep safely. The defendant admitted the delivery, and answered that he had been robbed of the goods by one J.S. "And, after argument at the bar, Gawdy and Clench, ceteris absentibus"—the other judges being absent—"held that the plaintiff ought to recover, because it was not a special bailment; that the defendant accepted them to keep as his proper goods, and not otherwise; but it is a delivery, which chargeth him to keep them at his peril. And it is not any plea in a detinue to say that he was robbed by one such; for he hath his remedy over by trespass, or appeal, to have them again." This passage from Croke's report implies what Lord Coke says outright in his own report of the case, that "to be kept, and to be kept safe, is all one." And both reports agree that the obligation was founded on the delivery alone. Croke's report also confirms the caution that Lord Coke adds at the end of his: "Note, reader, it is good policy for him who takes any goods to keep, to take them in special manner, scil. to keep them as he keeps his own goods,... or if they happen to be stolen or purloined, that he shall not be answerable for them; for he who accepted them ought to take them in such or the like manner, or otherwise he may be charged by his general acceptance." In other words, the careful bailee should say at the start that he takes the goods only on those terms.

Down to this time, at least, the law was clear. If a person accepted possession of goods to keep for another, even as a favor, and lost them by a wrongful taking wholly without his own fault, he was bound to make good the loss—unless, when he took possession, he had expressly stipulated against such a responsibility. Lord Holt in Coggs v. Bernard, and Sir William Jones in his book on Bailments, tried to show that Southcote v. Bennet was not supported by authority. The attempts were futile, as any one who will study the Year Books for himself may see. Chief Baron Peryam had laid down the same principle seven years before, in Drake v. Royman, and Southcote's Case was followed without question as a leading precedent for a hundred years.

Thus the circle of analogies between the English and the early German law is complete. In both there is the same procedure for lost property, turning on the single question whether the plaintiff had lost possession against his will. There is the same principle that, if the person trusted with the property parted with it to another, the owner could not recover it from that other, but had to get his indemnity from his own bailee. There is the same inverted explanation—that the bailee could sue because he was answerable over—and yet the substance of the true doctrine survives in the rule that when he had no remedy he was not answerable. And, finally, there is the same absolute responsibility for loss, even when the loss happened without any fault in the person trusted. The last and most important of these principles is seen in force as late as the reign of Queen Elizabeth. We now have to follow its later fortunes.

A common carrier—one who makes a public business of carrying goods for anybody who will pay—is liable for goods that are stolen from him, or otherwise lost from his charge, except by the act of God or the public enemy: that is, by natural forces no man could resist, or by a nation at war with his own. Two notions have been held about the source of this rule. One is that it was borrowed from the Roman law. The other is that it was introduced by custom, as an exception to the general law of bailment, in the reigns of Elizabeth and James I.

I shall try to show that both notions are wrong. This strict responsibility is a fragmentary survival from the general law of bailment which I have just explained. The changes the old law has undergone were due to three things: in part to a confusion of ideas which came in when the action on the case displaced detinue; in part to ideas of public policy which Lord Holt read into the precedents; and in part to still later ideas of policy which later judges have read into the reasoning of Lord Holt.

Southcote's Case was decided in the forty-third year of Queen Elizabeth, A.D. 1601. I think the first mention of a carrier that bears on the question occurs in Woodlife's Case, decided four or five years earlier, in 1596 or 1597. It was an action of account—the old action against one who had handled another's property and owed him a reckoning—for merchandise delivered to the defendant, apparently as a factor, or agent to buy and sell ("pur merchandizer"), and clearly not as a carrier. The defendant's plea was that the goods had been taken by robbers at sea along with his own. Gawdy, one of the judges who later decided Southcote's Case, thought the plea bad. But Chief Justice Popham said that, although it would not be a good plea for a carrier, because a carrier is paid for his carriage, there was a difference on this point between carriers and other servants and factors.

This is repeated in Southcote's Case, and it seems to involve a double distinction: first, between paid and unpaid bailees; and next, between bailees and servants. If the defendant was a servant without control over the goods, he might not fall within the law of bailment at all, and in the early law factors were treated on the footing of servants.

The other distinction, between paid and unpaid, marked the entrance of the doctrine of consideration into the law of bailment. Consideration originally meant quid pro quo—something given in exchange—as will be explained in Lecture VII. That is how it was treated in Doctor and Student, the sixteenth-century dialogue between a doctor of divinity and a student of the common law, written when the principle was still young. Chief Justice Popham probably borrowed his distinction between paid and unpaid bailees from that work, where common carriers are given as an example of the paid class. A little earlier, reward had made no difference.

But in Woodlife's Case, in reply to what the Chief Justice had said, Gawdy cited the case of the Marshal of the King's Bench, stated above. At that Popham fell back on the old distinction: the jailer had a remedy over against the rebels who broke his prison, but in the case before the court there was no remedy over.

The other cases relied on were some of those on bailment in general collected above—the same authorities, in short, on which Southcote's Case was founded. The principle adopted was the same as in Southcote's Case, and the only question was whether the defendant fell within it. Nothing was said of any custom of the realm, nor had anything ever been said of one in any reported case before this time. I believe this is the first instance in which carriers are distinguished in any way from any other class of persons trusted with goods. There is no hint in the old books of any special obligation peculiar to them; and it is certainly not true that this case introduced one. It will be noticed, with reference to what follows, that Popham does not speak of common carriers, but simply of carriers.

Next came Southcote's Case itself, in 1601, which presented the old law pure and simple, without regard to reward or any modern innovation. In this case, and in the earlier instances of loss by theft, the action was detinue, and the plaintiff counted—that is, stated his claim—simply, we may presume, on a delivery and a wrongful detaining of the goods.

But about this time important changes took place in the procedure usually adopted, and they must be explained. If the chattel could be returned in specie—the thing itself—detinue gave no satisfaction for any damage it might have suffered through the bailee's neglect. The natural remedy for such damage was the action on the case. But before case could be made entirely satisfactory, certain difficulties had to be overcome. The neglect that caused the damage might be a mere omission. And what was there in a nonfeasance—a failure to act—that resembled trespass, to support the analogy on which trespass on the case was founded? Moreover, to charge a man for not acting, you must show that it was his duty to act. As pleadings were construed in those days, it would not have been enough to allege that the plaintiff's goods were damaged by the defendant's negligence. These troubles had been got over by the well-known words super se assumpsit, which will be explained later. For a long time assumpsit did not become an independent action of contract. The allegation was simply the inducement—the introductory statement of circumstances—to an action of tort. The ground of liability was that the defendant had begun upon the undertaking, so that his negligent omission, which let in the damage, could be connected with his acts as part of his dealing with the thing. We shall find Lord Holt recognizing this original meaning of assumpsit when we come to Coggs v. Bernard. Of course it was not confined to cases of bailment.

But there was another way, besides this, by which the defendant could be charged with a duty and made liable in case. Although it is less familiar to lawyers, it has a special bearing on the law of carriers in later times. If damage had been done or brought about by the act or omission of the defendant in the pursuit of one of the more common callings—that of a farrier, for instance, who shoes horses—it seems that the action could be maintained without laying an assumpsit, on the allegation that he was a "common" farrier. This principle, too, was wholly independent of bailment. It expressed the general obligation of those who carried on a public or "common" business to practise their art on demand, and to show skill in it. "For," as Fitzherbert says, "it is the duty of every artificer to exercise his art rightly and truly as he ought."

Footnote: In the same way an innkeeper must be a common innkeeper. In Blackstone the reader will find that "the transition from status to contract" has already taken place.

Once it had been established in this way that case would lie for damage occasioned by the defendant's omission as well as for damage caused by his act, there was no reason to deny it even when the negligent custody had resulted in the destruction of the property. From there it was only a step to extend the same form of action to every case of loss by a bailee, and so to take away the defendant's right to wage his law—the ancient privilege of clearing himself by his own oath, backed by the oaths of neighbors who swore to his credit. Detinue, the primitive remedy, kept that mark of primitive procedure. The last extension was made at about the time of Southcote's Case. But when one form of action came to be used alike for damage or destruction by the bailee's neglect and for loss by a wrong-doer against whom the bailee had a remedy over, a source of confusion was opened about the foundation and nature of the defendant's duty.

Footnote: The process may be traced by reading the cases in order, from the reign of Henry VII, through the early years of Henry VIII and the later years of Elizabeth, down to the eleventh year of James I.

In truth there were two sets of duties. One was not peculiar to bailees, and arose from the defendant's assumpsit or his public calling, as just explained. The other was the ancient obligation peculiar to bailees as such, of which Southcote's Case was an example. But by Lord Coke's time assumpsit had been taken over by contract and the doctrine of consideration had been developed. After that, any obligation of a bailee might be thought of as part of a contract of bailment, and it seemed unnecessary to distinguish nicely between the two sets of duties, provided a consideration and a special promise could be alleged. Furthermore, since the defendant's public calling had formerly had the same effect as an assumpsit for the purpose of charging him in tort, it now seems to have been thought an equally good substitute for a special promise in order to charge him in assumpsit. In Rogers v. Head the argument was that to charge a man in assumpsit you must show either his public calling at the time of the delivery, or a special promise on sufficient consideration. This argument assumes that a bailee who received goods in the course of a public employment—as a common carrier, for instance—could be charged in this form of action for a breach of either set of duties, by alleging either his public calling or his reward and a special promise. It seems to have been admitted, as was repeatedly decided both before and after that case, that one who was not a common carrier could have been charged for failing to deliver in a special action—that is, in case, as distinguished from assumpsit.

Footnote: There is an obscurely reported case of the late years of Elizabeth, Mosley v. Fosset, which seems to have been assumpsit against an agistor—a man paid to pasture another's cattle—for a horse stolen while in his charge. It asserts in passing that "without such special assumpsit the action does not lie." This must refer to the form of the action, since the judges who decided Southcote's Case took part in the decision.

Suppose, next, that the plaintiff sued in case, for a tort. As before, the breach of duty complained of might be damage to property of the kind that had always been sued for in that form of action. Or it might be a loss by theft, for which detinue would formerly have been brought, and which fell on the bailee only because of the bailment. If the goods had been stolen, the bailee's liability rested neither on his common calling nor on his assumpsit and his neglect. It arose from the naked facts that he had accepted a delivery and that the goods were gone, and in such cases it ought to have been enough to allege those facts in the declaration. But it was very natural that the time-honored foundations of the action on the case, in its narrower use, should still be laid in the pleadings even after the scope of the action had been enlarged. We shall have to ask later whether the principles of Southcote's Case were not also stretched in the opposite direction, to cases that did not fall within them. The reasons for the rule it laid down had lost their meaning centuries before Gawdy and Clench were born, when owners acquired the right to sue for the wrongful taking of property in the hands of a bailee. The rule itself was a dry precedent, likely to be followed according to the letter because the spirit had departed. It had begun to totter when the reporter warned bailees to accept goods on terms that would get rid of it.

Footnote: The second count in Morse v. Slue, below, shows that the averment of negligence was mere form.

Accordingly, although that decision was the main authority relied on, whenever a peculiar responsibility was placed on bailees, for the hundred years between it and Coggs v. Bernard, we find that the pleaders did various things. Sometimes an assumpsit was laid, as in the early precedents. More often the bailee was alleged to be a common bargeman, or common carrier, or the like, without much reference to the special nature of the tort in question. And the true bearing of that allegation was sometimes lost sight of. At first, however, there were only slight signs of confusion in the language of one or two cases; and if the duty was understood to fall within the principle of Southcote's Case, pleaders did not always allege the common or public calling, which was held to be unnecessary. But they also borrowed other devices from the precedents in case, or used them to strengthen an obligation they did not well understand. Chief Justice Popham had approved a distinction between paid and unpaid bailees, so it was thought prudent to lay a reward. Negligence was of course alleged. And finally it became common to allege an obligation "by the law and custom of the realm." This last deserves a little more attention.

There is no writ in the Register—the official collection of the forms of writ—that alleges any special obligation of common carriers by the custom of the realm. But the writ against innkeepers did lay a duty "by the law and custom of England," and it was easy to adopt the phrase. The allegation did not so much imply the existence of a special principle as state a proposition of law in the form then usual. Other writs of trespass allege a common-law duty in the same way, and still others set out a duty imposed by statute. So, too, "the judges were sworn to execute justice according to law and the custom of England."

The duties of a common carrier, so far as the earlier evidence goes, were simply those of bailees in general, together with the liabilities generally attached to the exercise of a public calling. The word "common" spoke only to the second point, as has been shown above. This is further illustrated by the fact that, when the duty was set out in this way, it was not alleged as an obligation peculiar to common carriers as such. It was laid as the custom or law of common hoymen, or lightermen—the men who worked the small coasting boats and the barges of a harbor—or whatever the business of the party happened to be. It will be noticed that Chief Justice Holt, in Coggs v. Bernard, states the liability as applying to all bailees for reward who exercise a public employment, and mentions common hoymen and masters of ships alongside common carriers, not as included under them. It will also be noticed that in the cases before that time there is no settled formula for the obligation in question. In each case it is set out that the defendant was answerable for what he was said to have done or failed to do in that particular instance.

Footnote: Note especially the variations of statement in Morse v. Slue, set out below in the text.

Returning now to the cases in their order, Rich v. Kneeland comes next, in the eleventh year of James I, A.D. 1613. It was an action on the case, that is, in tort, against a common hoyman. In Croke's report nothing is said of custom. But the declaration alleges that the defendant was a common bargeman; that the plaintiff delivered him a portmanteau and other things to carry, and paid him for it; and that the defendant tam negligenter custodivit—kept it so negligently—that it was taken from him by persons unknown. This is like the second count in Morse v. Slue, below. The defendant's plea was demurred to, and judgment was given for the plaintiff. A writ of error was then brought, to have the judgment reviewed, and the error assigned was that "this action lies not against a common bargeman without special promise. But all the Justices and Barons held, that it well lies as against a common carrier upon the land." If we follow this report, it seems at first glance that importance was attached to the common calling. But the loss was clearly within the principle of Southcote's Case, which required neither a special promise nor a common calling for its application, and which remained unquestioned law for three quarters of a century afterwards. So the court must have been referring to the form of action used (case), and not to whether the defendant was liable in some form of action (detinue). The objection was that "this action lies not" without special promise—not that the defendant was not liable. Even narrowed in this way, the decision rather encourages the notion that allegations which were needed to charge a man for damage happening through his neglect, in the older and narrower use of this action, were also needed in its new extension to a different class of wrongs. Since it was by now fairly clear that case would lie for a nonfeasance, the notion was mistaken, and we shall see that later decisions denied it.

Footnote: The use which has been made of this case in later times shows how extremely difficult it is, in the older books, to distinguish between principles of substantive law and rules relating only to procedure.

According to Hobart's report of the same case, it was alleged that the defendant was a common hoyman, carrying goods by water for hire, and that by the custom of England such carriers ought to keep the goods so that they should not be lost by the default of themselves or their servants. "And it was resolved that, though it was laid as a custom of the realm, yet indeed it is common law." This last resolution may mean only that the custom of the realm and the common law are the same thing, as had been said about innkeepers long before. But the law as to innkeepers, which the writ called the custom of the realm, had something of the air of a special principle reaching beyond the law of bailment, since an innkeeper's liability extended to goods within the inn of which he did not have the custody.
