The court may have meant to set such a special principle against the common law, the general law of bailment, which governed the case before it.

Whatever doubts some of Croke's language might raise, standing alone, one fact remains beyond dispute. For nearly a century from Woodlife's Case, the liability of carriers for loss of goods—whether the custom of the realm or the defendant's common calling was alleged or not—rested on the authority of Southcote's Case, and was meant to be decided on its principle.

Symons v. Darknoll, in 1628, is precisely in point. The declaration was that by the common law every lighterman ought so to manage his lighter that the goods carried in it should not perish. "And although no promise laid, it seemed to the court that the plaintiff should recover; and not alleging that defendant was common lighterman was no harm. Hyde, C. J., delivery makes the contract." This did not mean that delivery was a good consideration for a promise. It meant, as Southcote's Case had laid down, that delivery, without a special acceptance to keep the goods only as one's own, bound the bailee to keep them safely, and therefore made it unnecessary to allege either an assumpsit or the defendant's common calling. Justice Whitlock pointed out that the action was in tort, not contract. "Et en cest case... Southcote's Case fuit cite"—and in this case Southcote's Case was cited.

The same year, Sergeant Maynard, arguing in Williams v. Hide, stated the same rule for bailments in general, again citing Southcote's Case.

In Kenrig v. Eggleston, in 1648, which was "case against a country carrier for not delivering a box," and so on, of which he had been robbed, nothing was said about custom, nor about the defendant's being a common carrier, unless the words just quoted imply that he was. But it was laid down, as in Southcote's Case, that "it must come on the carrier's part to make special acceptance" if he wanted to lessen his liability as a bailee.

Nichols v. Moore, in 1661, was case against a "water carrier" between Hull and London, alleging a delivery to him at York. It was moved in arrest of judgment—a motion that judgment not be entered because the record is defective—that the defendant had not undertaken to carry the goods from York to Hull. "But notwithstanding this per totam curiam"—by the whole court—"the defendant shall be charged on his general receipt at York, according to Southcote's Case."

It is fair to mention that in Matthews v. Hopkins, in the seventeenth year of Charles II, the declaration was on the custom of the realm against a common carrier, and there was a motion in arrest of judgment on three grounds: that the custom of the realm was misrecited; that the defendant was not alleged to have been a carrier at the time he received the goods; and that counts in trover and in case on the custom had been joined. Judgment was arrested, apparently on the last ground. But the court went on: "And, although the declaration may be good without recital of the custom of the realm, as Hobart says, still it is the better way to recite it."

We now come to the great case of Morse v. Slue, decided in 1671 and 1672. It was an action against the master of a ship lying in the river Thames, for the loss of goods entrusted to him. The goods had been carried off by robbers, and it was found that the ship had the usual guard aboard at the time. There seem to have been two counts. The first was on the law and custom of England, under which masters of ships were bound "carefully to govern, preserve, and defend goods shipped, so long as said ship should remain in the river Thames"; or, in another report, to keep safely goods shipped to be carried from London beyond sea, "without loss or subtraction," so that for want of such keeping they should not come to any damage; or, in a third, "to keep safely goods delivered to them to carry, dangers of the sea excepted." (The exception last stated was perhaps drawn by the reporter from the usual form of bills of lading, which were referred to in argument.) The second count, which is usually overlooked, was a special count "in case on delivery and being stolen by his neglect."

The case was argued twice, and all the reports agree, as far as they go, in their account of the points pressed.

Holt, for the plaintiff, maintained, first, that the master receives goods generally, without special terms, citing Southcote's Case; and that "only guardian in socage who hath the custody by law, and factor who is servant at the master's dispose, and so cannot take care, are exempt." (A guardian in socage held the land of an heir under age by force of law; a factor was an agent who bought and sold goods for a merchant.) Second, that the master is paid for his keeping, and is therefore a proper person to be sued. Third, that the master has a remedy over against the wrongdoers, citing the case of the Marshal of the King's Bench. Further, that the mischief would be great if the master were not liable, since merchants put their trust in him and could never show any particular default, as the bill of lading made plain; and, finally, that neglect in fact appeared.

On the other side, it was urged that no neglect had been found, and that the master was only a servant, so that if anyone was liable, it was the owners of the ship. It was also suggested that, since there would have been no liability had the goods been taken at sea—for then the case would have fallen under the admiralty law—it was absurd that one rule should govern the beginning of the voyage and another the rest of it.

Footnote: This last is the main point mentioned by two of the reporters.

On the second argument, it was again maintained for the plaintiff that the defendant was liable "at the common law on the general bailment," citing Southcote's Case, and also that he was liable under the Roman and maritime law as a public carrier and master of a ship.

The opinion of the court was delivered by Chief Justice Hale. It was held that, since the ship lay within the body of a county, the admiralty law did not apply; or, as one report puts it, "the master could not avail himself of the rules of the civil law, by which masters are not chargeable pro damno fatali"—that is, for loss by unavoidable accident. The master was liable to an action because he took a reward, and "he might have made a caution for himself, which he omitting and taking in the goods generally, he shall answer for what happens." The case of Kenrig v. Eggleston seems also to have been referred to. It was further said that the master was an officer rather than a servant, and in effect received his wages from the merchant who paid the freight. Finally, on the question of negligence, the court said it was not enough to have the usual number of men guarding the ship; it was neglect not to have enough to guard the goods, unless against the common enemies of the realm. For this it cited the case of the Marshal, which, it will be remembered, was merely the principle of Southcote's Case and the common law of bailment in another form.

It will be observed that this case did not turn on any special custom, either as to common carriers or as to shipmasters. All the arguments, and the opinion of the court, assumed that if the case was governed by the common law, and not by the milder rules of the civil law relied on for the defence; and if the defendant could be regarded as a bailee, and not merely a servant of the owners; then the general law of bailment would apply, and the defendant would be charged, as in Southcote's Case, "by his general acceptance."

It can hardly be supposed, however, that so enlightened a judge as Sir Matthew Hale would not have broken away from the Year Books if a case had come before him in which property had been received as a pure favor to the plaintiff, without consideration or reward, and had then been taken from the defendant by robbery. Such a case was tried before Chief Justice Pemberton in 1681, and he very sensibly ruled that no action lay, declining to follow the law of Lord Coke's time to such extreme results.

About the same time, the defendant's common calling began to take on a new importance. The other alternative allegation, the assumpsit, was in the end the more important of the two: it led to the doctrine, not objectionable in itself, that all duties arising from a bailment are founded on contract. But the assumpsit had by now given rise to a special action of its own, and so was not much used where the action was in tort, while the other averment, the common calling, occurs more and more often. The notion was plainly gaining ground that the liability of common carriers for loss of goods, whatever the cause of the loss, came from a special principle peculiar to them and not applicable to bailees in general. The confusion of independent duties which has been explained, and of which the first trace was seen in Rich v. Kneeland, was soon to become complete.

Footnote: The reader will find in Boorman v. Brown the primitive assumpsit, which was only the inducement to a declaration in tort, interpreted as meaning contract in the modern sense. It will be seen directly that Lord Holt took a different view. Note, too, the way the Marshal's case was dealt with in one of the later reports. Modern illustrations of the doctrine will be found in Fleming v. Manchester, Sheffield, and Lincolnshire Railway Company, and the cases cited there.

Holt became Chief Justice. Three of the cases of this period were rulings of his.

Footnote: I have in mind Lovett v. Hobbs, Chamberlain v. Cooke, Boson v. Sandford (which cites Southcote's Case), Upshare v. Aidee, and Middleton v. Fowler.

In Lane v. Cotton, in 1701, he showed his disapproval of Southcote's Case, and his impression that the common law of bailment had been borrowed from Rome. The overthrow of Southcote's Case and of the old common law may be said to date from Coggs v. Bernard, in 1703. Lord Holt's famous opinion in that case quotes largely from the Roman law, as it filtered down to him through Bracton. But whatever influence that may have had on his general views, the point decided, and the distinctions he drew about common carriers, were of English growth.

The action did not sound in contract. The complaint was of damage to the goods, and the plaintiff sued in tort, laying an assumpsit as inducement—the introductory allegation leading up to the charge—to a charge of negligence, just as in the days of Henry VI. The plea was not guilty. But after a verdict for the plaintiff, the defendant moved in arrest of judgment—"for that it was not alleged in the declaration that the defendant was a common porter, nor averred that he had anything for his pains." Consideration was never alleged or thought of in the primitive assumpsit, but in the modern action of contract in that form it was required. Hence it was inferred that wherever an assumpsit was laid, even in an action of tort for damage to property, it was the allegation of a contract, and that a consideration must be shown for the undertaking—although the contrary had been decided in the reign of Queen Elizabeth.

But the motion did not prevail, and judgment was given for the plaintiff. Lord Holt was well aware that the use of an assumpsit was not confined to contract. It is true that he said, "The owner's trusting [the defendant] with the goods is a sufficient consideration to oblige him to a careful management," or to return them. But this means a consideration as distinguished from one sufficient to oblige him to carry them, which Holt thought the defendant would not have been bound to do. He then says expressly, "This is a different case, for assumpsit does not only signify a future agreement, but, in such cases as this, it signifies an actual entry upon the thing and taking the trust upon himself"; and in this he followed the earlier cases in the Year Books.

Footnote: How little Lord Holt meant to adopt the modern view, that delivery, being a detriment to the owner, was a consideration, may be further seen by examining the cases he put and agreed to from the Year Books. The subject is taken up in Lecture VII.

That was enough to decide the case, and the rule in Southcote's Case had nothing to do with it. But the duty of common carriers by reason of their calling was now supposed to extend to all kinds of losses, and the doctrine of Southcote's Case was probably supposed to extend to many kinds of damage. So in a general discussion it became necessary to reconcile the two principles, or to choose between them.

The Chief Justice therefore went on to distinguish between bailees for reward exercising a public employment—common carriers, common hoymen, masters of ships, and so on—and all other bailees. He denied the rule in Southcote's Case as to the second class. He said that the principle of strict responsibility was confined to the first class, and was applied to them on grounds of public policy. And he said that factors were exonerated, not because they were mere servants, as had always been laid down (among others, by Holt himself in arguing Morse v. Slue), but because they were not within the reason of the rule.

The reader who has followed the argument so far will hardly need convincing that this did not mean the adoption of the Praetor's Edict. There is further evidence at hand if it is wanted.

In the first place, as we have seen, there was a century of precedents, ending with Morse v. Slue, argued by Holt himself, in which the liability of masters of ships, hoymen, carriers, and the like had been decided. Morse v. Slue is cited and relied on, and there is no hint of dissatisfaction with the other cases. On the contrary, they furnished the examples of bailees for reward exercising a public calling. The distinction between bailees for reward and others is Chief Justice Popham's. The further qualification, exercising a public calling, was also English, as has partly appeared already and as will be explained further on.

In the next place, the strict rule is not confined to nautae, caupones, and stabularii—the shipmasters, innkeepers, and stable-keepers of the Edict—nor even to common carriers. It is applied to all bailees for reward exercising a public calling.

In the next place, the degree of responsibility is precisely that of bailees in general, as worked out by the earlier decisions; and it is quite unlike, and much more severe than, the responsibility imposed by the Roman law, as others have observed.

And finally, the exemption from liability for acts of God or the public enemy is characteristically English, as will be proved further on.

But it has been partly shown in this Lecture that the law of today has made the carrier's burden heavier than it was in the time of the Year Books. Southcote's Case, and the earlier authorities that have been cited, all concern a loss by robbery, theft, or trespass, and hold the bailee liable where, in theory at least, he has a remedy over against the wrongdoer. It was with such cases in view, as we have seen, that the rule arose, although it is not improbable that it would also have been applied to a loss nobody could explain; the writ against innkeepers reads absque subtractione seu amissione custodire, "to keep without removal or loss." In later times the principle may have been extended from loss by theft to loss by destruction. In Symons v. Darknoll, in the reign of Charles I, already cited as decided on the authority of Southcote's Case, the goods were spoiled, not stolen, and probably had not even perished in specie—that is, ceased to exist as the things they were. By then the old rule had become an arbitrary precedent, followed according to its form with little thought of its true purpose.

Footnote: In the reports of the early sixteenth century the encroachment of case on detinue and the corresponding confusion in principle may be seen pretty clearly taking place. But compare what was said earlier in this Lecture.

The language of Coggs v. Bernard is that "the law charges the person thus intrusted to carry goods as against all events but acts of God and the enemies of the king." This was adopted by solemn decision in Lord Mansfield's time, and it is now settled that the common carrier "is liable for all losses which do not fall within the excepted cases." That is to say, he has become an insurer to that extent: not only against the disappearance or destruction of the goods, but against every form of damage to them, except as excepted above.

The process by which this came about has been traced above, but a few words may be added here. The Year Books, even when dealing with the destruction (as distinguished from the conversion) of chattels in the hands of a bailee, always state his liability as based on his fault—though it must be admitted that the language is used alio intuitu, with a different question in view. A jettison in a tempest—throwing the goods overboard to save the ship—seems to have been a good plea for a factor in the time of Edward III; but that cannot be relied on as an analogy. The argument from the Marshal's case is stronger. There it seems to have been thought that the burning of the prison was as good an excuse for an escape as a release by alien enemies. This must mean an accidental fire, and would seem to imply that the Marshal was not liable in that event if he was not at fault. The writs in the Register against bailees to keep or carry goods all carry the general allegation of negligence, and so do the older precedents of declarations, so far as I have observed, whether they state the custom of the realm or not. But a bailee was answerable for goods wrongfully taken from him, as an innkeeper was for goods stolen from his inn, whether he was negligent or not.

It is true that the Marshal's case speaks of his negligent keeping when the prisoners were released by rebels (although that was far less likely to come from negligence, one would think, than a fire in the prison), and that after Lord Coke's time negligence was alleged even though the goods had been lost by a wrongful taking. So, too, the writ against innkeepers is pro defectu hujusmodi hospitatorum, "for the default of such innkeepers." In these instances neglect only means a failure in fact to keep safely. As was said at a much later date, "everything is a negligence in a carrier or hoyman that the law does not excuse." The allegation is simply the usual allegation of actions on the case, and seems to have spread from the earlier declarations for damage when case supplanted detinue and the use of case became universal. It can hardly have been immaterial to the kind of case for which it was first introduced. But the short reason for disbelieving that the old law gave any warrant for making the carrier an insurer against damage is that there seem to be no early cases in which bailees were held to such a responsibility, and that it was not within the principle on which they were made answerable for a loss by theft.

Having traced the process by which a common carrier has been made an insurer, it only remains to say a word on the origin of the admitted exceptions from the risk he assumes. We have already seen how loss by the public enemy came to be mentioned by Chief Justice Holt. It is the old distinction taken in the Marshal's case: against the public enemy the bailee has no remedy over.

As for the act of God, it was a general principle, not peculiar to carriers or to bailees, that a duty was discharged if an act of God made it impossible to perform. Lord Coke mentions the case of a jettison from a Gravesend barge, and another of a party bound to keep up sea-walls against flooding, as subject to the same limitation; and a similar statement about contracts in general will be found in the Year Books. It is another form of the principle which has been laboriously reargued in our own day: that parties are excused from performing a contract which has become impossible before breach, whether because the thing has perished or because of a change in circumstances whose continued existence was the foundation of the contract, provided there was no warranty and no fault on the part of the contractor. Whether the act of God has now acquired a special meaning with regard to common carriers may be left for others to consider.

It appears from the foregoing evidence that we cannot determine which classes of bailees are subject to the strict responsibility imposed on common carriers by turning to the Praetor's Edict and then consulting the dictionaries under nautae, caupones, or stabularii. The question of precedent is simply how far the old common law of bailment still survives. We can answer it only by listing the decisions in which the old law is applied, and we shall find it hard to bring them together under a general principle. The rule in Southcote's Case has been done away with for bailees in general: that is clear. But it is equally clear that the rule has not held its ground even within the limits of the public policy invented by Chief Justice Holt. It is not true today that all bailees for reward exercising a public calling are insurers. No such doctrine is applied to grain elevators or safe-deposit vaults.

How Lord Holt came to distinguish between bailees for reward and others has been shown above. It is more to the point here to notice that his further qualification, exercising a public calling, was part of a protective system which has passed away. One inclined to be hostile might say that it was one of many signs that the law was administered in the interest of the upper classes. It has been shown above that if a man was a common farrier, he could be charged for negligence without an assumpsit. The same judge who threw out that suggestion established in another case that the farrier could be sued if he refused to shoe a horse on reasonable request. Common carriers and common innkeepers were liable in the same way, and Lord Holt stated the principle: "If a man takes upon him a public employment, he is bound to serve the public as far as the employment extends, and for refusal an action lies." An attempt to apply this doctrine generally at the present day would be thought monstrous. But it formed part of a consistent scheme for holding those who followed useful callings up to the mark. Another part was the liability of persons exercising a public employment for loss or damage, heightened in cases of bailment by what remained of the rule in Southcote's Case. The scheme has given way to more liberal notions; but the disjecta membra—the scattered limbs—still move.

Lord Mansfield stated his views of public policy in terms not unlike those Chief Justice Holt used in Coggs v. Bernard, but he distinctly confines their application to common carriers. "But there is a further degree of responsibility by the custom of the realm, that is, by the common law; a carrier is in the nature of an insurer.... To prevent litigation, collusion, and the necessity of going into circumstances impossible to be unravelled, the law presumes against the carrier, unless," etc.

At the present day it is assumed that the principle is confined in this way, and the argument is shifted to the question of who are common carriers. It is thus conceded, by implication, that Lord Holt's rule has been abandoned. But the trouble is that with it disappear not only the general system which we have seen Lord Holt held, but also the special reasons repeated by Lord Mansfield. Those reasons apply to other bailees as well as to common carriers. Besides, hoymen and masters of ships were not originally held liable because they were common carriers; all three were treated as co-ordinate species, even in Coggs v. Bernard, where they were mentioned only as so many instances of bailees exercising a public calling. We do not get a new and single principle simply by giving a single name to all the cases to be accounted for. If there is a sound rule of public policy which ought to impose a special responsibility on common carriers, as those words are now understood, and on no others, it has never yet been stated. If, on the other hand, there are considerations that apply to a particular class among those so called—for instance, to railroads, which may have a private individual at their mercy, or may wield a power too vast for the common welfare—we do not prove that the reasoning extends to a general ship or a public cab by calling all three common carriers.

If there is no common rule of policy, and common carriers remain a merely empirical exception to general doctrine, courts may well hesitate to stretch the meaning of those words. Furthermore, notions of public policy that would not leave parties free to make their own bargains are somewhat discredited in most departments of the law. Hence it may perhaps be concluded that, if any new case should arise, the degree of responsibility, and the validity and interpretation of any contract of bailment there may be, should stand open to argument on general principles, and that the matter has been set at large so far as early precedent is concerned.

I have treated the law of carriers at greater length than its share of the subject deserves, because it seems to me an interesting example of the way in which the common law has grown up, and especially because it is an excellent illustration of the principles laid down at the end of the first Lecture. I now proceed to the discussion for the sake of which this account of the law of bailment was introduced, and to which an understanding of that part of the law is a necessary preliminary.

Lecture VI: Possession

Possession is a conception only less important than contract. But the interest of the theory of possession does not stop at its practical importance in the body of English law. The theory has fallen into the hands of the philosophers, and with them it has become a cornerstone of more than one elaborate structure. It will be a service to sound thinking to show that a far more civilized system than the Roman is built on a plan that cannot be reconciled with the a priori doctrines of Kant and Hegel—doctrines, that is, reasoned out in advance of experience. Those doctrines were worked out in careful correspondence with German views of Roman law. And most of the speculative jurists of Germany, from Savigny to Ihering, have been at once professors of Roman law and deeply influenced, if not controlled, by some form of Kantian or post-Kantian philosophy. So everything has combined to give German speculation a special bent, and that bent deprives it of any claim to universal authority.

Why does the law protect possession when the possessor is not also the owner? That is the general problem, and it has greatly exercised the German mind. Kant, as is well known, was deeply influenced in his views on ethics and law by the speculations of Rousseau. Kant, Rousseau, and the Massachusetts Bill of Rights agree that all men are born free and equal, and from that day to this one branch or the other of that declaration has supplied the answer to the question why possession should be protected. Kant and Hegel start from freedom. The freedom of the will, Kant said, is the essence of man. It is an end in itself. It needs no further explanation; it is to be respected absolutely; and to realize and affirm it is the very end and object of all government. Possession is to be protected because a man, by taking possession of an object, has brought it within the sphere of his will. He has extended his personality into or over that object. As Hegel would have put it, possession is the objective realization of free will. And by Kant's postulate, the will of any individual, once manifested in this way, is entitled to absolute respect from every other individual. It can be overcome or set aside only by the universal will—that is, by the state, acting through its organs, the courts.

Savigny did not follow Kant on this point. He said that every act of violence is unlawful, and he seemed to treat the protection of possession as a branch of the protection of the person. But the answer came back that possession is protected against disturbance by fraud as well as by force, and his view is discredited. Those who have been content with humble grounds of expediency seem to have been few, and they have either recanted or fallen out of favor.

The majority have gone in the direction Kant pointed out. Bruns, an admirable writer, expresses a characteristic yearning of the German mind when he demands an internal juristic necessity, drawn from the nature of possession itself, and on that account rejects reasons drawn from experience. He finds the necessity he is looking for in the freedom of the human will, which the whole legal system does nothing but recognize and carry out. To constrain that will is a wrong, and the wrong must be righted without asking whether the will conforms to law—and so on, in a Kantian vein. So Gans, a favorite disciple of Hegel: "The will is of itself a substantial thing to be protected, and this individual will has only to yield to the higher common will." So Puchta, a great master: "The will which wills itself, that is, the recognition of its own personality, is to be protected."

Footnote: Further Hegelian discourse may be found in Dr. J. Hutchison Stirling's Lectures on the Philosophy of Law.

The chief variation on this view is Windscheid's, a writer now in vogue. He prefers the other branch of the declaration in the Bill of Rights. He thinks that possession is protected on the same grounds as a person is protected against injuria, a wrong to his personal standing: everyone in the state is the equal of everyone else, and no one shall raise himself over another. Ihering, to be sure, a man of genius, made an independent start. He said that possession is ownership on the defensive: in the owner's interest, the person who is exercising ownership in fact (that is, the possessor) is spared the need of proving his title against someone who stands in an unlawful position. But Bruns, in his later work, answered this well. It assumes that the title of disseisors—those who have wrongfully put another out of his land—is generally worse than the title of the disseisees they put out. That cannot be taken for granted, and it is probably not true in fact.

It follows from the Kantian doctrine that a man in possession is to be confirmed and maintained in it until he is put out by an action brought for that purpose. Perhaps one more fact, besides those already mentioned, has influenced this reasoning: the sharp division in Continental procedure between possessory actions or defenses, which deal only with who holds the thing, and petitory ones, which deal with who has the right to it. When a defendant in a possessory action is not allowed to set up his own title, a theorist readily finds a mystical importance in possession.

But when does a man become entitled to this absolute protection? On Kant's principle it is not enough that he has custody of a thing. A protection founded on the sacredness of human personality requires that the object have been brought within the sphere of that personality—that the free will have set itself into the object without restraint. There must therefore be an intent to appropriate it: that is, to make it part of oneself, or one's own.

Here the prevailing view of Roman law comes in to fortify principle with precedent. We are told that, of all the people who might have the actual charge or custody of a thing, Roman law recognized as possessor only the owner, or one holding as owner and on his way to becoming owner by the lapse of time. In later days it made a few exceptions on practical grounds. But beyond the pledgee (the person holding goods as security for a debt) and the sequester (a receiver appointed by the court), these exceptions are unimportant and disputed. Some of the Roman jurists say in so many words that depositaries—people with whom goods are left for safekeeping—and borrowers do not have possession of the things entrusted to them. Whether or not the German reading of the sources goes too far, it must be taken into account in examining the German theories.

By denying possession to bailees in general, philosophy cunningly fitted itself to Roman law, and so put itself in a position to claim the authority of that law for the theory—a theory of which the treatment of bailees was merely a corollary. That is why I say it is important to show that a far more developed, more rational, and mightier body of law than the Roman gives no sanction to either the premise or the conclusion held by Kant and his successors.

In the first place, English law has always had the good sense to allow title to be set up as a defense to a possessory action. In the assize of novel disseisin—the old proceeding for recovering land from which one had lately and wrongfully been put out—which was a true possessory action, the defendant could always rely on his title. Even when possession is taken or kept in a way the criminal law punishes, as in forcible entry and detainer (taking or holding land by force), proof of title lets the defendant keep it, and in many cases it has been held an answer to an action of trespass. So in trespass for taking goods the defendant may set up title in himself. There might seem to be a trace of the Continental distinction in the general rule that title cannot be tried in trespass quare clausum. But this is an exception, and it is commonly put on the ground that the judgment in that action cannot change the property in the land, as a judgment in trespass for chattels or in trover can. The rule that you cannot go into title in a possessory action presupposes great difficulty of proof—the probatio diabolica, or "devil's proof," of the Canon law—delays in the process, and great importance in possession for the time being. All of these mark a stage of society long since passed. In ninety-nine cases out of a hundred it is about as easy and cheap to prove at least a prima facie title as it is to prove possession.

Footnote: Heusler thinks this merely a result of English formalism, and of a narrow reading of the word suo, "his," in the writ, which charged that the defendant had disseised the plaintiff of "his" tenement. But there was no such narrowness in dealing with catalla sua, "his chattels," in trespass, as will be seen below.

In the next place—and this was the bearing of the last Lecture on this subject—the common law has always given the possessory remedies to all bailees without exception. The right to these remedies extends not only to pledgees, lessees, and those having a lien, who can exclude their bailor, but also to simple bailees, as they have been called. These have no interest in the chattels, no right to hold them against the owner, and neither give nor receive any reward.

Modern German statutes have followed the same path so far as to give the possessory remedies to tenants and some others. Bruns says, as the spirit of the Kantian theory required him to say, that this is a sacrifice of principle to convenience. But I cannot see what is left of a principle that avows itself inconsistent with convenience and with the actual course of legislation. The first call on a theory of law is that it should fit the facts. It must explain the observed course of legislation. And since it is pretty certain that men will make the laws that seem convenient to them without troubling themselves much about what principles their legislation runs into, a principle that defies convenience is likely to wait some time before it finds itself permanently realized.

It remains, then, to look for some ground for protecting possession outside the Bill of Rights or the Declaration of Independence—a ground consistent with the larger scope that modern law gives the conception.

The courts have said little on the subject. In one case it was laid down that the protection of possession was an extension of the protection the law throws around the person, and on that ground it was held that the right to sue in trespass quare clausum did not pass to an assignee in bankruptcy. It has also been said that to deny a bankrupt the action of trover against strangers for goods that came into his possession after his bankruptcy would be "an invitation to all the world to scramble for the possession of them"; and the court referred to "grounds of policy and convenience." I may also refer to the cases of capture, some of which will be cited again. In the Greenland whale fishery, by the English custom, if the first striker lost his hold on the whale and it was then killed by another, the first had no claim; but he had the whole whale if he kept fast to it until it was struck by the other, even though it then broke away from the first harpoon. By the custom in the Galápagos, on the other hand, the first striker had half the whale even though he had lost control of the line. The English courts have upheld and acted on each of these customs, and Judge Lowell has decided in accordance with still a third, which gives the whale to the vessel whose iron first remains in it, provided the claim is made before cutting in—before the whale is cut up for its blubber. The ground, as Lord Mansfield put it, is simply that without such customs there would have to be a sort of warfare perpetually going on between the adventurers. If courts adopt different rules on similar facts, according to the point at which men will fight in the different cases, that tends, so far as it goes, to shake any a priori theory of the matter.

Those who see in the history of law the formal expression of the development of society will be apt to think that the proximate ground of law must be empirical—drawn from experience—even when that ground is the fact that a certain ideal or theory of government is generally held. Law, being a practical thing, must found itself on actual forces. It is quite enough for the law, therefore, that man, by an instinct he shares with the domestic dog, and of which the seal gives a most striking example, will not allow himself to be dispossessed of what he holds, either by force or by fraud, without trying to get it back. Philosophy may find a hundred reasons to justify the instinct, but it would make no difference at all if philosophy condemned it and told us to surrender without a murmur. As long as the instinct remains, it will be more comfortable for the law to satisfy it in an orderly way than to leave people to themselves. If the law did otherwise, it would become a matter for pedagogues, wholly devoid of reality.

I think we are now in a position to begin the analysis of possession. It will be instructive first to say a word on a preliminary question that has been debated with much zeal in Germany: Is possession a fact or a right? The question must be taken to mean possession and right in the sense the law gives those words, and not something else that philosophers or moralists may mean by them; for as lawyers we have nothing to do with either except in a legal sense. If this had always been kept steadily in mind, the question would hardly have been asked.

A legal right is nothing but a permission to exercise certain natural powers and, on certain conditions, to obtain protection, restitution, or compensation by the aid of the public force. Just so far as the aid of the public force is given to a man, he has a legal right, and the right is the same whether his claim is founded in righteousness or in iniquity. Just so far as possession is protected, it is as much a source of legal rights as ownership is when ownership secures the same protection.

Every right is a consequence the law attaches to one or more facts that the law defines. Wherever the law gives anyone special rights not shared by the body of the people, it does so because certain special facts, not true of the rest of the world, are true of him. When a group of facts singled out by the law in this way exists in the case of a given person, he is said to be entitled to the corresponding rights. What is meant is that the law helps him to constrain his neighbors, or some of them, in a way it would not if all the facts in question were not true of him. It follows that any word which denotes such a group of facts connotes the rights attached to it as legal consequences, and any word which denotes the rights attached to a group of facts connotes the group of facts in the same way.

The word "possession" denotes such a group of facts. So when we say of a man that he has possession, we state directly that all the facts of a certain group are true of him, and we convey indirectly, by implication, that the law will give him the advantage of the situation. Contract, or property, or any other substantive notion of the law, may be analyzed in the same way, and should be treated in the same order. The only difference is this. Possession denotes the facts and connotes the consequence; property always, and contract with more uncertainty and wavering, denote the consequence and connote the facts. When we say that a man owns a thing, we state directly that he has the benefit of the consequences attached to a certain group of facts, and, by implication, that the facts are true of him. The important thing to grasp is that each of these legal compounds—possession, property, and contract—is to be analyzed into fact and right, antecedent and consequent, just like every other. It does not matter at all that one word puts the accent on one element and the other two words on the other. We are not studying etymology, but law. There are always two things to ask: first, what are the facts that make up the group in question; and then, what consequences does the law attach to that group. The first generally offers the only difficulties.

So it is almost a tautology to say that the protection the law attaches to possession as its consequence is as truly a right, in the legal sense, as the consequences attached to adverse holding for the period of prescription—the long, open holding of another's property that ripens into title—or to a promise made for value or under seal. If the statement needs dramatic reinforcement, I may add that possessory rights pass by descent or by will, as well as by conveyance, and that in some of the States they are taxed as property.

We are now ready to analyze possession as the common law understands it. To discover the facts that make it up, it will be best to study them at the moment when possession is first gained. For then they must all be present, in the same way that both consideration and promise must be present at the moment a contract is made. But when we turn to the continuance of possessory rights—or, as it is commonly put, the continuance of possession—all schools will agree that fewer than all the facts needed to call those rights into being must remain true at present in order to keep them alive.

To gain possession, then, a man must stand in a certain physical relation to the object and to the rest of the world, and he must have a certain intent. These relations and this intent are the facts we are looking for.

The physical relation to others is simply a relation of manifested power that reaches as far as the intent does, and little will need to be said about it once the nature of the intent is settled. When I come to the intent, I shall not attempt an analysis like the one pursued earlier with regard to intent as an element of liability. The principles worked out about intent in that connection have nothing to do with the present subject, and any such analysis, so far as it did not fail, would be little more than a discussion of evidence. The intent inquired into here must perhaps be outwardly manifested; but all theories of the grounds on which possession is protected seem to agree in requiring that it be actual—subject, of course, to the necessary limits of legal investigation.

But besides our power and intent with regard to our fellow men, there must be a certain degree of power over the object. If there were only one other man in the world, and he was safely under lock and key in jail, the person holding the key would not possess the swallows that flew over the prison. Cases of capture illustrate this element, although no doubt where the line is drawn is affected by the degree of power gained against other people, as well as by the power gained over the object. Roman law and the common law agree that, in general, fresh pursuit of wild animals does not give the pursuer the rights of possession. Until escape has been made impossible by some means, another may step in and kill or catch the game and carry it off if he can. So it has been held that no action lies against a person for killing and taking a fox that another man had been pursuing, and that was at that moment actually in view of the man who had found it, started it, and chased it. The Court of Queen's Bench went so far as to decide, in spite of a jury's verdict the other way, that fish nearly surrounded by a seine—a large fishing net—with an opening of seven fathoms between its ends, where boats were stationed to frighten them from escaping, had not been reduced to possession as against a stranger who rowed in through the opening and helped himself. But the difference between power over the object that is enough for possession and power that is not is plainly one of degree only, and the line may be drawn at different places at different times, on the grounds just mentioned. Thus we are told that the legislature of New York enacted in 1844 that anyone who started and pursued deer in certain counties of that State should be deemed in possession of the game as long as he kept up fresh pursuit of it, and to that extent modified the New York decisions just cited. So too, while Justinian decided that a wild beast so badly wounded that it might easily be taken must actually be taken before it belongs to its captors, Judge Lowell, with equal reason, has upheld the contrary custom of the American whalemen in the Arctic Ocean, mentioned above, which gives a whale to the vessel whose iron first remains in it, provided the claim is made before cutting in.

We may leave the physical relation to the object with these few examples, because it can rarely come into question except with living and wild things. And so we come to the intent, which is the really troublesome matter. It is just here that we find the German jurists unsatisfactory, for the reasons I have already given. The best-known theories have been framed as theories of the German interpretation of Roman law, under the influence of some form of Kantian or post-Kantian philosophy. The type of Roman possession, in German opinion, was that of an owner, or of one on his way to becoming owner. Following this out, Savigny—the only writer on the subject whom English readers generally know—said that the animus domini, the intent to deal with the thing as owner, is in general necessary to turn a mere physical holding into possession in the legal sense. We need not stop to ask whether this modern form, or the Greek phrase of Theophilus and the Greek sources (animus dominantis or animus dominandi, the intent of one who is master), is more exact. Either one excludes most bailees and termors—tenants for a term of years—from the list of possessors, as the civilians and canonists (the scholars of Roman and of Church law) exclude them, and as the German theories must.

Footnote: I shall use animus domini here as a short way of indicating the general nature of the intent required, even by those who deny that the expression fits; and especially because Savigny's opinion is the one English writers have adopted.

The effect of this exclusion, as interpreted by the Kantian philosophy of law, has been to lead the German lawyers to regard the intent necessary to possession as primarily self-regarding. Their philosophy teaches them that a man's physical power over an object is protected because he has the will to make it his, so that it has become part of his very self, the outward manifestation of his freedom. Once the possessor's will is conceived as self-regarding in this way, the intent with which he must hold is pretty clear: he must hold for his own benefit. Furthermore, the self-regarding intent must rise to the height of an intent to appropriate; for otherwise, it seems to be implied, the object would not truly be brought under the possessor's personality.

The grounds for rejecting the tests of Roman law have been shown above. Let us begin afresh. Legal duties are logically prior to legal rights. What their relation may be to moral rights, if there are any, and whether moral rights are not in the same way the logical offspring of moral duties, are questions that do not concern us here. They belong to the philosopher, who approaches the law from outside, as one part of a larger series of human manifestations. The business of the jurist is to make known the content of the law—that is, to work on it from within, or logically, arranging and distributing it in order, from its summum genus, its highest class, down to its infima species, its narrowest kind, so far as that can be done. Legal duties, then, come before legal rights. To put it more broadly, and to avoid the word duty, which is open to objection: the direct working of the law is to limit, in certain specified ways, the freedom of action or choice of a greater or smaller number of persons. The power of removing or enforcing this limitation, which is generally entrusted to certain other private persons—in other words, a right corresponding to the burden—is not a necessary or universal counterpart. Again, a large part of the advantages enjoyed by one who has a right are not created by the law. The law does not enable me to use or abuse this book that lies before me. That is a physical power I have without the law's help. What the law does is simply to prevent other men, to a greater or lesser extent, from interfering with my use or abuse. And this analysis and example apply to possession as well as to ownership.

Since that is how the law directly works in the case of possession, one would think that the intent we are looking for is the intent that runs most nearly parallel to it. If what the law does is to keep others from interfering with the object, it would seem that the intent the law should require is an intent to exclude others. I believe that such an intent is all the common law thinks necessary, and that on principle no more should be required.

It may be asked whether this is not simply the animus domini seen from the other side. Even if it were, it would still be better to look at the front of the shield than at the back. But it is not the same thing, if we give the animus domini the meaning the Germans give it—the meaning that denies possession to bailees in general. The intent to appropriate a thing, or to deal with it as owner, can hardly exist without an intent to exclude others, and something more besides; but the intent to exclude may very well exist where there is no intent to hold as owner. A tenant for years means to exclude everyone, the owner included, until his term runs out; yet he has not the animus domini in the sense just explained. Still less has a bailee with a lien, who does not even mean to use the thing, but only to keep it until he is paid. Further, the common law protects a bailee against strangers when it would not protect him against the owner, as in the case of a deposit or any other bailment that the owner can end at pleasure. We may therefore say that even the intent to exclude need not be as wide as the animus domini would imply. If a bailee intends to exclude strangers to the title, that is enough for possession under our law, although he is perfectly ready to hand the thing back to its owner at any moment. It is of the essence of the German view, by contrast, that the intent must not be relative: it must be an absolute, self-regarding intent to take the benefit of the thing. Again, even if the motives, wishes, and intentions most present to a possessor's mind were all self-regarding, it would not follow that his intent toward others was not the important thing in analyzing the law. But, as we have seen, a depositary is a true possessor under the common-law theory, although his intent is not self-regarding at all, and he holds solely for the owner's benefit.

Besides the cases of bailees and tenants, there is another class of cases which will probably—though not necessarily—be decided one way or the other according to whether we adopt the test of an intent to exclude or the test of the animus domini. Bridges v. Hawkesworth will serve as a starting point. There a customer dropped a pocketbook on the floor of a shop, and another customer picked it up before the shopkeeper knew anything about it. Common-law judges and civilians would agree that the finder got possession first, and so could keep it as against the shopkeeper. For the shopkeeper, not knowing of the thing, could not have intended to appropriate it; and, having invited the public into his shop, he could not have intended to exclude them from it. But suppose the pocketbook had been dropped in a private room. How should the case be decided then? There can be no animus domini unless the thing is known of. But an intent to exclude others from it may be contained in the larger intent to exclude others from the place where it is, without any knowledge that the object exists.

In McAvoy v. Medina, a pocketbook had been left on a barber's table, and it was held that the barber had a better right than the finder. The opinion is rather obscure. It draws a distinction between things deliberately put down on a table and things dropped on the floor. It may possibly rest on the ground that, when an owner leaves a thing in that way, there is an implied request to the shopkeeper to look after it, which gives him a better right than someone who actually finds it before he does. This is rather strained, however, and perhaps the court thought that the barber had possession as soon as the customer left the shop. A little later the same court heard a suit for a reward offered to whoever found a pocketbook. The plaintiff had discovered it where the owner had left it, on a desk provided for customers in a bank, outside the teller's counter. The court said that this was not the finding of a lost article, and that "the occupants of the banking house, and not the plaintiff, were the proper depositaries of an article so left." This language might seem to imply that the plaintiff was not the first person to get possession after the owner lost it; and that, although the floor of a shop may be likened to a street, the public are to be treated as excluded from a shop's desks, counters, and tables except for the particular use allowed them. Perhaps, however, the case decides only that the pocketbook was not lost within the terms of the offer.

I should not have thought it safe to draw any conclusion from the English cases on wreck, which are tangled up with questions of prescription and other rights. But the exact point seems to have been decided here in Massachusetts. For it has been held that, if a stick of timber washes ashore on a man's land, he thereby acquires a "right of possession" as against an actual finder who comes onto the land to carry it off. A right of possession is said to be enough to support trespass; but the court seems to have meant possession itself by the phrase. Chief Justice Shaw states the question as which of the parties had "the preferable claim, by mere naked possession, without other title," and there does not seem to have been any right of possession in the case unless there was actual possession.

In a criminal case, where a stranger took iron from the bottom of a canal, the indictment was held rightly to lay the property in the canal company, although it does not appear that the company knew the iron was there, or had any lien on it.

The only intent concerning the thing that can be found in such cases is the general intent which the occupier of land has to exclude the public from the land, and so, as a consequence, from whatever is on it.

The Roman lawyers would probably have decided all these cases the other way, although they cannot be supposed to have worked out the refined theories that have since been built on what remains of their writings.

I may return here to the case of goods delivered in a locked chest, or in a bale, and the like. It is a rule of the criminal law that if the bailee of such a chest or bale wrongfully sells the whole chest or bale, he does not commit larceny; but if he breaks bulk, he does, because in the first case he does not commit a trespass and in the second he does. The reason sometimes given is that by breaking bulk the bailee ends the bailment, so that the goods at once go back into the possession of the bailor. This is perhaps an unnecessary fiction, as well as an inadequate one. The rule comes from the Year Books, and the theory of the Year Books was that, although the chest was delivered to the bailee, the goods inside it were not; and this theory was applied to civil as well as criminal cases. The bailor has the power and the intent to exclude the bailee from the goods, and may therefore be said to be in possession of them as against the bailee.

Footnote: It may not be necessary to go quite so far, however, and I do not rely on these cases as establishing the theory.

On the other hand, a case in Rhode Island goes against the view taken here. A man bought a safe and then, wanting to sell it again, sent it to the defendant, giving him leave to keep his books in it until it was sold. The defendant found some banknotes stuck in a crack in the safe. When this came to the plaintiff's ears, he demanded the safe and the money. The defendant sent back the safe but refused to give up the money, and the court upheld his refusal. I venture to think this decision wrong. Nor would my opinion change if we assumed—what the report does not make perfectly clear—that the defendant received the safe as a bailee, and not as a servant or agent, and that his permission to use it was general. The court's argument turns on the plaintiff's not being a finder. The question is whether he needed to be one. It is hard to believe that, if the defendant had stolen the notes out of the safe while it was in its owner's hands, the property could not have been laid in the owner of the safe, or that the owner could not have maintained trover for them had they been converted in those circumstances. Sir James Stephen seems to have drawn a similar conclusion from Cartwright v. Green and Merry v. Green; but I believe there is no warrant for it in the cases, and still less for the reason he suggests.

Footnote: Stephen says it is "because [the owner of the safe] cannot be presumed to intend to act as the owner of it when he discovers it"—a reason drawn from Savigny, and not fitted to the English law, as has been shown.

It will be understood, however, that Durfee v. Jones, the Rhode Island case, is perfectly consistent with the view maintained here of the general nature of the intent required. It touches only the subordinate question whether the intent to exclude must be aimed at the specific thing, or may be included, even unconsciously, in a larger intent—as I am inclined to believe it may.

So far nothing has been said about the custody of servants. It is a well-known doctrine of the criminal law that a servant who criminally converts his master's property, entrusted to him and in his custody as a servant, is guilty of theft, because he is regarded as having taken the property out of his master's possession. That is the same as saying that a servant who has custody of his master's property as a servant does not have possession of it, and the Year Books say exactly that.

There is an odd distinction by which, if the servant receives the thing from some third person for his master, the servant has the possession, and so cannot commit theft. The old cases make it more rational. For the distinction they draw is that while the servant is in the house or with his master, the master keeps possession; but if the master hands his horse to his servant to ride to market, or gives him a bag to carry to London, then the thing is out of the master's possession and in the servant's. In this more intelligible form the rule would not prevail today. But one half of it—that a guest at a tavern does not have possession of the plate he is served on—is no doubt still law, for guests in general are likened to servants in their legal position.

Footnote: It may be doubted whether the old law would have sanctioned the rule in the modern form.

Footnote: Guests have been said to be a part of the family pro hac vice—for the occasion.

Outside the criminal law there are few English decisions on whether a servant has possession. But the Year Books suggest no difference between civil and criminal cases, and there is an almost unbroken tradition of courts and approved writers that he has not, in any case. A master has maintained trespass against a servant for converting cloth the servant was employed to sell, and the American cases go the full length of the old doctrine. It has often been remarked that a servant must be distinguished from a bailee.

Footnote: Some of the American cases have been denied, on the ground that the custodian was not a servant.

But it may be asked how denying possession to servants can be squared with the test proposed here; and it will be said, truly, that a servant has as much intent to exclude the world at large as a borrower does. The law of servants is unquestionably at odds with that test. And there can be no doubt that those who have built their theories on the Roman law have been led by this fact, together with the Roman doctrine about bailees in general, to look for their formula of reconciliation where they have. But in truth the exception for servants stands on purely historical grounds. A servant is denied possession not because of anything peculiar in his intent toward the things in his custody—either toward his master or toward other people—that sets him apart from a depositary, but simply as one of the incidents of his status. It is familiar that the status of a servant keeps many marks of the time when he was a slave. The master's liability for his servant's torts is one instance. The present rule is another. A slave's possession was his owner's possession, on the practical ground of the owner's power over him, and because the slave had no standing before the law. The notion that his personality was merged in that of the head of his family survived the era of emancipation.

I have shown in the first Lecture that agency grew out of this earlier relation in the Roman law, by extending to a freeman, pro hac vice, ideas drawn from the law of slaves. The same is true, I think, of our own law, whose later development seems to have been largely under Roman influence. As late as Blackstone, agents appear under the general heading of servants, and the first precedents cited for the special law of agents were cases of master and servant. Blackstone's language is worth quoting: "There is yet a fourth species of servants, if they may be so called, being rather in a superior, a ministerial capacity; such as stewards, factors, and bailiffs: whom, however, the law considers as servants pro tempore, with regard to such of their acts as affect their master's or employer's property."

Footnote: Factors are always called servants in the old books, and bailiffs are spoken of in the same way.

It is quite true that in modern times many of the effects of either relation—master and servant, or principal and agent—can be explained as the result of acts done by the master himself. If a man tells another to make a contract in his name, or orders him to commit a tort, no special idea is needed to explain why he is held liable; although even in such cases, where the go-between was a freeman, the law did not reach that conclusion until it had become fairly mature. But if Agency deserves to stand as a title of the law at all, it must be because some peculiar consequences are attached to the fact of the relation. If the mere power to bind a principal to a contract he authorized were all, we might as well have a chapter on ink and paper as on agents. But it is not all. Even in the field of contract we find the striking doctrine that an undisclosed principal—one whose existence the other party did not know of—has the rights as well as the obligations of a known contractor: he can be sued, and, more remarkable still, he can sue on his agent's contract. The first precedent cited for the rule that a promise made to an agent may be pleaded as a promise to the principal is a case of master and servant.

Footnote: These rules seem to be somewhat modern even as to servants. In the earlier Year Books a master's liability for debts his servant contracted is very narrowly limited.

Since my present object is only to show what the doctrine of identification—the treating of servant and master as one person—means for the theory of possession, it would be out of place to consider at any length how far that doctrine must be called on to explain a principal's liability for his agent's torts, or whether some more reasonable rule governs other cases than the one applied where the actor has a fairly definite status as a servant. I allow myself a few words, because I shall not be able to come back to the subject.

If the courts had so far treated a master's liability for his servant's torts as the decaying remnant of an obsolete institution, it would not be surprising to find it confined to the cases settled by ancient precedent. But that has not happened. It has been extended by analogy to new relations. It exists where the principal does not stand toward the actual wrongdoer in the position of paterfamilias, the Roman head of the household. A man may be held liable for another where the relation was so passing as to rule out any idea of status—as for the negligence of someone else's servant acting for the defendant for the moment, or of a neighbor helping him as a volunteer. And, so far as is known, no principal has ever escaped on the ground of the dignity of his agent's employment. The courts habitually speak as if the same rules applied to brokers and other agents as to servants properly so called. Indeed, it has been laid down in so many words that the liability of employers is not confined to servants, although the usual cases are, of course, those of menial servants and the like, who could not pay a large verdict.

Footnote: I am inclined to think that this extension has been largely due to the influence of the Roman law. See Lecture I, and notice the part that the precedents about fire have played in shaping the modern doctrine of master and servant. Lord Holt's examples in one of the leading cases come from the Roman law; and in another, Doctor and Student is referred to for the general principles of liability—and Doctor and Student states Roman law.

On the other hand, if the peculiar doctrines of agency are anomalous, and form, as I believe, the vanishing point of the servile status, it may well happen that common sense will refuse to carry them out to their furthest applications. We may see such conflicts between tradition and the instinct of justice on the question of identifying a principal who knows the truth with an agent who makes a false statement, so as to make out a fraud, as in Cornfoot v. Fowke; or on the question, argued in many English cases, of a principal's liability for his agent's frauds. But so long as the fiction at the root of a master's liability is left alive, it is as hopeless to reconcile the differences by logic as to square the circle.

Footnote: One judge has commented on Cornfoot v. Fowke in the same way as the text. The other grounds for that decision do not matter here.

Footnote: The objections which Baron Bramwell raises to holding one man liable for the frauds of another are really objections to the peculiar consequences attached to the relation of master and servant in general, and the same learned judge has urged them in that more general form.

In an article in the American Law Review I referred to a phrase of Godefroi's about agents: eadem est persona domini et procuratoris—"the master and the agent are the same person." This idea of a fictitious unity of person has been called a darkening of counsel in a recent useful book. But it has the approval of Sir Henry Maine, and I believe it must stand as expressing an important aspect of the law, if, as I have tried to show, there is no adequate and complete explanation of the modern law except that rules survived in practice which lost their true meaning when the people they governed ceased to be slaves. There is no difficulty in understanding what is meant by saying that a slave has no legal standing, but is absorbed in the family which his master represents before the law. The meaning seems just as clear when we say that a free servant, in his relations as a servant, is in many respects likened by the law to a slave—not, of course, to his own harm as a freeman. The next step is simply that others who are not servants in a general sense may be treated as if they were servants in a particular connection. That is the progress of ideas as history shows it to us. And that is what is meant by saying that the characteristic feature which justifies agency as a title of the law is the absorption, pro hac vice, of the agent's legal individuality in that of his principal.

If this were carried out logically, it would follow that an agent appointed to hold possession in his principal's name would not be treated as having the legal possession, or as entitled to bring trespass. But after what has been said, no opinion can be given on whether the law would go that far unless precedent shows it. Notice the nature of the case. It is that of an agent appointed for the very point and purpose of possession. A bailee may be an agent for some other purpose. A free servant may be made a bailee. But the bailee holds in his own name, as we say, following the Roman idiom, and the servant or agent holding as such does not.

Footnote: Compare the Roman rule with the passage from Blackstone quoted above: "He possesses in whose name the thing is possessed; the agent lends his services to another's possession."

Even if space allowed, it would hardly be worth while to search the books on this subject, because of the great confusion of language to be found in them. It has been said, for instance, in this connection, that a carrier is a servant; yet nothing can be clearer than that goods in his custody are in his possession. Again, where goods remain in the custody of the seller, the courts have confused the setting apart of the goods for the contract, and the buyer's acceptance of them, with delivery. Our law has adopted the Roman doctrine that there may be a delivery—that is, a change of possession—merely by a change in the character in which the seller holds. But it has not always imitated the caution of the civilians as to what amounts to such a change. Bailees are constantly spoken of as if they were agents to possess for someone else—a confusion made easier by the fact that they generally are agents for other purposes. Finally, there are the cases that attribute possession to a buyer of goods in the hands of a middleman, without asking whether the middleman holds in his own name or in the buyer's. They generally reach the right result, no doubt, but they have added to the confusion of thought upon the subject.

Footnote: The Digest puts the Roman rule in so many words: what I possess in my own name I can come to possess in another's name; I do not thereby change the ground of my possession, but I cease to possess, and by my service I make another the possessor. For to possess is not the same as to possess in another's name: he possesses in whose name the thing is possessed, and the agent merely lends his service to another's possession. This shows that the seller changed possession by holding in the name of the buyer, as the buyer's agent to possess.

Footnote: It should be kept in mind also that the Roman law denied possession to bailees.

German writers are a little apt to value a theory of possession in proportion to how broad a line it draws between juridical possession, the kind the law protects, and mere actual detention. But from the point of view taken here, it will be seen that the grounds for denying possession and the possessory remedies to servants, and to agents holding as such—if, indeed, agents lack those remedies—are merely historical. The general theory can only take account of that denial as an anomaly. It will also be seen that servants and depositaries have often been likened to each other on the ground that both hold for the benefit of another and not for themselves. That ground has had no influence whatever on our law, which has always treated depositaries as having possession. Nor is it the true explanation of the Roman doctrine, which did not decide either case upon that ground, and which decided each for reasons different from those on which it decided the other.

It will now be easy to deal with the question of power as to third persons. This is naturally a power coextensive with the intent. But we must bear in mind that the law deals only or mainly with manifested facts; and hence, when we speak of a power to exclude others, we mean no more than a power which appears so in its manifestation. A ruffian may be within equal reach and sight when a child picks up a pocket-book; but if he does nothing, the child has manifested the needful power as well as if it had been backed by a hundred policemen. Narrowed in this way, it might be suggested that the manifestation of power matters only as a manifestation of intent. But the two things are distinct, and the power becomes decisive when there are two conflicting intents at the same time. Thus, two parties, neither of whom had title, each claimed a crop of corn against the other, and they cultivated it by turns. The plaintiff gathered the corn and threw it in small piles in the same field, where it lay for a week; then each party at the same moment began to carry it away. It was held that the plaintiff had not gained possession. But if the defendant's first interference had come after the gathering into piles, the plaintiff would probably have recovered. So, again, trustees in possession of a schoolroom put in a schoolmaster. He was afterwards dismissed, but the next day, June 30, he re-entered by force. On the fourth of July he was required by notice to leave, and he was not thrown out until the eleventh. It was considered that the schoolmaster never got possession as against the trustees.

We are led, in this connection, to the subject of how long the rights acquired by gaining possession continue. To gain possession, as we have seen, there must be certain physical relations, as explained, and a certain intent. It remains to ask how far these facts must go on being true of a person at each moment in order that he may keep the rights which follow from their presence. The prevailing view is Savigny's. He thinks there must always be the same animus as at the moment of acquisition, and a constant power to reproduce at will the original physical relations to the object. Everyone agrees that it is not necessary to have a present power over the thing at every moment; otherwise one could possess only what was under his hand. But it is a question whether we cannot dispense with even more. The facts which constitute possession are by their nature capable of remaining true for a lifetime. Hence there has arisen an ambiguity of language which has led to much confusion of thought. We use the word "possession" indifferently for two things: the presence of all the facts needed to gain it, and the condition of a man who, although some of those facts no longer exist, is still protected as if they did. Consequently it has been only too easy to treat the end of the facts as the loss of the right, as some German writers very nearly do.

But it does not follow, from the single circumstance that certain facts must come together to create the rights incident to possession, that they must continue in order to keep those rights alive. It follows no more than it does from the need for a consideration and a promise to create a right in contract that the consideration and promise must go on passing between the parties until the moment of performance. When certain facts that confer a right have once been made manifest, there is no general ground on which the law need hold the right at an end, except the manifestation of some fact inconsistent with its continuance—although the reasons for conferring the particular right may weigh heavily in deciding which facts shall count as inconsistent. The end of the original physical relations to the object might be treated as such a fact; but it never has been, unless in times of more ungoverned violence than the present. On the same principle, it is only a question of tradition or policy whether losing the power to reproduce the original physical relations shall affect the continuance of the rights. That loss does not stand on the same ground as a new possession taken adversely by another. We have adopted the Roman law as to animals ferae naturae—wild by nature—but the general tendency of our law is to favor appropriation. It abhors the absence of proprietary or possessory rights as a kind of vacuum. Accordingly, where a man found logs afloat and moored them, and they broke loose again and floated away and were found by another, it has been expressly decided that the first finder kept the rights that sprang from his having taken possession, and that he could maintain trover against the second finder, who refused to give them up.

Footnote: Bruns comes to the same conclusion on practical grounds of convenience, although on theory he utterly repudiates it. I must refer to what I said above about these conflicts between theory and convenience.

Suppose that the finder of a purse of gold has left it in his country house, which is lonely and lightly barred, and that he is a hundred miles away, in prison. The only person within twenty miles is a thoroughly equipped burglar at his front door, who has seen the purse through a window and intends to go in and take it at once. The finder's power to reproduce his former physical relation to the gold is rather limited. Yet I believe no one would say that his possession was at an end until the burglar, by an overt act, had manifested his power and intent to exclude others from the purse. The reason is the same one already given about the power to exclude at the moment of gaining possession. The law deals, for the most part, with overt acts and facts which can be known by the senses. So long as the burglar has not taken the purse, he has not manifested his intent; and until he breaks through the barrier which measures the present possessor's power of excluding him, he has not manifested his power. It may be observed, further, that by the tests adopted in this Lecture the owner of the house has a present possession in the strictest sense. He lacks the power which Savigny says is necessary, but he has the present intent and power to exclude others.
