It is conceivable that the common law should go so far as to treat possession the same way as a title, and should hold that once possession has been acquired, the rights acquired with it continue to prevail against all the world but one, until something has happened that would be enough to divest ownership.

The possession of rights, as it is called, has been a battleground on the Continent for centuries. It is not uncommon for German writers to go so far as to maintain that there may be a true possession of obligations. This seems to fit a general view that possession and right are, in theory, terms of equal reach: the mastery of the will over an external object in general—whether that object is a thing or another will—is called right when it accords with the general will and is therefore lawful, and is called possession when it exists merely in fact. Bearing in mind what was said on the question whether possession was a fact or a right, it will be seen that such an opposition between possession and right cannot be admitted as a legal distinction. The facts constituting possession generate rights as truly as do the facts which constitute ownership, although the rights of a mere possessor are less extensive than those of an owner.

Footnote: A learned writer of an older day asks why a doctor has no possessory action if you stop employing him, and answers: "I feel that the action does not lie; but I only feel it, and I could not tell you why if you threatened me with death. You, reader, if you are wise, supply the reasons for the decision."

Conversely, rights spring from certain facts supposed to be true of the person entitled to them. Where these facts are such that they can be made true of different persons one after another, as in the case of the occupation of land, the corresponding rights may be enjoyed one after another. But when the facts are past and gone—such as the giving of a consideration and the receiving of a promise—no one can set up a claim to the resulting rights except the party of whom the facts were originally true: in the case supposed, the original contractee, the one to whom the promise was made. For no one but the original contractee can fill the situation from which those rights spring.

English readers will probably grant that one of the essential facts consists in a certain relation to a material object. But this object may be a slave as well as a horse; and conceptions that began in this way may be extended, by a survival, to free services. It is worth noticing that even Bruns, in applying his theory, does not seem to go beyond cases of status and cases where, in common language, land is bound for the services in question, as it is for rent. Free services are treated so far like a slave's, even by our law, that the master has a right of property in them against all the world; so it is only a question of degree where the line shall be drawn. It would be possible to hold that, as one might be in possession of a slave without title, so one might have all the rights of an owner in free services rendered without a contract. Perhaps something of that sort is to be seen when a parent recovers for the seduction of a daughter over twenty-one, although there is no actual contract of service. So, too, throughout the whole course of the canon law and in the early law of England, rents were regarded as so far a part of the land as to be capable of possession and of disseisin, and they could be recovered like land by assize.

But the most important case of the so-called possession of rights in our law, as in the Roman, concerns easements—rights to make some limited use of another's land, such as a right of way. An easement is capable of possession in a certain sense. A man may use land in a certain way, with the intent to exclude all others from using it in any way inconsistent with his own use, but no further. If this is true possession, however, it is a limited possession of land, not of a right, as others have shown. Where an easement has actually been created, whether by deed or by prescription, it is undoubtedly true that anyone in possession of the dominant estate—the land the easement serves—would be protected in its enjoyment. But he has not been protected in the past on the ground that the easement was in itself an object of possession; the protection comes from a survival of precedents explained in Lecture XI. So to test whether there is a mere possession of this sort which the law will protect, we will take a way used in fact for four years, over which no easement has yet been acquired, and ask whether the possessor of the would-be dominant land would be protected in his use as against third persons. It is conceivable that he should be, but I believe that he would not.

Footnote: If the law should protect a possessor of land in the enjoyment of water flowing to it, it would do so because the use of the water was regarded as part of the enjoyment of that land; this would by no means imply that it would do the same in the case just put, of a way over another's land.

The chief objection to the doctrine seems to be that there is almost a contradiction between two assertions: that one man has a general power and intent to exclude the world from dealing with the land, and that another has the power to use it in a particular way and to exclude the world from interfering with that. Reconciling the two takes somewhat artificial reasoning. However, it should be borne in mind that the question in every case is not what the actual power of the parties was, but what their manifested power was. If their manifested powers stood balanced in this way, the law might recognize a kind of split possession. But if the law does not recognize it until a right has been acquired, then the protection of a disseisor in the use of an easement must still be explained by the facts set out in Lecture XI.

The consequences attached to possession are substantially those attached to ownership, subject to the question of how long possessory rights continue, which I have touched on above. Even a wrongful possessor of a chattel may recover full damages for its conversion by a stranger to the title, or get back the specific thing.

It has been supposed, to be sure, that a "special property" was necessary in order to maintain replevin—the action to recover goods wrongfully taken—or trover. But modern cases establish that possession is enough, and an examination of the sources of our law proves that special property never meant anything more. It has been shown that the procedure Bracton describes for recovering chattels lost against one's will, like its predecessor on the Continent, was based on possession. Yet Bracton, in the very passage in which he says so expressly, uses a phrase which, but for that explanation, would seem to mean ownership: "Poterit rem suam petere"—he may claim his thing. The writs of later days used the same language. When it was objected, as it often was, that a bailee's suit for the taking of bona et catalla sua—"his goods and chattels"—should instead have been for bona in custodia sua existentia—"goods being in his custody"—the answer was always that the clerks of the Chancery would not frame a writ in that form.

The substance of the matter was that goods in a man's possession were his (sua) within the meaning of the writ. But it was very natural to try to reconcile, on paper, that formal word with the fact, by saying that although the plaintiff did not have the general property in the chattels, he had a property as against strangers, or a special property. This is what happened, and, curiously enough, two of the earliest cases in which I have found the phrase used are cases of a depositary and of a borrower. Brooke says that a wrongful taker "has title against all but the true owner." In this sense the special property would have been better described as a "possessory property"—as indeed it was, when it was decided that in an indictment for larceny the property could be laid in the bailee from whom the goods were taken.

I have explained the inversion by which a bailee's right of action against third persons was supposed to rest on his responsibility over to the owner, although in truth that right was the foundation of the responsibility, and arose simply from his possession. The step was short from saying that bailees could sue because they were answerable over, to saying that they had the property as against strangers, or a special property, because they were answerable over; and next to saying that they could sue because they had a special property and were answerable over. And that is how the notion that special property meant something more than possession, and was required in order to bring an action, got into the law.

The error was made easier by a different use of the phrase in a different connection. A bailee was in general answerable for goods stolen from his custody, whether he had a lien or not. But the law was otherwise for a pledgee, if he had kept the pledge with his own goods and the two were stolen together. This distinction was accounted for, at least in Lord Coke's time, by saying that the pledge was in a sense the pledgee's own: that he had a special property in it, so that the ordinary relation of bailment did not exist, or that his undertaking was only to keep it as he kept his own goods. The same expression was used in discussing the pledgee's right to assign the pledge. In this sense the term applied only to pledges. But its meaning in that particular connection was easily carried over into the others in which the phrase was used, with the result that the special property required to maintain the possessory actions was supposed to mean a qualified interest in the goods.

As to the legal consequences of possession, it only remains to mention that the rules laid down for chattels also hold for land. It is true that the plaintiff in ejectment—the action to recover land—must recover on the strength of his own title as against a defendant in possession. But it is now settled that prior possession is enough if the defendant stands on his possession alone. Possession is of course enough for trespass. And although the early remedy by assize was restricted to those who had a technical seisin, this was for reasons which do not affect the general theory.

Before closing I must say a word about ownership and the ideas akin to it. Following the order of analysis used for possession, the first question must be: what are the facts to which the rights called ownership are attached as a legal consequence? The most familiar way of gaining ownership is by conveyance from a previous owner. But that presupposes an ownership already existing, and the problem is to discover what calls it into being.

One fact which has this effect is first possession. The captor of wild animals, or the man who takes fish from the ocean, has not merely possession but a title good against all the world. But the most common way of getting an original and independent title is by certain proceedings, in court or out of it, adverse to all the world. At one extreme is the admiralty's proceeding in rem—against the thing itself—which conclusively disposes of the property in its power. When the court sells or condemns the property, it does not deal with this man's title or that one's; it gives a new title paramount to all previous interests, whatever they may be. The other and more familiar case is prescription, where an open, adverse holding continued for a certain time has a similar effect. A title by prescription is not a presumed conveyance from this or that owner alone; it extinguishes all previous and inconsistent claims. The two meet in the ancient fine with proclamations—a collusive lawsuit publicly proclaimed—where the combined effect of the judgment and the lapse of a year and a day was to bar all claims.

So, too, the legislature may give rights like those of ownership to persons of whom some other set of facts is true. A patentee is an example: one to whom the government has issued a certain instrument, and who has in fact made a patentable invention.

But what are the rights of ownership? They are substantially the same as those incident to possession. Within the limits set by policy, the owner is allowed to exercise his natural powers over the thing without interference, and is more or less protected in excluding other people from interfering. The owner is allowed to exclude all, and is accountable to no one. The possessor is allowed to exclude all but one, and is accountable to no one but him. The great body of questions which have made the subject of property so large and important are questions of conveyancing, not necessarily or generally dependent on ownership as distinguished from possession. They are questions about the effect of having no independent and original title, but coming in under a title already in existence, or about the ways in which an original title can be cut up among those who come in under it. These questions will be dealt with and explained where they belong, in the Lectures on Successions, Lectures X and XI.

Lecture VII: Contract—I. History

The doctrine of contract has been so thoroughly remodelled to meet the needs of modern times that there is less need here than elsewhere for historical research. It has been so ably discussed that there is less room here than elsewhere for essentially new analysis. But a short account of how the modern doctrines grew up, whether necessary or not, will at least be interesting. And an analysis of their main characteristics cannot be left out, and may show some new features.

It is popularly supposed that the oldest forms of contract known to our law are covenant, the action on a promise made under seal, and debt, the action for a fixed sum owed. They are of early date, no doubt. But there are other contracts still in use which, although they have in some degree put on modern dress, at least raise the question whether they did not appear just as early.

One of these, the promissory oath, is no longer the foundation of any rights in private law. It is still used, but mainly as a solemnity that goes with entering upon a public office. The judge swears that he will do justice according to law; the juryman, that he will find his verdict according to law and the evidence; the newly naturalized citizen, that he will bear true faith and allegiance to the government of his choice.

But there is another contract which plays a more important part. It may sound paradoxical to name the contract of suretyship—the promise of one person to answer for another's debt or default. Suretyship nowadays is only an accessory obligation. It presupposes a principal undertaking by someone else, and so far as the nature of the contract goes, it is just like any other. But, as Laferrière has pointed out, and very likely earlier writers too, the surety of ancient law was the hostage. And the giving of hostages was by no means confined to dealings between nations.

In the old verse romance of Huon of Bordeaux, Huon has killed the son of Charlemagne, and the Emperor requires him, as the price of forgiveness, to perform a series of seeming impossibilities. Huon sets out on the task, leaving twelve of his knights behind as hostages. He comes back successful, but at first the Emperor is made to believe that his orders have been disobeyed. At that Charlemagne cries out, "I summon hither the pledges for Huon. I will hang them, and they shall have no ransom." In the same way, when Huon is to fight a duel to establish the truth or falsehood of a charge against him, each side begins by producing some of his friends as hostages.

When hostages are given for a duel that is to settle the truth of an accusation, the transaction comes very close to giving the same kind of security in the trial of a case in court. That was in fact the usual course of Germanic procedure. It will be remembered that law first appeared as a substitute for the private feuds between families or clans. A defendant who would not peaceably submit to the court's jurisdiction might be put outside the protection of the law, so that any man might kill him on sight. But at first there was no way to secure the compensation the plaintiff was entitled to unless the defendant chose to give security for it.

The English customs that have come down to us are somewhat more advanced, but one of the noticeable features of their procedure is the giving of security at every step. All lawyers will remember a trace of this in the fiction of John Doe and Richard Roe, the imaginary pledges who stood surety for the plaintiff that he would prosecute his action. A more significant example is the rule, repeated in many of the early laws, that a defendant accused of a wrong must either find security or go to prison. This security was the hostage of earlier days. Later, when the actions for punishment and the actions for redress were separated, it became the bail of the criminal law—the persons who answer for a prisoner's appearance in return for his release. And the liability was still conceived of in the same way as when the bail actually put his own body into the power of the party he secured.

One of Charlemagne's additions to the Salic Law speaks of a freeman who has committed himself to the power of another by way of surety. The very phrase is copied in the English laws of Henry I. We have seen what this meant in the story of Huon of Bordeaux. The Mirror of Justices says that King Canute used to judge the mainprisors—the sureties who took a man out of custody on their undertaking for him—in the same way as their principals when the principals failed to appear in court. King Henry I, it says, confined Canute's rule to mainprisors who had consented to the crime.

As late as the reign of Edward III, Shard, an English judge, first states the law as it still is—that bail are a prisoner's keepers, and shall be charged if he escapes—and then observes that some say the bail shall be hanged in his place. That was the law in the parallel case of a jailer. The old notion can still be traced in the form modern writers give for the undertaking of bail in a case of felony. The bail are bound "body for body," and modern law-books find it necessary to explain that this does not make them liable to the principal offender's punishment if he fails to appear, but only to a fine. The contract also differed from our modern ideas in the way it was made. It was simply a solemn admission of liability in the presence of an officer authorized to take it. The bail did not have to sign, and the person bailed did not have to bind himself as a party.

But these peculiarities have been modified or abolished by statute. I have dwelt on the case not so much because it is a special form of contract unlike all others, as because the history of its origin shows one of the first appearances of contract in our law. It can be traced to a gradual growth of faith that the hostage would honorably surrender himself if the occasion arose, and to the consequent relaxing of actual imprisonment. An illustration may be found in the parallel way of dealing with the prisoner himself. His bail, into whose hands his body is supposed to be delivered, have a right to seize him at any time and anywhere, but he is allowed to go at large until he is surrendered. It will be noticed that this form of contract looked to the body of the contracting party as the satisfaction. So did debt as the Roman law of the Twelve Tables dealt with it, and for the same motive, although by a different process.

Debt is another and more popular candidate for the honor of being first. Since the time of Savigny, the first appearance of contract in both Roman and German law has often been traced to a sale that by some accident was left incomplete. The question does not seem to have much philosophical significance. To explain how mankind first learned to promise, we would have to go to metaphysics and find out how it ever came to frame a future tense. The nature of the particular promise that happened to be enforced first in a given system can hardly lead to any truth of general importance. But the history of the action of debt is instructive, if in a humbler way. One must know something about it to understand the enlightened rules that make up the law of contract today.

In Glanvill's treatise the action of debt is already one of the well-known remedies. But the law of those days was still in a somewhat primitive state, and it is easy to imagine that a form of action going back that far was not founded on any very delicate distinctions. It was, as I shall try to show directly, simply the general form in which any money claim was collected. The one exception was unliquidated claims—claims whose amount had not been fixed—for damage done by force, and for those there was the equally general remedy of trespass.

Some have thought the action was adopted from the procedure of the Roman law, which was then more civilized. It is a natural opinion, since all the early English law-writers take their phrasing and their classifications from Rome. Still, it seems much more probable that the action is of pure German descent. It has the features of the primitive procedure found on the Continent, as Laband describes it.

The substance of the plaintiff's claim, as set out in the writ of debt, is that the defendant owes him so much and wrongfully withholds it. For a claim framed like that, it does not matter how the defendant's duty arises. It is not confined to contract. It is satisfied if there is a duty to pay on any ground at all. It states a bare conclusion of law, not the facts from which the conclusion follows and the liability arises. The old German complaint was the same: "A owes me so much."

It was characteristic of German procedure that the defendant could meet that complaint by answering, in an equally general form, that he did not owe the plaintiff anything. If the plaintiff wanted to keep the defendant from escaping in that way, he had to do more than simply allege a debt. In England, if the plaintiff had nothing to show for his debt, the defendant's denial put him out of court. And even if he had something, he could still be defeated by the defendant's swearing, with some of his friends to back him, that he owed nothing. The chief reason why debt was pushed aside for centuries by a later remedy, assumpsit, was the survival of this relic of early days.

Finally, in England as in Germany, debt for withholding money was the twin brother of the action for wrongfully withholding any other kind of chattel. In either case the gist of the complaint was the same.

It seems strange that this crude product of the infancy of law should matter to us today. Yet whenever we trace a leading doctrine of substantive law far enough back, we are very likely to find some forgotten circumstance of procedure at its source. Illustrations of this truth have been given already. The action of debt and the other actions of contract will furnish more. Debt throws the most light on the doctrine of consideration.

Our law does not enforce every promise a man may make. Promises made as ninety-nine out of a hundred are made—by word of mouth or by simple writing—are not binding unless there is a consideration for them. That is, as it is commonly explained, unless the promisee has either conferred a benefit on the promisor or incurred a detriment as the inducement to the promise.

It has been thought that this rule was borrowed from the Roman law by the Chancery, and that after some modification there it passed into the common law.

But this account of the matter is at least questionable. So far as the words go, I do not know of consideration being plainly called "cause" before the reign of Elizabeth; in the earlier reports it always appears as quid pro quo. Its first appearance, so far as I know, is in Fleta's account of the action of debt. I am inclined to think Fleta's statement is not to be trusted. But a careful look at the cases in the Year Books, in their chronological order, will show, I think, that the doctrine was fully developed in debt before any mention of it in equity can be found. One of the earliest references to what a promisor was to get for his undertaking was in the action of assumpsit. But the doctrine certainly did not start there. The first mention of consideration in connection with equity that I have seen takes the form of quid pro quo, and it comes after the requirement had been thoroughly established in debt.

Footnote: Glanvill's phrase "justa debendi causa," a just cause of owing, seems to me remote from consideration.

Consideration was never required for contracts under seal—unless Fleta is to be trusted against the great weight of nearly contemporary evidence. That single fact goes far to show that the rule cannot have begun as a rule of substantive law, adopted on grounds of policy. And the other way round, the fact that the doctrine coincides with a peculiar mode of procedure points very strongly to the likelihood that the peculiar requirement and the peculiar procedure were connected. It will throw light on the question to put together a few undisputed facts and consider what consequences naturally followed from them. So it will be worth examining the action of debt a little further. But it is only fair to admit at the outset that I offer the explanation which follows with great hesitation, and, I think, with a full appreciation of the objections that might be raised against it.

I said a moment ago that, to recover against a defendant who denied his debt, the plaintiff had to show something for it. Otherwise he was turned over to the limited jurisdiction of the spiritual courts, the courts of the Church. This requirement did not mean evidence in the modern sense. It meant simply that he must maintain his case in one of the ways the law then recognized. There were three: the duel, a writing, and witnesses. The duel need not be discussed; it soon ceased to be used in debt, and it has no bearing on what I have to say. Trial by writing and trial by witnesses, on the other hand, must both be carefully studied. It will be convenient to take the witnesses first and find out what they were.

One thing we know from the start: they were not witnesses as we understand the word. They were not brought before a jury to be examined and cross-examined, and their testimony did not depend for its effect on being believed by the court that heard it. Nowadays a case is not decided by the evidence, but by a verdict, or a finding of facts, followed by a judgment. The oath of a witness has no effect unless it is believed. But in the time of Henry II our trial by jury did not exist. When an oath was allowed to be sworn, it had the same effect whether it was believed or not. There was no second body to sift it. In the cases where trial by witnesses was possible, if the party called on to go forward could find a certain number of men willing to swear in a certain form, that was the end of the matter.

Now this seems a more primitive way of proving a debt than producing the defendant's written acknowledgment, and it is important to discover where it came from.

The early books and reports show that the cases in which this mode of trial was used were almost wholly confined to claims arising out of a sale or a loan. And the question at once arises whether we are not on the traces of an institution that was already ancient when Glanvill wrote. For centuries before the Conquest, Anglo-Saxon law had required the choosing of a certain number of official witnesses, two or three of whom were to be called in to every bargain of sale. The purpose of these witnesses is not commonly supposed to have been the proof of debts. They go back to a time when theft and similar offenses were the chief ground of lawsuits, and they were appointed to give a means of deciding whether a person charged with holding stolen property had come by it rightfully or not. A defendant could clear himself of the felony by their oath that he had bought or received the thing openly, in the way the law appointed.

Having been present at the bargain, the witnesses were able to swear to what they had seen and heard if any question arose between the parties. So their use was not confined to disposing of a charge of felony. But that particular service is what identifies the transaction witnesses of the Saxon period. Now we know that these witnesses did not disappear at once under Norman influence. They are found with their old function in the laws of William the Conqueror. And the language of Glanvill seems to prove that they were still known under Henry II. He says that a purchaser may be able to summon in the man he bought from, to warrant the property to him and defend the suit—in which case the danger shifts to the seller. But if he cannot, then if he has sufficient proof that he lawfully bought the thing, de legittimo marcatu suo, "of his lawful market," it will clear him of felony. If he has not sufficient suit, he will be in danger. This is the law of William over again. It follows that purchasers still used the transaction witnesses.

Footnote: Suit, secta, was the term for the persons whose oath a party offered in support of his case.

But Glanvill also seems to allow the use of witnesses to prove debts. Since the transaction witnesses had formerly served this purpose, I see no reason to doubt that they still did, and that he is speaking of them here as well. Moreover, for a long time after Henry II, whenever an action was brought for a debt of which there was no written evidence, the plaintiff, when asked what he had to show for it, always answered "good suit" and offered his witnesses, who were sometimes examined by the court. I think it is not straining the evidence to infer that the "good suit" of the later reports was the descendant of the Saxon transaction witnesses, just as Glanvill's secta has been shown to be.

Footnote: It was no doubt true, as Glanvill says, that the usual mode of proof was by a writing or by duel, and that the King's Court did not generally give protection to private agreements made anywhere but in the King's Court itself. But it can hardly be that debts were never proved by witnesses in his time, in view of the continuous evidence from Bracton onward.

Footnote: I do not go so far as to say that the transaction witnesses were still a living institution. However that may be, tradition must at least have modelled itself on what had been the function of the former official body.

With this step in the argument taken, it will be well to recall once more the original nature of the witness oath. It was confined to facts the witnesses knew by sight and hearing. And since the purposes for which witnesses were provided required their presence only when property changed hands, the main case in which they could be of use between the parties to a bargain was when a debt was claimed because property had been delivered. The purpose did not reach agreements that were executory on both sides—where neither side had yet done anything—because there no question of theft could arise. And Glanvill shows that in his time the King's Court did not enforce such agreements. Now, if the oath of the secta could only be used to prove a debt where the transaction witnesses could have sworn, it is easy enough to see how an accident of procedure may have led to a most important rule of substantive law.

Footnote: It is possible that Glanvill means no more here than his often repeated statement that the King's Court did not, generally speaking, take notice of private agreements. The substantive law was perhaps still limited by traditions from the infancy of contract, as discussed elsewhere in this Lecture. The proposition in its broadest form may have rested on the impossibility of trying such agreements in any way but those which have been named.

Put the pieces together. Witnesses could only swear to facts within their knowledge. By accident, these witnesses were not used in transactions that might create a debt, except for one particular fact, the delivery of property. And by a further accident, this delivery was quid pro quo. Taken together, these came to the same thing as a rule that when a debt was proved by witnesses, there must be quid pro quo. But debts proved by witnesses instead of by deed are what we call simple contract debts—debts resting on a promise not under seal. And so, beginning with debt and later spreading to other contracts, our peculiar and most important doctrine is established: that every simple contract must have a consideration. This was never the law for debts or contracts proved in the usual way, by the defendant's seal. And the fact that it applied only to obligations which were formerly proved by a procedure of limited use goes far to show that its connection with procedure was not accidental.

The mode of proof soon changed, but as late as the reign of Queen Elizabeth we find a trace of this original connection. It is said, "But the common law requires that there should be a new cause (i. e. consideration), whereof the country may have intelligence or knowledge for the trial of it, if need be, so that it is necessary for the Public-weal." The "country" there is the jury, who must be able to know of the cause if the case is tried. Lord Mansfield showed his intuition of the historical grounds of our law when he said, "I take it that the ancient notion about the want of consideration was for the sake of evidence only; for when it is reduced into writing, as in covenants, specialties, bonds, etc., there was no objection to the want of consideration."

It may be objected that the argument so far is necessarily confined to debt, whereas the requirement of consideration applies equally to all simple contracts. The answer is that in all probability the rule began with debt and spread from debt to other contracts.

But it may be asked again whether there were no contracts proved by witnesses besides those already mentioned. Were there none proved in that way which lacked the accidental consideration? To this, too, there is an easy answer. The contracts enforced by the civil courts, even as late as Henry II, were few and simple. The witness procedure was no doubt broad enough for all the contracts made in early times. Besides sale, loan, and the like, which have been mentioned, I find only two contractual obligations. These were the warranties that went with a sale, and the suretyship referred to at the beginning of the Lecture. Of the first, the warranty of title was regarded rather as an obligation the law raised out of the relation of buyer and seller than as a contract. Other express warranties were matters within the knowledge of the transaction witnesses, and in Saxon times the witnesses swore to them.

But in the Norman period warranty is very little heard of except with regard to land, and then it was decided by the duel. Apart from warranties set down in a deed, it disappeared so completely that it can have had no influence on the law of consideration. I shall therefore assume, without more detail, that it does not bear on the question.

Then there is the pledge, or surety. He no longer paid with his body, except in very rare cases. His liability was translated into money and enforced in an action of debt. This time-honored contract, like the other debts of Glanvill's time, could be proved by witnesses without a writing. And here there was no consideration of the kind the law required when the doctrine was first stated—no benefit to the promisor. But this too is unimportant, because the surety's liability on the oath of witnesses came to an end, as did the warrantor's, before the foundations were laid for the rule I am trying to explain. A writing soon came to be required, as will be seen in a moment.

The result so far is this. The only action of contract in Glanvill's time was debt. The only debts recovered without a writing were those that have been described. And the only one of these for which there was no quid pro quo had ceased to be recoverable in that way by the reign of Edward III.

But great changes were beginning in the reign of Henry II. More varied and complex contracts soon came to be enforced. It may be asked why the scope of the witness oath was not enlarged. Or, if some better proof was to be had, why the secta was not done away with and other oral testimony admitted. And in any case, what can the law of Henry II's time have to do with consideration, which was not heard of until centuries later?

It is plain that a witness oath, which settles a case by the bare fact that it is sworn, is not a satisfactory mode of proof. A written admission of debt, produced in court and sufficiently identified as coming from the defendant, is obviously much better. The only weak point about a writing is the means of identifying it as the defendant's, and that difficulty disappeared as soon as the use of seals became common. This had more or less happened by Glanvill's time. After that, all a party had to do was to produce the writing and satisfy the court, by looking at it, that the impression in the wax fitted his opponent's seal. The oath of the secta, the plaintiff's band of witnesses, could always be met successfully by wager of law—that is, by a counter-oath from the defendant, backed by the same number of fellow-swearers the plaintiff had brought, or double that number. But a writing proved to be the defendant's could not be contradicted. For if a man said he was bound, he was bound. There was no question of consideration, because there was as yet no such doctrine. He was equally bound if he acknowledged the obligation in any place that kept a record, such as the superior courts, by which his acknowledgment could be proved. Indeed, to this day some securities are taken simply by an oral admission made before the clerk of a court and noted by him in his papers. The advantage of the writing was not only that it furnished better proof in the old cases. It also made it possible to enforce obligations for which there would otherwise have been no proof at all.

Footnote: The favorite proof by duel was also allowed, but it disappeared. When the inquest became general, the question whether the deed had really been executed was tried by that means, like any other fact.

What has been said sufficiently explains why proof by writing was preferred to proof by the old-fashioned witness oath. But there were other reasons, equally good, why the witness oath should not be extended beyond its ancient limits. The transaction witnesses were losing their statutory and official character. Already in Glanvill's time the usual ways of proving a debt were by the duel or by writing. A hundred years later Bracton shows that the secta had sunk to the retainers and household of the party, and he says that their oath raises only a slight presumption.

Moreover, a new mode of trial was growing up. It was not used in these cases for a good while, but by contrast it must have tended to lower the value set on the witness oath. This was the beginning of our trial by jury. At first it was an inquest of the neighbors most likely to know about a disputed matter of fact. They spoke from their own knowledge, but they were chosen by an officer of the court instead of by the interested party, and they were meant to be impartial. Soon witnesses were summoned before them—not, as of old, to settle the case by their oath, but to help the inquest reach a verdict by their testimony. With the arrival of this enlightened procedure, the secta soon ceased to decide the case, and it may well be asked why it did not disappear altogether and leave no trace.

Take into account the conservatism of the English law, and the fact that, before deeds came in, the only debts for which there had been a remedy were debts proved by the transaction witnesses. It would not have been surprising to find the tender of suit—the offering of the secta—persisting in those cases. But there was another reason, more compelling still. Where there was no deed, the defence in debt was by wager of law. A section of Magna Carta was interpreted to forbid putting a man to his law on the plaintiff's own statement without good witness. So the statute required witness—that is, the secta—in every case of debt where the plaintiff did not rely on a writing. Thus it happened that suit went on being tendered in the cases where it had been tendered of old. And since the defendant in such cases, if he did not admit the debt, always waged his law, it was a long time before the inquest got much foothold.

To establish a debt that arose merely by way of promise or acknowledgment, and for which no mode of trial had formerly been provided, you had to have a writing—the new form of proof that had brought such debts into the law in the first place. The rule was laid down, "by parol the party is not obliged"; that is, a man is not bound by a mere spoken word. But the old debts were not thought of as raised by a promise. They were a "duty" springing from the plaintiff's receipt of property—a fact which could be seen and sworn to. In these cases the old law held its ground, and even extended itself a little, by strict analogy.

But the undertaking of a surety, whatever form it was clothed in, did not really arise out of any such fact. It had become of the same nature as other promises, and doubts soon arose whether it ought not to be proved by the same evidence. By the reign of Edward III it was settled that a deed was necessary, except where the customs of particular cities had kept the old law in force.

This reign may be taken as marking the time when the divisions and rules of procedure were fixed that have lasted to the present day. It is therefore worth while to repeat and sum up the condition of the law at that time.

It was still necessary to tender the secta in every action of debt in which no writing was produced. For this reason, as well as for the others already mentioned, the range of such actions was not materially enlarged beyond the cases that had formerly been established by the witness oath. As suretyship was no longer one of these, they became strictly limited to cases in which the debt arose from the receipt of a quid pro quo. Moreover, there was no other action of contract that could be maintained without a writing. New kinds of contract were now enforced by an action of covenant, but there a deed was always necessary. At the same time the secta had shrunk to a form, although it was still argued that its function was more important in contract than elsewhere. It could no longer be examined before the court. It was a mere survival, and the transaction witness had ceased to be an institution. Hence the necessity of tendering the witness oath no longer fixed the limits of debt upon simple contract except by tradition. And it is not surprising to find that the action was slightly extended by analogy beyond its scope in Glanvill's time.

But debt stayed substantially at the point I have indicated, and no new action available for simple contracts was introduced for a century. In the meantime the inversion I have explained took place, and what had been an accident of procedure became a doctrine of substantive law. The change was easy, when the debts that could be enforced without a deed all sprang from a benefit to the debtor.

The influence of the Roman law no doubt helped to bring this result about. It will be remembered that in the reign of Henry II most simple contracts and debts for which there was no evidence of deed or witness were left to the ecclesiastical courts to enforce, so far as their jurisdiction reached. Perhaps it was this circumstance that led Glanvill and his successors to apply the terminology of the civilians to common-law debts. But whether he borrowed it from the church courts or went straight to the fountainhead, it is certain that Glanvill uses the classification and technical language of the Corpus Juris, the great body of Justinian's Roman law, throughout his tenth book.

The Roman system had certain special contracts called real contracts. These bound the contractor either to return a particular thing put into his hands by the other party, as in a lease or a loan, or to deliver other articles of the same kind, as when grain, oil, or money was lent. This class corresponded with the common-law debts only in the most superficial way. But Glanvill adopted the names, and later writers began to draw conclusions from them. The author of Fleta, a writer by no means always intelligent in following and adopting his predecessors' use of the Roman law, says that to raise a debt there must be not only a certain thing promised, but a certain thing promised in return.

If Fleta had confined his statement to debts by simple contract, it might well have been suggested by the existing state of the law. But since he also required a writing and a seal, on top of the thing given or promised in return, the doctrine he laid down can hardly have prevailed at any time. It was probably nothing more than a slight vagary of reasoning, built on the Roman elements he borrowed from Bracton.

It only remains to trace the gradual appearance of consideration in the decisions. A case of the reign of Edward III seems to distinguish between a parol obligation—one not under seal—founded on payments the obligee made of his own accord, and one founded on a payment made at the obligor's request. It also speaks of the debt, or "duty," in that case as arising by cause of the payments. Somewhat similar language is used in the next reign. So, in the twelfth year of Henry IV, there is an approach to the idea: "If money is promised to a man for making a release, and he makes the release, he will have a good action of debt in the matter." In the next reign it was decided that in such a case the plaintiff could not recover without having executed the release. The editor explains this on the ground that ex nudo pacto non oritur actio—from a bare promise no action arises. But the most important fact is that from Edward I to Henry VI we find no case in which a debt was recovered unless a consideration had in fact been received.

Another fact to notice is that from Edward III on, debts arising from a transaction without writing are said to arise from contract, as distinguished from debts arising from an obligation—a sealed bond. Hence, when consideration came to be required as such, it was required in all contracts not under seal, whether they were debts or not. Under Henry VI quid pro quo became a necessity in all such contracts. In the third year of that reign it was objected to an action of assumpsit for not building a mill that the plaintiff had not shown what the defendant was to have for building it. In the thirty-sixth year of the same reign (A.D. 1459) the doctrine appears full grown, and is assumed to be familiar.

That case turned on a question that was debated for centuries before it was settled: whether debt would lie for a sum of money the defendant had promised the plaintiff if he would marry the defendant's daughter. Formerly the debate had been whether the promise was so closely bound up with the marriage that it belonged exclusively to the spiritual courts. Now it turned on the purely worldly doubt whether the defendant had had quid pro quo.

It will be remembered that the fact formerly sworn to by the transaction witnesses was a benefit to the defendant—namely, the delivery to him of the things sold or the money lent. Such cases also offer the most obvious form of consideration. The natural question is, what was the promisor to get for his promise? Only by analysis does one see that the supposed policy of the law is equally satisfied by a detriment incurred by the promisee. So it happened, not unnaturally, that when the judges first laid down the law that there must be quid pro quo, they were slow to recognize a detriment to the promisee as meeting the requirement. In the case I have mentioned, some of the judges were inclined to hold that getting rid of his daughter was a benefit to the defendant sufficient to make him a debtor for the money he had promised. There was even some hint of the opinion that marrying the lady was a consideration because it was a detriment to the promisee. But the other opinion prevailed, at least for a time, because the defendant had had nothing from the plaintiff to raise a debt.

So it was held that a service rendered to a third person, at the defendant's request and on his promise of a reward, would not be enough—though not without strong opinions to the contrary—and for a time the precedents were settled. It became established law that an action of debt would lie only upon a consideration actually received by the debtor and enuring to his benefit.

It was, however, no peculiarity of either the action or the contract of debt that led to this view. It was the imperfectly developed theory of consideration that prevailed between the reigns of Henry VI and Elizabeth. The theory was the same in assumpsit and in equity. Wherever consideration was mentioned, it was always as quid pro quo—as what the contractor was to have for his contract.

Moreover, before consideration was ever heard of, debt was the time-honored remedy on every obligation to pay money that the law enforced, except the liability to pay damages for a wrong. It has been shown already that until the time of Edward III a surety could be sued in debt without a writing. Yet a surety receives no benefit from the dealing with his principal. For instance, if a man sells corn to A, and B says, "I will pay if A does not," the sale does B no good, so far as the terms of the bargain show. For that reason debt cannot now be maintained against a surety in such a case.

It was not always so. It is not so to this day if there is an obligation under seal. In that case it does not matter how the obligation arose, or whether there was any consideration for it or not. But in Glanvill's time a writing was a more general way of establishing a debt than witness, and it is absurd to fix the scope of the action by looking at only one class of the debts it enforced. Moreover, for a long time a writing was only another mode of proof, though a more conclusive one. The foundation of the action was the same however it was proved. It was a duty, or "duity," to the plaintiff—in other words, that money was due him, no matter how—as anyone may see by reading the earlier Year Books. That is why debt lay equally upon a judgment, which established such a duty by matter of record, or upon the defendant's admission recorded in the same way.

To sum up, the action of debt has passed through three stages. At first it was the only remedy to recover money due, except where the liability was simply to pay damages for a wrongful act. It was closely akin to—indeed it was only a branch of—the action for any form of personal property which the defendant was bound, by contract or otherwise, to hand over to the plaintiff. If there was a contract to pay money, the only question was how you could prove it. Any such contract that could be proved by any of the means known to early law constituted a debt. There was no theory of consideration, and therefore, of course, no limit on either the action or the contract based on the nature of the consideration received.

The second stage came when the doctrine of consideration was introduced in its earlier form, as a benefit to the promisor. While that form prevailed it applied to all contracts not under seal. But it was established at a time when debt was the only action for money payable under such contracts, and so the precedents are, for the most part, precedents in debt.

The third stage was reached when a larger view was taken of consideration, and it was expressed in terms of detriment to the promisee. This change was a change in substantive law, and logically it should have been applied throughout. But it arose in another and later form of action, under circumstances peculiarly connected with that action, as will be explained below. The result was that the new doctrine prevailed in the new action and the old in the old. What was really the anomaly of two inconsistent theories carried out side by side disguised itself as a limitation on the action of debt. That action did not remain, as before, the remedy for all binding contracts to pay money. So far as parol contracts were concerned, it could be used only where the consideration was a benefit actually received by the promisor. With regard to obligations arising in any other way, it has remained unchanged.

I must now say a few words about the effect on our law of the other mode of proof I have mentioned. I mean charters. A charter was simply a writing. Since few people could write, most had to authenticate a document in some other way—for instance, by making their mark. This was in fact the universal practice in England until Norman customs were introduced. Seals came in with them. But as late as Henry II the Chief Justice of England said that seals belonged properly only to kings and to very great men. I know of no ground for thinking that an authentic charter had any less effect at that time when it was not under seal than when it was sealed. It was only evidence either way, and it is called evidence in many of the early cases. It could be waived, and suit tendered in its place. Its conclusive effect came from the satisfactory nature of the evidence, not from the seal.

But when seals came into use, they obviously made the evidence of the charter better, in so far as a seal was harder to forge than a stroke of the pen. Seals acquired such importance that for a time a man was bound by his seal even though it had been affixed without his consent. At last a seal came to be required if a charter was to have its ancient effect.

A covenant, or contract under seal, was no longer a promise well proved. It was a promise of a distinct nature, for which a distinct form of action came to be provided. I have shown how the requirement of consideration became a rule of substantive law, and also why it never had any foothold in the domain of covenants. The exemption of covenants from the requirement became a rule of substantive law as well. The man who had set his hand to a charter had been bound because he had consented to be bound and because there was a writing to prove it. Now he was held by force of the seal, and by the deed alone as distinguished from all other writings. And to keep an inadequate theory intact, a seal was said to import a consideration.

Nowadays it is sometimes thought more philosophical to say that a covenant is a formal contract, which survives alongside the ordinary contract made by mere agreement, just as happened in the Roman law. But this is not a very instructive way of putting it either. In one sense, everything is form that the law requires, over and above the mere expression of the promisor's will, in order to make a promise binding. Consideration is a form as much as a seal. The only difference is that one form is of modern introduction and has a foundation in good sense, or at least in our common habits of thought, so that we do not notice it; the other is a survival from an older condition of the law, and is less plainly sensible, or less familiar. I may add that under the influence of this last consideration the law of covenants is breaking down. In many States it is held that a mere scroll or flourish of the pen is a sufficient seal. From there it is a short step to abolish the distinction between sealed and unsealed instruments altogether, and this has been done in some of the Western States.

While covenants survive in a somewhat feeble old age, and debt has disappeared, leaving a vaguely disturbing influence behind it, the whole modern law of contract has grown up through the medium of the action of assumpsit, which must now be explained.

After the Norman Conquest all ordinary actions were begun by a writ issued in the king's name, ordering the defendant to be summoned before the court to answer the plaintiff. These writs issued as a matter of course in the various well-known actions that took their names from them. There were writs of debt and of covenant; there were writs of trespass for forcible injuries to the plaintiff's person, or to property in his possession; and so on. But writs were issued only for the actions the law knew, and without a writ the court had no authority to try a case. In the time of Edward I there were only a few such actions. The cases in which you could recover money from another fell into a small number of groups, and each group had its own particular form of suing and stating your claim.

These forms had ceased to be adequate. There were many cases, for instance, that did not quite fall within the definition of a trespass, but for which a remedy ought to be furnished. To furnish a remedy, the first thing to be done was to furnish a writ. Accordingly, the famous statute of 13 Edward I, chapter 24, authorized the office from which the old writs issued to frame new ones in cases that were like those covered by existing writs in principle and called for a like remedy, but did not fall exactly within the scope of the writs already in use.

So writs of trespass on the case began to appear—that is, writs stating a ground of complaint analogous to a trespass, but not quite amounting to a trespass as it had been sued for in the older precedents. Take an instance that is substantially one of the earliest cases. Suppose a man left a horse with a blacksmith to be shod, and the smith negligently drove a nail into the horse's foot. The owner of the horse might not be able to have one of the old writs, because the horse was not in his possession when the damage was done. A strict trespass to property could be committed only against the person in possession of it, and it could not be committed by one who was in possession himself. But laming the horse was equally a wrong whether the owner held the horse by the bridle or left it with the smith; and the wrong was closely analogous to a trespass, though it was not one. So the law gave the owner a writ of trespass on the case.

An example like this raises no difficulty. It is as much an action of tort for a wrong as trespass itself. No contract was stated, and on principle none was necessary. But it does not belong to the class of cases we have to consider. The problem before us is to trace the origin of assumpsit, which is an action of contract. Assumpsit, however, began as an action of trespass on the case, and what has to be discovered is how trespass on the case ever came to be available for a mere breach of agreement.

It will be well to examine some of the earliest cases in which an undertaking (in Latin, assumpsit, "he undertook") was alleged. The first one reported in the books comes from the reign of Edward III. The plaintiff alleged that the defendant had undertaken to carry the plaintiff's horse safely across the Humber, but had overloaded the boat, and because of that the horse perished. It was objected that the action should have been either covenant, for breach of the agreement, or else trespass. But the answer was that the defendant committed a wrongful act when he overloaded the boat, and the objection was overruled. So this case, although an undertaking was stated, hardly introduced a new principle. The force did not come directly from the defendant, to be sure. But it was brought to bear by the combination of his overloading the boat and then pushing it out into the stream.

The next case is from the same reign, and it goes further. The writ set out that the defendant undertook to cure the plaintiff's horse of a sickness, and did his work so negligently that the horse died. This differs in two ways from the case of laming the horse with a nail. It does not charge any forcible act—indeed, it charges no act at all, only an omission. On the other hand, it states an undertaking, which the other case did not. The defendant at once objected that this was an action for breach of an undertaking, and that the plaintiff should have brought covenant. The plaintiff replied that he could not bring covenant without a deed, and that his action was for negligently causing the death of the horse—that is, for a tort, not for a breach of contract. Then, said the defendant, you could have had trespass. The plaintiff answered that the horse was not killed by force, but died through the defendant's failure in his cure. On this argument the writ was held good, and Justice Thorpe said that he had seen a man indicted for killing, by want of care, a patient whom he had undertaken to cure.

Both these cases, you will see, were treated by the court as pure actions of tort, even though an undertaking on the defendant's part was alleged. But you will also see that each is further than the last from an ordinary case of trespass. In the second case especially, the force that destroyed the horse did not come from the defendant in any sense. And so we are faced with the question: What possible analogy could have been found between a wrongful act that produces harm and a failure to act at all?

Before I attempt an answer, let me illustrate a little further with examples of somewhat later date. Suppose a man undertook to work on another's house, and by his lack of skill spoiled his employer's timbers. That would be like a trespass, though not one, and the employer would sue in trespass on the case. One of the judges stated this as clear law in the reign of Henry IV. But suppose that, instead of spoiling the materials directly, the carpenter had simply left a hole in the roof, and the rain had come in through it and done the damage. The likeness to the first case is marked, but we are a step farther from trespass, because the force does not come from the defendant. Yet here too the judges thought that trespass on the case would lie. In the time of Henry IV the action could not have been brought for a simple refusal to build as agreed. But the court suggested that if the writ had said "that the thing had been commenced and then by not done, it would have been otherwise."

I come back now to the question: What likeness could there have been between an omission and a trespass, close enough to justify a writ of trespass on the case? To find the answer, we must notice that in all the earlier cases the omission happened in the course of dealing with the plaintiff's person or property, and caused damage to one or the other. In view of this, two things are full of meaning: Thorpe's reference to indictments for killing a patient by want of care, and the later distinction between neglect before the task is begun and neglect after. The first is all the more suggestive when we remember that it is the earliest argument or analogy to be found on the subject.

The meaning of that analogy is plain. A man has a perfect right to stand by and watch his neighbor's property destroyed—or, for that matter, to watch his neighbor die for want of his help. But once he meddles, he no longer has the same freedom. He cannot withdraw at will. To take a more specific example: if a surgeon, out of kindness, cuts the umbilical cord of a newborn child, he cannot stop there and watch the baby bleed to death. To let death come about in that way on purpose would be murder, just as much as if he had meant it when he cut the cord. It would not matter whether the wickedness began with the act or with the omission that followed.

The same reasoning applies to civil liability. A carpenter need not go to work on another man's house at all. But if he accepts the other's confidence and takes the work in hand, he cannot stop when he pleases and leave the roof open to the weather. So with the farrier: once he had taken charge of the horse, he could not stop at the critical moment and leave the consequences to chance. And still more clearly with the ferryman who undertook to carry a horse across the Humber. It was the water that drowned the horse. But his earlier acts, overloading his boat and pushing it into the stream in that condition, brought about the loss, and he was answerable for it.

In these cases the duty did not depend on contract, or at least the judges who decided them did not think it did. It rested on the general rules that apply to human conduct, rules that even the criminal law applies. The immediate cause of the damage may have been a mere omission, which let natural forces do their work. But connect that omission, as in fact it was connected, with what the defendant had done before, and you have a course of action and conduct which, taken as a whole, has caused the harm.

It may be objected, to be sure, that there is a considerable step between two things. One is holding a man liable for the consequences of his own acts, consequences he might have prevented. The other is making him answerable for not interfering with the course of nature, when he neither set it going nor opened the door for it to do harm. And it may be said that exactly this difference separates making a hole in a roof and leaving it open, or cutting the cord and letting the child bleed, on one side, from the farrier who takes in a sick horse and fails to take proper precautions, on the other.

There seem to be two answers to this. First, it is not clear that the court which decided the farrier's case ever turned its mind to such a distinction. The allegation was that the defendant carried out his cure so negligently that the horse died. It may not have occurred to the judges that the defendant's conduct possibly went no further than leaving out a series of helpful measures. They probably assumed that it was a combination of acts and neglects which, taken together, amounted to an improper dealing with the thing.

Second, it is doubtful whether the distinction is sound on practical grounds. It may well be that so long as a man allows a trust to be placed in him, he is bound to use the precautions he knows of. This would be so even though he has made no contract, and is free to give up the trust in any reasonable way. The view gets some support from the issue on which the parties went to trial. It was that the defendant performed the cure as well as he knew how, and denied that the horse died for default of his care (cure?).

But it cannot be denied that the allegation of an undertaking carried the idea of a promise, as well as the idea of entering upon the business in hand. Indeed, the second idea is perhaps sufficiently conveyed without it. So it may be asked whether the promise did not count for something in raising a duty to act. If that means that the action was really for breach of a contract, the answer has already been given, and too great a weight of authority supports it for it to be doubted. To bind the defendant by a contract, an instrument under seal was essential. As has already been shown, even the ancient territory of debt had been narrowed by this requirement, and in the time of Edward III a deed was needed even to bind a surety—a person who answers for another's debt. It was all the more necessary, then, for bringing in a liability on promises that the ancient law did not enforce. Nevertheless, the suggestion was made at an early date that an action on the case for damage by negligence—that is, by leaving out proper precautions—which alleged an undertaking by way of inducement, was in fact an action of contract.

Five years after the action for negligence in curing a horse, an action was brought in similar form against a surgeon. It alleged that he undertook to cure the plaintiff's hand, and that by his negligence the hand was maimed. There was one difference, however: the writ set out that the plaintiff's hand had been wounded by a certain T. B. So it appeared that, however much the bad treatment may have made matters worse, the maiming was properly to be laid at T. B.'s door, and the plaintiff had an action against him. This may be what led the defendant to take the course he did, because he was unsure whether any action of tort would lie. He took issue on the undertaking, assuming it to be essential to the plaintiff's case. Then he objected that the writ did not show where the undertaking was made, and so was bad, because it did not show from what place the inquest should be summoned to speak to that point. The writ was held bad on that ground, which looks as if the court accepted the defendant's view. Indeed, one of the judges called it an action of covenant, and said that "of necessity it was maintainable without specialty"—that is, without a sealed writing—"because for so small a matter a man cannot always have a clerk at hand to write a deed." At the same time, the earlier cases I have mentioned were cited and relied on, and it is evident that the court was not prepared to go beyond them, or to hold that the action could be maintained on its merits apart from the technical objection. In another connection it seems to have looked at the action from the point of view of trespass.

Footnote: Compare the reasons Justice Candish gave for allowing wager of law with the old rules of pleading printed at the end of the medieval tract on holding a hundred court or a court of record.

Whatever questions this case may raise, the class of actions alleging an undertaking by the defendant went on being treated as actions of tort for a long time after Edward III. Liability was limited to damage to person or property that arose after the defendant had entered upon the employment. And it was mainly through reasoning drawn from the law of tort that the liability was later extended, as we shall see.

At the beginning of the reign of Henry VI it was probably still the law that the action would not lie for a simple failure to keep a promise. But, as has been shown, it had been suggested several times that it would be otherwise if the omission or neglect happened in the course of performance and the defendant's conduct had been followed by physical damage. This suggestion took its most striking form in the early years of Henry VI, when the case of the carpenter who left a hole in the roof was put. Once the courts had got that far, it was easy to go one step farther, and give the same effect to an omission at any stage, followed by similar damage.

A few years later the question was asked: What is the difference in principle between the cases where the action is admitted to lie, and the case of a smith who undertakes to shoe a horse and does not, so that the horse goes lame? Or the case of a lawyer who undertakes to argue your case, and then, having led you to rely on him, fails to appear, so that you lose it? It was said that in the earlier cases the duty depended on the covenant, as an accessory to it, and that if the action would lie on the accessory matter, it would lie on the principal. On demurrer it was held that an action would lie for failing to procure certain releases that the defendant had undertaken to get.

Five years later another case came up, very like the farrier's case in the reign of Edward III. It was alleged that the defendant undertook to cure the plaintiff's horse, and applied medicine so negligently that the horse died. Here, as in the earlier case, issue was taken on the assumpsit. But now the difference between an omission and an act was clearly stated. The declaration was held not necessarily to mean anything more than an omission, and it was said that, but for the undertaking, the defendant would have owed no duty to act. So the allegation of the defendant's promise was material, and an issue could properly be taken on it.

This decision plainly separated out, from the mass of actions on the case, a special class in which the source of the defendant's obligation was a promise. It was only a matter of time before that class became a new and distinct action of contract. Had the change happened at once, the doctrine of consideration, which was first definitely stated at about the same time, would no doubt have been applied, and a quid pro quo would have been required for the undertaking. But the notion of tort was not at once given up. At the beginning of the reign of Henry VII the law was laid down in line with the earlier decisions: the action would not lie for a failure to keep a promise, but only for negligence after the defendant had entered upon his undertaking.

So long as the action stayed within the true limits of tort, it made no difference whether there was a consideration for the undertaking or not. But then the mistake was made of supposing that all cases in which an assumpsit was alleged, whether they were proper torts or not, rested equally on the promise. From that mistake one of two wrong conclusions naturally seemed to follow. Either no assumpsit needed any quid pro quo, since there was clearly none in the older precedents (they being cases of pure tort), or else those precedents were wrong, and a quid pro quo ought to be alleged in every case. It was long recognized, with more or less understanding of the true limit, that where the heart of the action was negligent damage to property, no consideration was necessary. And there are traces of the notion that consideration was always unnecessary as late as the reign of Charles I.

In a case of that reign, the defendant hired an attorney to act in a lawsuit for a third person, and promised to pay him all his fees and expenses. The attorney did the work and then brought debt. It was objected that debt would not lie, because there was no contract between the parties and the defendant had received no quid pro quo. The court accepted the argument. It said there was no contract or consideration to ground this action, but that the plaintiff might have sued in assumpsit.

It was perhaps the lingering of this idea, together with the often repeated notion that an assumpsit was not a contract, that produced a wider theory of consideration in assumpsit than prevailed in debt. It was settled that assumpsit would lie for a mere omission, or nonfeasance. The cases of Henry VI's reign that I have mentioned were followed by others in the later years of Henry VII, and the point was never doubted again. An action on such a ground was plainly for a breach of promise, as had been recognized since the time of Edward III. If so, a consideration was necessary. Despite occasional wanderings, that too had been settled, or taken for granted, in many cases of Queen Elizabeth's time. But the bastard origin of the action had given rise to the doubt whether any consideration at all was necessary, and that made it possible to hold considerations sufficient in assumpsit which had been held insufficient in debt.

Another circumstance may have had some influence. It seems that in the period when assumpsit was just growing to its full size, there was some slight inclination to identify consideration with the Roman causa, the ground or reason of an obligation, taken in its broadest sense. The word "cause" was used for consideration in the early years of Elizabeth, in connection with a covenant to stand seized to uses—a promise by an owner of land to hold it for the benefit of a relative. It was used in the same sense in the action of assumpsit. In the report last referred to, the main case laid down only a doctrine that would be followed today. But the report also gave an anonymous case, which was read to mean that a consideration already performed at the defendant's request, with no promise of any kind at the time, would support a later promise to pay for it. Starting from this authority and the word "cause," the courts soon reached the conclusion that there was a great difference between a contract and an assumpsit. In a contract, they said, "everything which is requisite ought to concur and meet together, viz. the consideration of the one side, and the sale or the promise on the other side,... to maintain an action upon an assumpsit, the same is not requisite, for it is sufficient if there be a moving cause or consideration precedent; for which cause or consideration the promise was made."

Footnote: I return to this in Lecture VIII. Mr. Langdell suggests an ingenious explanation of this doctrine: that it was then held that no promise could be implied in fact from the request. There may be evidence I do not know of. But the case he cites for this statement was not decided until 1603, while the implication of the case of 1568 that the later cases followed as their authority is all the other way.

So where the defendant engaged the plaintiff to serve his aunt at ten shillings a week, it was held that assumpsit would lie. The service did the defendant no good, but it was a charge, or detriment, to the plaintiff. The old questions were argued over again, and views that had very nearly prevailed in debt under Henry VI prevailed in assumpsit under Elizabeth and James.

A surety could be sued in assumpsit, although he was no longer liable in debt. There was the same remedy on a promise made in consideration of the plaintiff's marrying the defendant's daughter. Once assumpsit had been extended this far, the illusion that it did not mean contract could not be kept up. With this admitted, and with the ancient precedents in view, the law swung for a time toward reward as the true essence of consideration. But the other view won, and in doing so it actually changed the substantive law. For the courts of Henry VI to recognize a simple contract as binding, it had to rest on a benefit to the debtor. Now a promise could be enforced in consideration of a detriment to the promisee, the person to whom it was made. But in the true archaic spirit, the doctrine was not separated or distinguished from the remedy that brought it in. And so in modern times debt has taken on the altered look of a duty limited to cases where the consideration was of a special kind.

The later fortunes of assumpsit can be told briefly. It brought in bilateral contracts—contracts of a promise for a promise—because a promise was a detriment, and therefore a sufficient consideration for another promise. It pushed out debt. That was because the existence of the duty to pay was a sufficient consideration for a promise to pay; or rather because, even before a consideration was required, and as soon as assumpsit would lie for a nonfeasance, this action was used to escape the defendant's wager of law. It enormously enlarged the number of contracts that could be sued on, which had formerly been confined to debts and covenants, whereas nearly any promise could be sued on in assumpsit. And it brought in a theory that has had great influence on modern law—that all the liabilities of a bailee are founded on contract. Whether the prominence thus given to contract as the foundation of legal rights and duties had anything to do with the similar prominence it soon gained in political speculation, it is beyond my province to inquire.

Footnote: Lord Coke's warning not to rely on the abridgments is very necessary to a proper study of the history of consideration. The abridgments apply the doctrine to cases which make no mention of it, and which were decided before it was ever heard of.

Lecture VIII: Contract—II. Elements

The general method for analyzing contract is the same one already explained for possession. Wherever the law gives special rights to one person, or lays special burdens on another, it does so because certain special facts are true of those persons.
