In every such case, then, the task is twofold. First, find out what the facts are to which the special consequences are attached; second, find out what the consequences are. The first is the main field of legal argument. With contracts, the facts are not always the same. They may be that a certain person has signed, sealed, and delivered a writing to a certain effect. They may be that he has made a spoken promise, and that the person he promised, the promisee, has furnished him a consideration.

The element common to all contracts might be said to be a promise—although even a promise was not necessary for liability in debt, the old action for a fixed sum owed, as that action was formerly understood. But since it will not be possible to discuss covenants (promises under seal) any further, and since consideration was the main topic of the last Lecture, I will take consideration up first. And because consideration in debt differs historically from consideration in assumpsit, I shall confine myself to assumpsit, which is the later and more philosophical form.

It is said that any benefit the promisee confers on the promisor, or any detriment the promisee incurs, may be a consideration. It is also thought that every consideration can be reduced to a case of the second sort, if the word "detriment" is used in a somewhat broad sense.

To illustrate the general doctrine, suppose a man wants a cask of brandy carried from Boston to Cambridge, and a truckman—a man who hauls goods by wagon—says he will carry it, whether out of kindness or from some other motive, and the cask is delivered to him accordingly. If he carelessly staves the cask in, there would perhaps be no need to allege that he undertook to carry it; and on principle, and according to the older cases, if an undertaking was alleged, no consideration for it needed to be stated. The complaint in that case would be of a wrong, apart from any contract. But if the complaint was that he did not carry the cask as agreed, the plaintiff's difficulty would be that the truckman was not bound to carry it unless there was a consideration for his promise. Suppose, then, it was alleged that he promised to carry it in consideration of its being delivered to him. Would that be a sufficient consideration? The oldest cases, working from the notion of benefit to the promisor, said it could not be: the delivery was a trouble to him, not a benefit. Then look at it from the side of detriment. The delivery is a necessary condition of the promisor's doing the kindness at all, and if he does it, the delivery, far from being a detriment to the promisee, is a clear benefit to him.

But this argument is a fallacy. Plainly the delivery would be consideration enough to let the owner sue in assumpsit for the breach of those duties which arose, apart from contract, from the defendant's having undertaken to deal with the thing. It would be consideration enough for any promise whose performance did not involve dealing with the thing at all—a promise to pay a thousand dollars, for instance. And the law has never pronounced a consideration good or bad according to the nature of the promise built on it. The delivery is a sufficient consideration for any promise.

The argument on the other side loses sight of the moment at which the sufficiency of the consideration is to be judged. That moment is when the consideration is furnished. At that moment the delivery of the cask is a detriment in the strictest sense. The owner has given up a present control over the cask, which he had a right to keep, and what he has got in return is not a performance for which the delivery was necessary, but a mere promise of performance. The performance is still in the future.

But it will be seen that, although the delivery may be a consideration, it need not be one. A promise to carry might be made and accepted on the understanding that it was merely a favor, without consideration and not legally binding. In that case the promisee would incur the detriment of delivery just as before, but obviously he would incur it for the sole purpose of enabling the promisor to carry the cask as agreed.

It seems to me that it has not always been kept sufficiently in mind that the same thing may or may not be a consideration, depending on how the parties deal with it. The popular explanation of Coggs v. Bernard is that the delivery was a consideration for a promise to carry the casks safely. I gave what I believe is the true explanation, and the one I think Lord Holt had in view, in the fifth Lecture. But whether mine is right or not, there is a serious objection to the commonly accepted one: the declaration does not allege that the delivery was the consideration.

The same caution should be used in construing the terms of an agreement. It is hard to see why any detriment an instrument happens to reveal or provide for should be raised into a consideration, unless the parties have dealt with it on that footing. In many cases a promisee may incur a detriment without thereby furnishing a consideration. The detriment may be nothing but a condition precedent—something that must happen before the promise has to be performed—as where a man promises to pay another five hundred dollars if he breaks his leg.

The courts, however, have gone a long way toward wiping out this distinction. Acts which, on a fair reading of the language, seem to have been meant only as the fulfilling of a condition have been treated as the consideration for the promise. So have counter-promises in an agreement which expressly named other things as the consideration. It should also be mentioned—leaving open whether there may be some special explanation for the doctrine—that an assignment of a lease is said not to be "voluntary," that is, a gift, under the statute of 27 Elizabeth, chapter 4, because the assignee takes on the obligations of the tenant. Yet the assignee's taking on that detriment may not have been meant as the inducement for the assignment at all. In many cases it only amounts to a deduction from the benefit conferred, as a right of way over the land would, especially if the only obligation is to pay rent, which in legal theory issues out of the land itself.

But although the courts may sometimes have gone a little far in their eagerness to uphold agreements, there can be no doubt of the principle I have laid down: that the same thing may or may not be a consideration, depending on how the parties deal with it. This raises the question how a thing must be dealt with in order to make it a consideration.

It is said that consideration must not be confused with motive. It is true that it must not be confused with what may in actual fact be the prevailing or chief motive. A man may promise to paint a picture for five hundred dollars while his chief motive is a desire for fame. A consideration may in fact be given and accepted solely for the purpose of making a promise binding. But, all the same, it is the essence of a consideration that, by the terms of the agreement, it is given and accepted as the motive or inducement of the promise. And the other way round: the promise must be made and accepted as the conventional motive or inducement for furnishing the consideration—conventional in the sense of agreed upon, whatever the real motives may be. The root of the whole matter is the relation of reciprocal conventional inducement, each for the other, between consideration and promise.

A good example of the first half of this proposition is a Massachusetts case. A man had made a spoken bargain to buy some wood standing on the plaintiff's land and had given his promissory note for it, but the plaintiff refused to let the wood be taken away unless he got additional security. So the buyer and the plaintiff went to the defendant, and the defendant put his name on the note. The plaintiff then let the buyer carry off the wood. But according to the testimony, the defendant signed without knowing that the plaintiff was going to change his position in any way on the strength of the signature, and it was held that, if that account was believed, there was no consideration.

An illustration of the other half of the rule is found in the cases where a reward is offered for doing something, and the thing is afterward done by a person who does not know of the offer. In such a case the reward cannot be claimed, because the supposed consideration was not furnished on the faith of the offer. The promise that was held out did not induce the furnishing of the consideration. A promise cannot be set up as the conventional motive for an act when nobody knew of it until after the act was done.

Footnote: The New York court criticized the English case of Williams v. Carwardine on this point. But in that case it does not appear that the plaintiff did not know of the offer of a reward; it appears only that the jury found she was in fact moved by other motives—a finding wholly beside the mark.

Both sides of the relation between consideration and promise, and the conventional nature of that relation, can be illustrated by the case of the cask. Suppose the truckman is willing to carry the cask, and the owner willing to let him, without any bargain, and each knows the other's state of mind. But the truckman, seeing some advantage to himself in the matter, says to the owner, "In consideration of your delivering me the cask, and letting me carry it, I promise to carry it," and the owner thereupon delivers it. I suppose the promise would be binding. The promise is offered in terms as the inducement for the delivery, and the delivery is made in terms as the inducement for the promise. It may be very likely that the owner would have delivered the cask without any promise, and that the truckman would have made his promise as a free favor if it had not been accepted on consideration; but that is only a guess, after all. The owner need not have delivered the cask unless he chose to, and since he delivered it as a term of a bargain, the promisor cannot set up what might have happened in order to destroy the effect of what did happen. It seems, then, that the same transaction, in substance and spirit, may be voluntary or binding according to the form of words the parties choose to use for the purpose of fixing its legal consequences.

If these principles are accepted, they will be seen to explain a doctrine which the courts have had some trouble in establishing: the doctrine that an executed consideration—something already done and over with—will not support a later promise. It has been said, to be sure, that such a consideration was enough if the thing had been done at the promisor's request. But the objections to that view are plain. If the request was of such a kind, and so put, as reasonably to imply that the other person was to be rewarded, then there was an express promise, even though not put into words, and that promise was made at the same time the consideration was given, not afterward. If, on the other hand, the words did not justify the understanding that the service was to be paid for, the service was a gift; and a past gift can no more be a consideration than any other act of the promisee that was not induced by the promise.

The source of the error can be traced, at least in part, in history. Some suggestions about it were made in the last Lecture; a few words should be added here. In the old cases of debt, where there was some question whether the plaintiff had shown enough to maintain his action, a "contract precedent"—a bargain made beforehand—was mentioned several times as what raised the duty. Thus, where a man had bound himself in one hundred shillings to pay his servant on a certain day for his services and for payments the servant had made on his account, it was argued that there was no contract precedent, and that a party is not bound by mere words; and further, that for all that appeared, the servant had made the payments on his own initiative and at no one's request, and from that no duty could arise.

So, too, when debt was brought on a deed to pay the plaintiff ten marks if he would marry the defendant's daughter, and it was objected that the action should have been covenant, the answer was that the plaintiff had a contract precedent, which gave him debt.

The first case in assumpsit meant only to adopt this long-familiar idea. A man went bail for his friend's servant, who had been arrested. Afterward the master promised to indemnify him—to make good any loss the bail might cost him—and when the master failed to do so, the man sued him in assumpsit. It was held that there was no consideration for which the defendant should be charged, unless the master had promised to indemnify the plaintiff before the servant was bailed; "for the master did never make request to the plaintiff for his servant to do so much, but he did it of his own head." This is perfectly plain sailing, and means no more than the case in the Year Books. The report, however, also mentions a case in which it was held that a later promise, made in consideration that the plaintiff had married the defendant's cousin at the defendant's special request, was binding, and that the marriage was "good cause... because [it] ensued the request of the defendant." Whether this was meant to establish a general principle, or was decided with an eye to the peculiar consideration of marriage, it was soon read in the broader sense, as was shown in the last Lecture. It was held several times that a past and executed matter was a sufficient consideration for a promise made at a later day, provided only that the thing relied on had been done or furnished at the promisor's request.

It is now time to analyze the nature of a promise, which is the second and most conspicuous element in a simple contract. The Indian Contract Act of 1872, in Section 2, says:

"(a.) When one person signifies to another his willingness to do or to abstain from doing anything, with a view to obtaining the assent of that other to such act or abstinence, he is said to make a proposal:

"(b.) When the person to whom the proposal is made signifies his assent thereto, the proposal is said to be accepted. A proposal when accepted becomes a promise."

By this definition, what may be promised is limited to the conduct of the promisor himself. If that only meant that the promisor alone must bear whatever legal burden his promise may create, it would be true. But that is not what it means. For this is a definition of a promise, not of a legally binding promise. We are not looking for the legal effects of a contract, but for the possible contents of a promise which the law may or may not enforce. So we must consider only what can possibly be promised in a legal sense, not what the secondary consequences will be of a promise that is binding but not performed.

An assurance that it shall rain tomorrow, or that some third person shall paint a picture, may just as well be a promise as an assurance that the promisee shall receive a hundred bales of cotton from some source, or that the promisor will pay the promisee a hundred dollars. What is the difference between these cases? It lies only in how much power the promisor has over the event. In the first case he has none. He has just as little legal authority to make a man paint a picture, although he may have greater means of persuasion. He will probably be able to make sure that the promisee gets the cotton. And being a rich man, he is certain to be able to pay the hundred dollars, barring some most improbable accident.

But the law does not, as a general rule, ask how far the fulfilment of an assurance about the future lies within the promisor's power. In the moral world it may be that the obligation of a promise is confined to what lies within reach of the promisor's will (except so far as the limit is unknown to one side and misrepresented by the other). But unless some consideration of public policy intervenes, I take it that a man may bind himself at law that any future event shall happen. He can therefore promise it in a legal sense. It may be said that when a man covenants that it shall rain tomorrow, or that A shall paint a picture, he is only saying, in short form, "I will pay if it does not rain, or if A does not paint a picture." But that is not necessarily so. A promise could easily be framed which would be broken by fair weather, or by A's not painting. A promise, then, is simply an accepted assurance that a certain event or state of things shall come to pass.

But if this is true, it matters for more than just enlarging the definition of the word promise. It bears on the theory of contract. At common law, the consequences of a binding promise do not depend on how much power the promisor has over the promised event. If the promised event does not come to pass, the promisor's property is sold to satisfy the damages—within certain limits—that the promisee has suffered by the failure. The consequences are the same in kind whether the promise is that it shall rain, or that another man shall paint a picture, or that the promisor will deliver a bale of cotton.

If the legal consequence is the same in every case, it seems right that all contracts should be looked at from the same legal point of view. In the case of a binding promise that it shall rain tomorrow, the immediate legal effect of what the promisor does is that he takes the risk of the event, within certain defined limits, as between himself and the promisee. He does no more when he promises to deliver a bale of cotton.

If it is proper to state the common-law meaning of promise and contract in this way, it has the advantage of freeing the subject from the needless theory that contract is a qualified subjection of one will to another, a kind of limited slavery. It might be seen that way if the law compelled men to perform their contracts, or allowed promisees to compel them. If, when a man promised to work for another, the law made him do it, his relation to the promisee might with some truth be called a servitude ad hoc—a bondage for that one purpose. But that is what the law never does. It never steps in until a promise has been broken, and therefore can no longer be performed according to its terms. It is true that in some instances equity does what is called compelling specific performance—ordering the promisor to do the very thing he promised. But, in the first place, I am speaking of the common law; and, in the second, this only means that equity compels performance of those parts of the whole promise which can still be performed. Take, for instance, a promise to convey land within a certain time. A court of equity does not usually interfere until the time has gone by, so that the promise can no longer be performed as it was made. But if the conveyance matters more than the time, and the promisee would rather have it late than never, the law may compel performance of that much. Even then it does not literally compel it; it puts the promisor in prison unless he will convey. This remedy is an exceptional one. The only universal consequence of a legally binding promise is, that the law makes the promisor pay damages if the promised event does not come to pass. In every case it leaves him free from interference until the time for fulfilment has gone by, and therefore free to break his contract if he chooses.

A more practical advantage of looking at a contract as the taking of a risk is the light it throws on the measure of damages—the rule for how much a plaintiff can recover. If a breach of contract were regarded in the same light as a tort, it would seem to follow that if, while the contract was being performed, the promisor were told of some particular consequence that would result from its not being performed, he should be held liable for that consequence if he failed to perform. Such a suggestion has been made. But it has not been accepted as the law. On the contrary, according to the opinion of a very able judge, which seems to be generally followed, notice of special circumstances out of which special damages would arise in case of breach—even notice given at the time the contract is made—is not enough, unless the assumption of that risk is to be taken as having fairly entered into the contract. If a carrier undertook to carry the machinery of a sawmill from Liverpool to Vancouver Island and failed to do so, he probably would not be held liable for the hire value of the machinery during the unavoidable delay, even though he knew it could not be replaced without sending to England—unless he was fairly understood to accept "the contract with the special condition attached to it."

It is true that when people make contracts they usually have the performance in mind rather than the breach. The express language they use does not generally go further than to define what will happen if the contract is carried out. A statute requiring a written memorandum of the contract would be satisfied by a written statement of the promise as it was made. That is so because to require more would run against the ordinary habits of mankind; and also because the statement that the effect of a contract is the assumption of the risk of a future event does not mean that there is a second, subsidiary promise to assume that risk. It means that the assumption follows as a consequence enforced directly by the law, without the promisor's co-operation. So parol evidence—evidence of what was said outside the writing—would no doubt be admissible to enlarge or narrow the extent of the liability assumed for non-performance, where it would not be admissible to change the scope of the promise.

But these concessions do not affect the view taken here. Since the relation between the parties to a contract is voluntary, the consequences attached to that relation must be voluntary too. What event the promise contemplates—in other words, what will amount to a breach of contract—is a matter of interpretation and construction. Which consequences of the breach are assumed is likewise, though more remotely, a matter of construction, taking into account the circumstances in which the contract is made. Knowledge of what depends on performance is one of those circumstances. It is not necessarily conclusive, but it may have the effect of enlarging the risk assumed.

The very business of construction is to work out, from what was expressly said and done, what would have been said about events not definitely before the parties' minds, if those events had been considered. In mercantile contracts the price paid generally rules out the reading that exceptional risks were meant to be assumed. The foregoing analysis is believed to show that the result the courts have reached on grounds of practical good sense falls in with the true theory of contract under the common law.

The discussion of the nature of a promise has led me to analyze contract and its consequences somewhat ahead of their place. I must say a word more about the facts that make up a promise. It is laid down, with theoretical truth, that besides the assurance or offer on one side, there must be an acceptance on the other. But I find it hard to think of a case in which a simple contract fails to be made that could not be explained on other grounds—generally by the lack of the relation between offer and consideration as reciprocal inducements, each of the other. Acceptance of an offer usually follows by mere implication from the furnishing of the consideration. And since by our law an accepted offer, or promise, stands on no different footing from an offer not yet accepted until the consideration is furnished—each being revocable until then, and each continuing until then unless it has expired or been revoked—the question of acceptance is rarely of practical importance.

Assuming that the general nature of consideration and promise is now understood, some questions peculiar to bilateral contracts—contracts in which each side makes a promise to the other—remain to be considered. They concern the sufficiency of the consideration, and the moment when the contract is made.

A promise may be a consideration for a promise, though not every promise for every other. It may be doubted whether a promise to make someone a gift of a hundred dollars would be supported by his promise to accept it. But where there were mutual promises, one to transfer and the other to accept unpaid shares in a railway company, it has been held that a binding contract was made. Here one party agrees to part with something that may prove valuable, and the other to take on a liability that may prove burdensome.

But now suppose there is no element of uncertainty except in the minds of the parties. Take, for instance, a bet on a horse race that has already been run. It has been thought that this would amount to an absolute promise on one side and no promise at all on the other. But this does not seem to me sound. Contracts are dealings between men by which they make arrangements for the future. In making such arrangements, what matters is not what is objectively true but what the parties know. Any present fact unknown to the parties is just as uncertain, for the purpose of making an arrangement at this moment, as any future fact. It is therefore a detriment to undertake to be ready to pay if the event turns out not to have been as expected. This seems to be the true explanation of why forbearing to sue on a claim the plaintiff believes to be good is a sufficient consideration, even though the claim was in fact bad and the defendant knew it was bad. If this view were unsound, it is hard to see how bets on any future event, short of a miracle, could be upheld. For if the happening or not happening of the event is subject to the law of cause and effect, the only uncertainty about it lies in our foresight, not in its happening.

The question when a contract is made arises for the most part with bilateral contracts made by letter. The doubt is whether the contract is complete at the moment the return promise is put into the mail, or at the moment it is received. If convenience weighs more heavily on the side of either view, that is reason enough to adopt it. As far as purely logical grounds go, the most ingenious argument for the later moment is Professor Langdell's. According to him, the conclusion follows from the fact that the consideration which makes the offer binding is itself a promise. Every promise, he says, is an offer before it is a promise, and the essence of an offer is that it should be communicated. But this reasoning seems unsound. When, as in the case supposed, the consideration for the return promise has been put within the offeree's power, and the return promise has been accepted in advance, there is not an instant, either in time or in logic, when the return promise is an offer. It is a promise, and a term of a binding contract, as soon as it is anything. An offer is a revocable and unaccepted communication of willingness to promise. When an offer of a certain bilateral contract has been made, the same contract cannot be offered by the other side. The so-called offer would be neither revocable nor unaccepted. It would complete the contract the moment it was made.

If it is said that it is of the essence of a promise to be communicated, whether or not it passes through the stage of offer—meaning by "communicated" brought to the actual knowledge of the promisee—the law is believed to be otherwise. A covenant is binding when it is delivered and accepted, whether it is read or not. On the same principle, it is believed that whenever the obligation is to be entered into by a tangible sign—as, in the case supposed, by a letter containing the return promise—and the consideration for the promise and the assent to it have already been given, the only question is when the tangible sign is sufficiently put into the promisee's power. I cannot believe that, if the letter had been delivered to the promisee and then snatched from his hands before he had read it, there would be no contract. If I am right, it matters little whether the post office is regarded as the offerer's agent, or as his bailee, or as a mere box to which he has access. The offeree, when he drops the letter containing the counter-promise into the letter box, does an overt act which, by general understanding, gives up control over the letter and puts it into a third hand for the benefit of the offerer, who is at liberty to take it at any moment afterward.

The principles governing revocation are wholly different. A person to whom an offer is made has a right to assume that it stays open according to its terms until he has actual notice to the contrary. The effect of one communication must be undone by a counter-communication. But the making of a contract does not depend on the state of the parties' minds; it depends on their overt acts. When the sign of the counter-promise is a tangible object, the contract is complete when control over that object changes hands.

Lecture IX: Contract—III. Void and Voidable

We have now discussed the facts that must be present to bring a contract into existence, and the legal consequences of a contract once it is formed. It remains to consider, one after the other, the cases in which a contract is said to be void, and those in which it is said to be voidable. A void contract is one that fails to be made when it seems to have been made. A voidable contract is one that has been made, but can be rescinded—undone—by one side or the other, and treated as if it had never existed. I take up the first class of cases first.

When a contract fails to be made, although the usual forms have been gone through, the reason for the failure is commonly said to be mistake, misrepresentation, or fraud. But I shall try to show that these are merely dramatic circumstances. The true reason is the absence of one or more of the primary elements which have been shown, or are seen at once, to be necessary to the existence of a contract.

Suppose a man goes through the form of making a contract with A through B, as A's agent, and B is not in fact A's agent. There is no contract, because there is only one party. The promise offered to A has not been accepted by him, and no consideration has moved from him. In such a case there is generally mistake on one side and fraud on the other. But it is very clear that no special doctrine is needed, because the primary elements of a contract explained in the last Lecture are not yet present.

Take next a different case. The defendant agreed to buy, and the plaintiff agreed to sell, a cargo of cotton "to arrive ex Peerless from Bombay"—that is, to arrive on the ship Peerless. There were two ships of that name sailing from Bombay, one in October, the other in December. The plaintiff meant the December ship, the defendant the October one. It was held that the defendant was not bound to accept the cotton. It is commonly said that such a contract is void because of mutual mistake as to the subject-matter, and because the parties therefore did not consent to the same thing. But this way of putting it seems to me misleading. The law has nothing to do with the actual state of the parties' minds. In contract, as elsewhere, it must go by externals, and judge parties by their conduct. If there had been only one "Peerless," and the defendant had said "Peerless" by mistake, meaning "Peri," he would have been bound. The true ground of the decision was not that each party meant a different thing from the other, as the usual explanation implies, but that each said a different thing. The plaintiff offered one thing; the defendant expressed his assent to another.

A proper name, when used in business or in pleading, means one individual thing and no other, as everyone knows. Therefore a person to whom such a name is used must find out at his peril what object it designates. If there are no circumstances that make the use of the name deceptive on either side, each party is entitled to insist on the meaning favorable to him for the word as he used it, and neither is entitled to insist on that meaning for the word as the other used it. So far from mistake, as mistake, being the ground of the decision, its only bearing, it seems to me, was to establish that neither party knew that the other understood him to use the word "Peerless" in the sense the other gave it. Had one of them known that, there would perhaps have been a binding contract. For if a man uses a word to which he knows the other party attaches a certain meaning, and understands him to attach it, he may be held to that meaning, and not be allowed to give it any other.

Next, suppose a case in which the offer and the acceptance do not differ, and both parties have used the same words in the same sense. Suppose that A agreed to buy, and B agreed to sell, "these barrels of mackerel," and that the barrels in question turn out to contain salt. There is mutual mistake as to what is in the barrels, and no fraud on either side. I suppose the contract would be void.

It is commonly said that the contract fails in such a case because the actual subject-matter differs in kind from the thing the parties intended. It is perhaps more instructive to say that the terms of the supposed contract, although they seemed consistent, were in fact contradictory, in matters that went to the root of the bargain. For by one of the essential terms, the subject-matter of the agreement was the contents of certain barrels, and nothing else. By another, equally important, it was mackerel, and nothing else. But as a matter of fact it could not be both, because the contents of the barrels were salt. Neither term could be left out without forcing on the parties a contract they did not make. It follows that A cannot be required to accept, nor B to deliver, either these barrels of salt or other barrels of mackerel; and unless one term is left out, the promise is meaningless.

If there had been fraud on the seller's part, or if he had known what the barrels really contained, the buyer might have had a right to insist on delivery of the inferior article. Fraud would perhaps have made the contract valid at the buyer's option. For when a man qualifies words that make sense with other words which he knows, for reasons he keeps to himself, make no sense when applied to them, he may fairly be taken to authorize his promisee to insist on the possible part of the promise being performed, if the promisee is willing to give up the rest.

Take one more illustration like the last. A policy of insurance is issued on a certain building, which the policy describes as a machine-shop. In fact the building is not a machine-shop but an organ factory, which is a greater risk. The contract is void—not because of any misrepresentation, but, as before, because two of its essential terms are repugnant, that is, contradict each other, and put together they make no sense.

Of course, the principle of repugnancy just explained might be stretched to cover any inconsistency between the different terms of a contract. It might be said, for instance, that if a piece of gold is sold as eighteen-carat gold and is in fact less pure, or if a cow is sold as giving an average of twelve quarts of milk a day and in fact gives only six, then by the explanation just offered there is no logical difference between those cases and that of the barrel of salt sold as mackerel. Yet those bargains would not be void. At most, they would only be voidable, if the buyer chose to throw them up.

The distinctions of the law are founded on experience, not on logic. The law therefore does not make men's dealings depend on mathematical accuracy. Whatever is promised, a man has a right to be paid for if it is not given. But it does not follow that the absence of some insignificant detail will entitle him to throw up the contract, still less that it will prevent a contract from being formed, which is the question now before us. The repugnant terms must both be very important—so important that the court thinks that, if either were left out, the contract would be different in substance from the one the parties' words seemed to express.

A term that refers directly to an identification by the senses always has this degree of importance. If a promise is made to sell this cow, or this mackerel, to this man, then whatever else may be struck from the contract, it can never be enforced except as to this object and by this man. If this barrel of salt is fraudulently sold as a barrel of mackerel, the buyer may perhaps choose to take this barrel of salt if he likes, but he cannot choose to take another barrel of mackerel. Suppose the seller is introduced by the name B, and the buyer takes him for another person of the same name, and under that impression hands him a written promise to buy from B. The B to whom the writing is handed is the contractee, if anyone is; and notwithstanding what has been said about the use of proper names, I should suppose that a contract would be made. For it must further be said that, so far as one of the terms of a contract identifies the thing promised, or the promisee, by sight and hearing, that term so far outweighs all the others that it is very rare for the failure of any other part of the description to prevent a contract from being made. The most obvious of the seeming exceptions is where it is not the object itself that is so identified, but only its covering or wrapper.

Footnote: The maxim is that the presence of the thing itself cures a mistake in its name. It would seem that a proper name, or any other identification of an object or person as a specific one, may have the same effect as an actual identification by the senses, because it refers to such an identification, although less directly.

Of course, the performance of a promise may be made conditional on the other side's complying with all the terms it has agreed to. But conditions attached to performance can never come into question until a contract has been made, and so far the question has been whether a contract exists in the first place.

A case different from any yet considered may be suggested. Instead of a contradiction between offer and assent, which prevents an agreement, or between the terms of an agreement, which makes it meaningless on its face, there may be a similar contradiction between a term of the contract and an earlier representation of fact that was not expressly made part of the contract. The representation may have been the chief inducement to the bargain and its very foundation. It may be more important than any of the terms expressed, and yet the contract may have been put into writing in words that cannot fairly be read to include it. A seller may have stated that barrels filled with salt contain mackerel, while the contract is only for the barrels and their contents. An applicant for insurance may have misstated facts essential to the risk, while the policy simply insures a certain building or a certain life. It may be asked whether these contracts are not void as well.

There might conceivably be cases in which, taking into account the nature of the contract, the words used could be said to embody the representation as a term by construction—that is, by the court's reading of what the words mean. For instance, it might be said that the true and well-understood purpose of a contract of insurance is not, as the words seem to say, to take the risk of any loss by fire or perils of the sea, however great the risk may be. It is to take a risk of a certain size, and no other, a risk that has been calculated mathematically from the statements of the insured party. The extent of the risk is not stated in the policy, because the old forms and established usage do not state it, but the meaning is perfectly understood.

If this reasoning were adopted, there would be the same contradiction in the terms of the contract whether the nature of the risk was written in the policy or fixed by an earlier description. But, subject to possible exceptions of this kind, it would seem that a contract would be made, and that the most that could be claimed would be a right to rescind. Where parties who have the power to bind themselves do acts and use words that are fit to create an obligation, I take it that an obligation arises. If there is a mistake about a fact not mentioned in the contract, it goes only to the motives for making the contract. But a contract is not prevented from being made by the mere fact that one party would not have made it if he had known the truth. In what cases a mistake affecting motives only is a ground for avoiding a contract does not concern this discussion. The subject now before us is when a contract is made, and the question of avoiding or rescinding it assumes that it has been made.

I think it may now be taken as settled that, when fraud, misrepresentation, or mistake is said to make a contract void, no new principle comes in to set aside an otherwise perfect obligation. In every such case one or more of the first elements explained in the last Lecture is missing. Either there is no second party, or the two parties say different things, or essential terms that seem consistent are really inconsistent as used.

When a contract is said to be voidable, it is assumed that a contract has been made, but that it may be unmade at the choice—the election, as lawyers say—of one party. This must be because of the breach of some condition attached to its existence, either expressly or by implication.

If a condition is attached to the contract's coming into being, there is as yet no contract. Either party may withdraw at will until the condition is settled one way or the other. There is no obligation, although there may be an offer or a promise, and so there is no relation between the parties that needs discussing here. But some conditions that seem to arise out of a contract already made are really of this sort. That is always the case if the condition of a promise lies within the control of the promisor's own will. For instance, a promise to pay for clothes if they are made to the customer's satisfaction has been held in Massachusetts to make the promisor his own final judge. So interpreted, it seems to me to be no contract at all until the promisor declares himself satisfied. His promise is only to pay if he sees fit, and such a promise cannot be a contract, because it cannot impose any obligation. If the promise were read to mean that the clothes should be paid for provided they were such as ought to satisfy the promisor, and so to make the jury the judge, there would be a contract, because the promisor gives up control over the outcome. But it would be subject to a condition in the sense of the present analysis.

Theorists have divided the conditions a contract may contain into conditions precedent and conditions subsequent—conditions that must be met before a duty arises, and conditions whose happening ends a duty already in being. The distinction has even been declared to be of great importance. It must be admitted that it is, if the course of pleading is taken as the test. In some cases the plaintiff must state that a condition has been performed before the defendant is called on to answer; in others it is left to the defendant to set up that a condition has been broken.

In one sense all conditions are subsequent; in another, all are precedent. All are subsequent to the first stage of the obligation. Take, for instance, a promise to pay for work if it is done to the satisfaction of an architect. The condition is a clear case of what is called a condition precedent: there can be no duty to pay until the architect is satisfied. But there can be a contract before that moment, because the decision whether the promisor shall pay or not is no longer within his control. So the condition is subsequent to the existence of the obligation.

On the other hand, every condition subsequent is precedent to the moment when the burden of the law actually falls. Look at the law as it would be seen by a man with no scruples against doing anything he could do without legal consequences. To him it is obvious that the main consequence the law attaches to a contract is a greater or smaller chance of having to pay money. From the purely legal point of view, the only question is whether the promisor will be made to pay, and the important moment is the one at which that is settled. All conditions are precedent to that.

But all conditions are precedent not only in this extreme sense but also to the existence of the plaintiff's cause of action—his right to sue. As strong a case as can be put is that of an insurance policy which is to be void if it is not sued on within one year after a failure to pay as agreed. The condition does not come into play until a loss has happened, the duty to pay has been neglected, and a cause of action has arisen. Nevertheless, it is precedent to the plaintiff's cause of action. When a man sues, the question is not whether he has had a cause of action in the past, but whether he has one now. He has not, unless the year is still running. If it were left to the defendant to raise the lapse of the year, that would be because the order of pleading does not require a plaintiff to meet every possible defense and set out a case that can be answered only by denying it. The point at which the law calls on the defendant for an answer varies from case to case. Sometimes it seems to be governed simply by convenience of proof, requiring the party who asserts a fact to plead and prove it. Sometimes there seems to be a reference to the usual course of events, and matters belong to the defense because they are true only exceptionally.

The most logical distinction would be between conditions that must be satisfied before a promise can be broken, and conditions which, like the last, discharge the liability after a breach has occurred. But this is of the slightest possible importance, and it may be doubted whether another case like the last could be found.

It is much more important to mark the difference between a stipulation that merely confines a promise to certain cases and a condition properly so called. Every condition, it is true, has this confining effect on the promise it is attached to. Whatever the rule of pleading may be, a promise is as truly kept and performed by doing nothing, when the condition of the stipulated act has been broken, as it would have been by doing the act had the condition been fulfilled. But if that were all, every clause in a contract that showed what the promisor did not promise would be a condition, and the word would be worse than useless. The characteristic feature is quite different.

A condition properly so called is an event whose happening entitles the person in whose favor the condition is reserved to treat the contract as if it had not been made—to avoid it, as it is commonly put—that is, to insist that both parties be put back where they stood before the contract was made. When a condition works as a condition, it lets in an outside force to destroy the existing state of things. For although its existence is due to the consent of the parties, its operation depends on the choice of one of them. When a condition is broken, the person entitled to insist on it may do so if he chooses; but he may, if he prefers, choose to keep the contract alive. He gets his right to avoid it from the agreement, but the avoidance comes from him.

So it is important to distinguish the stipulations that have this extreme effect from those that only interpret the extent of a promise, or define the events to which it applies. And since it has just been shown that a condition need not be insisted on as such, we must further distinguish between its working by way of avoidance, which belongs to it alone, and its incidental working by way of interpretation and definition, which it shares with other clauses that are not conditions.

This is best illustrated by a bilateral contract between A and B, where A's undertaking is conditional on B's doing what he promises, and where, after A has gone a certain distance in his task, B breaks his half of the bargain. For instance, A is employed by B as a clerk, and is wrongfully dismissed in the middle of a quarter. In A's favor, the contract is conditional on B's keeping his agreement to employ him. Whether A insists on the condition or not, he is not bound to do any more. So far, the condition works simply by way of definition: it establishes that A has not promised to act in the case that has happened. But beyond this, for which no condition was needed, A may choose between two courses. First, he may elect to avoid the contract. Then the parties stand as if no contract had been made. A has done work for B which was understood not to be free, and for which no rate of pay has been fixed, so he can recover what the jury thinks his services were reasonably worth. The contract no longer fixes the quid pro quo. Alternatively, A may stand by the contract if he prefers, and sue B for breaking it. In that case he can recover, as part of his damages, pay at the contract rate for what he had done, and also compensation for losing the chance to finish. But the points that matter for the present discussion are these. The two remedies exclude each other, one relying on the contract, the other setting it aside. And A's stopping work and doing no more after B's breach is equally consistent with either choice, and in fact has nothing to do with the matter.

One word should be added to prevent a misunderstanding. When it is said that A has done all he promised to do in the case that has happened, it is not meant that he is necessarily entitled to the same pay as if he had done the larger amount of work. B's promise in the case supposed was to pay so much a quarter for services. Although the consideration for that promise was A's promise to perform them, its scope was limited to the case of their actually being performed. So A could not simply wait until the end of his term and then recover the full amount he would have had if the employment had continued. Nor is he any more entitled to do so because it was B's fault that the services were not rendered. B's answer to any such claim is perfect. He is liable only on a promise, and he in his turn only promised to pay in a case that has not happened. He did promise to employ A, however, and for not doing that he is liable in damages.

One or two more illustrations will be useful. A promises to deliver certain goods, and B promises to accept and pay for them, at a certain time and place. When the time comes, neither party is there. Neither would be liable to an action, and, according to what has been said, each has done all he promised to do in the event that has happened—namely, nothing. It might be objected that if A has done all he is bound to do, he ought to be able to sue B, since performance or readiness to perform was all that was needed to give him that right; and the same might be said the other way round of B. On the other hand, taking either B or A as defendant, the same facts would be a complete defense. The puzzle is largely one of words.

It is true that A and B have each performed all they promised to do at the present stage, because each promised only to act in the event of the other being ready and willing to act at the same time. But that readiness and willingness, although not necessary to the performance of either promise, and therefore not a duty, was necessary to present a case to which the promise of action on the other side would apply. So although A and B have each performed their own promise, they have not performed the condition of their right to demand more from the other side. Performing that condition is purely optional until one side has brought it within the scope of the other's undertaking by performing it himself. But it is performance in this second sense—satisfying all the conditions, as well as keeping one's own promises—that is necessary to give A or B a right of action.

Conditions may be created by the very words of a contract. Of such cases there is nothing to say, for parties may agree to whatever they choose. But conditions may also be held to arise by construction, where the contract makes no provision in terms for rescinding or avoiding it in any case. The nature of the conditions the law reads in this way needs explaining. Speaking generally, they are aimed at the existence of the evident grounds, on the side of the rescinding party, for making the bargain, or at the achievement of its evident purposes. But that is not enough. As a rule, the disappointment must be caused by wrongdoing on the other side; and the most obvious kinds of such wrongdoing are fraud and misrepresentation, or failure to perform one's own part of the contract.

Fraud and misrepresentation therefore have to be considered once more in this connection. I take misrepresentation first. The first question it raises is whether the representation is or is not part of the contract. If the contract is in writing and the representation is set out on the face of the paper, it may be material or immaterial, but the effect of its being untrue will be decided on much the same principles as govern a failure to perform a promise on the same side. If the contract is made by word of mouth, there may be wide latitude in connecting words of representation with later words of promise; but once they are found to be part of the contract, the same principles apply as if the whole were in writing.

The question now before us is the effect of a misrepresentation that leads to the contract but is not part of it. Suppose the contract is in writing but does not contain the representation. Does such an earlier misrepresentation ever authorize rescission? And if so, does it ever do so except where it rises to the level of fraud? The promisor might say: It does not matter to me whether you knew your representation was false or not; the only thing I care about is whether it was true. If it is untrue, I suffer equally whether you knew it or not. But it was shown in an earlier Lecture that the law does not go on the principle that a man is answerable for all the consequences of all his acts. An act is indifferent in itself. It takes its character from the surrounding facts known to the actor at the time. If a man states a thing reasonably believing that he is speaking from knowledge, it is contrary to the analogies of the law to throw the risk of its truth on him unless he agrees to take that risk. In the case supposed he did not, since the representation was not made part of the contract.

It is very different when there is fraud. Fraud may lead to the making of a contract through a statement outside the contract just as well as through one contained in it. And the law would hold the contract no less conditional on good faith in the one case than in the other.

To illustrate, we may take a somewhat extreme case. A says to B, "I have not opened these barrels myself, but they contain No. 1 mackerel. I paid so much for them to so-and-so," naming a well-known dealer. Afterward A writes to B, "I will sell the barrels you saw, and their contents, for so much," and B accepts. The barrels turn out to contain salt. I suppose the contract would be binding if the statements about the contents were honest, and voidable if they were fraudulent.

Fraudulent representations made outside a contract can never, it would seem, go to anything except the motives for making it. Being outside the contract, they can seldom affect how it is interpreted. A promise in certain words has a definite meaning, which the promisor is presumed to understand. If A says to B, "I promise you to buy this barrel and its contents," his words point to a person and a thing identified by the senses, and they signify nothing more. There is no repugnancy. If that person is ready to deliver that thing, the purchaser cannot say that any term of the contract itself has not been complied with. He may have been fraudulently led to believe that B was another B, and that the barrel held mackerel. But however much his belief on those points may have affected his willingness to make the promise, it would be somewhat extravagant to give his words a different meaning on that account. "You" means the person standing before the speaker, whatever his name, and "contents" applies to salt as well as to mackerel.

It is no doubt only because a condition is construed into the contract—read into it by the law—that fraud is a ground for rescission at all. The parties could agree, if they chose, that a contract should bind them regardless of the truth or falsehood of anything said outside it on either side.

But, as has been said before in these Lectures, although the law starts from the distinctions and uses the language of morality, it necessarily ends in external standards not dependent on the actual consciousness of the individual. So it has happened with fraud. If a man makes a representation while knowing facts which, by the average standard of the community, are enough to warn him that it is probably untrue, and it is untrue, he is guilty of fraud in the eye of the law whether he believes his statement or not. The courts of Massachusetts, at least, go much further. They seem to hold that any material statement a man makes as of his own knowledge, or in such a way that it may fairly be understood as made of his own knowledge, is fraudulent if it is untrue—whatever reasons he may have had for believing it, and for believing that he knew it. It is clear, therefore, that a representation may be morally innocent and yet fraudulent in the eye of the law. Indeed, the Massachusetts rule seems to stop little short of the principle laid down by the English courts of equity, which I criticized in Lecture IV. For most positive statements of fact would at least entitle a jury to find that they were reasonably understood as made of the speaker's own knowledge, and so might justify rescission if they turned out to be untrue. The moral vocabulary has ceased to fit, and an external standard of responsibility has been reached. But the starting point is fraud all the same, and except on the ground of fraud, as the law defines it, I do not think that misrepresentations made before the contract affect its validity, even though they lead directly to its making. And neither the contract nor the implied condition calls for the existence of the facts about which the false representations were made. They call only for the absence of certain false representations. The condition is not that the promisee shall be a certain other B, or that the barrel shall contain mackerel, but that the promisee has not lied to the promisor about material facts.

Then the question arises: how do you decide which facts are material? Since the facts are not required by the contract, the only way they can be material is that a belief in their truth is likely to have led to the making of the contract.

It is not true, then, as is sometimes said, that the law does not concern itself with the motives for making contracts. On the contrary, the whole business of fraud outside the contract is the creation of false motives and the removal of true ones. And this gives us a reasonable test of the cases in which fraud will justify rescission. It is said that a fraudulent representation must be material to have that effect. But how are we to decide whether it is material or not? If the argument above is correct, it must be by appealing to ordinary experience, and asking whether a belief that the fact was as represented would naturally have led to the making of the contract, or a contrary belief would naturally have prevented it.

If the belief would not naturally have had that effect, either in general or under the known circumstances of the particular case, the fraud is immaterial. Suppose a man is induced to contract with another by the other's lie that he is a great-grandson of Thomas Jefferson. I do not suppose the contract would be voidable, unless the contractee knew that, for special reasons, his lie would tend to bring the contract about.

The conditions, or grounds for avoiding a contract, that I have dealt with so far concern the conduct of the parties outside the contract itself. I still confine myself to conditions that arise by construction of law—that is, conditions not attached to a promise directly and in so many words by the literal meaning of the language in which it is expressed. I now come to those which concern facts to which the contract does in some way refer.

Such conditions may be found in contracts where the promise is on one side only. It has been said that where the contract is unilateral—a promise by one party only—and all its language is therefore the promisor's, clauses in his favor will be construed as conditions more readily than the same words would be in a bilateral contract. Indeed, it has been said that they must be so construed. For if they do not create a condition they do him no good, since by hypothesis they are not promises by the other party. How far this ingenious suggestion has actually affected doctrine may perhaps be doubted.

But for the purposes of this general survey it will be enough to deal with bilateral contracts, where there are undertakings on both sides, and where the condition implied in favor of one party is that the other shall make good what he, on his part, has undertaken.

The undertakings of a contract may be for the existence of a fact in the present or in the future. Only in the second case can they be promises; but in the first they may be just as essential terms of the bargain.

Here again we come upon the law of representations, but in a new phase. When representations are part of the contract, it is always possible that their truth should be a condition of the contract, entirely apart from any question of fraud. And it often is so in fact. It is not, however, every representation contained in the words used on one side that will make a condition in favor of the other party. Suppose A agrees to sell, and B agrees to buy, "A's seven-year-old sorrel horse Eclipse, now in the possession of B on trial," and in fact the horse is chestnut, not sorrel. I do not suppose that B could refuse to pay for the horse on that ground. If the law were so foolish as to aim at merely formal consistency, it might indeed be said that there was as absolute a repugnancy between the different terms of this contract as in an agreement to sell certain barrels of mackerel where the barrels turned out to contain salt. If that view were adopted, there would not be a contract subject to a condition; there would be no contract at all. But in truth there is a contract, and there is not even a condition. As has been said already, not every repugnancy makes a contract void, and not every failure in the terms of the other side's undertaking makes it voidable. Here it plainly appears that the buyer knows exactly what he is going to get, and therefore the mistake about the color has no bearing on the bargain.

If, on the other hand, a contract contained a representation that was fraudulent, and that misled the party to whom it was made, the contract would be voidable on the same principles as if the representation had been made beforehand. But words of description in a contract are very often held to amount to what is sometimes called a warranty—an undertaking that a fact is as stated—quite apart from fraud. Whether they do or not is a question for the court to decide on grounds of common sense, looking to the meaning of the words, the importance in the transaction of the facts the words convey, and so on. But when words of description are held to be a warranty, the decision means not merely that the party using them binds himself to answer for their truth, but that their truth is a condition of the contract.

For instance, in a leading case, Behn v. Burness, the agreement was that the plaintiff's ship, "now in the port of Amsterdam," should with all possible dispatch proceed direct to Newport, England, and there load a cargo of coal for Hong Kong. The agreement was a charter-party—the contract by which a ship is hired. At the date of the charter-party the vessel was not in Amsterdam, though she arrived there four days later. The plaintiff had notice that the defendant considered time important. It was held that the vessel's presence in the port of Amsterdam at the date of the contract was a condition, and that its breach entitled the defendant to refuse to load and to rescind the contract. Suppose we adopted the view that a condition must be a future event, and that a promise which purports to be conditional on a past or present event is either absolute or no promise at all. It would follow that in this case the defendant had never made a promise; he had only promised if circumstances existed which did not exist. I have already stated my objections to this way of looking at such cases, in Lecture VIII, and will only add that the courts, so far as I am aware, do not sanction it, and certainly did not in this instance.

There is another ground for holding the charter-party void and no contract at all, rather than merely voidable. It is equally against authority, and yet I have never been able to answer it wholly to my satisfaction. In the case just put, the owner's representation concerned the vessel itself, and therefore entered into the description of the thing the charterer agreed to take. I do not quite see why there is not as fatal a repugnancy between the different terms of this contract as was found in the one for the sale of the barrels of salt described as containing mackerel. Why is the repugnancy between those two terms fatal to the existence of a contract—first, that the thing sold is the contents of these barrels, and second, that it is mackerel? It is because each of those terms goes to the very root and essence of the contract. To compel the buyer to take something that answers to one requirement but not the other would be to hold him to do a substantially different thing from what he promised; and so a promise to take one and the same thing answering to both requirements is contradictory in a substantial matter. We have seen that the law does not proceed on any merely logical ground, and does not hold that every slight repugnancy will make a contract even voidable. But on the other hand, when the repugnancy is between terms that are both essential, it is fatal to the very existence of the contract.

How, then, do we decide whether a given term is essential? Surely the best way to find out is to see how the parties have dealt with it. If they have said nothing, we may refer to the speech and dealings of everyday life, and say that if the term's absence would make the subject matter a different thing, its presence is essential to the existence of the agreement. But the parties may agree that anything, however trifling, shall be essential, just as they may agree that anything, however important, shall not be. And if that essential term is part of the contract's description of a specific thing which is also identified by reference to the senses, how can there be a contract in its absence, any more than if the thing were, in popular speech, different in kind from its description? The qualities that make sameness or difference of kind for the purposes of a contract are not determined by Agassiz or Darwin, or by the public at large, but by the will of the parties, which decides that for their purposes the characteristics insisted on are such and such. Now, if this is true, what better evidence can there be that a requirement is essential—that without it the subject matter will be different in kind from the description—than that one party has demanded, and the other has given, a warranty of its presence? Yet the contract's description of the specific vessel as now in the port of Amsterdam, though held to be an implied warranty, does not seem to have been regarded as making the contract repugnant and void, but only as giving the defendant the option of avoiding it. Even an express warranty of quality in a sale does not have that effect; in England, indeed, it does not allow the purchaser to rescind at all in case of breach. On this last point the law of Massachusetts is different.

One explanation offered for the English doctrine about sales is this: once the title has passed, the purchaser has already had some benefit from the contract, and so he cannot wholly restore the seller in statu quo—to the position he was in before—as must be done when a contract is rescinded. This reasoning seems doubtful even as proof that the contract is not voidable, and it has no bearing at all on the argument that it is void. For if the contract is void, the title does not pass.

It might be said that there is no repugnancy in the charterer's promise, because he promises only to load a certain ship, and the words "now in the port of Amsterdam" are mere history by the time for loading comes, and no part of the description of the vessel he promised to load. But the moment those words are held to be essential, they become part of the description. The promise is then to load a certain vessel which is named the Martaban and which was in the port of Amsterdam at the date of the contract. So interpreted, it is repugnant.

Probably the true solution is to be found in practical considerations. At any rate, the fact is that the law has established three degrees in the effect of repugnancy. If one of the repugnant terms is wholly insignificant, it is simply disregarded, or at most will ground a claim for damages. The law would be reluctant to hold a contract void for repugnancy in terms describing the present, when, if the same terms had only been promised, a failure of one of them would not justify a refusal to perform on the other side. If, on the other hand, both terms are of the utmost importance—so that enforcing the rest of the promise or bargain without one of them would not merely deprive one party of a stipulated incident but would force a substantially different bargain on him—the promise will be void. There is an intermediate class of cases in which it is left to the disappointed party to decide. But since the lines between the three are this vague, it is not surprising that different jurisdictions have drawn them differently.

The examples I have given of undertakings for a present state of facts have been confined to those touching the present condition of the subject matter of the contract. Of course there is no such limit to their use. A contract may warrant the existence of other facts as well. Examples of this kind could probably be found or imagined in which it would be clear that the only effect of the warranty was to attach a condition to the contract in favor of the other side, and in which the question would not arise whether there was something more than a condition—a repugnancy that prevented any contract from being formed at all. But the illustrations already given are enough for the present purpose.

We may now pass from undertakings that certain facts are true at the time the contract is made to undertakings that certain facts shall be true at some later time—that is, to promises properly so called. The question is when performance of the promise on one side is a condition of the obligation of the contract on the other. In practice this question tends to be treated as the same as another which, as has been shown earlier, is a distinct point: namely, when performance on one side is a condition of the right to call for performance on the other. It is of course conceivable that a promise should be limited to the case where the things promised on the other side are performed, and yet that a failure of the other side should not justify rescinding the contract. Wherever one party has already received a substantial benefit under a contract, of a kind that cannot be given back, it is too late to rescind, however serious a breach the other side may commit later. Yet he may be excused from going any further. Suppose a contract is made for a month's labor: ten dollars to be paid down, not to be recovered except if the contract is rescinded for the laborer's fault, and thirty dollars at the end of the month. If the laborer wrongfully stops work at the end of two weeks, I do not suppose that the contract could be rescinded and the ten dollars recovered as money had and received—the action for money that in fairness belongs to the plaintiff. But on the other hand, the employer would not be bound to pay the thirty dollars, and of course he could sue for damages on the contract.

For the most part, however, a breach of promise that releases the promisee from further performance on his side will also justify rescission, so that no great harm is done by the popular confusion of the two questions. Where the promise to perform on one side is limited to the case of performance on the other, the contract is generally conditioned on that performance too. In what follows I shall take up the cases I wish to notice without stopping to consider whether the contract was, in the strict sense, conditioned on performance of the promise on one side, or whether the true construction was merely that the promise on the other side was limited to that event.

Now, how do we settle whether such a condition exists? It is easy to go wrong by seeking too eagerly for simplicity, and by trying too hard to reduce all cases to artificial presumptions, which are less obvious than the decisions they are supposed to explain. The foundation of the whole matter is, after all, good sense, as the courts have often said. The law means to carry out the intention of the parties. So far as they have not provided for the event that has happened, the law has to say what they would naturally have intended if their minds had been turned to the point. It will be found that decisions based on the direct implications of the language used, and decisions based on a remoter inference of what the parties must have meant, or would have said if they had spoken, shade into each other by imperceptible degrees.

Mr. Langdell has called attention to a very important principle, and one which no doubt throws light on many decisions. It is this: in a bilateral contract, although the consideration for each promise is the counter-promise, yet on the face of it the payment for the performance of one is the performance of the other. The other party's performance is what each means to have in return for his own. If A promises B a barrel of flour, and B promises A ten dollars for it, A means to have the ten dollars for his flour, and B means to have the flour for his ten dollars. If no time is set for either act, neither can call on the other to perform without being ready to perform at the same time himself.

But this principle of equivalence is not the only one to be drawn even from the form of contracts, without considering their subject matter; and of course Mr. Langdell's work does not offer it as the only one.

Another very clear principle is found in contracts for the sale or lease of a thing, and the like. Here the qualities or characteristics that the owner promises the thing shall have go to describe the thing the buyer promises to accept. If any of the promised traits are missing in the thing offered, the buyer may refuse to accept it—not merely on the ground that he has not been offered the equivalent for keeping his promise, but also on the ground that he never promised to accept what is offered him. We have seen that where the contract contains a statement about the condition of the thing at a time earlier than the moment for accepting it, the past condition may not always be held to enter into the description of the thing to be accepted. But no such escape is possible here. Still, there are limits to the right of refusal even in this class of cases. If the thing promised is specific, the dominance of that part of the description which identifies the object by reference to the senses is sometimes strikingly shown. One case has gone so far as to hold that performance of an executory contract—one still to be carried out—to purchase a specific thing cannot be refused because the thing fails to come up to the warranted quality.

Footnote: Possibly Behn v. Burness, stated above, might have been dealt with in this way. The ship offered was not a ship that had been in the port of Amsterdam at the date of the contract. It was therefore not such a ship as the contract called for.

Another principle of dependency to be drawn from the form of the contract itself is that performance of the promise on one side may be plainly intended to furnish the means for performing the promise on the other. If a tenant promised to make repairs, and the landlord promised to supply him with wood for the purpose, it is believed that today, whatever the old decisions may have been, the tenant's duty to repair would depend on the landlord's supplying the material when it was called for.

Footnote: Mr. Langdell puts this as a case of equivalence, but I believe the explanation above is the true one. It will be noticed that this is hardly a true case of condition, but merely a limitation of the scope of the tenant's promise. So a covenant to serve as an apprentice in a trade, which the other party covenants to teach, can only be performed if the other will teach, and must therefore be limited to that event.

Another case, of a somewhat exceptional kind, is where a party to a bilateral contract agrees to do certain things and to give security for his performance. Here it is plain good sense to hold that giving the security is a condition of performance on the other side, if that is possible. For the demand for security shows that the party making it was not content to rely on the other side's bare promise. He would be forced to rely on it if he had to perform before the security was given, and so the very object of requiring it would be defeated.

This last case suggests what is very forcibly impressed on anyone who studies the cases: that, after all, the most important element of decision is not any technical principle of contracts, or even any general one, but a consideration of the nature of the particular transaction as a practical matter. Suppose A promises B to do a day's work for two dollars, and B promises A to pay two dollars for a day's work. Here the two promises cannot be performed at the same time. The work will take all day; the payment, half a minute. How are you to decide which is to be done first—that is, which promise depends on performance by the other side? Only by reference to the habits of the community and to convenience. It is not enough to say that on the principle of equivalence a man is not presumed to mean to pay for a thing until he has it. The work is payment for the money as much as the money is payment for the work, and one of them must be paid for in advance. The question is why, if one man is not presumed to mean to pay money until he has money's worth, the other is presumed to mean to give money's worth before he has money. No answer can be got from any general theory. The fact that employers as a class can be trusted for wages more safely than the employed can be trusted for their labor, or that the employers have had the power and have been the lawmakers, or other considerations—it does not matter which—have determined that the work is to be done first.
