But the grounds of decision are purely practical, and can never be drawn out of grammar or logic.

A reference to practical considerations will be found running all through the subject. Take another instance. The plaintiff declared on a mutual agreement between himself and the defendant that he would sell, and the defendant would buy, certain Donskoy wool, to be shipped by the plaintiff at Odessa and delivered in England. Among the terms of the contract was one that the names of the vessels should be declared as soon as the wool was shipped. The defense was that the wool had been bought, as both parties knew, to be resold in the course of the defendant's business; that it was an article of fluctuating value, and could not be sold until the names of the ships carrying it had been declared as the contract provided; and that the plaintiff did not declare the names of the vessels as agreed. The court's decision was given by one of the greatest technical lawyers who ever lived, Baron Parke. Yet he never dreamed of giving any technical or merely logical reason for it. After stating the facts deemed material to the question whether declaring the names of the vessels was a condition of the duty to accept—in the words just given—he stated the ground of decision thus: "Looking at the nature of the contract, and the great importance of it to the object with which the contract was entered into with the knowledge of both parties, we think it was a condition precedent."

Footnote: Mr. Langdell says that a bought note—the broker's written note of a purchase—though part of a bilateral contract, is to be treated as unilateral; that it may be presumed the language of the contract relied on was that of a bought note; and that it was therefore a condition in favor of the defendant, who made it. I do not quite understand how this can be assumed, when the declaration states a bilateral contract, and the question arose on demurrer to a plea which also states that the plaintiff "was by the agreement bound to declare" the names. How remote this explanation is from the actual ground of decision will be seen.

Lecture X: Successions after Death

In the Lecture on Possession, I tried to show that the idea of possessing a right as such was absurd in itself. All rights are consequences attached to filling some situation of fact. A right that can be acquired by possession differs from others only in this: the situation it is attached to is of a kind that can be filled by different persons one after another, or by anyone at all without regard to whether he fills it lawfully—as is the case where the situation consists in having a tangible object within one's power.

When the law recognizes a right of this sort, there is no difficulty in transferring it. Or, more accurately, there is no difficulty in different persons enjoying similar rights over the same thing one after another. If A, who possesses a horse or a field, gives up the possession to B, the rights B acquires stand on the same ground as A's did before. The facts from which A's rights sprang have ceased to be true of A, and are now true of B. The consequences the law attaches to those facts now exist for B, as they did for A before. The situation of fact from which the rights spring is a continuing one, and anyone who occupies it, no matter how, has the rights attached to it. But there is no possession possible of a contract. The fact that yesterday A gave B a consideration and received a promise in return cannot be laid hold of by X and transferred from A to himself. The only thing that can be transferred is the benefit or the burden of the promise—and how can these be separated from the facts that gave rise to them? How, in short, can a man sue or be sued on a promise in which he had no part?

Up to this point, in dealing with any particular right or obligation, it has been assumed that the facts from which it sprang were true of the person entitled or bound. But it often happens, especially in modern law, that a person acquires a special right and is allowed to enforce it, although the facts that give rise to it are not true of him, or are true of him only in part. One of the chief problems of the law is to explain the machinery by which this result has been brought about.

It will be observed that the problem does not cover the whole field of rights. Some rights cannot be transferred by any device or contrivance—for instance, a man's right to bodily safety or to his reputation. Others, again, are incidents of possession, and within the limits of that idea no other is needed. As Savigny said, "Succession does not apply to possession by itself."

But the idea of possession will carry us only a very little way toward understanding the modern theory of transfer. That theory depends very largely on the idea of succession, to use the word just quoted from Savigny, and so successions will be the subject of this Lecture and the next. I shall begin by explaining the theory of succession to persons who have died. When that is done, I shall pass to the theory of transfer between living people, and shall consider whether any relation can be established between the two.

The first is easily shown to rest on a fictitious identification between the dead man and his successor. As a first step toward the further discussion, and also for its own sake, I shall briefly state the evidence concerning the executor (the person named in a will to settle the dead man's affairs), the heir (the person on whom land descends when its owner dies), and the devisee (the person to whom land is left by will). In order to understand the theory of our law about the first of these, at least, scholars agree that it is necessary to consider the structure and position of the Roman family as it was in the infancy of Roman society.

Continental jurists have long been collecting the evidence that in the earlier periods of Roman and German law alike, the unit of society was the family. The Twelve Tables still recognize the interest of the lower members of the family in the family property. Heirs are called sui heredes—that is, heirs of themselves, or of their own property—as Gaius explains. The Roman jurist Paulus says that they are regarded as owners in a certain sense even in their father's lifetime, and that after his death they do not so much receive an inheritance as obtain the full power of dealing with their own property.

Starting from this point, it is easy to understand how heirs succeeded to a dead paterfamilias in the Roman system. If the family owned the property that the paterfamilias administered, its rights were unaffected by the death of its temporary head. The family went on, although the head died. And when, probably by a gradual change, the paterfamilias came to be regarded as the owner instead of a mere manager of the family's rights, the nature and continuity of those rights did not change along with the title to them. The familia, the household and all that belonged to it, passed on to the heirs as the ancestor had left it. The heir succeeded not to the ownership of this or that thing separately, but to the whole hereditas, the headship of the family, with certain rights of property attached to it. And of course he took this headship, this right of representing the family's interests, subject to whatever changes the last manager had made.

The whole bundle of the ancestor's rights and duties—or, to use the technical phrase, the total persona he sustained—was easily separated from his natural personality. For this persona was only the sum of what had once been family rights and duties, and originally any individual sustained it only as head of the family. Hence it was said to be carried on by the inheritance, and when the heir took it up, he could bring an action for injuries committed before he did.

So the Roman heir came to be treated as identified with his ancestor for the purposes of the law. And so it is clear how, in that instance, the impossible transfers I am trying to explain were accomplished. Rights to which B, as B, could show no title, he could readily maintain under the fiction that he was the same person as A, whose title nobody denied.

It is not necessary at this point to study family rights in the German tribes. For no one disputes that the modern executor gets his characteristics from the Roman heir. Wills, too, were borrowed from Rome; the Germans whom Tacitus describes did not know them. Administrators—persons appointed to settle an estate where there is no executor—were a later imitation of executors, introduced by statute for cases where there was no will, or where for any other reason there were no executors.

The executor has the legal title to the whole of the testator's personal property, and, generally speaking, the power to dispose of it. Formerly he was entitled to the residue—whatever was left after the bequests had been paid out—if the will did not dispose of it. He took it, one may fairly guess, not as a legatee of those particular chattels, but because he represented the person of the testator, and therefore had all the rights the testator would have had after the distribution if he had been alive. Nowadays the residue is generally left by the will itself, but even now it is not regarded as a specific gift of the chattels that remain undisposed of. I cannot help thinking that this doctrine echoes the one under which the executor took the residue in former times.

No such rule has governed residuary devises of land—gifts by will of "all the rest" of a man's real estate. In England these have always been held to be specific, down to the present day. So if a devise of land should fail, that land would not pass under the residuary clause, but would descend to the heir as if there had been no will.

Again, the appointment of an executor relates back to the date of the testator's death: it is treated as if it had taken effect at that moment. The continuity of the person is preserved by this fiction, as in Rome it was preserved by personifying the inheritance ad interim, in the meantime.

Enough has been said to show the likeness between our executor and the Roman heir. And bearing in mind what was said about the heres, the Roman heir, it will easily be seen how it came to be said, as it often was in the old books, that the executor "represents the person of his testator." The meaning of this feigned identity has been found in history, but we must also appreciate the help it gave in overcoming a technical difficulty. If the executor represents the person of the testator, there is no longer any trouble about allowing him to sue or be sued on his testator's contracts. In the reign of Edward III, when an action of covenant was brought against executors, Persay objected: "I never heard that one should have a writ of covenant against executors, nor against other person but the very one who made the covenant, for a man cannot oblige another person to a covenant by his deed except him who was party to the covenant." But it is useless to object that the promise sued on was made by A, the testator, and not by B, the executor, when the law says that for this purpose B is A. Here, then, is one class of cases in which a transfer is accomplished with the help of a fiction—a fiction that reflects, as fictions so often do, the facts of an early stage of society, and that could hardly have been invented if those facts had been otherwise.

Executors and administrators are the chief, if not the only, example of universal succession in English law—succession to a dead man's affairs as a whole, rather than to this or that thing. But although they succeed per universitatem, to the whole, in the way just explained, they do not succeed to every kind of property. The personal property goes to them, but land takes another course. All real estate not disposed of by will goes to the heir, and the rules of inheritance are quite distinct from those that govern the distribution of chattels. So the question arises whether the English heir, the successor to real estate, shows the same likeness to the Roman heres as the executor does.

The English heir is not a universal successor. Each and every parcel of land descends as a separate and specific thing. Nevertheless, within his narrower sphere he unquestionably represents the person of his ancestor. Different opinions have been held about whether the same was true in early German law. Dr. Laband says that it was; Sohm takes the opposite view. It is commonly supposed that among the German tribes ownership by the family, at least of land, came before ownership by individuals, and it has been shown how naturally representation followed from a similar state of things at Rome. But there is no need to consider whether our law on this subject is of German or Roman origin, because the principle of identification has clearly prevailed from the time of Glanvill to the present day. If the Germans did not know it, it is plainly accounted for by the influence of the Roman law. If there was anything of the sort in the Salic Law, it was no doubt due to natural causes like those that gave rise to the principle at Rome. But either way, I cannot doubt that the modern doctrine has taken a good deal of its form, and perhaps some of its substance, from the mature system of the civilians, in whose language it was expressed for so long. For the same reasons, there is also no need to weigh the evidence of the Anglo-Saxon sources, although several passages in their laws make it fairly clear that there was some identification.

As late as Bracton, two centuries after the Norman Conquest, the heir was not the successor to lands alone, but represented his ancestor in a much more general sense, as will be seen directly. The Anglo-Saxons did not know the office of executor in the sense of an heir, and even in Bracton's time it does not seem to have been what it has since become. So there is no need to go back further than the early Norman period, after the appointment of executors had become common and the heir was more nearly what he is now.

When Glanvill wrote, a little more than a century after the Conquest, the heir was bound to warrant the reasonable gifts his ancestor had made—that is, to guarantee them to the grantees and their heirs. And if the ancestor's goods were not enough to pay his debts, the heir was bound to make up the shortfall out of his own property. Neither Glanvill nor his Scottish imitator, the Regiam Majestatem, limits this liability to the amount of property the heir inherited from the same source. That makes the identification of heir and ancestor as complete as it was in the Roman law before Justinian introduced such a limit. On the other hand, a century later, Bracton makes it plain that the heir was bound only so far as property had descended to him, and the same limit appears in the early sources of the Continent, Norman and others. The heir's liabilities were probably shrinking. Britton and Fleta, the imitators of Bracton, and perhaps Bracton himself, say that an heir is not bound to pay his ancestor's debt unless the ancestor's deed specially binds him to it. The later law required that the heir be named in the deed if he was to be held.

But at any rate, in Bracton's time the identification of heir and ancestor still came close to a universal succession, as another statement of his shows. He asks whether a testator can leave his rights of action by will, and answers no, as far as concerns debts that were not proved and recovered in the testator's lifetime. Actions of that sort belong to the heirs, and must be sued in the secular court. For until such debts are recovered in the proper court, the executor cannot proceed for them in the Church's court.

This shows that the identification worked both ways. The heir was liable for the debts his ancestor owed, and he could recover the debts owed to his ancestor, until the executor took his place in the King's Courts as well as in those of the Church. Within the limits just explained, the heir was also bound to warrant property his ancestor had sold, to the buyer and the buyer's heirs. After this evidence that the modern heir began by representing his ancestor generally, it is not necessary to hunt for expressions in later books, since his position has since been narrowed. But just as we have seen that the executor is still said to represent the person of his testator, so in the time of Edward I the heir was said to represent the person of his ancestor. And at a much later date it was said that "the heir is in representation in point of taking by inheritance eadam persona cum antecessore"—the same persona as his ancestor.

A great judge who died only a few years ago repeats language that would have been just as familiar to the lawyers of Edward or of James. Baron Parke lays down that in general a party is not required to make profert of an instrument—to produce it in court—when he is not entitled to possess it. Then he says that there is an exception "in the cases of heir and executor, who may plead a release to the ancestor or testator whom they respectively represent; so also with respect to several tortfeasors, for in all these cases there is a privity between the parties which constitutes an identity of person." (Tortfeasors are wrongdoers; privity is a legal relation between persons that the law treats as binding one by the acts of the other.)

But this is not all. The identity of person was carried further still. If a man died owning land in fee—that is, as an estate of inheritance—and left sons, the land went to the eldest son alone. But if he left only daughters, it descended to them all equally. In that case several individuals together carried on their ancestor's persona. Yet it was always laid down that they were only one heir. To work out this result, not only was one person identified with another, but several persons were reduced to one, so that they might sustain a single persona.

Footnote: The same thing was said where there were several executors: "They are only in the place of one person."

What was the persona? It was not the sum of all the ancestor's rights and duties. We have seen that for many centuries his general status—the sum of all his rights and duties except those connected with real property—has been taken up by the executor or administrator. The persona carried on by the heir was confined from an early day to real estate in its technical sense: that is, to property governed by feudal principles, as distinguished from chattels, which, as Blackstone tells us, include whatever was not a feud, a feudal holding.

But the heir's persona was not even the sum of all the ancestor's rights and duties connected with real estate. It has been said already that every fee descends as a specific thing, and not as part of some larger universitas, some whole. This appears not so much from the fact that different parcels might be governed by different rules of descent, so that the same person would not be heir to both, as from the very nature of feudal property. Under the feudal system in its full strength, holding land was only one incident of a complex personal relation. The land was forfeited for failure to render the services for which it was granted; the tenant could renounce the service if the lord broke the duties he owed in return. It rather seems that at the beginning of the feudal period, under Charlemagne, a man could hold land of only one lord. Even when it had become common to hold of more than one, the strict personal relation was modified only enough to save the tenant from having to perform services that conflicted. Glanvill and Bracton tell us that a tenant who held of several lords was to do homage—the ceremony by which a tenant became his lord's man—for each fee, but to reserve his allegiance for the lord of whom he held his chief estate. But if the different lords should make war on each other, and the chief lord should command the tenant to serve him in person, the tenant ought to obey, saving the service due to the other lord for the fee he held of him.

We see, then, that the tenant had a distinct persona, or status, in respect of each of the fees he held. The rights and duties attached to one of them had no relation to the rights and duties attached to another. A succession to one had no connection with the succession to another. Each succession was the taking up of a distinct personal relation, in which the successor was to be determined by the terms of that relation.

The persona we are trying to define is the estate. Every fee is a distinct persona, a distinct hereditas, or inheritance, as it has been called since the time of Bracton. We have already seen that it may be sustained by more than one person where there are several heirs, as well as by one, just as a corporation may have more members or fewer. But it may not only be divided lengthwise, so to speak, among persons interested in the same way at the same time. It may also be cut across into successive interests, to be enjoyed one after another. In technical language, it may be divided into a particular estate—a lesser interest such as an estate for life—and remainders, the interests that follow it. But they are all parts of the same fee, and the same fiction still governs them. We read in an old case that "he in reversion and particular tenant are but one tenant." (The one in reversion is the person to whom the land comes back when the lesser estate ends.) This is only a statement of counsel, to be sure. But it was made to account for a doctrine that seems to need the explanation: that after the death of the tenant for life, the one in reversion might bring error or attaint—a proceeding to overturn a false verdict by a jury—against an erroneous judgment or false verdict given against the tenant for life.

To sum up the results so far: the heir of modern English law gets his characteristic features from the law as it stood soon after the Conquest. At that time he was a universal successor in a very broad sense. Many of his functions as such were soon transferred to the executor. The heir's rights came to be confined to real estate, and his liabilities to those connected with real estate and to obligations of his ancestor that expressly bound him. The succession to each fee, or feudal inheritance, is distinct; it is not part of the sum of all the ancestor's rights regarded as one whole. But to this day the executor in his sphere, and the heir in his, represent the person of the dead man, and are treated as if they were one with him for the purpose of settling their rights and obligations.

The bearing this has on the contracts of the dead man has been pointed out. But its influence is not confined to contract; it runs through everything. The most striking instance, however, is the acquisition of rights by prescription. Take the case of a right of way. A right of way over a neighbor's land can be acquired only by grant, or by using it adversely—as of right, without the owner's leave—for twenty years. A man uses a way for ten years and dies. Then his heir uses it for ten years. Has any right been acquired? If common sense alone is consulted, the answer must be no. The ancestor did not get any right, because he did not use the way long enough. And just as little did the heir. How can it improve the heir's title that another man trespassed before him? Clearly, if four strangers to each other used the way for five years each, the last would acquire no right. But here the fiction that has been so carefully explained comes in. From the point of view of the law, it is not two persons who have each used the way for ten years, but one who has used it for twenty. The heir has the advantage of sustaining his ancestor's persona, and the right is acquired.

Successions Inter Vivos

I now come to the most difficult and obscure part of the subject. It remains to be discovered whether the fiction of identity was extended to anyone besides the heir and the executor. And if we find, as we do, that in express terms it went only a little further, the question will still arise whether the way of thinking, and the ideas made possible by the doctrine of inheritance, have not silently changed the law of dealings between the living. It seems to me demonstrable that their influence has been profound, and that without understanding the theory of inheritance it is impossible to understand the theory of transfer inter vivos—between living persons.

The difficulty in dealing with the subject is to convince the sceptic that there is anything to explain. Nowadays the idea that a right is valuable is almost the same as the idea that it can be turned into money by selling it. But it was not always so. Before you can sell a right, you must be able to make a sale thinkable in legal terms. At the beginning of this Lecture I put the case of transferring a contract. I have just mentioned the case of gaining a right by prescription when neither party has met the requirement of twenty years' adverse use. In that instance there is not even a right at the time of the transfer, but only the bare fact of ten years' past trespassing. A way, until it becomes a right of way, is just as little capable of being held by a possessory title as a contract is. If, then, a contract can be sold, and if a buyer can add the time of his seller's adverse use to his own, what is the machinery by which the law works out the result?

The most superficial acquaintance with any system of law in its earlier stages will show with what difficulty, and by what slow degrees, such machinery has been provided, and how the lack of it has narrowed the range of things that could be alienated, or transferred. It is a great mistake to assume that it is a mere matter of common sense that the buyer steps into the shoes of the seller, to use our telling metaphor. Suppose that sales and other civil transfers had kept the form of capture in war that they seem to have had in the infancy of Roman law—a form that was partly kept in at least one instance, the acquiring of wives, after the transaction had in fact taken the more civilized shape of a purchase. The idea that the buyer came in as the seller's adversary would probably have gone along with the fiction of a hostile taking, and the buyer would have stood on his own position as founding a new title. Without the help of ideas drawn from some other source, it would have been hard to work out a legal transfer of objects that did not admit of possession.

Footnote: In the American Law Review for October 1872 I mentioned one or two signs of this fact. But I have since had the satisfaction of finding it worked out in such detail and with such learning in Ihering's Spirit of the Roman Law that I cannot do better than refer to that work—only adding that for my purposes it is not necessary to go as far as Ihering, and that he does not seem to have been led to the conclusions it is my object to establish.

A possible source of such other ideas was to be found in family law. The principles of inheritance supplied a fiction and a way of thinking that might at least have been extended into other fields. To prove that they were in fact so extended, it will be necessary to examine the law of Rome once more, as well as what remains of German and Anglo-Saxon customs.

I will take up first the German and Anglo-Saxon laws, which are the ancestors of our own on one side of the house. For although what we get from those sources is not in the direct line of the argument, it lays a foundation for it by showing the course of development in different fields.

The obvious analogy between purchaser and heir seems to have been used in the folk-laws of the Germanic peoples, but mainly for a different purpose from those we shall have to consider in the English law. The purpose was to widen the range of things that could be alienated—given or sold away. It will be remembered that there are many traces of family ownership in early German law, as there are in early Roman law. And it seems that property which originally could not be given outside the family came to be transferred by the device of making the grantee an heir.

The history of language points to this conclusion. The Latin word heres, as Beseler and others have remarked, first meant a successor to the property of a dead person. It was then extended to the donee mortis causa—the person who receives a gift made in contemplation of death—and more broadly still to grantees in general. The verb hereditare was used in the same way for the transfer of land. Laferrière quotes Hevin as pointing out that the ancient usage in French was to say heriter, "to inherit," for buying, heritier, "heir," for the buyer, and desheriter, "to disinherit," for selling.

The texts of the Salic Law give us evidence that cannot be disputed. A man could transfer the whole or any part of his property by delivering possession of it to a trustee, who within twelve months handed it over to the beneficiaries—to those, the text reads, "whom the donor has named heirs" (quos heredes appellavit). Here, then, was a voluntary transfer of as much or as little property as the owner pleased, to persons freely chosen, who were not necessarily universal successors, if they ever were, and who nevertheless took under the name of heirs. The word, which must at first have meant persons taking by descent, was extended to persons taking by purchase. If the word grew in meaning, it was probably because the thought it carried was being turned to new uses. The transaction seems to have stood halfway between the naming of an heir and a sale. The later law of the Ripuarian Franks treats it more plainly as the naming of an heir. It allows a man who has no sons to give all his property to whomever he chooses, relatives or strangers, as an inheritance—either by adfathamire, as the Salic form was called, or by writing, or by delivery.

The Lombards had a similar transfer, in which the donee was not only called heres but was made liable, like an heir, for the donor's debts when he received the property after the donor's death. By the Salic Law, a man who could not pay the wergeld—the price of a life, owed by a killer to the dead man's kin—was allowed to make a formal transfer of his house-lot, and the liability went with it. But that transfer was to the next of kin.

Footnote: This, no doubt, was due to Roman influence. But it recalls what Sir Henry Maine quotes from Elphinstone's History of India about a sale by a member of one of the village communities: "The purchaser steps exactly into his place, and takes up all his obligations."

The house-lot, or family curtilage—the enclosed ground around the dwelling—at first passed strictly within the family. Here again, at least in England, freedom to alienate seems to have grown up by a gradual widening of the choice of successors. The early charters are few, but if we may trust the order of development they seem to show—and it is hard to believe the order is accidental—royal grants at first allowed the grantee to choose his heirs from among his kindred, and then extended the choice beyond them. In a deed of the year 679 the language is, "as it is granted so do you hold it and your posterity." One of a century later reads, "which let him always possess, and after his death leave to which of his heirs he will." Another says, "and after him with free power (of choice) leave to the man of his kin to whom he wishes to" (leave it). A charter of 736, somewhat earlier, goes a step further: "So that as long as he lives he shall have the power of holding and possessing (and) of leaving it to whomsoever he choose, either in his lifetime, or certainly after his death." At the beginning of the ninth century the donee has power to leave the property to whomever he will; or, in still broader terms, to exchange or grant it in his lifetime and after his death to leave it to whom he chooses; or to sell, exchange, and leave it to whatever heir he chooses. This choice of heirs recalls the "whom he has named heirs" of the Salic Law just mentioned. It may be compared with the language of a Norman charter of about the year 1190: "To W. and his heirs, to wit those whom he may constitute his heirs."

Footnote: The phrase "let him leave it to whichever heir he will" is very common in the later charters. It may be broader than "to whichever of his heirs he will."

A perfect example of a singular succession—a succession to one specific thing rather than to a whole estate—worked out by the fiction of kinship is found in the story of Burnt Njal. This Icelandic saga gives us a living picture of a society hardly more advanced than the Salian Franks as we see them in the Salic Law. A lawsuit was to be transferred by the proper plaintiff to another man who knew the laws better and was better able to carry it on—in fact, to an attorney. But a lawsuit at that time was the alternative to a feud, and both were the private affair of the family concerned. So when a suit for the killing of a member of the family was to be handed over to a stranger, the innovation had to be reconciled with the theory that such a suit belonged only to the next of kin. Mord is to take over Thorgeir's suit against Flosi for killing Helgi, and the form of transfer is described as follows.

Footnote: Under the Welsh laws, the champion in a cause decided by combat acquired the rights of the next of kin, the next of kin being the proper champion.

"Then Mord took Thorgeir by the hand and named two witnesses to bear witness, 'that Thorgeir Thofir's son hands me over a suit for manslaughter against Flosi Thord's son, to plead it for the slaying of Helgi Njal's son, with all those proofs which have to follow the suit. Thou handest over to me this suit to plead and to settle, and to enjoy all rights in it, as though I were the rightful next of kin. Thou handest it over to me by law; and I take it from thee by law.'" Afterwards these witnesses come before the court and bear witness to the transfer in the same words: "He handed over to him then this suit, with all the proofs and proceedings which belonged to the suit, he handed it over to him to plead and to settle, and to make use of all rights, as though he were the rightful next of kin. Thorgeir handed it over lawfully, and Mord took it lawfully." The suit went on, despite the change of hands, as if the next of kin were the plaintiff. A later step in the proceedings shows this. The defendant challenges two members of the court on the ground of their connection with Mord, the transferee, by blood and by baptism. But Mord answers that this is no good challenge; for "he challenged them not for their kinship to the true plaintiff, the next of kin, but for their kinship to him who pleaded the suit." And the other side had to admit that Mord was right in his law.

I now turn from the German sources to the Roman. These bear most closely on the argument, because much of the doctrine found there has been transplanted unchanged into modern law.

The early Roman law recognized as relatives only those who would have been members of the same patriarchal family, under the same patriarchal authority, if the common ancestor had still been alive. Since wives passed into their husbands' families and lost all connection with the family they were born into, relationship through women was excluded altogether. The heir was someone who traced his relationship to the dead man through males alone. As civilization advanced, this rule was changed. The praetor—the Roman magistrate in charge of the courts—gave the benefits of the inheritance to the blood relations, even though they were not heirs and could not be admitted to the succession under the ancient law. But the change was not made by repealing the old law, which lived on under the name of the jus civile, the civil law proper. The new principle was fitted to the old forms by a fiction. The blood relation could sue on the fiction that he was an heir, although in fact he was not one.

One of the early ways of naming an heir was a sale of the familia—the headship of the family, with all its rights and duties—to the intended heir. This sale of the universitas, the whole bundle of rights and duties taken together, was later extended beyond inheritance to bankruptcy, when it was desired to put the bankrupt's property into the hands of a trustee for distribution. This trustee, too, could use the fiction, and sue as if he had been the bankrupt's heir. One of the great jurists tells us that, in general, universal successors stand in the place of heirs.

The Roman heir, with one or two exceptions, was always a universal successor; and the fiction of heirship, as such, could hardly be used with propriety except to widen the range of universal successions. But as far as it went, every consequence attached to the original fiction of identity between heir and ancestor followed as a matter of course.

To return to the case of rights acquired by prescription: every universal successor could add the time of his predecessor's adverse use to his own in order to make out the right. Legally speaking there was no adding at all, but one continuous possession.

The express fiction of inheritance perhaps stopped there. But when a similar joining of times was allowed between a legatee or devisee (legatarius)—one who takes a specific thing under a will—and his testator, the same explanation was given. It was said that when a specific thing was left to a person by will, the legatee was in a certain sense quasi an heir—"as if" an heir—so far as having the benefit of the testator's time in possession toward acquiring a title. Yet a legatarius was not a universal successor, and for most purposes stood in sharp contrast with such successors.

Thus the strict law of inheritance had made familiar the notion that one man might have the advantage of a position filled by another, even though he had not filled it himself, or had filled it only in part. And the second fiction, by which a legal heir's privileges, in this respect and in others, had been extended to other persons, broke down the walls that might otherwise have confined those privileges to a single case. A new conception had entered the law, and nothing stood in the way of applying it further. As has been shown, it was applied in so many words to a sale of the universitas for business purposes, and to at least one case in which the succession was limited to a single specific thing. Why, then, might not every gift or sale be treated as a succession, far enough to secure the same advantages?

The joining of times to make out a title was soon allowed between buyer and seller. From the language the Roman lawyers always used, I have no doubt that it was reached in the way I have suggested. A passage from Scaevola (B.C. 30) will be proof enough. Joinder of possessions, he says—that is, the right to add the time of one's predecessor's holding to one's own—plainly belongs to those who succeed to the place of others, whether by contract or by will. For heirs, and those who are treated as holding the place of successors, are allowed to add their testator's possession to their own. Accordingly, if you sell me a slave, I shall have the benefit of your holding.

The joining of times is given to those who succeed to the place of another. Ulpian quotes a similar phrase from a jurist of the time of the Antonines: "to whose place I have succeeded by inheritance, or purchase, or any other right." Succedere in locum aliorum, "to succeed to the place of others," like sustinere personam, "to sustain the persona," is the Roman lawyers' expression for those continuations of one man's legal position by another of which the model was the succession of heir to ancestor. Succedere by itself is used to mean "inherit," and successio to mean "inheritance." The succession above all others was the inheritance; and it is believed that hardly an instance will be found in the Roman sources where "succession" does not carry that analogy and point to the taking over, at least in part, of a persona formerly sustained by someone else. It plainly does so in the passage before us.

But the succession that allows a joining of times is not hereditary succession alone. In the passage just cited, Scaevola says that it may be by contract or purchase as well as by inheritance or will. It may be singular as well as universal. The jurists often set universal successions against those confined to a single specific thing. Ulpian says that a man succeeds to another's place whether his succession is universal or to the single object.

If further evidence were needed for the present argument, it would be found in another expression of Ulpian's. He speaks of the benefit of joinder as derived from the persona of the grantor: "He to whom a thing is granted shall have the benefit of joinder from the persona of his grantor." A benefit cannot be derived from a persona except by sustaining it.

Footnote: Other cases Ulpian puts may rest on a different fiction. After a precarium ends, for instance—a holding at the owner's pleasure, revocable whenever he likes—the land is treated by a fiction as never having been possessed by the holder at all.

It further appears fairly plainly from Justinian's Institutes and the Digest that the benefit was not extended to purchasers in all cases until quite a late period.

Savigny came very near the truth when he said, somewhat broadly, that "every accessio, for whatever purpose, presupposes nothing else than a relation of juridical succession between the previous and present possessor. For succession does not apply to possession by itself." The accessio is the joining of times. And I may add, by way of further explanation, that every relation of juridical succession presupposes either an inheritance or a relation to which, as far as it goes, the analogies of the inheritance may be applied.

The way of thinking that led to the accessio, or joining of times, is equally visible elsewhere. The time during which a former owner failed to use an easement was charged to the person who had succeeded to his place. The defense that the plaintiff had sold and delivered the thing in dispute was open not only to the purchaser, but to his heirs, or to a second purchaser even before delivery to him, against the seller's successors, whether universal or successors only to the thing in question. If someone used a way wrongfully as against the predecessor in title, the use was wrongful as against the successor too, whether by inheritance, purchase, or any other right. The formal oath of a party to an action was conclusive in favor of his successors, universal or singular. Successors by purchase or gift had the benefit of agreements made with the seller. A multitude of general expressions show that for most purposes, whether in attack or defense, the buyer stood in the shoes of the seller, to use the metaphor of our own law. And what matters more than the result, which could often have been reached by other routes, is that the language and the analogies are drawn throughout from succession to the inheritance.

Footnote: The passage about the unused easement seems to be written of a rural servitude—a right in a neighbor's land, here a right to water—which was lost by mere disuse, without any adverse use by the owner of the burdened land.

Footnote: Savigny argued for reading actore in the passage about the wrongful use of a way; Mommsen, in his edition of the Digest, rejects that reading—rightly, it seems to me.

Understood this way, there could be no succession between a person dispossessed of a thing against his will and the wrongful possessor. Without the element of consent, there is no room for the analogy just explained. Accordingly, it is laid down that there is no joining of times when the possession is wrongful, and the only listed means of succeeding to a thing are by will, sale, gift, or some other right.

The argument now returns to the English law, strengthened by some general conclusions. It has been shown that in both of the systems whose union produced our law, the rules governing conveyance—the transfer of specific things between living persons—were deeply affected by notions drawn from inheritance. It was shown earlier that in England the principles of inheritance applied directly to the singular succession of the heir to a specific fee, as well as to the universal succession of the executor. Given their history, it would be remarkable if the same principles had not affected other singular successions too. It will soon appear that they have. Without being too careful about the order of proof, I will take up first the joining of times in prescription, since that has just been discussed so fully. The English law on the subject turns out, on examination, to be the same as the Roman in extent, in reason, and in expression. Indeed it is largely copied from the Roman. For servitudes—rights of way, of light, and the like—make up the chief class of prescriptive rights, and our law of servitudes is mainly Roman. Prescriptions, it is said, "are properly personal, and therefore are always alleged in the person of him who prescribes, viz. that he and all those whose estate he hath, etc.; therefore, a bishop or a parson may prescribe,... for there is a perpetual estate, and a perpetual succession and the successor hath the very same estate which his predecessor had, for that continues, though the person alters, like the case of the ancestor and the heir." So too in a modern case, where by statute twenty years' dispossession extinguished the owner's title, the Court of Queen's Bench said the right would probably pass to the possessor "if the same person, or several persons, claiming one from the other by descent, will or conveyance, had been in possession for the twenty years." "But.... such twenty years' possession must be either by the same person, or several persons claiming one from the other, which is not the case here."

In a word, it is equally clear that the continuous possession of privies in title—persons linked one to the next in a single chain of title, successors in the Roman phrase—has all the effect of the continuous possession of one person, and that no such effect is given to the continuous possession of different persons who are not in the same chain of title. One who dispossesses another of land cannot add the time during which his disseisee used a way to the period of his own use, while one who bought the land can.

Footnote: Of course, if a right had already been acquired before the disseisin, different considerations would apply. If the right claimed is one of those regarded as incident to land, as explained in the following Lecture, the disseisor will have it.

The authorities quoted make it plain that the English law proceeds on the same theory as the Roman. One who buys land from another gets the very same estate his seller had. He is in of the same fee, or hereditas, which means, as I have shown, that he sustains the same persona. On the other hand, one who wrongfully dispossesses another—a disseisor—gets a different estate. He is in of a new fee, although the land is the same; and a good deal of technical reasoning rests on this doctrine.

In the matter of prescription, therefore, buyer and seller were identified, like heir and ancestor. But the question remains whether this identification bore fruit in other parts of the law as well, or whether it stayed confined to one branch, where the Roman law was grafted onto the English stock.

There can be no doubt which answer is more probable, but it cannot be proved without difficulty. As has been said, the heir ceased at an early date to be his ancestor's general representative. And how far even he was identified with the ancestor came to be a matter of dispute. Common sense kept fiction in check here, as elsewhere in the common law. But there can be no doubt that in matters directly concerning the estate, the identification of heir and ancestor has continued to the present day. And since an estate in fee simple has been shown to be a distinct persona, we should expect to find a similar identification of buyer and seller in this part of the law, if anywhere.

Where the land was devised—left by will—the analogy applied with particular ease. There is no difference in principle between a devise of a piece of land by will and a conveyance of it by deed; but the dramatic resemblance of a devisee to an heir is stronger than that of a grantee. It will be remembered that one of the Roman jurists said a legatarius (legatee or devisee) was in a certain sense quasi heres, "as if an heir." The English courts have now and then used similar expressions. In one case a testator owned a rent and divided it by will among his sons, and one of the sons then brought debt for his share. Two of the judges admitted that the testator could not have divided the tenant's liability by a grant or deed in his lifetime, but thought it was otherwise with a division by will. Their reasoning was that "the devise is quasi an act of law, which shall inure without attornment, and shall make a sufficient privity, and so it may well be apportioned by this means." (Attornment was the tenant's formal acknowledgment of a new landlord; privity is the legal relation that lets one person sue another.) So too Lord Ellenborough said, in a case where a lessor and his heirs were entitled to end a lease on notice, that a devisee of the land, as heres factus—an heir made by the will—would be understood to have the same right.

But wills of land were allowed only exceptionally, by local custom, until the reign of Henry VIII; and since the main doctrines of conveyancing had been settled long before then, we must look further back, and to other sources, for their explanation. We shall find it in the history of warranty. That, and the modern law of covenants running with the land, will be treated in the next Lecture.

Lecture XI: Successions—II. Inter Vivos

A century after the Norman Conquest, the principal contracts known to the common law and suable in the King's Courts were suretyship and debt. The heir, as the general representative of his ancestor's rights and obligations, was liable for the ancestor's debts, and he was the proper person to sue for the debts owed to the estate. By the time of Edward III, in the fourteenth century, this had changed. Debts had ceased to concern the heir except secondarily. The executor took his place, both for collecting debts and for paying them. It is said that even when the heir was bound, he could not be sued unless the executor had no assets.

But there was another ancient obligation which had a different history. I mean the warranty which arose upon the transfer of property—the seller's duty to stand behind the title he had passed. We should call it a contract. But it probably presented itself to the minds of Glanvill's predecessors simply as a duty or obligation which the law attached to a transaction aimed at something else. In the same way, the liability of a bailee, which is now treated as arising from his undertaking, was originally raised by the law out of the position in which he stood toward third persons.

After the Conquest we do not hear much of warranty except in connection with land, and this fact at once accounts for its having had a different history from debt. The obligation of warranty was to defend the title, and, if the defence failed, to give the evicted owner other land of equal value. If an ancestor had conveyed lands with warranty, this obligation could not be fulfilled by his executor, but only by his heir, to whom his other lands had descended. Conversely, when a warranty had been made to a grantee who then died, his heir was the only person with an interest in enforcing it, because the land descended to him. So the heir continued to represent his ancestor in the rights and obligations of warranty after the executor had relieved him of the debts, just as, before that time, he had represented his ancestor in every respect.

If a man was sued for property which he had bought from another, the regular course of litigation was for the defendant to summon in his seller to take charge of the defence. The seller in turn summoned in his own seller, if he had one, and so on, until the suit reached a party in the chain of title who finally took the burden of the case upon himself. To call in a warrantor this way was to "vouch" him; he was then the "vouchee." A contrast which was stated early between the Lombard law and the Roman held equally between the Anglo-Saxon and the Roman. It was said that the Lombard presents his grantor, while the Roman stands in his grantor's shoes—Langobardus dat auctorem, Romanus stat loco auctoris.

Suppose, now, that A gave land to B, and B conveyed it on to C. If C was sued by D, who claimed a better title, C practically got the benefit of A's warranty. When C summoned B, B would summon A, and so A would defend the case in the end. But it might happen that B had died between the time he conveyed to C and the time the action was begun. If B left an heir, C might still be protected. But suppose B left no heir. Then C got no help from A, who in the other event would have defended his suit. This no doubt was the law in the Anglo-Saxon period, but it was plainly unsatisfactory. We may guess, with a good deal of confidence, that a remedy would be found as soon as there was machinery to make one possible. The machinery was furnished by the Roman law. Under that system the buyer stood in the place of his seller, and a fusion of the Roman rule with the Anglo-Saxon was all that was needed.

Bracton, who modelled his book on the writings of the medieval civilians, shows how this thought was used. He first puts the case of a conveyance with the usual clause binding the grantor and his heirs to warrant and defend the grantee and his heirs. Then he goes on: "Again one may make his gift greater and make other persons quasi heirs [of his grantee], although, in fact, they are not heirs, as when he says in the gift, to have and to hold to such a one and his heirs, or to whomsoever he shall choose to give or assign the said land, and I and my heirs will warrant to the said so and so, and his heirs, or to whomsoever he shall choose to give or assign the said land, and their heirs, against all persons. In which case if the grantee shall have given or assigned the land, and then have died without heirs, the [first] grantor and his heirs begin to hold the place of the first grantee and his heirs, and are in place of the first grantee's heir (pro herede) so far as concerns warranting to his assigns and their heirs according to the clause contained in the first grantor's charter, which would not be but for the mention of assigns in the first gift. But so long as the first grantee survives, or his heirs, they are held to warranty, and not the first grantor."

Here we see that, to entitle the assign—the person to whom the grantee later transferred the land—to the benefit of the first grantor's warranty, assigns had to be mentioned in the original grant and covenant. The scope of the ancient obligation was not extended without the warrantor's assent. But when it was extended, it was not by a contrivance like a modern letter of credit, which is an open offer to whoever comes forward under its terms. Such a conception would have been impossible at that stage of the law. By mentioning assigns, the first grantor did not offer a covenant to anyone who might afterward buy the land. If that had been the notion, a contract would have bound the first grantor directly to the assign as soon as the land was sold, and there would have been two warranties arising from the same clause—one to the first grantee, and a second to the assign. But in fact the assign recovered on the original warranty to the first grantee. He could come on the first grantor only after his immediate grantor's heirs had failed. By mentioning assigns, the first grantor simply enlarged the limits of his grantee's succession. The assign could vouch the first grantor only on the principles of succession. That is to say, he could do so only when, the first grantee's blood having failed, the first grantee's feudal relation to the first grantor—his persona—came to be carried on by the assign.

Footnote: Bracton says so in another place: what is said of heirs may equally be said of assigns, and that warranty must be made to assigns by way of the gift is proved by a case in the circuit of W. de Ralegh—"and this especially, if the first chief lord and first feoffor has taken the homage and service of the assign." Bracton also gives the reason which led to assigns being mentioned at all.

This was not only carrying out the fiction with technical consistency; it was using the fiction with good sense, as fictions generally have been used in the English law. In practice it made little difference whether the assign got the benefit of the first grantor's warranty mediately or immediately, so long as he got it. The trouble arose where he could not summon the mesne grantor—the one in the middle of the chain—and the new right was given him for that case alone. Later, the assign did not have to wait for his immediate grantor's blood to fail, but could take advantage of the first grantor's warranty from the beginning.

Footnote: I do not stop to inquire whether this was due to the statute of Quia Emptores, by which the assign was made to hold directly of the first grantor, or whether some other explanation must be found.

It might be suggested that all this goes to show that the first grantor's duty to warrant arose from the assign's becoming his man and owing him homage. The answer is that the first grantor was not bound unless he had mentioned assigns in his grant, homage or no homage. In this Bracton is confirmed by all the later authorities.

Another rule, on which there are vast stores of forgotten learning, will show how exactly the fiction fell in with the earlier law. Only those who were privy in estate with the person to whom the warranty was originally given could vouch the original warrantor—that is, only those who held the very estate which that person had held. Looking back to the early procedure, we can see that of course only those in the same chain of title could get the benefit of a former owner's warranty, even at one remove. The ground on which a man was bound to warrant was that he had conveyed the property to the person who summoned him. So a man could summon no one but his own grantor, and the successive vouchers came to an end when the last vouchee could not call on anyone from whom he had bought. Now, when the process was shortened, no one was made liable to summons who would not have been liable before. The present owner was allowed to vouch directly those who otherwise would have been bound indirectly to defend his title, but no others. So he could summon only those from whom his grantor derived his title. But this was expressed equally well in terms of the fiction. To vouch, the present owner must have the estate of the person to whom the warranty was made. As every lawyer knows, the estate does not mean the land. It means the status, or persona, in regard to that land which another formerly sustained. The same word was used in alleging a right by prescription—"that he and those whose estate he hath have for time whereof memory runneth not to the contrary," etc.—and it will be remembered that there the word answers to the same requirement of succession.

To return to Bracton: it must be understood that his description of assigns as quasi heredes, "quasi heirs," is not accidental. He describes them that way whenever he has occasion to speak of them. He even pushes the reasoning from the analogy of inheritance to extremes, and refers to it in countless passages. For instance: "It should be noted that of heirs some are true heirs and some quasi heirs, in place of heirs, etc.; true heirs by way of succession quasi heirs, etc. by the form of the gift; such as assigns," etc.

If it should be suggested that Bracton's language is only a piece of medieval scholasticism, there are several answers. In the first place, it is nearly contemporary with the first appearance of the right in question. This is shown by his citing authority for it, as for something which might be disputed. He says, "And that warranty must be made to assigns according to the form of the gift is proved [by a case] in the circuit of W. de Ralegh, about the end of the roll," etc. It is not justifiable to assume that a contemporary explanation of a new rule had nothing to do with its appearance. Again, it is clear, as has been shown, that the assign got the benefit of the warranty to the first grantee, not of a new one to himself. And Bracton's explanation of how this was worked out falls in with what we have seen of the course of the German and Anglo-Saxon law, and with the pervading thought of the Roman law. Finally, and most important, the requirement that the assign should be in of the first grantee's estate—should hold that same estate—has remained a requirement from that day to this. The fact that the same thing is required, in the same words, as in prescription goes far to show that the same technical thought has governed both.

I have said that Glanvill's predecessors probably regarded warranty as an obligation incident to a conveyance rather than as a contract. But when it became usual to insert the undertaking to warrant in a deed, or charter of feoffment, it lost something of its former isolation as a duty standing by itself, and it could be generalized. It was a promise by deed, and a promise by deed was a covenant. No doubt it was a covenant with peculiar consequences attached to it. It also differed from some other covenants in the scope of its obligation, as will be shown later. But still it was a covenant, and it could sometimes be sued on as such. The Year Books of Edward III speak of it as a covenant which "falls in the blood," as distinguished from those where the acquittance fell on the land and not on the person.

The importance of this circumstance lies in the way the law of warranty worked upon the other covenants which took its place. When the old actions for land gave way to more modern and speedier forms, warrantors were no longer vouched in to defend, and if a grantee was evicted, damages took the place of a grant of other land. The ancient warranty disappeared. It was replaced by the covenants which we still find in our deeds—the covenants for title: for seisin, for right to convey, against incumbrances, for quiet enjoyment, of warranty, and for further assurance. But the principles on which an assign could have the benefit of these covenants were derived from those which governed warranty, as anyone may see by looking at the earlier decisions.

For instance, the question of what was a sufficient assignment to give an assign the benefit of a covenant for quiet enjoyment was argued and decided on the authority of the old cases of warranty.

The assign, as in warranty, came in under the old covenant with the first covenantee, not by any new right of his own. Thus, in an action by an assign on a covenant for further assurance, the defendant set up a release given by the original covenantee after the suit had begun. The court held that the assignee should have the benefit of the covenant. "They held, that although the breach was in the time of the assignee, yet if the release had been by the covenantee (who is a party to the deed, and from whom the plaintiff derives) before any breach, or before the suit commenced, it had been a good bar to the assignee from bringing this writ of covenant. But the breach of the covenant being in the time of the assignee,... and the action brought by him, and so attached in his person, the covenantee cannot release this action wherein the assignee is interested." The covenantee, even after assignment, remains the legal party to the contract. The assign comes in under him, and does not end his control over it until, by breach and action, a new right attaches in the assign's person, distinct from the rights derived from the persona of his grantor. Later, as the original foundation of his rights sank gradually out of sight, the assign got a more independent standing, and a release after assignment became ineffectual, at least in the case of a covenant to pay rent.

Footnote: These cases show an order of development parallel to the history of the assignment of other contracts which are not negotiable.

Only privies in estate with the original covenantee can have the benefit of covenants for title. It has been shown that the earlier history of the ancient warranty required a similar limit on its benefits before the assign was allowed to sue, and that the fiction by which he got that right could not carry it beyond that limit. This analogy also was followed. For instance, a tenant in tail male—one whose estate could pass only to his male descendants—made a lease for years with covenants of right to let and for quiet enjoyment, and then died without male issue. The lessee assigned the lease to the plaintiff. The plaintiff was soon turned out, and brought an action on the covenant against the lessor's executor. It was held that he could not recover, because he was not privy in estate with the original covenantee. The lease, which was the original covenantee's estate, had come to an end before it was assigned to the plaintiff: it ended with the lessor's death and the termination of the estate tail out of which it had been granted.

The only point still needed to make the analogy between covenants for title and warranty complete was a requirement that assigns be mentioned before they could sue. In modern times, of course, such a requirement, if it existed, would be purely formal. It would matter only as an ear-mark by which to trace the history of a doctrine. It would help our studies if we could say that wherever assigns are to get the benefit of a covenant as privies in estate with the covenantee, they must be mentioned in the covenant. Whether such a requirement exists or not would be hard to tell from the decisions alone. It is commonly supposed not to. But the popular opinion on this trifling point springs from a failure to understand one of the great antinomies of the law—one of its built-in contradictions—which must now be explained.

So far as we have gone, we have found that wherever one party steps into the rights or obligations of another without in turn filling the situation of fact of which those rights or obligations are the legal consequences, the substitution is explained by a fictitious identification of the two individuals, derived from the analogy of inheritance. We have seen this identification consciously worked out in the creation of the executor, whose entire status is governed by it. We have seen it still consciously applied in the narrower sphere of the heir. And we have found it hidden at the root of the relation between buyer and seller in at least two cases, prescription and warranty, when the history of that relation is dug into deeply enough.

But although it would be more symmetrical if this analysis exhausted the subject, there is another class of cases in which rights are transferred on a wholly different plan. In the last Lecture, explaining the succession between buyer and seller by which a prescriptive right is created—such as a right of way over neighboring land to the land bought and sold—I showed that a man who had wrongfully taken possession of the land by force, instead of buying it, would not be treated as a successor, and would get no benefit from his disseisee's earlier use of the way. But when the former possessor has already gained a right of way before he is turned out, a new principle comes into play. Suppose the owner of the land over which the way ran stopped it up, and was sued by the wrongful possessor. A defence that the disseisor had not succeeded to the former owner's rights would not prevail. The disseisor would be protected in his possession of the land against everyone but the rightful owner, and he would be protected equally in his use of the way. This rule of law does not rest on a succession between the wrongful possessor and the owner, which is out of the question. Nor can it be defended on the same ground as the protection given to the occupation of the land itself. That ground is that the law defends possession against everything except a better title. But, as I have said before, the common law does not recognize possession of a way. A man who has used a way for ten years without title cannot sue even a stranger for stopping it. He was a trespasser at the beginning, and he is nothing but a trespasser still. There must be a right against the servient owner—the owner of the land the way runs over—before there is a right against anybody else. At the same time, it is clear that a way is no more capable of possession because somebody else has a right to it than if no one had.

How comes it, then, that one who has neither title nor possession is so far favored? The answer is to be found, not in reasoning, but in a failure to reason. In the first Lecture of this course, the thought we now have to deal with was shown in its theological stage, to borrow Comte's well-known phrase, as where an axe was made the object of criminal process; and also in its metaphysical stage, where only the language of personification survived, but survived to confuse reasoning. The case I have put seems to illustrate the latter. The language of the law of easements was built up out of similes drawn from persons at a time when noxal surrender was still familiar. Then, as often happens, language reacted upon thought, and conclusions about the rights themselves were drawn from the terms in which they happened to be expressed. When one estate was said to be enslaved to another, or a right of way was said to be a quality or incident of a neighboring piece of land, men's minds were not alert to see that these phrases were only so many personifying metaphors, which explained nothing unless the figure of speech was true.

The French commentator Rogron deduced the negative nature of servitudes—the Roman name for easements—from the rule that the land owes the services, not the person: Praedium non persona servit. For, said Rogron, since the land alone is bound, it can be bound only passively. Austin called this an "absurd remark." But the jurists from whom we have inherited our law of easements were content with no better reasoning. Papinian himself wrote that servitudes cannot be partially extinguished, because they are owed by lands, not by persons. Celsus decides in this way the very case I took for my illustration: even if possession of a dominant estate is acquired by forcibly throwing out the owner, the way will be retained, since the estate is possessed in the quality and condition in which it is taken. The commentator Godefroi tersely adds that there are two such conditions, slavery and freedom; and his antithesis is as old as Cicero. So, in another passage, Celsus asks what else the rights attaching to land are but qualities of that land. So Justinian's Institutes speak of servitudes which inhere in buildings. So Paulus speaks of such rights as being accessory to bodies. "And thus," adds Godefroi, "rights may belong to inanimate things." It followed easily from all this that a sale of the dominant estate carried existing easements with it—not because the buyer succeeded to the seller's place, but because land is bound to land.

All these figures imply that land is capable of having rights, as Austin recognizes. Indeed, he even says that the land "is erected into a legal or fictitious person, and is styled 'praedium dominans.'" But if this means anything more than an explanation of what the Roman metaphors imply, it goes too far. The dominant estate was never "erected into a legal person," either by conscious fiction or as a result of primitive beliefs. It could not sue or be sued, as a ship can in the admiralty. No one supposes that its possessor could maintain an action for an interference with an easement that happened before his time, as an heir could for an injury to property of the hereditas jacens—the estate lying vacant between the death and the heir's entry. If land had ever been systematically treated as capable of acquiring rights, the time of a disseisee might have been added to that of the wrongful occupant. The ground would have been that the land, and not this or that individual, was gaining the easement, and that a long association between the enjoyment of the privilege and the land was enough. That has never been the law.

All that can be said is that the metaphors and similes naturally led to the rule which has prevailed. And since this rule was as good as any other, or at least was unobjectionable, it was drawn from the figures of speech without attracting attention, before anyone had seen that they were only figures, which proved nothing and justified no conclusion.

Because easements were said to belong to the dominant estate, it followed that whoever possessed the land had a right of the same degree over what was incidental to it. If the true meaning had been that a way or other easement admits of possession, is taken possession of along with the land to which it runs, and is protected in its enjoyment on the same grounds as possession in other cases, the thought could have been understood. But that was not the meaning of the Roman law, and, as has been shown, it is not the doctrine of ours. We must take it that easements have become an incident of land through an unconscious and unreasoned assumption that a piece of land can have rights. It need not be said that this is absurd, although the rules of law which are based upon it are not so.

Absurd or not, the similes as well as the principles of the Roman law reappear in Bracton. He says, "The servitude by which land is subjected to [other] land, is made on the likeness of that by which man is made the slave of man." "For rights belong to a free tenement, as well as tangible things.... They may be called rights or liberties with regard to the tenements to which they are owed, but servitudes with regard to the tenements by which they are owed.... One estate is free, the other subjected to slavery." "[A servitude] may be called an arrangement by which house is subjected to house, farm to farm, holding to holding." No passage has met my eye in which Bracton expressly decides that an easement goes with the dominant estate upon a disseisin. But what he says leaves little doubt that he followed the Roman law in this as in other things.

The writ against a disseisor was for "so much land and its appurtenances"—its attached rights—which must mean that he who had the land, even wrongfully, had the appurtenances. So Bracton says an action is in rem "whether it is for the principal thing, or for a right which adheres to the thing,... as when one sues for a right of way, ... since rights of this sort are all incorporeal things, and are quasi possessed and reside in bodies, and cannot be got or kept without the bodies in which they inhere, nor in any way had without the bodies to which they belong." And again: "Since rights do not admit of delivery, but are transferred with the thing in which they are, that is, the bodily thing, he to whom they are transferred forthwith has a quasi possession of those rights as soon as he has the body in which they are."

Footnote: All these passages assume that a right has already been acquired and inheres in the land.

There is no doubt about the later law, as was said at the outset.

We have thus traced into our law two competing and mutually inconsistent principles. On the one hand is the conception of succession, or privity; on the other, that of rights inhering in a thing. Bracton seems to have wavered a little, from a sense that the two might come into conflict. The benefit of a warranty was confined to those who, by the act and consent of the grantee, succeeded to his place. It did not pass to assigns unless assigns were mentioned. Bracton supposes grants of easements both with and without mention of assigns, which looks as if he thought the difference might matter with regard to easements also. He further says that if an easement is granted to A, his heirs and assigns, all of these are allowed the use in succession by the form of the grant, and all others are wholly excluded. But he is not speaking of what a disseisor's rights would be as against someone without a better title, and he immediately adds that these are rights over a bodily object belonging to a bodily object.

It may be doubted whether the mention of assigns was ever necessary to attach an easement to land, and it is very certain that it did not stay necessary for long. Even so, the difficulty I have referred to grew greater as time went on. It would have been easily disposed of if the only rights which could be annexed to land were easements, such as a right of way. It might then have been said that these were limited interests in land, less than ownership in extent but like it in kind, and therefore properly transferred by the same means as ownership. A right of way, it might have been argued, is not to be approached from the point of view of contract. It does not presuppose any promise on the part of the servient owner. His obligation, though more troublesome to him than to others, is the same as everyone else's. It is the purely negative duty not to obstruct or interfere with a right of property.

But although the test of which rights go with the land may have been something of that kind, it will not help us understand the cases without a good deal of explanation. For such rights might exist to active services, which had to be performed by whoever held the servient estate. It strikes our ear strangely to hear a right to services from an individual called a right of property, as distinguished from contract. Still, this will be found to have been the way such rights were regarded. Bracton argues that it is no wrong to the lord for the tenant to alienate—to transfer—land held by free and perfect gift, on the ground that the land is bound and charged with the services into whose hands soever it may come. The lord is said to have a fee in the homage and services; and therefore no entry upon the land which does not disturb them injures him. It is the tenement which imposes the obligation of homage, and the same is true of villein and other feudal services.

The law remained unchanged when feudal services took the form of rent. Even in our modern leases for a term of years, rent is still treated as something issuing out of the leased premises. So to this day, if you hire a whole house and it burns down, you have to pay without abatement, because you still have the land out of which the rent issues. But if you hire only a suite of rooms and they are burned, you pay rent no longer, because you no longer have the tenement out of which it comes.

It is obvious that this reasoning leads to the conclusion that a disseisor of the tenant would be bound as much as the tenant himself, and the early law adopted that conclusion. The lord could demand the services, or collect the rent, from anyone who had the land, because, as was said in language very like Bracton's, "the charge of the rent goes with the land."
